Three Readers sat behind a table. One was a sleepy old man who never opened his eyes. One was a florid gentleman with a gold cord and rings on every finger, who smiled at her as if she were a cake. The third was a woman of about sixty with grey hair drawn back tight enough to hurt and a blue cord at her shoulder. Her mouth looked as though it had been ruled with a straight-edge. The card in front of her said *Reader M. Crisp. Keeping.*

On the table were twelve covered dishes and a basin of brine.

"Grade the grains," said Reader Crisp. "Then tell us which of the objects in the room is kept. Then enter the hold and keep a drop of that brine. Begin."

The grains were easier than Aske's tray. Hester did them fast and aloud, and after the fourth the florid gentleman was making little murmuring noises of pleasure. Crisp wrote on a card and made no noise at all.

"The objects."

There were a dozen: a sprig of lavender under a glass bell, a pewter inkstand, a candle, a pear on a plate, a book, a clock, the Readers' own water-jug and glasses. Hester went and stood beside each in turn and breathed in. At the pear she stopped.

"Not that," said the florid gentleman, delighted. "Everyone picks the pear."

Hester did not pick the pear. It smelt of ripeness and nothing else. She moved on to the jug, and to the third glass beside it, the one nobody had drunk from, with a little water in the bottom. When she leaned close there was sweetness at the back of her tongue and a feather's weight on her eyelids. Shelf grain. Then she saw it: the water stood a hair higher on one side of the glass than the other. It had been kept in the act of being poured.

"The water in that glass," she said.

The florid gentleman began to laugh, helplessly, into his ringed hand. The sleeping man opened one eye. Crisp wrote.

"The hold," she said.

Hester had gone into the hold perhaps a thousand times, but only to read. She had never put her stillness into anything. She took a pipette of brine and let one drop fall, and as it fell she went down, all the way, as she had on the customs stool with the *Wren* grain on her tongue. She did not know what else to do, so she pushed: pushed the stillness out of herself and at the drop, as you put your shoulder to a door.

The drop stopped a quarter-inch below the pipette's mouth, a clear bead the morning light came through. It did not fall.

Her hands went cold to the wrist. There was a grey edge to the room and a roaring in her ears, and when it cleared the clock on the wall had moved a full minute, and the florid gentleman was on his feet.

"Sit down, Bassano," said Crisp, without looking at him. She touched the drop with a glass rod. It came away hard: a bead of salt-glass, round as a pearl, with water locked in it. She held it to the light.

"Crude," she said. "Deep. Square. You lagged a minute on a drop of brine. That's absurd." She laid the bead on her card. "Pass. Next."

* * *

Teo won his fleck, and was still telling her how pleased he was when the Proctor at the inner doors called the intake to order. The hundred and ten who had passed shuffled into a line along the brass, and the porter swung the far doors open.

There was no speech. There was only the Proctor, a heavy grey man with a list, calling names, and at each name a person stepping over the Line onto the stones beyond and being handed a folded grey gown. Some stepped over solemnly. Some hopped. The Haskish giant took it in one stride and looked back at it, puzzled, as if he had expected it to feel like something. The woman with the baby passed the baby to her husband, stepped over, turned, and took it back across, so that the baby was below the sea too.

"Anselm, Teodor."

Teo stepped over as he might have stepped into a church. On the far side he touched the brass wave at his throat and laughed at himself.

"Ferrers, Lucan."

One of the young men from the far wall went over. He was tall and fair and very straight, with a long elegant face and the expression of a man asked to attend something slightly beneath him. He took his gown as if it might be catching. When he turned, his eye passed along the line of waiting candidates and stopped on Hester's customs blue, and went faintly amused.

"I see Customs sends its sweepers now," he remarked, to nobody, not quite loud enough to be rude and not quite quiet enough not to be heard.

"Sweepers and assayers," said Hester. "Somebody has to check the weights. Your father's especially."

Somebody along the Line choked. Lucan Ferrers looked at her for a long, level moment with no expression at all, and turned away.

"Vail, Hester."

She stepped over. It was a strip of brass. It felt like nothing. She took her gown.

* * *

"Well, that was a good start," said a dry voice, as the intake was herded through the far doors. "Below the sea for a count of twenty and you've already insulted the Lord Exchequer's only son."

The woman who had fallen into step beside her was about thirty, small and quick, with a pointed face and dark hair cut square at the jaw. She wore the grey gown already, with a gold cord, and wore it well; she had altered the shoulders. She had a flat, fast Quellish accent and the air of someone pricing everything she passed, Hester included.

"Saskia Orme. Lumen. Bassano's Bench. Thaw Intake, so I've been here half a year longer than you and know where the privies are. Bassano likes to inflict me on people." She glanced at the carpetbag, the darned elbow. "Aske's charity case."

"Aske's privilege."

"Same thing, said nicely." Saskia did not smile. "Keep up. It's nine hundred steps to the Fifth and I'm not carrying anyone."

The tour went down.

That was the whole of it: down. The far doors gave onto a gallery cut in the salt, and the gallery gave onto the Stair, and Hester stood at its rail and looked.

The Stair was a spiral wide enough for four abreast, winding round a central well so deep that its bottom was lost in a soft brown dusk. The walls were salt, polished by nine hundred years of hands to the look of old ivory, and where lamps hung the light went into them a little way, so that they glowed pink near the brackets and paled to cream between. Down the centre of the well, from far above to far below, fell the Lantern's light, sent down from the mirrors through a shaft in the dome, straight and gold and solid as a rope. Salt motes turned in it.

On the walls, all the way down as far as she could see, there were names. Thousands of them, close-written in chalk in a hundred hands, the newest white and sharp, the oldest faded almost to the colour of the salt.

"The Settled," said Saskia. "Everyone who's gone down and not come back up quite right. They rechalk them every year at the Silent Supper. Don't read them. People stop and read them and then they cry and then they're late for Lumen."

The First Fathom: the Benches, tiers of salt seats worn into dishes by nine centuries of scholarly behinds; the Long Table, carved from the living floor. The Second: the Brinestacks, glimpsed through a door, shelves of books going back into the dark and shelves of white tablets that caught the light. At a desk by the door an old man sat so motionless that Hester took him for a carving until she saw that he was holding a pen.

"Callow," said Saskia. "Under-Archivist. He's past eighty and mostly Settled and nobody's seen him move since the Thaw. We call him the furniture. Don't knock him over."

The Third: a long gallery of numbered doors with brass plates. "Readers' workrooms. They call them Cells, which tells you what they think of us." Kilns breathing heat through a grating; a smell of hot glass. A door marked *3-17* with no name on it, only a tongue drawn in chalk that somebody had tried and failed to rub off.

"Aske's," said Saskia. "His aspirants did that. He thinks it's funny."

* * *

The Fourth Fathom was colder, and still. The Stair here was lined not with doors but with alcoves, and in the alcoves, on plinths, behind brass rails, were keepings.

"The Quiet Rooms," said Saskia, and for the first time her voice went down a little, as voices do in churches whether their owners believe or not.

There was a single red rose, open, in a casing no bigger than a jar: *Kept 304 Y.E.* There was dew on it. Hester looked at the dew, which had been on that rose for six hundred years.

And in the largest alcove there was a thing that stopped the whole group. It was a casing the height of a tall man, rising from a plinth of black stone, and inside it, top to bottom, ran a crooked fork of light. It was white and blinding, so bright it threw shadows on the alcove walls, and it did not flicker. It was a lightning bolt. Somebody, two hundred years ago, had caught one out on the White as it struck, and held it.

*Kept 712 Y.E. Reader Abelard Fenn and seven of the Choir. Of whom four Settled in the keeping.*

Teo had his hand on the brass wave at his throat. The Haskish giant took off his cap.

"Down," said Saskia, after a while, in her ordinary voice.

They passed the Choir, a vast oval chamber with a domed roof, empty, its floor worn in rings, where the sound of their feet came back off the roof a long time after. Then a pair of tall iron-grey doors with a brass plate: *FATHOMERS ONLY. BY ORDER OF THE WARDEN OF THE LOWER FATHOMS.*

The Stair went on past them a little way and ended in a landing, and on the landing was another door, smaller, of a grey stuff that was neither wood nor stone. It had been grown, Hester thought: salt, latticed in a close diamond pattern like a pantry screen, with a brass halcyon in the middle of it. It had no handle. There was a keyhole.

"And that," said Saskia, "is the Hush. Founders' workings. Nobody goes in, nobody comes out, don't ask."

Hester was not listening. Something was wrong with her teeth.

Not pain: a pressure so low it was not a sound, coming up through the soles of her feet and her shinbones and settling in her jaw, like standing on a bridge while a loaded dray goes over. Her back teeth ached with it. When she put her tongue to them she could almost taste it: a sweetness, an enormous weight. Something under the floor was holding itself still with all its might.

"You feel it?" said Teo. He had gone a little pale. "I hear it. Like a man humming in another room. A very big man. In a very big room."

"The Hush hums," said Saskia. "It always has. You get used to it." She looked at the two of them, and something that was not sardonic crossed her pointed face. "I didn't, much. Come on. Up. You'll want the Lantern before the Hour."

* * *

The Lantern gallery ran round the head of the tower, two hundred feet above the dome and open to the Sere, which came howling across the White from the east with salt in its teeth. Hester held the iron rail with both hands. Around her the mirrors, each the size of a door, turned on their iron arms and threw the sun off in sheets.

To the west lay Ambry. She found Coldwater Street without trying, and the long low roof of the Cold House at the end of it, against the wall.

To the east there was nothing. The plain went out flat and blazing to a place where the light was too much to look at, and landships moved on it, far off, their sails like flakes.

"What's that?" said Teo.

He was pointing down and east. A quarter of a mile out from the foot of the dome there was something dark on the White: a circle the size of a cart-wheel, crusted round the edge with salt, with a dull iron gleam at its centre and something that might have been chain.

"The Well-Cap," said the Proctor with the list, who had brought the intake up and was leaning on the rail beside them as if he owned it. "Top of the Founders' Shaft. Runs all the way down to the Hush, so they say. Capped and chained before anyone's grandmother was born, and never opened. We walk out once a year and check the chain."

"Lowside children dare each other to go and knock on it," said Saskia. "Your lot, Vail."

Hester looked at the dark circle on the white. It was a lonely thing to put in the middle of a plain.

* * *

By dusk she had a key and a cell. R-41, in the Rookery on the dome's western flank: a room cut in the salt, two cots, two chests, a washstand, a window of salt-glass the size of a tablecloth looking out toward Ambry, and cold. Teo had asked at the Bursar's table whether he might share with her, since he knew no one and she knew no one and they had both walked here. The clerk wrote them in without looking up.

"I should tell you I snore," Hester said.

"I'll hum," said Teo. "Hymns, in my sleep, in the third mode. We'll drown each other out."

Now they sat at the Long Table among a hundred and ten new grey gowns and five hundred old ones. By custom no lamp was lit, so that the only light came from candles: a long double row of small flames down three hundred feet of salt, and the dark going up overhead into the shaft.

Somebody high above rang a bell, once.

And the light came down.

It came from the head of the shaft, where the Glaziers stood on the Lantern gallery with their jars, and it came not as a beam but as a flood, so that for a moment it seemed to fall like water, splashing gold off the edges of the shaft and spilling into the hall. Then it was simply there. Noon. Full summer noon, at dusk, in a hall cut under the ground. Hester felt it on the top of her head and the backs of her hands. All down the table faces came up into it with their eyes shut. The candles went pale and useless. Somebody laughed out loud. There was a smell in it, faint, of cut hay and hot dust, and under the gold she heard, for a breath, a lark singing high up and a long way off, on some summer noon on the Shelf when the jars were filled.

"Oh," said Teo. Just that. He had not opened his eyes.

The light lasted perhaps a quarter of the bell. Then it went amber, and faded, and the candles came back, and the hall was a hall again, with six hundred people blinking in it and somebody starting to clap.

* * *

The First Taste came after the soup.

Second-years set five bowls of white salt-glass along the table, each with a little heap of grain and a placard: *First Fathom. Second. Third. Fourth. Fifth.* Then they stood back along the walls, grinning like people who know what is about to happen to someone else. The new intake rose a bench at a time and filed past, each taking a pinch from each dish on a horn spoon.

It started at the third dish: a gasp, then a woman laughing in a high startled voice with her hand to her face. At the fourth, people stopped laughing. At the fifth, the Haskish giant, just ahead of Hester, put his pinch on his tongue and stood there while tears ran out of his eyes and into his red beard. He was not crying. His face was perfectly calm. The tears simply ran, and in the candlelight they left white lines on his cheeks as they dried to salt.

And the second-years along the walls, all of them, began to bang their hands on the salt, slowly, a long rolling thunder of hands.

The first four were Shelf, Middling and Deep, and she knew them. At the fifth dish she put a few grains on her tongue.

The weight came down. Not so heavy as the *Wren*'s. Near it. The world went white and pressed against her face, and her eyes streamed, and she let them.

Sweet. Deep. And under the sweet, faint but there, the other thing. Bright and metallic, a bitten tongue: the taste of the beads on her brother's casing, standing in their lines toward the east.

Quick.

Teo was beside her, crying like a fountain, white-streaked, laughing at himself through it. He took her arm.

"You've gone the colour of the plate."

"The Fifth," she said. She wiped the salt from her face with her sleeve. "Do they always use the same grain? Every year?"

"No idea. Why?"

She did not answer. She looked up the table to the dais, where the Readers sat in their grey gowns and coloured cords. Near the middle a white head was bent toward a neighbour: Orsolya Thane, laughing at something, her square face pink in the candlelight. Two places along sat Aske, peering down the length of the table through his thick spectacles, searching, she thought, for her. When he found her he lifted his water-glass an inch, gravely, and drank.

* * *

That night the cold came into R-41 through the salt walls as water comes into a boat, slowly and from everywhere. Hester lay in her cot under her mother's coat and every blanket she owned. Across the cell Teo was not asleep either. She could tell, because he was not humming.

"May I ask you a thing?" he said, after a long time.

"You can ask."

"Why did you come? You don't seem like someone who wanted a gown."

She did not answer that. "Why did you leave the Abbey?"

"I had questions." He said it lightly, to the ceiling. "The Order's answer was more vespers. After nine years I found I wanted to know more than I wanted to be told. Which is either the beginning of wisdom or the end of my immortal soul, and I've a bet on with myself which." A pause. "Your turn."

She lay and looked at the window. Out across the White, Ambry's lights were small and orange and few. One of them, she thought, was the plinth-lamp in the Cold House, which Agathe kept trimmed all night.

"I've a brother," she said. "He's kept. At a brinery in the city. Eleven years."

Teo did not say *I'm sorry*. She was grateful for that.

"What's his name?"

"Tobias. Tib."

"What's he like?"

Nobody had asked her that in eleven years. She lay in the dark and found she did not know how to begin; and then she found she did.

"He draws landships," she said. "On everything."


# Chapter 5: First Principles

> What is kept is owed.
>
> — *The Halcyon Primer, Law the Fourth*

"The hold," said Reader Crisp, "is a posture. It is not a feeling. Anyone who tells you it is a feeling is selling you something, generally a book. Stand up."

Thirty-one aspirants stood up. They were on the Pans, the shallow evaporation ponds on the dome's southern shoulder, in the open air, in the Sere. Each pond was the size of a room and a hand deep, the brine in it the colour of rhubarb, and duckboards ran between them. Each aspirant stood at a little iron stand with a brass pipette and a dish of live brine, mixed fresh from Middling that morning and carried up by a porter in a steaming kettle. Live brine went dead in a few minutes if it was not worked, Crisp had told them, so they would kindly not waste it.

"Feet," said Crisp. "Under your hips. Knees soft. Not bent; soft. Shoulders down. Lower than that, Anselm, you are not a coat on a hook. Hands open. Jaw open. The jaw is where most of you keep your fear and I want it out of there. Now breathe in for six and out for nine. Nine, Ferrers, not seven. Again."

Hester breathed. The wind lifted the brine into ripples and the ripples caught the low sun. Beside her Teo was breathing with an expression of great effort, like a man trying to remember a name. Beyond him the Haskish giant, whose name was Radik, had his eyes shut and was swaying slightly, as if listening to music. At the end of the row Lucan Ferrers stood with his heels together, breathing with the bored precision of a man who has been taught to do everything correctly the first time and resents being asked twice.

"The heart follows the breath," said Crisp, walking behind them on the duckboards. "The body follows the heart. The attention follows the body. You are not *trying* to be still. Trying is motion. You are arranging yourself so that motion has nowhere to start. When you are arranged, the stillness is already there; you are merely uncovering it. Then you lend it. A drop. One. From the pipette, onto the brine. Keep it as it falls."

Pipettes came up all along the row. Most of the drops fell into the pans with small, ordinary splashes. One, three stands down, a Quellish girl's, slowed in the air like honey and then fell anyway. Lucan Ferrers's did not fall. It hung a quarter-inch under the mouth of his pipette, clear and small and exact, and Crisp, passing behind him, said, "Shallow. Neat. Again," and walked on, and Lucan's jaw tightened.

Hester lifted her pipette. She went down, the way she knew how, all the way, and the Pans and the wind went away and there was only the weight at the back of her eyes and the drop forming at the brass mouth, swelling, letting go.

She kept it.

It did not hang the way Lucan's had hung. It stopped dead two inches below the pipette, and it stopped hard. In the instant before the world came back she felt the stillness go out of her and into it like coins pouring out of a purse, all at once. Then the cold hit. She came up gasping, her hands like ice on the iron stand, a grey fog at the edges of everything, and the knowledge that a little time had gone missing. Not a minute, this time. A few seconds. The wind had changed on the pond.

The drop was not a drop any more. It was a bead of salt-glass the size of a pea, hard and clear and slightly flattened on the bottom where it had begun to fall. Water trembled in it.

"Vail," said Crisp, behind her. "Return it."

Hester did not know how.

"The release," said Crisp. "We covered it in the first quarter of the bell. Were you attending?"

She had been attending. Crisp had said that the release was the reverse of the hold: that one opened the stillness and let it go, as a hand opens. She had said it in five sentences, briskly, as if it were obvious, and Hester had written all five down. She opened her hands, which were stiff with cold, and tried to do with her mind what her hands were doing.

The bead hung. She went still again and found it, the small hard weight of her own stillness in it, and tried to give it up. She could not. Every time she reached for it, something in her closed round it, the way a fist closes round a coin in a crowd.

"Again," said Crisp.

Down the row, Teo had kept a drop on his fourth attempt, wobbling, and returned it on his first, so that it fell into the pan with a little splash and a puff of steam. He looked at the steam with his mouth open. Saskia, two stands down, kept and returned and kept and returned like someone shuffling cards. Lucan returned his neatly, and then, glancing along the row at Hester's bead, did not smile, quite.

"Again," said Crisp.

It took a quarter of the bell. In the end Crisp leaned past her and touched the bead with a glass rod, and it came away whole, still kept, and Crisp put it in a little dish and set the dish on Hester's stand.

"You hold like a miser, Vail," she said. Not unkindly; as if reading off a scale. "Deep, square, and you'll not let go of a fleck. It is generally the people who have had to hold on to everything who cannot let go of anything." She looked at Hester with her ruled mouth. "That bead's yours. Carry it. When you can return it, you'll have learned something. Next."

* * *

"Horology," said Teo, "is the study of time."

"I know what it is."

"Then you know more than I do. At the Abbey we were told time was a gift of the Tide. I asked what it was a gift *of*, exactly, and was set to scrub the refectory for a month." He was eating a bread roll as they went down the Stair, scattering crumbs on the chalk names. "Reader Sorle is supposed to be a monster. The second-years say he makes people cry. Not salt crying. Actual crying. A Physic aspirant last year walked out in the middle and joined the Glaziers."

"Why does anyone take it?"

"It's required. Without Horology, says the Primer, a Keeper is a cook." He considered. "I like cooks."

The Black Bench was a small steep lecture-room off the First Fathom, its tiers so close that the back row had their knees in the necks of those in front. At the bottom stood a slate on an easel and a table, and on the table two things under a cloth. A man was writing on the slate with his back to the room. He did not turn round.

He was tall, stooped, and thin as a heron. His grey gown had a black cord and was worn to green at the elbows, and his long white hair was tied back with a piece of string. When the last aspirant had sat down he went on writing for some time. Then he put down the chalk, dusted his fingers one by one, and turned.

"I am Sorle," he said. His voice was a surprise: deep and beautiful, the voice of a man who might have been an actor if he had not been something more disagreeable. "This is Horology. You are compelled to attend it, as I am compelled to deliver it, so we may at least begin from a position of mutual resentment. I am told you have learned to keep a drop of brine. Congratulations. So has every washerwoman in Quell." On the slate, in a fine clear hand, was written: *What is kept is owed.*

"The Fourth Law. You will have learned it as a jingle. I am going to tell you what it means. When you kept your drop, you held it in its moment. The drop was falling; it had somewhere to be. You prevented it. Time, however, did not stop. The world proceeded about its business and left your drop behind, and every instant your drop stays kept, the difference between where it is and where the world has got to grows. That difference is a pressure. We call it arrears." He wrote a line of figures. "It is proportional to the mass of what you keep. To the duration. And to the *liveliness* of the thing kept: how much it was doing, how much it wanted to go on doing, at the moment you stopped it. What happens to the arrears when you return the drop?"

The Black Bench studied its knees.

"Ferrers," said Sorle, without looking.

Lucan, in the third tier, sat up. "It discharges, sir. As heat."

"As heat. And?"

"And sound. A small report."

"And? Come, Ferrers. You've had tutors. Gerrit Moll taught you your arrears at the age of twelve, at four sols the hour, and he was a fool, but he wasn't that much of a fool. And?"

Lucan's long face had gone faintly pink. "And motion, sir. If the keeping is large enough."

"Large enough to do what?"

"I—to do damage, sir."

"To do damage." Sorle looked up at him with his pale heron's eyes. "To break bones, Ferrers. To crack stone. To shatter glass at forty yards. A grown man kept ten years and returned in a hurry discharges enough arrears to blow the doors off a Brinery, which is why the Physics return them over six hours in a warm bath, and why it costs what it does to have one's grandmother back. You knew that. You said *damage* because you have never in your life seen any, and you thought it the polite word. There are no polite words in Horology. Sit down." Lucan had not stood up. He sat, somehow, lower. "Arrears are the bill. Time keeps the books, and she is a better clerk than any of you will ever be. Every keeping is a lie we tell time, and time collects."

He took the cloth off the table.

Under it were two keepings on little glass stands. One was a big brown moth with eyes on its wings, kept in flight in a casing the size of a hen's egg, so old that the salt had gone faintly amber. The other was a fresh casing, clear as water, round a single green leaf. Between them, casing to casing, ran a thread of plain white cotton, wet with brine.

"The Fifth Law," said Sorle. "Who has it?"

"Still runs to quick," said several voices.

"Still runs to quick. Stillness is not a property, fixed in a casing like colour in a glass. It is a quantity, and like heat it moves from where there is more of it to where there is less, if you give it a road. A road means live brine. Continuous. Unbroken. Air will not carry it. Dry salt will not. Glass will not. Wishing will not. The moth"—he touched the amber casing with one long finger—"was kept by my late wife, in the year eight hundred and sixty-four. It has been growing stiller for thirty-nine years, as old keepings do, until it is very still indeed. The leaf I kept this morning before breakfast. It is quick. And I have joined them with a thread of live brine." He stepped back. "Watch."

For a while nothing happened. The back rows shifted. Someone yawned.

Then the moth's casing began to weep.

Hester saw it before anyone. She had been watching for it without knowing she was watching, and when the first bead came up on the amber salt she was already leaning forward with her hands flat on the tier in front of her. A pinhead, on the near side, where the thread was tied. Then another. They were not scattered. They came up in a line, a thumb's length, running across the curve of the casing toward the knot. Then another beside it. All the beads on the moth's casing stood in lines, and all the lines pointed down and across, toward the thread, toward the leaf, toward the quick.

She was not in the room. She was in the Cold House, in the fourth row under the third window, looking down at beads above a boy's curled hand, running in lines toward the eastern foot of the plinth.

"The stiller keeping," said Sorle's voice, from a long way off, "gives up its stillness to the quicker, along the brine. You will observe the weeping. It is not damp. It is not age. It is the casing being drawn down, in the direction of the draw. Leave them so for a month, and the moth would come back and fly, and the leaf would be the stillest thing on this table." He dropped the cloth over them. "It is the only law of the five that has never been disputed, chiefly because it is the only one anybody can be bothered to watch. Questions."

Hester's hand went up. She did not remember deciding to put it up.

Sorle looked at it. Then at her: the white cord on her shoulder, the darned customs blue under the grey.

"Aske's," he said. "The customs woman. What?"

"The weeping," Hester said. Her voice sounded strange to her. "If you saw that pattern on a casing. A kept person. Lines, like that, all one way. What would it mean?"

The room had gone quiet. Sorle looked at her for a long moment, his head a little on one side, like a heron at the edge of a pond who has seen something move.

"It would mean," he said, "that someone had run a thread."

Then he wiped the slate and wrote across it in large letters: *THE STILLNESS OF THE GRAIN.* And began, in the same beautiful, indifferent voice, to tell them where stillness came from.

He said the Conclave held that stillness was a property of the Hollow Sea's salt, formed at the Ebb under pressures no one understood, and that this was what they would be examined on. There was another view, he said, in a tone that made plain it was his and he did not care what they thought of it. Grain did not *have* stillness. Grain *was* stillness: a single moment, held, and the salt was only what that moment looked like when one dug it up. It was possible, on this view, that the Ostrine Sea had not gone anywhere at all. That it was merely paused.

Somebody in the back row laughed. Then several people did. A voice, not quite low enough, said, *Sorle's Folly*, and the laughing got louder.

Sorle waited until it stopped. He looked, if anything, faintly pleased, as a man is pleased when a dog performs its trick.

"Yes," he said. "That is what they call it. Write that down, too. You will be expected to refute it, and you will receive full marks for doing so." He put down the chalk. "Good morning."

* * *

The days fell into a shape.

Keeping on the Pans at first bell, in the wind, with Crisp walking behind them like a sergeant. Horology at the third. Lumen with Reader Bassano, the florid gentleman from the Entry Assay, in the Glaziers' kilns on the Third Fathom, among racks of jars of every kind of kept light: candlelight, firelight, the green light of the Weeps at dusk, a single jar of moonlight that he took down with both hands and did not let them touch. "Light," Bassano said, waving a jar so that his rings flashed, "wants to be spent, my darlings. Everything else you keep is sulking in its casing. Light is *waiting*." Reading with Reader Odile Fane, a small dry woman with a green cord who spoke in a whisper and made them sit for an hour at a time with their tongues on tablets of old grain, listening to the Roar.

Hester heard it, a little: a rush, a breathing, like wind at a keyhole. Teo heard it as if someone had opened a door. He sat at the reading-basin with his eyes shut and tears running, and afterward could not say what he had heard. "Water," he said. "And something like voices. But not. Like people talking in the next house through a wall."

The cold, which no amount of wool kept out. The thirst. She drank a jug of water at every meal and woke in the night with her tongue stuck to her teeth, and every morning there was a faint white rim of salt at the corners of her eyes. The Lantern Hour every dusk, which she did not stop noticing.

Teo baking saltcake in R-41 on a spirit stove he had smuggled up the Stair in his laundry: caramel and butter and Mardens honey and flakes of dead Weeps salt crunching on the top, and second-years knocking at all hours to buy a slice at a fleck apiece. "Commerce," said Teo, pocketing flecks. "The Abbey was very wrong about a great many things."

And Saskia Orme, who sat behind Hester in Horology and kept a running commentary in an undertone on everything Hester did wrong, which was a great deal. On the fourth day Hester's drop hung over the brine unreturned again, and Crisp lifted it off with the rod and set it in a second dish beside the first. Saskia said, quite loudly, "Look, she's starting a collection. They'll put her in the Quiet Rooms." Half the Pans laughed.

"Better than being a jar," said Hester, without turning round. "What are you on Bassano's Bench for again? The light, or the rent?"

There was a silence. Then Saskia laughed, short and startled, as if she had not expected to.

"The rent," she said. "Always the rent. What are you on Aske's for, Vail?"

"The weights," said Hester.

That night the two dishes went on the shelf over her cot in R-41, side by side, with a bead in each.

* * *

On the fifteenth of Russet a note came down to R-41 in Aske's sloping hand, black ink: *My Cell, 9th bell, if you're free. Bring your tongue.*

The Third Fathom gallery was quiet at that hour, its doors shut, the lamps turned low; somewhere a kiln was breathing. The door of 3-17 stood open a crack, and light came out of it, and humming.

Aske's workroom was his Quill Street lodging over again, made larger and stranger: a deep cell cut in the salt with a vaulted roof, and papers in drifts and on every wall. Shelves of jars. Tasting spoons in racks. A brine basin in a stand, a spirit stove, a cot in the corner with a heap of blankets on it where he plainly slept more often than in the Readers' Close. A glass-fronted cabinet with a brass lock held rows of small labelled boxes. On the table under the lamp lay a black leather notebook, open at a page of cramped, quick script that was not quite writing: all hooks and ticks and little circles, like writing made into a code by someone in a hurry.

"Ah," said Aske, looking up. "Good. Shut the door. Sit. Have you eaten? I've a pear. Never mind. Look at this."

He unlocked the cabinet and took out a box, and from a nest of black wool inside it lifted, with a pair of tongs, a crystal the size of a walnut. It was the same white with a little blue in it that the *Wren* grain had been. Near its centre was a bead of liquid water the size of a lentil.

It was moving. Not sliding, as the bead in the *Wren* crystal had slid when she tipped it. Aske held the tongs perfectly still, and the bead, by itself, swelled a hair, and shrank, and swelled. Slow. Regular. Like a pulse.

Hester sat down.

"From the Fifth," said Aske. "From the edge of a stay, three days ago. The Fathomers pull them out with the residue when they reset a stay, and it goes up in sealed boxes to be disposed of. I took this one before it went." He set the crystal on a square of black felt under the lamp. The bead went on swelling and shrinking. "Do you know what that is?"

"No."

"Nor do I. I've four of them now. They all do that, at different rates. This one's the slowest." He sat on the edge of the table among his papers. He looked thinner than he had in Quill Street, and his eyes behind the thick lenses were red-rimmed. "Fourteen pockets this summer. But that's not what's keeping me up. What's keeping me up is the stays."

"The stays."

"When a pocket quickens, the Fathomers take the Choir down and pour stillness into it until it quiets. Stays. They're bloody hard work; they're what Settle the Choir. You'd think with fourteen pockets the Choir would be on its knees. It isn't. I've been through the Warden's returns. The stays are holding better than they did when there were four. They're taking less stillness from the Choir per stay, not more. The Fathomers are pleased with themselves. The stays are easier than they ought to be. Much easier. As if there were stillness coming into them from somewhere else."

Hester looked at the crystal breathing on its felt. She thought of a thread of wet cotton between an amber moth and a green leaf.

"I went to the Cold House," Aske said. "On the fifth, the day before you stepped the Line. I didn't write to you; I wanted to be sure. Your Mother Agathe walked me down the rows. I counted. Fourteen weeping, all in the charity ward. I went to the Harl Street Brinery and the private house on Varenne Hill, and they've not a bead between them." He looked at her. "And they weep in lines. Don't they? East."

"Yes."

"Sorle showed you the moth." It was not a question. "He shows every intake the moth. I sat in that room when I was twenty and thought, how pretty. I never thought about it again till this summer." He picked up his pen and, humming under his breath without a tune, wrote in the black notebook in the cramped hooked script, fast.

"I tasted the Fifth Fathom dish at the First Taste," Hester said. "It was quick. Like his beads. Like the *Wren*."

Aske stopped writing. He did not look up.

"Yes," he said. "It would be. How heavy?"

"Ten thornes. Not quite eleven."

Now he looked up. "Ten what?"

"Thornes." She felt her face go warm. "It's not a proper measure. Mr. Thorne made me put a number to the weight behind my eyes, so I'd stop saying *heavy* and *heavier* at him. A pinch of Shelf is one, about. The Deep at a hundred and forty is six. The *Wren* was eleven. I've never had anything past eleven."

"Thornes," said Aske. He wrote it down. "The first unit of stillness anybody's made that I'd trust." He sat with the pen lifted. Then he wrote three or four more marks, hard, and put the pen down. "Not tonight. I'm not ready, and I'm too tired to be careful, and I've told you I'm bad at careful. Tomorrow. Come tomorrow night at the tenth bell. I'll have it laid out for you, all of it: the readings, the returns, the Cold House, the stays. I want your tongue on all of it before I take it anywhere. And then—" He stopped. "And then I think we'll go and talk to somebody. Together. I'd rather not do that alone."

"Talk to who?"

"Tomorrow," said Aske. "Tenth bell. Bring your tongue." He smiled at her, tired and crooked. "And eat something first. You'll need it."

* * *

She went back up the Stair with her hands in her sleeves. It was late, and the Lantern's shaft was dark; there was only the lamplight in the walls and the chalk names going up and up.

Near the turn below the Second Fathom someone was coming down: a tall stooped figure in grey, white hair tied back with string, one hand on the rail. Reader Sorle. He looked up as she came round the turn, and his pale eyes went over her face, and down to the white cord at her shoulder, and back.

She stood to the wall to let him by, as an aspirant should. He went past her without a word, on down toward the Third Fathom, and she heard his slow step going round below her long after she had stopped hearing anything else.


# Chapter 6: Kept

> A person kept beyond hope of return shall be accounted dead in law, and their estate shall pass as though they had died on the day of their keeping.
>
> — *Charter of the Grain, §41 ("the Stillborn Clause")*

She ate something first, because he had told her to. Teo watched her do it.

"You're eating like a woman going to a hanging," he said. "Is he going to hang you?"

"He's going to show me something."

"At the tenth bell. In his Cell." Teo raised his eyebrows. "At the Abbey that was what Brother Absalom said to the novices, and it never ended well."

"It's grain, Teo."

"That's what Brother Absalom said."

She left him with his saltcake and went down the Stair a little before the tenth bell. The Halcyon at that hour was a different place. The Long Table was empty and dark, the Lantern's shaft a black hole going up into nothing. The lamps on the Stair had been turned down to small blue flames, so that the walls no longer glowed but only gleamed, like the inside of a shell. Her feet were loud on the steps. Far below, she could feel the Hush in her back teeth, the low enormous hum, as if the whole hill were a bell that somebody had struck a long time ago.

The Third Fathom gallery was empty. The kiln that had been breathing the night before was cold. 3-17 was shut, and there was no light under it.

She knocked.

Nothing. She knocked again, louder, and said, "Reader Aske. It's Vail."

Nothing. She tried the handle. Locked.

The tenth bell rang while she stood there, faint and far off, from the Gatehouse; the sound came down the Stair's well and along the gallery and died. She thought he had fallen asleep. She had seen the cot in the corner with its heap of blankets, and his red-rimmed eyes. She knocked a third time, and then put her ear to the door, which was not wood but grown salt, cold as a cellar step against her cheek. No humming. No breathing. Nothing at all.

She waited. It was the deep still cold of the lower Fathoms, and she put her hands in her sleeves and leaned against the wall opposite the door, because he had said tenth bell and he had said *I'll have it laid out for you*. A man like that did not forget. A man like that might be late; he would come up the Stair two at a time, humming, with ink on his nose and an apology already half out of his mouth.

He did not come.

A little before the half she heard something on the Stair.

It was below her. The sound came up the well and through the open end of the gallery: a slow heavy rolling. Iron wheels on salt steps, *grind* and a bump, *grind* and a bump, as something was hauled up one step at a time. Someone breathing hard. A creak of wood under weight. It was coming up from the Fourth, or lower.

She went to the gallery's end and looked over the rail. The well went down into the dark. On the curve of the Stair a turn and a half beneath her there was a lantern moving, small and blue, and in its light the edge of a trolley, the flat low kind the porters used for brine casks, with something on it under a canvas. Two people, perhaps three, hauling. She could not see their faces, only grey wool and the swing of the lantern. They heaved, and the wheels bumped up a step, and they rested.

Porters, she thought. Brine casks going up to the kitchens for the morning, or residue boxes going up to be got rid of. The Halcyon did a good deal of its carrying at night.

She went back to the door of 3-17 and knocked one last time, without much hope. The wheels below went on coming up, *grind* and a bump. Then they stopped, somewhere on the turn below, as if whoever it was had stopped to rest.

She gave up a little before the half bell. She was cold to the bone and angry in the tired, flat fashion of someone who has been stood up, and she went back up the Stair rehearsing what she would say to him in the morning, and how he would look at her through his thick spectacles and apologise, and mean it, and do it again. She passed nobody. In R-41 Teo was asleep and humming in the third mode, and she got into her cot in her mother's coat and lay awake listening to him, and was not, after all, as angry as she had meant to be.

* * *

She woke to bells.

Not the first bell, one stroke from the Gatehouse at dawn. This was a high hard clanging from somewhere below, rung fast and on and on. Doors were opening all along the Rookery terrace. Teo was sitting up with his hair on end.

"What is it?"

"I don't know." She was already pulling her gown over her shift. "Below."

The Stair was full of people in nightshirts and gowns, all going down, all asking each other what it was. On the Second Fathom someone said *a quickening*, and someone else said *no, that's the Fathom bell, this is the Proctors'*, and someone said *it's the Third*. At the Third Fathom the gallery was blocked by a crowd and two Proctors with iron-grey cords holding them back. Hester pushed. She was taller than most of them and stronger than all of them, and she did not apologise. At the front, a Proctor put his arm across her chest.

"Back, aspirant."

"I'm Aske's," she said. "His Bench. Is it Aske?"

The Proctor looked at her, and at her white cord, and lifted his arm.

The door of 3-17 stood open. The lamp inside was lit. There were two Proctors in the room, a porter in a leather apron with his face the colour of dough, a Physic in a red cord kneeling on the floor by the brine basin, and a heavy grey man with close-cropped iron hair and a soldier's back, standing with his hands behind him, looking at the thing that stood between the table and the cot.

It was Aske.

He was standing in his shirtsleeves in the middle of the floor with his right arm half-reached out in front of him, the fingers open, as though he had been reaching for something or someone a little beyond his grasp. His other hand was half-closed at his side. His head was a little forward. His mouth was open as a man's mouth is open in the middle of a word, the lips parted, the tongue just visible behind the teeth. His spectacles were on his nose and, behind them, his eyes were open and fixed on nothing. There was ink on his fingers, black on the right hand and blue on the left.

He was kept. A casing of salt held him from the soles of his feet to the crown of his head, a close clear skin a finger thick, so clear that the lamplight went straight through it and lay on his face. It fitted him as water fits whatever you drop into it. At his feet its edge was broken short, not quite meeting the floor, and it closed over his head in a rounded cap, his hair standing up inside it as it always stood up.

Hester stood in the doorway. She was aware of the porter being sick, quietly, in a bucket in the corner. She was aware of the heavy grey man turning round to look at her. She was not aware of much else, for a while.

* * *

"And you are?" said the grey man.

"Vail. Hester Vail. I'm on his Bench." She did not take her eyes off the casing. "His privilege."

"Ah." His voice was flat and clipped, a parade-ground voice worn down by years of indoors. "The customs assayer. Chief Proctor Gage." He did not offer his hand. "You'd better come in, Miss Vail, since you're here, and stand there, and touch nothing."

She stood where he pointed, against the wall by the cabinet. The Physic glanced up at her and back down. She was a young woman with a tired face, measuring something in the brine basin with a dipstick.

"Porter found him at first bell," Gage said, to the room; he was a man who reported. "Came to bring Reader Aske's water. Door unlocked. Reader as you see. Brine basin on the floor, empty, spilt. Residue box open on the table: Hush-grade grain, two ounces short of its label. Reader Aske was known for testing his workings on his own person. There's a note in the Proctory book from three years ago when he kept his own left hand for a day to see if he could read it from inside." He looked at the casing. "It would appear he tried something larger. With Hush grain, which he oughtn't to have had, and which keeps fast and hard. And he got it wrong. That right, Doctor?"

"Hush-grade right through," said the Physic, without looking up. "Thick, too. Kept beyond return, I'd say. You'd never get the arrears out of Hush grain without killing him. Not with a bath. Not with anything."

"Then that's how it'll be written," said Gage. "Kept beyond return. Under the Clause." He looked at Hester. "You understand what that means, Miss Vail."

She understood. Everybody in Ambry understood the Stillborn Clause; the Brineries printed it on the back of the forms. A person kept beyond return was dead in law. His estate passed. His casing went to the house that kept it or, in the Halcyon, to the Quiet Rooms, to stand on a plinth with a card.

"He was expecting me," she said. "Last night. At the tenth bell."

Gage's flat grey eyes did not change. "Was he."

"He was going to show me some work. The door was locked. I knocked. Nobody answered. I waited the best part of half an hour."

"Did anybody see you?"

"I don't know. I saw no one." She remembered the lantern on the Stair. "There were porters on the Stair. Below. With a trolley."

"Porters," said Gage. "On the Stair. At night." He sounded neither interested nor surprised. "There generally are. Well. That gives us a time." He turned to one of the Proctors. "Write that the door was locked at the tenth bell and the Reader didn't answer. He'd begun, then, or he'd done it." He turned back to her. "What work?"

She hesitated. It was a short hesitation. She thought she saw him notice it.

"Assay," she said. "He was teaching me. Grain from trade."

"I see. You'll make a statement to the Proctory this afternoon. Third bell. Now stand there and keep out of the way."

* * *

She stood there. She kept out of the way. She looked.

She did not know, afterward, how long she stood against that wall while the Proctors measured and wrote and the Physic dipped her stick and the porter took his bucket away. It was not long. It felt long. And for all of it, while her face stayed still and her hands stayed in her sleeves, a part of her was at the customs bench with the north light on the slate, grading a sample. She could not stop it. She did not try.

The casing was wrong.

She had seen a thousand keepings, in the Cold House and the Quiet Rooms and on the customs bench in a hundred grades of trade grain, and she had kept two drops of brine herself on the Pans, which still sat in their dishes on her shelf in R-41, unreturned. She knew now how a keeping grew. It grew *out*, from the place where the worker's stillness went into the brine. When you kept a drop, the salt set first where your stillness touched it and spread outward from there; Crisp had shown them under a glass. A man keeping himself would keep from the skin outward. The casing would be densest and clearest against his body and roughest, a little frosted, at its outer face.

Aske's casing was the other way round. She could see it in the lamplight from where she stood. The outer surface was smooth, glassy, the smoothest part of the whole thing. Close against his shirt and his face the salt grew cloudier, a fine frost of tiny crystals pointing inward. It had set from the outside first. It had been poured over him, and had grown inward toward the body, as ice grows on a pond from the air down.

And there was a pattern in it.

She would not have seen it if the lamp had not been low and to the side. In the thickness of the salt, a hair below the surface, there was a grain. Every casing had a grain, as wood has; she knew that now, from Crisp. Her own two beads, under the glass, had a dense square grain like a chequerboard, like the tiles on the White. This one was no chequerboard but a spiral: fine curving lines turning round and round in a long slow coil that went up his body from the floor. She followed it with her eyes. It turned left, as a screw turns when you take it out. Widdershins.

She did not know what it meant. She stored it.

And it was clear.

That was the last thing. The thick close skin of Hush-grade salt over a man who was dead in law was clear as the day. Clear as Tib's. Not a breath of grey in it anywhere, not a mist, not a bloom. *The living keep clear,* Agathe had said, drawing her finger through the air, *and the dead keep grey. If one goes grey you'll know it's done.*

"He's clear," she said aloud.

Gage looked round. "What?"

"The casing. It's clear. Agathe Linden at the Cold House says the dead keep grey. That if a casing goes grey—"

"Hush grain keeps everything clear," said Gage, before she had finished. "Ask the Doctor."

"It keeps clear," said the Physic from the floor, wearily. "It's Hush. It keeps fast. It doesn't bloom like trade grain. He's kept beyond return either way, aspirant. Clear or grey, nobody's getting him out of that."

Hester said nothing. She knew nothing about Hush grain except its taste, and she had no reason to doubt them. She did not doubt them. She stored it anyway, beside the spiral.

Then the Physic stood up to speak to Gage, and the Proctors were writing at the table, and the porter had not come back. For a few breaths nobody in the room was looking at the floor by Aske's feet.

She knelt as if to retie her bootlace. The broken edge of the casing was a thin glassy lip a hand's breadth from her knee. She touched the tip of her smallest finger to it, lightly, as you might touch a sleeping animal, and put the finger to her tongue.

The weight came down like a door slamming. White. The pressure on the far side of her face. Sweet so deep it was nearly bitter: Hush, the grain from under the Fifth, that nobody might sell. And under it, faint and bright and wet, the bitten tongue. Quick.

The *Wren*'s cargo. Sample twelve. The fifth dish at the First Taste. The beads on her brother's casing.

She stood up. Her eyes were watering. She wiped them and nobody noticed, because by then several people in that room had wet eyes.

* * *

The black notebook was gone.

She noticed that next, when the room steadied. On the fifteenth it had lain open on the table under the lamp, among his papers, the cramped hooked shorthand running down the page. It was not on the table now. Nor were the papers. The table that had been drifted a foot deep in his small fast hand, black and blue, was nearly bare: the open residue box, his tasting spoons in their rack, his blotter, a slab of green felt in a leather frame, a few stray sheets, a sketch of a crystal, a list of grain grades. Nothing in the shorthand.

"His papers," she said.

"What about them?" said Gage.

"He had papers. All over the table. And a notebook. Black."

Gage looked at the table. "He kept a tidy desk for a man in his line of work," he said, after a moment. "Perhaps he tidied before his experiment. Men do, before they risk themselves." He made a note. "We'll look."

She did not believe him. She did not disbelieve him. She looked at the blotter, which nobody was looking at. Under its leather corner, pushed half out of sight, was the white edge of a sheet of paper, folded once.

The Physic said something to Gage, and he turned to answer. Hester stepped to the table to steady herself against it, which was a natural thing for a woman to do in that room. She put her hand flat on the blotter, and when she took her hand away the sheet was in her sleeve.

She had never in her life taken a thing she was not entitled to take. Not a fleck. Not a grain off the customs bench. She stood with the paper against her wrist and her heart going hard and slow, and looked at the casing. He had said *bring your tongue*. He had wanted her to see it all before he took it anywhere. He had laid it out for her, and someone had taken it away, and this was what was left.

She kept her hand in her sleeve.

* * *

Others came. Readers in gowns over their nightshirts, standing in the doorway with their faces gone grey. The Deputy Chancellor, a tall smooth man silvered at the temples, with an ochre cord, who looked at the casing a long while with no expression, spoke to Gage in an undertone, and went away. Crisp, her ruled mouth set harder than ever, who stood beside Hester without speaking and then touched her arm, once, and went.

Then Orsolya Thane came in from the gallery in her iron-grey coat, and stopped in the door, and put her hand to her mouth.

"Oh," she said. "Oh, Corvin."

She came across the room slowly. She did not touch the casing. She stood in front of it, short and square, looking up into his open face behind the salt. Her own face had gone white under its weathered colour.

"You daft man," she said quietly. "You daft, daft man. What did you go and do."

Gage looked at the floor.

After a long time Orsolya turned away and saw Hester against the wall, and something in her face softened and broke at once.

"Hester Vail," she said. She came over and put her hand on Hester's shoulder, and even through the wool of the gown it was cold, deep cold, like a railing in winter. "You came all this way for him. Sixteen days. I'm sorry, chick. I'm that sorry."

"He asked me to come last night," Hester said. She did not know why she said it to this woman. "At the tenth bell. I came. He didn't answer."

Orsolya's hand tightened on her shoulder.

"Then thank God you didn't get in," she said. "If he'd had you in there when he did this—" She shook her head. "You'll be without a Bench now. They'll tell you today, and they'll not be kind about it; the Registry never is. Come and see me when it's sunk in. I'm on the Fifth, by the Warden's Gallery; anyone'll show you. We'll find you something." She looked back at the casing, and her square face crumpled, briefly, and set again. "We look after our own down there."

* * *

The statement at the Proctory took an hour. A Proctor wrote down what she said: that she had come at the tenth bell, knocked three times, waited, seen no one, gone up before the half. That Reader Aske had been teaching her assay. That she had seen porters with a trolley on the Stair below.

Then Gage came in, and sat down across the table, and asked her again when she had arrived. And again when she had left. Whether she was certain the door had been locked and not merely stuck. Whether anyone could say where she had been between the half bell and first bell. Teo could, if he had been awake, which he had not been. Whether Reader Aske had ever made her uneasy. Whether he had made improper suggestions. Whether she had known, when she accepted his Privilege, that he had a reputation for self-experiment. Whether she had quarrelled with him.

"No," she said, to that one. "Never."

"A porter saw you at the gallery's end, a little before the half bell," said Gage. "Looking over the rail."

"I told you. I heard wheels."

"So you did." He wrote. "You'll understand I ask everyone the same questions, Miss Vail. Reader Sorle was on the Third Fathom at about the same hour; I've asked him. A young man named Ferrers was heard having words with Reader Aske in the gallery yesterday afternoon; I'll ask him. I ask. It's what I'm for." He closed his book. "It was an accident. A clever man, careless with dangerous grain, alone at night. It happens down there oftener than they put on the Stair. But I ask."

"And the papers?"

"We'll look," said Gage, and stood up.

* * *

The Registrar was a thin anxious man in an office on the First Fathom with a ledger the size of the Cold House's. He found her name in it and cleared his throat, and told her, in a voice that tried to be kind and did not know how, what she had already been told.

An aspirant of the Halcyon must be attached to a Reader's Bench. Her Reader was—he cleared his throat again—no longer able to sponsor her. The Charter permitted an aspirant whose sponsor had died, or been *otherwise lost*, to seek another Bench until the Midwinter Assay on the twenty-first of Rime. An aspirant not attached to a Bench by then could not sit the Assay, and an aspirant who did not sit the Assay was sent down.

"Sent down," she said.

"Removed. From the rolls." He made a small gesture at the ledger, as if crossing out. "The Privilege covers your fees while you seek a Bench, but only until then. I'm sorry, Miss Vail. Reader Aske never used his Privilege before. It's rather sad, really. That he chose you, and then—" He stopped. "The twenty-first of Rime."

She thanked him. She went up the Stair.

* * *

Teo was in R-41, sitting on his cot, with the spirit stove unlit and a cake of saltcake on a plate in front of him that he had not touched. He stood up when she came in. He did not say anything. He looked at her face, as he had looked at it on the first night when she mentioned Tib, and then he went and lit the stove and put the kettle on.

She sat on her cot. The window was full of the White, blazing at noon, and the Sere was throwing salt against the glass in little rattling handfuls. On the shelf over her cot sat her two beads in their dishes, the two drops of brine she had kept and could not return. Somebody had kept Aske like that. Somebody had poured brine over him and kept him, mid-word, reaching, and then gone away and left him in his own room for a porter to find with his water-jug.

Somebody. She sat with the word, and with what she had stored beside it: the spiral, the clear salt, the casing grown from the outside in, the wheels on the Stair coming up from below. Then she took the folded sheet out of her sleeve, where it had been all morning against her wrist, and unfolded it on her knee.

It was one leaf, torn from the black notebook along its stitching. It was covered on one side, in black ink and a little blue, with the cramped hooked script: ticks and loops and little circles, here and there a number, something that might have been an arrow. She turned it to the light. She could read none of it, except, at the very bottom, in the same quick hand but in plain letters, a single capital: *O.* followed by a full stop.

"I can't read it," she said.

Teo came over with two cups of halcyon tea, salt and milk. He set one down beside her and looked over her shoulder at the paper on her knee. Then he put his own cup on the floor, carefully, and crouched beside her, and took the edge of the page between his fingers and tilted it to the window.

"That's brinescript," he said. "Shorthand. The old copyists' hand; we used it at the Abbey for taking down readings from the tablets, before they faded." He looked up at her. His round face was serious. "It's been a long time. But I can read that."


# Chapter 7: Ask O.

> Ask, and the tide shall answer.
> Ask not, and the tide shall answer still.
>
> — *Hymnal of the Order of the Returning Tide, "For the Vigil," verse four*

Cell R-41 was eleven feet by eight. Hester had paced it on her first night, from habit, and had not needed to pace it since. Two cots, one under each slope of the ceiling. A table between them, under the window, with a brine basin let into it that nobody used for brine. Two pegs. A shelf. The window was a single pane of salt-glass as thick as a thumb, set into the dome's western flank. By day it looked out over the White toward Ambry, and by night over nothing at all, because the White at night gave back no light and Ambry was only a smudge of orange on the edge of the dark.

It was the eleventh bell. Teo had lit both candles, which was extravagant. He sat on the end of his cot with Aske's sheet on his knee, a pencil behind his ear and his old grey Tider shawl round his shoulders, and he had been silent for nearly a quarter of an hour. For Teo this was something close to a medical event.

Hester sat on the other cot with her back against the salt and her hands under her arms and watched him.

"You're sure you can read it," she said.

"I'm sure I can read it. I'm not sure I can read it *quickly*." He held the sheet up to the candle. "It's Tidewater short-script. Or it started as Tidewater short. The Abbey copyists used it for brine-tablet drafts, so you could get a whole homily onto a tablet the size of a biscuit. I spent four years doing nothing else. My hand still cramps at the sight of a vowel-hook." He squinted. "But your Reader's done things to it. He's dropped half the vowel-hooks, and he's using an ink-dot for the plural, which is heresy, and—oh, look. This sign's meant to be *tide*. He's using it for *line*."

"Does it matter?"

"Only to God and the Abbot. Give me a minute." He wrote something on the back of his hand. "He's left-handed, isn't he? For this. The slope's all backward. He wrote this fast, in the left hand, and he was cold."

"How do you know he was cold?"

"Because I've written short-script with cold hands for four winters in a stone cell in Harl, and my strokes went exactly like this. See how the long downstrokes break? That's shivering."

Hester looked at the marks: small, dense, sloped hard to the left, hooks and dots and little crossed lines like the tracks of a bird that had walked through ink. She had slid the sheet off Aske's blotter into her sleeve that morning while Chief Proctor Gage was talking to the Physic. She had felt, doing it, exactly as she had once felt at nine years old, stealing a bun from the Corn Exchange: calm, sure, and sick.

She had not told the Proctor. She had not decided not to tell him. She had only not done it, all through the long cold questioning, where he asked her four times what time she had come to the door of 3-17 and four times wrote down the same answer. And then it was too late to do it without explaining why she hadn't, and by then she had begun to think she would not do it at all.

"Got him," Teo said.

He read it out slowly, his finger moving along the lines.

"*Cold House. Fourteen weeping.* Then a line. *Harl Street Brinery. Nought.* Line. *Varenne Private. Nought.* Line. *Charity only?*" He glanced up. "Question mark. *A draw?* Question mark again. Then a sign I'd read as *still*, then *runs*, then a hook that means *toward* or *into*, and then this—" he tapped it, "—*quick*. *Still runs to quick.* That's the Fifth Law, isn't it? That's what Sorle was banging on about on Thursday with his two little casings and his bit of string."

"Brine thread."

"Brine thread. Then. *Cistern.* Two question marks. *Old lines.* And then right at the bottom, on its own, underlined twice." He turned the sheet to the candle. "*Ask O.*"

Hester held out her hand. He gave her the sheet. She looked at the bird-tracks as if by looking hard enough she could taste them.

"I know the first part," she said. "He told me. On the fifteenth, in his Cell." She put her finger on the first line, where the marks meant *fourteen*. "He went to the Cold House on the third and counted. Fourteen. That's Agathe's number; she told me fourteen on the first, counting Tib. Then the other brineries. Harl Street's for shopkeepers, and Varenne's where the Upper Ward keep their grandmothers. Nought, and nought. Only the charity ward."

"Only the poor."

"Only the poor," she said. "Weeping in lines, toward the east. That's what he was going to lay out for me." She looked at the lower lines, the ones he had not told her. "This is the rest. *A draw.* *Cistern.* *Old lines.* He'd got further than he said."

"You'd better tell me the rest of it, then. The brother." Teo pulled the shawl tighter. "You've said *Tib* nine times since the Lantern Hour, and I've been good and not asked, but my goodness is a finite resource."

* * *

So she told him. Not all of it. She told him as she would have read a manifest aloud: the facts, in order, with the weights.

Tobias Vail, fourteen. Bloom fever, the autumn of eight-ninety-two. The Cold House, the charity ward, casing two hundred and eleven. Six sols a month for eleven years. Her father, who had stopped visiting. Mother Agathe, who said the living keep clear. The beads on the first of Russet, standing in their little lines.

She did not tell him what Tib had said. She came to the place in the story where it would have gone and stepped round it, and went on to the Customs House and the cargo and the cut ledger, and she did not think Teo noticed.

He listened without interrupting, which she had not known he could do. When she had finished he sat for a while with his chin on his knees.

"Eleven years," he said at last.

"Yes."

"Six sols a month for eleven years. That's—" He was counting. "Near on eight hundred sols."

"Five, for the first two years. Seven hundred and sixty-eight," Hester said. "I don't round."

He looked at her with an expression she could not read. "The Abbey would call that a vigil. Sitting up with someone who can't wake. They'd give you a candle for it and a line in the book." He rubbed his face. "I'm not sure what I'd call it. I'll think. Do you want saltcake? I have contraband saltcake."

"You have what?"

"I bribed a cook." He reached under his cot and brought out a thing wrapped in a handkerchief: a slab of dark caramel cake crusted with flakes of dead salt. It smelled of burnt sugar and butter, and of the kind of kitchen Hester had not stood in since her mother died. "It's a recognised remedy. For grief, shock, bad news, and Horology."

She took a piece. It was good.

"So," Teo said, with his mouth full. "*Ask O.* Who's O?"

"I don't know."

"Let's be scholarly about it. We're scholars now. I have the gown to prove it." He held up a finger. "Could be the Order. My Order. *Ask the Order.* The Tiders keep half the old charity foundations in Ambry; if anyone has the old water-board papers from before the Cistern was bricked up, it'd be some dusty abbess in a cellar." A second finger. "Could be a person. Could be a place. Could be *ocean*." He grinned. "*Ask the ocean.* That's very Tider. We'd have put it on a banner."

"There isn't an ocean."

"There isn't a lot of things. We pray to them anyway."

Hester did not smile. She was thinking.

"Orme," she said.

Teo stopped chewing.

"Saskia Orme. She's on Bassano's Bench. She runs his light samples round the Fathoms because Bassano can't be bothered with stairs; everyone's seen her with the box. Aske tested grain for anyone who asked. She'd have stood in that room."

"That's not evidence. That's a woman with legs."

"I didn't say it was evidence." But she was folding the sheet along its creases, small, smaller. "O. Somebody he was going to ask about the Cistern, about the old lines. Somebody he thought would know. Before he—" She stopped.

"Before he did what he did," Teo said gently. "Or before what was done to him."

She looked at him.

"You think so too," she said.

"I think," Teo said, "that a man who writes *ask O.* and underlines it twice the night before he's kept was planning to be about in the morning." He brushed crumbs off the shawl. "But I was brought up to believe the sea will come back when we're worthy, so you mustn't lean too hard on my opinions."

* * *

Hester went down to the Brinestacks at the third bell of the eighteenth, between Keeping and the Lantern Hour, with Lucan Ferrers's name in her mouth like a stone.

Everybody knew about the argument. A first-year on Fane's Bench had it from a second-year on Crisp's, who had been in the corridor outside 3-17 on the afternoon of the sixteenth and heard raised voices, Aske's and a young man's, cold, well-bred. The young man had come out white about the mouth and walked off so fast he knocked a sample-tray out of a porter's hands. Everyone agreed it had been Ferrers. Everyone agreed it was interesting. None of them had said so to Chief Proctor Gage, because none of them wanted to be the one who named the Lord Exchequer's son in the Proctory.

Hester did not much care about being the one.

She found him where the porter at the Stacks desk said he would be, in one of the Reading Wells off the north gallery: small round rooms carved out of the salt like the inside of an egg, each with a stone basin of brine at its centre, a lamp, a single chair, a reading-slope, and a curtain of heavy grey wool across the door to keep in a warmth there was not. Lucan was sitting at the slope with a brinescript tablet in front of him and his hands in gloves. He was not reading it. He was looking at it.

She put the curtain back without knocking. He looked up.

"Miss Vail." He did not rise. "The Customs House. You've come to impound me."

"You argued with him."

"I beg your pardon?"

"Reader Aske. On the sixteenth, in the afternoon, in his Cell. Half the Turning Intake knows it, and the other half is on its way. You argued with him, and that night he was put in a casing, and you've said nothing to the Proctor."

Lucan Ferrers looked at her for a long moment. He had a narrow, handsome, guarded face, the kind that had been taught early not to do anything in public. His gown was the same undyed grey as everyone's, but it fitted him, as if the wool knew whose it was. He wore the ochre cord of Vey's Bench at the shoulder.

"*Was put,*" he said. "Not *put himself*."

"I said what I said."

"You did." He turned the tablet face down on the slope. It made a soft heavy sound. "Sit down, Miss Vail. No, there's only the one chair. Stand, then. You're good at it." He folded his gloved hands. "Yes. I argued with Reader Aske on the afternoon of the sixteenth. He had asked me to his Cell. I assumed it was about my Assay work; he examined Brinescript students in discernment. It was not. He told me my father's firm had been buying deep grain under false declaration. He asked me whether I knew about it, and whether I would be prepared to say so in writing."

"And what did you say?"

"I said my father buys what the Halcyon sells him." His voice did not rise. "I said that every grain of stillsalt on the Rim comes out of the ground under a Halcyon licence, is graded under a Halcyon schedule and moved under a Halcyon seal. If Ferrers and Lisk had bought something it shouldn't have, the question was who had sold it, and that question belonged to the Halcyon and not to me. And I said that if he wished to accuse my family of fraud he could do it before the Assize like a gentleman, and not ambush the son in an examination room." A muscle moved in his jaw. "It may be that I said it more loudly than that."

"And then you knocked a tray out of a porter's hands."

"I did. I apologised to the porter." He looked at her. "Ask *your* Halcyon, Miss Vail. That's what I told him, and it's what I'll tell you. You've been here twelve days. You think because you've stepped over a brass line in a floor you know which side of the ledger you're on."

That landed. She let it land and did not show it.

"The *Constant Wren*," she said.

Something happened to his face. It was small: a check, as in a horse that sees something in the road and decides not to shy at it.

"I don't know what that is."

"A three-masted freighter. Out of Quell. Chartered to Ferrers and Lisk. On the first of Russet she came into the dry harbour with forty crates of what the manifest called Shelf grain for curing. I put it on my tongue. It was Hush grain, from under your feet, and it was quickened. I seized it. That night six men with an Exchequer paper took it out of the bond-shed, and somebody cut the page out of my ledger, and in the morning I was suspended without pay. The only other man who'd tasted it was Reader Aske, and he'd offered me his Privilege." She watched his face. "And a fortnight later he's in a casing, and you're the last person anybody heard shouting at him."

Lucan's gloved hands had not moved on the slope.

"I don't know what that is," he said again, but differently.

"Then perhaps you should ask your father," Hester said. "Since you're so keen on people asking."

She let the curtain fall behind her. She had gone ten paces along the north gallery before she heard the chair scrape in the Well, and then nothing, and she did not look back.

* * *

On the nineteenth they moved Corvin Aske to the Quiet Rooms.

She would not have known, except that she came down the Stair at the second bell on her way to Horology and found the Third Fathom landing blocked by aspirants and Fellows standing about with the particular silence of people who have stopped to look at something and are ashamed of it.

Down the corridor toward 3-17 six Fathom porters in leather aprons and iron-grey armbands were working with a long low brine-cask trolley. They had the casing on it already, on its back on a bed of folded sacking, strapped, and they were easing it out through the Cell door a few inches at a time. Each time they eased it a woman's voice said, "Steady. Steady, now. Mind his hand."

It was Warden Thane. She stood at the head of the trolley in her iron-grey gown with one hand flat on the casing, over Aske's chest. She was not doing anything with the hand. It was simply there, as you would put a hand on a horse to let it know you were near.

"Left a bit, Harro. That's it. Easy."

They got it out and turned it in the corridor, slowly, the wheels grinding on the salt floor. Hester had told herself she would not look, and she looked.

He was as he had been. One arm raised, the hand open, reaching for something past the end of the trolley. His mouth open on a word. His spectacles kept on his face, slightly askew, and behind them his eyes open and swimming, not frightened but surprised. He looked like a man who had been interrupted. The casing was so clear that the lamplight went through it without bending. You saw him, and not what he was in.

*The living keep clear, my dear.*

*Hush grain keeps everything clear*, the Proctor had said. He had said it with the finality of a man repeating a thing he had been told.

"Miss Vail."

Orsolya Thane had seen her. The crowd parted a little, from habit, to let the Warden's look through, and Hester found herself in a small cleared space with everyone watching.

"Come here, chick," Orsolya said. "Come and walk with him. He'd want somebody of his to walk with him."

Hester went. She took a place at the side of the trolley, opposite the Warden, and the porters took up the straps, and they went down the corridor toward the lift at the pace of a funeral. The wheels ground. At the lift the porters stopped and conferred about the weight. Orsolya stood with her hand on the casing, and her face, which had been composed, came apart. It did not crumple; it simply ceased to be held. She looked old. She looked as tired as any woman Hester had ever seen, and Hester had seen her mother at the end.

"Always tasting things," Orsolya said quietly. Not to Hester. To the casing. "Always going where you weren't wanted, with your nose and your notebooks." She drew a long breath. "Well. You're safe now. Nobody'll trouble you now."

The lift came. It took four porters and the Warden to load him, and the cage sank a hand's breadth on its chains as the weight came on. Orsolya stepped in after. She looked back at Hester through the bars.

"You're on nobody's Bench. I know; Registrar told me. Don't you fret about that today." The cold hand came through the bars and touched Hester's wrist, lightly, and was gone. "My door's open. You know where."

The lift bell rang twice. The cage went down into the dark with a sound of brine running in the counterweight tanks, and the casing went with it, glinting, until there was only the empty shaft and the chains moving.

Hester found that her wrist was cold where the Warden had touched it. She rubbed it, and it went on being cold.

She did not go to Horology. She went down the Stair to the Fourth and along the curving corridor to the Quiet Rooms, and arrived as the porters were leaving. The door-warden, a stooped Fellow with a ring of keys, let her in for a minute because she said she had been his student, which was nearly true.

The Quiet Rooms were long and dim and full of things that had stopped. She did not look at them. She found him at the end of the second room, already on his plinth between a Reader in a gown of a cut three hundred years old and a girl kept in the act of laughing. The label was already lettered, in a neat clerk's hand: *C. ASKE, Reader. Kept 16 Russet 903. Beyond return (Ch. §41).*

She stood with him for a minute, as she had stood with Tib every month for eleven years, with her hands in her pockets.

"You said you'd show me," she told him. "You said, bring your tongue."
