He reached past her for whatever it was. His spectacles were crooked.

"I brought it," Hester said.

* * *

At the Lantern Hour she saw Saskia Orme crying.

Not much: the kind of crying that has been done somewhere else and mostly put away. Hester came up the Stair late, with the Hour already begun, the kept noon light pouring down the Lantern Well into the dusk of the Long Table, gold and warm and smelling faintly of hay from whatever summer day it had been pressed on. Every face at the table was turned up into it. Saskia was not at the table. She was standing in the arch at its far end, in the shadow outside the fall of the light, with her back to the wall. Her face was red and swollen about the eyes. She had a handkerchief balled in one fist, and she was looking at nothing.

Hester went toward her. They had not exchanged a civil word since Matriculation. Saskia had called her a charity case on the first morning and Hester had called Saskia a rent-collector, and both of them had been, in their way, accurate.

"You knew him," Hester said, when she was close enough not to be overheard. "Aske. You'd been to his Cell. With Bassano's samples."

Saskia's head came round. Her swollen eyes went over Hester's face in one hard, quick, Quellish look, a glazier checking a pane for bubbles.

"What?"

"I'm sorry," Hester said. It came out stiff. She was not practised at it. "If you knew him. I'd only known him a fortnight. But I'm sorry."

For an instant Saskia's face came open. Then it shut, like a kiln door.

"I didn't know him," she said. "I knew his *door*. I knocked on it, and I left things on his bench, and I went away. That's all I did. That's all I ever did." Her voice cracked on the last words and she stopped it, angrily, as you stop a cup that is going to spill. "Mind your own business, Vail. You've enough of it."

She went past Hester into the corridor and was gone.

Behind Hester, at the Long Table, the light was fading as it always faded, all at once at the end, like a lamp turned down. A sigh went along the table, as it did every evening, a whole intake letting go of the same summer afternoon.

*I knew his door.* She turned it over. She did not like how it weighed.

* * *

That night, in R-41, while Teo slept with his mouth open under the slope of the ceiling, Hester took out the ledger she had brought from Tanner's Row. It was her own, a cheap quarto in marbled boards, half-filled with eleven years of household accounts. She turned to a clean page at the back.

She ruled it as she had ruled ten thousand schedules on the slate bench in the Dry Harbour: four columns, a narrow one for the number, a wide one for the description, one for the weight, one for remarks. At the head of the page she wrote, after some thought: *Declaration of Interest, 3-17, 16 Russet.*

Then she sat with the pen above the paper for a long time, while the candle went down and the night-chime started up faintly, tile by tile, out on the plain.

She wrote:

*1. — L. FERRERS, aspirant (Vey). — Argued with C.A. afternoon 16th re. Ferrers & Lisk deep grain. — Weight: motive, temper, family. — Did not know the* Wren. *Or says he did not. Face moved.*

*2. — B. SORLE, Reader (Horology). — On 3rd Fathom at tenth bell, 16th (Proctory). Passed me on the Stair 15th, going down. Known enemy of half the Conclave. — Weight: opportunity. — No motive known.*

*3. — S. ORME, aspirant (Bassano). — Courier, since summer. Knew his door. Crying at the Hour. "O." — Weight: access; the initial. — Grief? Fear? Not grief.*

*4. — "O." — Somebody who knows the old lines. The Order? Somebody in Ambry? — Weight: unknown.*

*5. — E.D. / W.L.F. — Shipper's mark on the* Wren *bill. A man in grey wool at the bond-shed with a lantern and a paper. — Weight: the cargo. — Not a firm. Not a person I know.*

*6. — The Proctor. — Ruled it a self-keeping before he'd looked. "Hush grain keeps everything clear." — Weight: haste. — Who told him so?*

It was not a good schedule. Half the weights were guesses and none of the remarks would have stood up before the Customs Board. Mr. Thorne would have struck it through and told her to come back when she had something she could put on the scale.

At the bottom of the page, under the last line, she wrote one more entry, smaller, and did not number it.

*— H. VAIL. — At the door at tenth bell. Saw nothing. Heard something heavy on the Stair, coming up from below. Looked over the rail: blue lantern, grey wool, a load under canvas. Thought: porters, casks. Went up. — Weight: —*

She left the weight blank. She sat with the pen in her hand until the candle drowned in its own wax and the cell went dark. Then she lay down in her clothes, with the ledger on her chest, and listened to the White ticking out in the cold, and to Teo breathing, and to nothing at all from the dome beneath her, which was large and full and did not make a sound.


# Chapter 8: The Stay Choir

> Hold the line, hold the line,
> breathe it slow and breathe it fine;
> hold the line, hold the line,
> so the others can sleep.
>
> — *Work-song of the Stay Choir, refrain (traditional)*

Reader Maudelaine Crisp refused her in eight words, on the twentieth of Russet, at the edge of the rose-coloured practice pan with brine to the wrists of her rolled sleeves: "I'm full, and you're unfinished. Next pan, please." The rest of the list went the same way, more slowly. Bassano took her hands in his warm rings and wept a little for Corvin and could not; Rusk of Weathering explained that Lowside constitutions settled early, and that it was not personal; Pym of Cellaring was full with his door open eight inches. Reader Odile Fane of Reading, who had Teo, was full and sorry for it. She was a small dry woman who spoke in a whisper, and she held Hester's eyes and added, lower still: "For what it's worth, you were seen in the Quiet Rooms. Go carefully. The Halcyon is a good place to be noticed, and a bad one." Hester left Benedek Sorle till last, because she expected nothing from him, and found him alone in the Black Bench lecture room wiping slates, a stick hooked over the lectern. He heard her out without turning round. "I am told you were at Corvin's door at the tenth bell on the night he was kept," he said to the slate, "and that you have been visiting him since in the Quiet Rooms, like a widow. I do not take charity cases from the dead. Good afternoon."

* * *

The Warden's Gallery was on the Fifth Fathom, and aspirants did not walk to it.

The Stair went on below the Fourth to the tall iron-grey doors she had seen on the tour, *FATHOMERS ONLY*, with a Proctor beside them now, and beside the doors a single small brine-lift cage, its bars worn bright at the height of a hand. A Fathom porter rode down with her and looked at her once, to see whether she would be sick. The salt of the shaft walls went clearer and darker as they sank, with a grey in it like smoke in glass. The air grew colder, and then, unexpectedly, warmer, and damp, and she tasted under it, faint and far off, the bitten tongue.

"Warden's Gallery," the porter said, drawing the gate. "Straight on, miss. You'll hear her."

She heard her. Orsolya Thane was laughing, down the corridor, through an open door where lamplight lay on the floor. It was a big unguarded Lowside laugh, the laugh of the women on Tanner's Row when the washing was in and the children were somebody else's for an hour. She had not heard one since she crossed the Line.

The Warden's office was a long low room cut into the salt, and it was warm. A real iron stove stood in the corner with a kettle on it and grey wool stockings drying on the rail. Rag rugs on the floor. A dresser of chipped blue-and-white Lowside crockery. On the walls, among the charts of the Fifth Fathom's galleries in coloured inks, were pinned sheets of names and dates, and some of the names had small crosses beside them in pencil. At the big table sat the Warden of the Lower Fathoms in her iron-grey gown with the sleeves pushed up, a teacup in one hand and a pen in the other, laughing at something said by the man at her elbow with a ledger.

"Miss Vail." The laugh changed to a smile without losing any warmth. "There you are. Mr. Dunmore, this is Corvin's girl. Sit down before you fall down, chick. That lift does it to everybody the first time. It's the warm. Nobody expects the warm."

Hester sat on a kitchen chair with a cushion on it. Orsolya poured her tea from a brown pot without asking, and put salt in it without asking, and milk, and pushed it across.

The man with the ledger nodded to her. He was perhaps forty, with a pleasant forgettable face, thinning fair hair combed carefully across, and a clerk's black coat over his grey gown, its cuffs banded with ink. He said "Miss Vail" in a voice that matched the face, and turned a page, and wrote something.

"Mr. Dunmore keeps me honest," Orsolya said. "Or keeps me in order. I can never remember which." Her hands round the cup were white at the knuckles and bluish at the nails. Hester had noticed them on the Causeway, the first morning: the hands of someone who had been cold for a long time. "Now. Let's not dance. You've been round the Benches, and they've all said they're full, and Crisp said you were unfinished."

Hester looked at her.

"She says it to everyone," Orsolya said. "She said it to me, in eight-seventy. She was right then too." She set her cup down. "I can't sponsor you. I'd like to. I've six on my Bench and two of them are useless, and if I had my way I'd send the useless ones home to their mothers and take you in an afternoon. But the Conclave counts heads, and Vey counts them twice." She said the Deputy Chancellor's name as a woman in Tanner's Row says the name of the landlord. "What I can do is put you in the Choir."

"Reader Aske mentioned the Choir."

"I expect he did. He knew I was short." Her face moved, briefly, at the name, and settled. "Three sols a week, in silver, no deductions; Mr. Dunmore sees to it. Four shifts a week, two of them nights. It's hard work and it's cold work and it'll put years on you if you do it long enough, and I'll not lie to you about that, because I've never lied to a chorister in my life." She leaned forward. "But while you're in the Choir you're on the Halcyon's books, and the Registrar can't send down a working chorister without the Warden's say. And I'll not say. That'll hold you till midwinter. By midwinter something'll turn up. It generally does."

Three sols a week. Twelve a month. Tib's fee was six.

Hester did the sum and found she had done it before she had decided anything, which meant she had decided.

"Why me?" she said. "You don't know me."

"I know you." The Warden looked at her across the table, steady and amused. "You walked the Causeway at dawn on your first morning with a carpetbag in one hand and your other hand in your pocket on your purse. Only Lowside girls walk like that. I walked like it myself, to the Coldbank pithead every morning with my da's dinner in one hand and my pennies in the other, till the Coldbank seam came down on him. I was eleven." She said it plainly, a fact with the weight already taken off it, and went on. "And I hear you've got a hold like a dropped anvil. Crisp says you can't let go of a thing to save your life, but by God you can grip it. That's a chorister. That's what the Choir *is*. Gripping." She turned her cup. "And Corvin asked me. A fortnight ago, before—" She stopped. "He came down here with you in his mouth, as he came down with everything, and he said *Orsolya, I've found a tongue*. So."

Hester looked down into her tea.

"Dunmore," Orsolya said. "The forms."

He had them ready: a stipend agreement, a declaration of fitness, and a release. *The chorister acknowledges that holding in the Stays may result in lag, cold injury, and the Settle, and holds the Halcyon blameless.* She read every word, because she was a customs assayer and had never signed a thing she had not read. Then she signed. Dunmore wrote her name at the head of each in his own hand, for the file, in small, upright, plain letters. Something about the letters tugged at her, as a familiar face tugs in a crowd. She looked again and it was gone. A clerk's hand. Ten thousand clerks wrote like that.

"Your first shift's Friday," Orsolya said. "Night. Choir hall, Fourth Fathom, the ninth bell. Wear everything you own. Eat first." She drank her tea. Then, in the voice of a woman thinking aloud: "You've come in a good season, at least. The Stays have been kind this season. Kinder than they've any right to be." She shook her head. "I'll not complain. God knows we've had our share of the other kind."

At her elbow, Dunmore turned a page.

* * *

She had seen the Choir hall from the Stair on the tour, empty, its floor worn in rings. Full, it was another room.

It was oval and old, its tiers of shallow steps worn by centuries of sitting into something nearer a bowl. There were no benches. You sat on the salt. A dozen lamps of kept light hung from the ceiling on long chains, pressed on some clear midwinter noon years ago, and they gave the hall the cold lucid look of a snowfield.

There were sixty-one choristers. Hester counted. They came in by twos and threes at the ninth bell of Friday in grey gowns and iron-grey armbands, bundled in shawls and mufflers and fingerless gloves, carrying flasks, and sat down on the steps in no order she could see, and did not talk much. Most were older than her. A man of seventy with a white beard sat down on the lowest step with a slowness she had only ever seen in Mr. Thorne. They had what she had seen on choristers in Lowside taverns, which was not a look so much as a lack of fidget. When they sat down, they sat down all the way.

A woman sat down next to her: sixty-five or so, small and square, with a face as brown and creased as a walnut and a mass of white hair bound in a red scarf. She wore three shawls, one on top of another, and she held out a fourth.

"You'll want that," she said. "Not now. After. Ama Tessaly." She put out a hand in a fingerless glove. "Forty-one years I've been holding, and I've never seen a first-nighter yet who thought she'd want a shawl. And then she wants it."

"Hester Vail."

"I know. Corvin's girl. The Warden said." Ama looked at her sidelong with small bright black eyes. "Strong hold, she says. Well. That's lucky for you and unlucky for you. The strong ones she puts nearest the line."

"What does that mean?"

"Means you'll find out, lamb." Ama patted her knee. "Here she is."

Orsolya came down the steps into the middle of the floor. She did not raise her voice; the shape of the hall carried everything. She looked round the tiers as a woman looks round her kitchen table at a family she has fed for thirty years.

"Fourteen holding tonight," she said. "Same as last night. Four's warm. Nine's warm. Eleven's warmer than I'd like. We'll go down to Eleven first and give it a long hold and then do the round. Mackey, you're lead on Nine. Ama, you'll take the new girl on Eleven, by the line." Her eyes found Hester. "Don't be frightened, chick. Ama's held more stays than anybody alive. Just do what she does. Right." She lifted her chin. "Let's have the breath."

Sixty-one people breathed in.

It was not loud: sixty-one people drawing breath together over a count of perhaps eight, and letting it out over twelve, a long low *hhhhhhh* that went round the walls and came back. Again, slower. And again. On the fourth breath someone low down on the left was singing.

It was a single note held for the length of the outbreath and dropped a tone at the end. On the next breath a dozen voices took it; on the next, all of them. They were not singing to make music; Hester understood that at once. They were singing to hold sixty-one bodies to the same slow count, so that sixty-one hearts would slow together. The note was the clock. Then the words came in on the outbreath, ragged and soft:

*Hold the line, hold the line,*
*breathe it slow and breathe it fine—*

By the third time round she did not need the tune. Her breath had gone with theirs without asking her, and her heart had gone with her breath. She sat on the cold salt with Ama's shoulder against hers and felt her body slowing, as you feel a cart slowing on a hill: the whole weight of it easing.

"Good," Ama said, between lines. "Good girl. Now come on."

* * *

They went down singing.

The Choir left the hall by a low door on its eastern side and went down a passage cut in steps, in a long grey file. The salt of its walls went clear and dark, and at one turning a door of iron-grey stood open with a Proctor beside it. Beyond it the air changed.

Warm. Wet. And the taste.

It came up her throat from the back of her tongue before she had put her tongue to anything: sweet, enormously heavy, so that the passage dimmed in front of her eyes. Under the sweetness, bright as a struck match, quick. Quick as the cargo. Quick as the beads on Tib's casing. Quick as the fifth dish at the First Taste, the one that had made half the intake weep.

The Fifth Fathom.

The rest of the Halcyon had been carved. The Fifth had been dug. Its galleries were low and rough, propped here and there with timber gone white with salt, running off in every direction into the dark. Along the floor of each, against the left wall, ran a channel two hands wide, sheathed in brass and full to the brim with brine that did not move. Where the sheathing joined there were valve-wheels the size of plates, painted with white numerals. At every junction more channels came in from the side and joined, as gutters join in a street, until they ran together into a single great channel as wide as a man's body that went away along the main gallery toward the east, out of the lamplight.

"Stay-lines," Ama said, between breaths, seeing her look. "That's the eastern trunk, that big one. All the lines go into her and she goes along to the Old Tap and round. You don't need to know. You need to know your line." She pointed with her chin down a side gallery. "That's ours. Eleven."

Stay Eleven was at the end of a short dead-end gallery where the roof came down low. Most of the Choir went on with Orsolya and Mackey toward the other stays. Twelve turned with Ama into Eleven.

At the end of the gallery the salt was wet.

Not damp. Running. Water stood in shallow pools on the floor and ran down the walls in threads that glittered in the lamplight. The salt had gone soft and grey and translucent, like ice in a thaw. And it was warm; she could feel it on her face from three paces off, a hearth's warmth. In the end wall, at the height of a person's chest, the salt bulged outward in a rough dome the size of a cartwheel, laced with fine cracks and glistening wet. From the cracks came a sound, low and continuous. It was not the Choir coming back off the walls. It came from *inside* the salt.

The brass stay-line ran up the wall from the floor and ended against the bulge in a broad collar sealed with grey wax. Over the whole face of the bulge was a casing, a thick clear shell of new-grown grain like a scab over a wound, and it was weeping. Beads of brine stood out all over it, and ran.

"Hands on the line," Ama said. "Where it comes up the wall. Both hands. Flat. Don't grip, lamb, it's not a rope, it's a bath. Lay them in." She put her own hands low on the brass where the line climbed the wall, and Hester put hers above, and the others ranged out along the line on either side. The brass was cold. Through a slot at the top of the sheathing Hester's fingertips touched the brine inside. That was cold too, and still.

"Now hold," said Ama. "Give it the hold. And sing."

They sang. Hester held.

It was like the Customs House, when she had gone into the hold to read the *Wren* grain: the breath going down, the heart going down, the whole of her settling round a single point like silt in a glass. But on the stool she had held alone. Here, through the line, she could feel the others: not as people but as weight, twelve stillnesses settling with hers. And she felt the stillness *go*. It went out of her hands into the brine, up the wall into the collar, out of the collar into the casing over the bulge, as warmth goes out of your hands into a cold cup, steadily, without your doing anything. *Still runs to quick.* Sorle's bit of string. She felt the quickening at the far end of the line pull like a drain. It wanted what she had. It drank it.

And as she held, the sound from inside the salt came up through the line into her hands, and through her hands into her, and it was no longer a sound behind a wall. It was all round her.

It was the Roar. She knew it from the *Wren*: the vast low rushing, rise and fall, the rise coming again before the fall was done. But on the customs stool it had been the sound in a shell. Here she was inside the shell. It had *shape*. The rises had heads that curled and broke, and the falls had a long drawn hiss after them, like a broom across a floor, like sand pulled back. There was a wind over it, a long steady push, cold, and under the wind a slapping, irregular, close, as of something hollow and wooden knocked against and knocked against. The Roar came in, and broke, and drew back hissing, and came in again. It had been doing that before she was born and before Ambry was built and before there was a Halcyon, and it was doing it now, in her hands.

Far off, under all of it, so faint she could not swear afterward that she had heard it at all, a bell rang once.

She did not know she was weeping until the salt of it stung the corners of her mouth. The tears ran into the neck of her gown, and where they dried they dried white. She was not sad; she was holding, and singing, and the sea was in her hands, and she had never in her life heard anything she wanted so much to go on hearing.

*Hold the line, hold the line—*

The beads on the casing slowed, and stood, and dulled, and dried. The cracks in the bulge went white and closed. The warmth on her face cooled. The sound drew back, and back, as a tide draws off a long shore, until it was the roar in a shell again, and then a murmur, and then only the Choir's voices in the low gallery.

"Ease off," said Ama softly. "Ease it now. Lift your hands. *Slow.*"

Hester lifted her hands.

* * *

She came to on the floor of the gallery with her back against the wall and Ama's fourth shawl round her shoulders.

She did not know how she had got there. That was the worst thing. She had been lifting her hands from the brass; now she was sitting, wrapped up, and the lamps had been moved, and the brass of the line was dry where it had been wet. Ama was crouched in front of her holding a tin cup to her mouth.

"Drink," Ama said. "Don't talk. Drink."

It was water, with salt in it. She drank it and was still so thirsty that her tongue stuck to the roof of her mouth. She was cold in her bones and her teeth, and she could not stop shaking.

"How long," she said. Her lips were clumsy.

"Couple of minutes. Two, maybe three." Ama tucked the shawl tighter. "That's lag, lamb. You gave it more than you had, first time, like they all do. You'll get most of it back." She sat back on her heels. "Told you you'd want the shawl."

Hester put her hand to her face. Her cheeks were stiff with dried salt, and it came away on her fingers in white flakes.

"It was the sea," she said.

Ama looked at her.

"I heard it. In the line. There was wind, and something knocking, and the—" She did not have the words. She had never had any reason to learn them. "It was the sea."

"Aye," said Ama. "It sounds like it, doesn't it." She said it peaceably, as you would agree with a child about the shape of a cloud. "Everybody hears something their first night. Residue, the Readers call it. Noise of the Ebb, caught in the grain." She got up slowly, with a hand on the wall, all the way, as the old man with the white beard had sat down. "Forty-one years I've heard it. Some nights I could swear it was the sea too. Up you come. Round's not done. Four and Nine yet."

Hester took the hand and came up. Her legs held her, mostly. At the mouth of the gallery Orsolya Thane was standing with a lamp, watching them come out. When she saw Hester, something came into her face that Hester had seen in her mother's once, long ago, at a school prize-giving in Lowside: a thing that had nothing to do with the prize.

"There she is," Orsolya said. "There's my girl. Eleven's quiet as a church, Ama says. First night." She put her cold hand on Hester's cheek, on the dried salt. "Didn't I say? Like a dropped anvil."

Hester could not answer. Her teeth were chattering.

"Go on," the Warden said gently. "Do the round. Then up, and eat something hot, and sleep." She turned away down the main gallery, lifting her lamp. Its light ran along the great brass channel of the eastern trunk, full and still, going away east into the dark, toward wherever it went.


# Chapter 9: The Brinestacks

> Readers are reminded that the tablets remember being touched. Gloves are to be worn. The Stacks accept no responsibility for what a careless hand may leave behind for the next reader to find.
>
> — *Notice, Brinestacks entrance desk, Second Fathom*

The Brinestacks smelled of paper, and under the paper, of the sea.

Hester had not noticed that on the tour. She had been looking at the shelves then, everyone had, because the shelves were the thing you were meant to look at. They ran away from the entrance desk in long radiating galleries like the spokes of a wheel, each gallery three tiers high with iron walkways and ladders. They were cut straight into the salt of the Second Fathom, and they held, the Proctor-guide had said, three hundred thousand bound volumes and forty thousand tablets. You could believe it. You could not see the end of any of the galleries. They went off into a soft amber dusk of kept-light lamps, and in the dusk there were the shapes of people on ladders, and the small endless sounds of a library: pages, a cough, a ladder's wheel turning on its rail.

But on the twenty-fourth of Russet, when she came down the Stair at the second bell with Teo and her customs ledger, her mouth was still raw from the Stay. She tasted the air as she came in at the great arched door, and under the leather and the paper and the lamp-oil there was the faint wet sweetness she had had in her hands on Friday night. It was faint and old. The tablets, she thought. Forty thousand tablets of grain, every one of them holding its scrap of the Roar.

"Where do we start?" said Teo.

"With what we can weigh," she said.

* * *

What they could weigh was the Exchequer returns.

Every licensed brinery on the Rim made a return to the Salt Exchequer each quarter, and a copy came to the Halcyon under the Charter, and the copies were bound by the year and shelved in the Cellaring gallery. Cellaring was the dullest gallery in the Stacks, and therefore the emptiest. It had kept goods, trade preservation and the arithmetic of fish. Hester and Teo had a long table at the end of it to themselves, under a lamp that had been pressed on a grey day and gave a light like weak tea.

The returns were in columns. Hester liked them at once. Name of house. Number of keepings at quarter's start. New keepings. Returns by family request. Returns by casing failure. Deaths following return. Number at quarter's end. Fees received. Each column was ruled, each figure was in a clerk's hand, and each page was signed at the foot by the house's director and countersigned by an exciseman. She had been reading documents like it since she was twelve years old. She sat down to them the way another woman might sit down to a piano.

"I can't read these," Teo said, after a quarter of an hour, with real sorrow. "I can read Tidewater short-script and High Marrane and the old Ostrine place-names off a map, but I look at a column of numbers and it simply lies there."

"Then read me the dates. I'll do the rest."

He read her the dates. She did the rest.

It took them till the fifth bell. The Cold House had been making returns since 788, the year of the Great Bloom. For a hundred years, quarter after quarter, the column headed *returns by casing failure* had stood mostly empty. A nought. A nought. A one, in 812, with a note in the margin: *casing cracked in the earthquake of Rime*. A nought. A one, in 851, an old woman kept eighty years whose casing had simply worn thin. Noughts. Hester ran her finger down the column through a century of noughts and ones and found a rate so slow you could set a clock by it. One casing failure every three years, roughly. One every three years, out of three hundred sleepers.

Then she came to this year, and the column was not empty.

*Fourth quarter, 902: nought. First quarter, 903: nought. Second quarter, 903: 1.* A man called Abel Corrie, kept in 879. *Third quarter, 903: 2.* Mary Ann Teesdale, kept in 864, and Ezra Hallam, kept 890. And in the next column along, *deaths following return*, the same figures in the same rows. One, and two.

Hester sat back.

"Three," she said. "In six months. Against one in three years for a century."

Teo looked at the page over her shoulder. "Hallam," he said. "Didn't your Mother Agathe say something about a Hallam?"

"The Hallam boy. She said Pell had him recast. Forty sols, from the charity fund." Hester looked at the date beside the name. "This is his father. Or his brother. Kept in eight-ninety. Returned by casing failure last quarter. Died."

"Then they've recast the son, because—"

"Because the son's weeping too." She ran her finger back to the director's signature at the foot of the page. *J. Pell, Director.* The exciseman's countersignature under it was an illegible scrawl. "Three of them. And nobody's written it anywhere but here, in a column nobody reads." She thought of what Aske had said in Quill Street. *It's in the Warden's returns to the Conclave, if anyone reads them, which nobody does.* "Is there anybody in this whole building who reads anything?"

"I'm told Callow does," Teo said. "But he's very slow."

* * *

The Stacks catalogue was in the long hall behind the entrance desk. It was a wall of narrow wooden drawers, three thousand of them, each with a brass label-holder and a brass ring. Each drawer was full of cards written in two hundred years of different hands. Hester stood in front of it on the twenty-fifth with Aske's sheet of shorthand folded in her pocket and one line of it in her head.

*Cistern. Old lines.*

She found the drawer under C. It held three cards for the Cistern. Two were for printed books, a Water Board history of 610 and a pamphlet of 455 complaining about the price of brine. The third card was larger and older, ruled in red, and read in a beautiful faded hand: *The Founders' Cistern & Lines of Supply. Plans, sections & elevations, 41 sheets, fol. Founders' Gallery, Press 9. Restricted: Reader's countersignature.*

Below that, ruled off in the same red, was a column headed *Requisitions*. It was nearly empty. In more than a hundred years three people had asked to see the Founders' Cistern plans, and each had signed the card, with the date.

The first was a Reader called Anselm Brack, in 788. Hester supposed it was the Great Bloom year and the city had wanted to know about its water.

The last, in small fast blue ink sloping hard to the right, was *C. Aske, R. — 1 Russet 903. East trunk survey.*

The first of Russet. The day she had walked down Coldwater Street and put her finger to a bead on her brother's casing. In the morning, while she was tasting the *Wren*'s grain, he had been here on a Fifth Fathom errand, before ever he came to the Customs House at the ninth bell with salt-mud on his gown, and before he could have known her brother's name.

And between Brack and Aske, a year earlier, there was one more line. It was in big plain capitals, leaning a little to the left, not a signature at all:

*W.L.F. (office) — 9 Fallow 902.*

She read it three times. Then she took the bill of lading out of the back of her ledger, where it had lived since the second of Russet. She unfolded it, and held it beside the card under the catalogue lamp, and looked at the shipper's box.

*W.L.F. per E.D.*

The hands were not the same. The card was written in those leaning capitals; the bill in small upright letters that she had seen somewhere lately and could not place. But the letters were the same three letters, in the same order, with the same full stops.

"Teo," she said.

He came. She showed him. He looked from one to the other for some time.

"Well," he said at last. "That's not a firm."

"No."

"*Office.* It says *office*. It's an office. Somebody's office signed for it." He frowned. "We could ask at the desk. They'd know their own abbreviations, surely."

They asked at the desk. The clerk on duty was a young Fellow with a cold and a stack of returns to file. She looked at the card for a while with her head on one side, sniffing.

"Departmental," she said. "Could be anything. Works. Lattice Fund. One of the Wardens. Lantern Fund, maybe. They all have codes; they change them every few years when somebody new comes in and wants their initials on things."

*One of the Wardens.* Something in Hester went toward the Fifth and stopped, like a hand short of a hot pan.

The clerk sniffed again. "Registry keeps the list. Write to the Registrar. They'll answer. Eventually."

"How eventually?"

"Fallow," said the clerk. "If you're lucky. Cinder if you're not."

Hester wrote to the Registrar. She wrote at the desk, standing, with the clerk's pen, a short polite request in the best hand she had, for the list of departmental requisition codes current in the year 902. She knew as she wrote it that it was the second note she had written in a month so that she could say afterward that she had written it. She posted it in the box for internal letters. It fell with a sound of paper landing on paper.

* * *

The plans themselves she could not have. *Restricted: Reader's countersignature.* She had no Reader. But there was a second set, Teo found by the end of the afternoon. It was a reduced copy, made by the Water Board in 400 at the time of the bricking-up, nine sheets instead of forty-one, and it sat unrestricted in a dusty folio in the Lattice gallery under *Municipal Works (superseded)*.

They spread it on a reading-slope in the Lattice gallery at the sixth bell, with the lamps going amber around them and the galleries emptying toward the Lantern Hour. The paper was brown and split along the folds and smelled of mice.

Sheet one was Ambry: not Ambry as it was now, but Ambry in the year 400, smaller, with fields where the Middle Ward was and the old shoreline drawn in a heavy line. Sheet two was Lowside. Hester's finger found Tanner's Row without looking. She found Pell Lane, Chandler's Lane, and Coldwater Street, running down to the sea-wall, with a warehouse at its foot marked *Excise Store*. And under the warehouse, under the whole lower end of Coldwater Street, there was a shape drawn in faint red ink. It was a long oval, ribbed like the inside of a ship, with a legend in the old Water Board hand:

*The Great Cistern. Capacity 40,000 tuns. Founders' work. Bricked 400.*

"The Cold House," said Teo slowly, "is sitting on the old reservoir."

Hester said nothing. She was looking at the eastern end of the oval. From it a single red line ran out under the sea-wall and off the edge of the sheet. Beside the line was written: *Main Line of Supply, E. — cont. sheet 6.*

Teo found sheet six. It was nearly blank, a long sheet of white paper representing the White, with nothing on it at all but a ruler-straight red line running across it from west to east, two miles at the scale. There were tiny cross-ticks every furlong, and at its eastern end a small drawn dome with a tower on it. Beside the dome, the same hand had written:

*Halcyon. Line enters at 5th Fm. per Founders' sections (restricted).*

They stood and looked at it in the amber light.

"Water," Teo said at last. "It was the water supply. The founders built the Halcyon and they piped water into it from the city. Or out of it to the city. Either way." He did not sound as if he believed it.

"Brine," said Hester.

"What?"

"Not water. It says *supply*, but the Water Board in four hundred called everything water. The founders didn't." She touched the red line where it crossed the sea-wall. "The Cold House was an excise store. Before that, the founders. Under it there's a reservoir that held forty thousand tuns of something. From the reservoir a line runs straight out under the White, two miles, into the Fifth Fathom of the Halcyon." She heard her own voice go flat and careful. "And on my brother's casing the brine runs in lines toward the east."

Down the gallery a Fellow was coming along with a long brass snuffer, putting out the reading lamps one by one for the Lantern Hour.

"It's bricked up," Teo said. "Five hundred years. It says."

"Aske didn't think so," said Hester. "*Cistern, two question marks. Old lines.*"

She folded the sheets back into their folio carefully, along their old splits, and put the folio back on its shelf exactly where it had been, with its spine flush to the next. Then she stood a moment with her hand flat on it.

* * *

On the twenty-seventh, Teo heard the cry.

They had gone back to the Stacks to look for anything about the Cistern in the founders' period. It was a hopeless task, Teo said cheerfully, since nearly everything of the founders' period had burned in the fire of 230, and what hadn't burned was restricted, and what wasn't restricted was in short-script so archaic it would take him a month a page. But there were a few unrestricted brinescript tablets from just after the fire, in the Reading gallery. They were copies of copies, Water Board accounts, nothing that mattered. Teo, who was on Fane's Bench, was allowed to read them.

Fane. Hester turned the name over on the way there. *Odile* Fane: a Reader who listened to old salt for a living, who would know the Water Board tablets if anyone did, and who had told her on the twentieth to go carefully. *O.*

"Fane?" Teo looked pained when she said it. "She was at the Abbey in Harl from the fourteenth to the nineteenth, reading Vigil tablets for the Abbot. She wrote to the Bench from there on the sixteenth; I've the letter. Corvin sat next to her in Conclave. He'd have known she was two days off down the Harl road. You don't write *ask* for the morning about a woman in Harl."

It was a fact, and it weighed what it weighed. Hester struck the name off in her head, and was a little sorry; she had liked the warning.

He took one into a Reading Well and Hester went in with him and drew the curtain.

She had never watched Teo read. He was a poor hand at the hold; everybody knew that. Crisp had said in front of the whole Turning Intake that he held like a man trying to sit still on an ant-hill. He fidgeted, his knee went, his fingers drummed. She had seen him at the Pans trying to keep a drop and lose it three times in a row. But Reading was not the same as Keeping. Reading was listening, Fane said, and listening was a thing you could do with your knee going.

He sat in the Well's one chair with the tablet in his gloved hands. It was a slab of grey-white salt the size of a large book, cut on one face with the close lines of brinescript, its edges worn soft. He took off one glove and laid his bare palm on the uncut face. He shut his eyes. His knee went for a while, and then it stopped.

Hester stood against the curved wall and watched him settle. Not a deep stillness, not the anvil Orsolya had said Hester had: something lighter and stranger. His face went open, as if he were listening to someone speak across a room and was trying not to miss a word. His lips moved. After a minute he was speaking aloud, softly, what he heard. It was not the text cut into the tablet. It was the other thing, the moment the tablet had been made in, held in the grain.

"Scratching," he murmured. "Stylus. Someone's cutting it. A cough. A man with a cold. Fire in a grate. A cart, outside, far off—iron wheels on stone. And—the Roar, under, the residue. It's always there in the old ones, you get used to it, you hear past it—" He stopped.

His face changed.

"What's that," he said.

"What?"

"Hush." He held up his free hand without opening his eyes. "There. In the Roar. Under the scratching. There." His lips parted. "Something crying."

Hester came off the wall.

"High," Teo said slowly, "and falling. Two notes. Then a third that—breaks. Like a—" He frowned with his eyes shut. "Like a hinge, or a—"

"A child," Hester said. "A long way off."

His eyes opened.

She was standing over him. She did not remember crossing the Well. Her hands were cold.

"I heard that," she said. "In the *Wren*'s grain. On the first of Russet, on my customs stool. In the Roar. Two notes and a third that cracks." She took a breath. "What is it, Teo? You've been reading old salt for four years. What is it?"

He sat with the tablet on his knees and stared at her. Then, without another word, he got up and went out through the curtain. She heard him walking fast down the gallery, and then running.

* * *

He came back a quarter of an hour later with a book.

It was a hymnal: not the small black pocket hymnal every Tider carried, but a great old illustrated folio from the Order's shelf in the Religion gallery. It was printed in Harl two hundred years ago, with woodcuts at the head of every psalm, and the leather of its binding was rubbed through at the corners. He laid it on the reading-slope over the Water Board tablet and turned the pages fast and then slowly.

"The Abbey had this edition," he said. "The novices weren't allowed to touch it. I used to sneak in and look at the pictures. They're all of the sea. Everything. Every psalm. It's a book of *things that aren't here*." He found the page. "There."

It was the woodcut at the head of the *Song for the Vigil*. It showed a wave, drawn as a curling line of crests, the way a child draws the sea, and above it a sky with a few lines for wind. In the sky there were three birds. They were not salt-larks. They were long-winged, angled, with heads drawn small and beaks hooked. The woodcutter had carved their wings bent at the middle like a drawn bow. Beneath them, in the small italic of a caption, the printer had set a line from the psalm:

*And the gulls, the birds of the sea, crying.*

Hester looked at it for a long time.

"A gull," she said.

"A gull."

"I've never seen one."

"Nobody has." Teo's voice was not quite steady. "Hester. There are no gulls. There haven't been gulls on the Rim since the Ebb. They lived on the sea. They're in the ballads and the hymns and the Ostrine place-names. Gull Point, out by the Weeps, where there isn't a point and there aren't any gulls. They went when the sea went. Everybody knows that. The Abbot used to say they flew away with it to wherever it went, and they'd come back with it when it came home." He laughed, not happily. "We used to sing about them at the Vigil. Hundreds of us, with candles, out on the White. *And the gulls crying.* I never once thought about what one would sound like."

"It sounds like that," said Hester. "It sounds like a hinge."

They looked at each other across the open hymnal.

"The residue," Teo said. "The Roar. The Readers say it's the noise of the Ebb, of the salt setting. Crisp says so. Fane says so. Every textbook in this building says so." He put his finger on the woodcut. "The salt setting doesn't make a sound like a *bird*."

"No," said Hester.

"Then what—"

She did not answer. She did not have an answer. She had a cargo of warm grain that should not exist, and a brother whose casing wept toward the east. She had a red line on a mouse-eaten map running two miles under the White into the Fifth Fathom. She had a sound in a stay-line that she would have sworn on Mr. Thorne's stool was waves, and wind, and something wooden knocking. And she had a gull. She had all of it laid out like samples on a slate, and she could not put a grade to any of it. She did not know what it weighed.

* * *

They went out of the Stacks at the eleventh bell, when the lamps were half of them out and the galleries were dark and the porters were going round with their keys. The entrance hall was empty except for the desk, which was empty too, and the long table beside the desk where the under-archivist sat.

She had seen him on the tour. The Proctor-guide had pointed him out with a wink: *Callow, our under-archivist, or as the aspirants call him, the furniture.* She had seen him every time she had come to the Stacks since, in the same chair at the same table, under the same lamp. He was an old man, so old that age seemed to have stopped being a process in him and become a material. He was white-haired, white-browed and white-lashed, his skin gone the colour and texture of good paper. His hands lay on the table in front of him, one on top of the other, and in five visits she had never seen them move. His eyes were open. They were pale grey, and as far as she could tell they did not blink. Aspirants said he had been Settled for twenty years and the Stacks had simply never got round to moving him. Aspirants said a great many things.

She had stopped noticing him, as everyone did. She was passing the table, a pace behind Teo, with the hymnal's woodcut still in her eyes, when he spoke.

"Corvin sat there too."

It was so slow that it hardly seemed a sentence. Each word came out separately, with a breath before it, as if each had had to be fetched from a long way off and set down with care. His voice was dry and light, like paper turning. His head had not moved. His hands had not moved. Only his eyes had come round, grey and steady, to rest on her.

Hester stopped.

"I'm sorry?"

The old man took a long time. In the silence the lamp over his table hissed, and somewhere far off down a gallery a porter's keys jingled and stopped. Teo had turned back and was standing quiet at Hester's shoulder.

"There," said Callow. His eyes went, slowly, to the catalogue hall behind her, to the wall of drawers. Then they came back. "On the first of the month."

"He's in the Quiet Rooms now," Hester heard herself say. "Clear as glass. The Proctor says Hush keeps everything clear."

That took longer. The lamp hissed through all of it.

"The dead bloom," said Callow. "A month or so. Whatever the grade."

He said nothing else. They waited. After a long while his eyes went back to wherever they had been looking before, at the lamp or the table or nothing, and stayed there. He did not seem to know they were still standing in front of him.

Hester went up the Stair to the Rookery with her ledger under her arm, Teo silent beside her. The Stair turned round and round in the dark, with the chalked names of the Settled going by on the walls in the light of the landing lamps. She thought about the old man at his table. He had sat there on the first of Russet while Corvin Aske signed his name in the catalogue with blue ink. He had sat there on the ninth of Fallow last year while somebody wrote *W.L.F. (office)* in big capitals that leaned to the left. He had sat there, she supposed, for every requisition on every card in three thousand drawers, for twenty years, and nobody had ever asked him what he had seen, because he was the furniture.

At the window of R-41, before she slept, she stood a while and looked out. The White lay black to the edge of the world. Two miles off Ambry was a smudge of orange on the dark. Somewhere between here and there, under the salt, under the dark, a line ran straight as a rule from her brother's floor to the Fifth Fathom. She could not see it. She stood with her forehead against the cold glass, and for the first time since she had stepped over the Line she let herself think a thing she could not weigh. Then she went to bed.


# Chapter 10: Lantern Night

> Summer in a jar, summer in a jar,
> pour it on the rooftops where the cold folk are.
>
> — *Ambry street-song, sung by children on the last night of Russet*

Saskia Orme was a different woman on the Ambry side of the Causeway, and Hester could not decide whether she liked her better.

On the Shelf, Saskia moved through the Halcyon as if every corridor charged a toll. She answered questions in as few words as would pass, and looked at Hester, when she looked at her at all, as a glazier looks at a bubble in a pane. But when they came off the Causeway at the Eastgate at the fourth bell on the thirtieth of Russet, into the smell of hot fat and the shouting of the lantern-sellers, something in her shoulders let go. She pulled the pins out of her hair as she walked and talked round them. She bought three twists of burnt sugar from a barrow without haggling, which Hester had never seen a Quell woman do, and handed them out as if it were nothing.

"Don't thank me," she said. "It's for the walk. The Glassmarket's uphill and Teodor complains."

"I don't complain," said Teo. "I lament. It's a liturgical form."

"You lament uphill."

"Most of the great laments are uphill. It's the incline that does it." He bit his sugar and closed his eyes. "Oh. Oh, that's burnt properly. That's burnt by somebody who understands burning."

Saskia almost smiled. Hester watched it arrive and be put away again, and thought: *so she can*.

Ambry on the last day of Russet was a city getting ready to be lit. Hester had stood in these streets on thirty-three Lantern Nights and it still surprised her. Ladders leaned on every house-front on the Corn Exchange. Men at the tops of them hung iron brackets under the eaves, and women came behind with the jars: Quell glass, round-bellied, the size of a child's head, the stoppers sealed in wax and stamped with the halcyon. Inside each, a pound or so of white grain sat in the bottom. Kept light always looked like nothing until it was let out. You could hold a jar of it up to your eye at noon and see only salt.

"There's eleven thousand of them this year," Saskia said. "Bassano says. Pressed in the Pans between Seedmonth and Highsummer, every one carried over the Causeway by hand in the last three days. Two broke on the Causeway this morning and a carter lost his eyebrows."

"Broke how?" said Hester.

"Badly." Saskia took a pin out of her mouth. "I'll show you. After."

* * *

The Glassmarket was a steep street of narrow shops that climbed from the Corn Exchange to the Middle Ward, all Quell people: glaziers and lens-grinders and the women who painted fine lines on kept-light lanterns for the rich. In the window of every shop, today, there was a jar on a stand, and a child guarding it.

Saskia's mother kept a shop near the top. It had a green door, a bell that rang two notes, and a window of spectacle-glass laid out on black velvet like a jeweller's tray. Hennie Orme was a small square grey woman with Saskia's jaw and Saskia's hands, broader. She came round the counter wiping them on her apron, looked Hester up and down once, as Saskia did, and then looked at Teo and laughed.

"You'll be the monk," she said.

"Former," said Teo. "Lapsed. Fallen, in the technical sense."

"You've crumbs on your gown, fallen." She turned to the back of the shop. "Pip. *Pip.* Your mother's here, and she's brought a monk."

A child came out from under the counter. She did not run. She was six, thin, with a cloud of pale hair that had got loose from two plaits, and she held in both hands a small jar of kept light, her own, the size of an apple. She stopped in the middle of the floor and looked at her mother.

Saskia went down on her knees on the shop boards.

Hester had not seen that face before. Nobody at the Halcyon had seen it, she was nearly sure. Not softer, exactly. More open, as a shutter is more open: the same wood, swung back. Saskia did not reach for the child. She waited, on her knees, with her hands on her thighs, and the child came to her across the floor at her own pace and held out the jar.

"I didn't drop it," Pip said.

"I can see you didn't."

"Gran said if I dropped it there'd be no summer."

"Gran's a liar," said Saskia. "There'd be a bit less summer. Come here."

Then the child was in her arms and the jar was somehow safe between them. Hennie turned to the counter and began rearranging spectacles that did not need it, and Hester looked at the window.

They had tea in the back room, Quell-fashion, which meant no salt and a great deal of sugar. Teo sat on the floor and let Pip explain a game involving buttons, which he lost thoroughly and with evident pleasure. Saskia sat with her cup and her daughter's hair in her free hand, plaiting it, unplaiting it, plaiting it again.

On the shelf above the stove was a small painted portrait of a young man in a pilot's leather coat, laughing, his cap pushed back. It was the only painted thing in the room. Hester looked at it once and away.

"Jory," said Saskia, without looking up from the plait. "My husband. He's dead. You were going to ask."

"I wasn't."

"No. You weren't. You're like me, you don't ask." She tied the plait off with a bit of thread. "He sailed freighters for the Lodge. Quell to Harl and back, the long southern run. Two years ago in Cinder his ship went through the crust on the eastern White, forty miles out. A sink. The whole ship. They found the mainmast sticking up out of the salt like a grave-post, and they found him, because he was at the masthead when she went and he climbed. He'd got as far as the truck." She said it in the voice for prices. "A sink's only open for a minute. Then it skins over and sets. He was standing on the top of the mast with the salt round his chest. They had to cut it."

Pip, on the floor, was teaching Teo to flick a button into a cup. Teo was bad at it.

"That's why the Halcyon," Hester said.

"That's why the Halcyon. A Lodge widow gets a pension of nine sols a quarter. A Reader of Lumen in Ambry gets four hundred a year and a house in the Close. I did the sum." Saskia drank her tea. "I don't do causes, Vail. I do rent."

"I know," Hester said. "I do it too."

Something passed between them over the teacups. Not friendship; something earlier than that, a recognition of weights.

When they left, Saskia gave her mother money for the week. Hester saw the coin go across: a sol, a new one, bright, the edges still sharp from the mint, though there was no reason for that to mean anything. Pip stood in the door with her jar and watched them down the street.

* * *

The Glassmarket's lantern-jars were being loaded off a dray at the bottom of the street, into baskets for the ladder-women. Saskia stopped there and showed Hester what she had promised.

She took a jar out of a basket, over the carter's protests, and held it up against the late sun. "Look at the belly. Through it. At the edge."

The glass was clear and slightly greenish, as Quell glass was. There was nothing in it.

"Now this one." Saskia took another from a basket off a different dray, with a cheaper mark on the stopper.

Hester saw it at once, and was annoyed not to have seen it in the first. Near the base of the second jar there were faint lines, almost not there, like the lines in water that has been stirred and not quite settled. They ran in a stretched curl.

"Stress," Saskia said. "That's the glass remembering how it cooled. When you blow a jar it's all one heat. Then the outside cools first, because the air's on it, and shrinks, and the inside's still hot and big and gets squeezed. Let it cool fast, out on the bench in the draught, and the outside sets hard round an inside that's still pushing. The push stays in it. Months. Years. And one day you knock it, or you warm it, and it just goes. All at once." She tapped the jar with a fingernail. "This one's going to go. You pour kept summer out of a jar and the arrears come off it warm. That's enough. It'll be in pieces in somebody's gutter by Midwinter."

"And the first one?"

"Annealed." Saskia put it back gently in its basket. "You take it off the pipe and put it in a box of hot ash and let it come down slow. A day. Two for a big one. The outside and the inside cool together, near enough. The stress walks out of it a bit at a time, and when it's cold there's nothing left in it pushing. You can drop that jar on a stone floor and it'll bounce." She glanced at the carter. "Don't drop it on a stone floor."

"The two on the Causeway," Hester said.

"From Hallam's works. They save on ash." Saskia shrugged. "Cool it slow and the stress walks out. Cool it fast and it waits for you. That's the whole of glass. My father used to say it's the whole of most things."

Hester thought, for no reason she could name, of her father at pan nineteen, drawing the coals a shovelful at a time. She did not say so.

* * *

They went down to the Cold House at dusk because Hester said she would like to, and nobody argued. She had expected Saskia to leave them. Saskia walked beside her down the long slope of Coldwater Street with her hands in her coat pockets and said nothing about it at all, which was, Hester was coming to understand, how a Quell woman was kind.

Ennis was not at the desk. A girl Hester did not know sat there, with the Cold House's one charity jar on the felt beside the ledger, unopened.

"We're not open. It's the holiday."

"I'm Vail. Two hundred and eleven."

"Oh. Mother Agathe said you might." The girl looked at Teo and Saskia, then at Hester's gown and the white cord at the shoulder, and decided it was not worth the trouble.

The ward at dusk was not the ward in the morning. The high windows had gone indigo. A small oil flame burned in brass at the foot of every casing, and each casing had taken its flame into itself and held it, so that the long room was full of held lights, three hundred and four of them, in rows. Teo stopped at the bottom of the six steps.

"Oh," he said. "Oh, Hester."

"Don't," she said.

"I wasn't going to."

Tib was where Tib was. The beads on his casing had multiplied. In the lamplight they were a fine dew over the whole eastern flank, running in lines, and in three places the lines had joined into runnels. There was a damp stain on the plinth's stone at the eastern foot, the size of her palm. It had not been there four weeks ago.

She did not touch it. She had no need to.

"This is your brother," said Saskia. It was not a question.

"Tobias. Tib."

Saskia looked at him for a long moment: at the hand curled on his chest, the pencil-grip, and then at the beads. Her face gave nothing away.

"How old?"

"Fourteen."

"How long?"

"Eleven years."

Saskia nodded slowly. "That's a lot of rent," she said, and Hester, astonishingly, laughed.

Agathe came up the aisle with her brush. She was slower than at the start of the month, or Hester was seeing her differently. She took in Teo's shawl, and the brass wave of the Order still pinned to it, and her face changed.

"A brother," she said.

"Was," said Teo. "Tidewater. I'm sorry, Mother. I left."

"Many do." She took his hand in both hers, briefly, the Tider way. "Tide willing, it'll bring you round again, one way or another." Then, to Hester, low: "Come and see, my dear. I want you to see."

She took them to the sixth row. In the third casing from the end lay a stout woman of thirty or so in a brown dress, on her side, knees drawn up, both hands pressed to her mouth as if she had coughed and been surprised by it. Her hands and chin were dark. Not with shadow.

"Bett Linnow," Agathe said. "Twenty years this Rime. Haemorrhage of the lungs."

Bett Linnow's casing was not beaded. It was running. The whole eastern side was wet, and the wet ran into a groove cut in the plinth and from there into a little brass channel along the floor. And the casing was thinner. Over Bett's shoulder, where it should have been a hand's breadth thick, it was the thickness of a slate. Light came through it differently there. The shoulder of the brown dress looked closer to the air than the rest of her, as a thing looks closer when it lies near the surface of water.

"The brinewright recast her in Highsummer," Agathe said. "And again on the twelfth. Forty sols each time, from the charity fund, and Mr. Pell put up a hand and said there's no more after this. It doesn't hold."

"How long?" said Hester.

Agathe only looked at the casing.

At the end of the sixth row, on a stool beside the plinth, sat a very old woman Hester had taken for a heap of shawls. She had one hand flat on the stone, an inch from the casing. She did not look round.

"Ruth," Agathe said quietly. "Her mother. She'll sit through the light."

Hester went back to Tib for a minute before they left. Then the three of them went up the six steps and out into Coldwater Street, and found Lucan Ferrers on the doorstep.

He was in a long dark coat with a fur collar that cost more than the Cold House's charity fund, and he had the look of a man who had been standing in a cold street rehearsing. As the door opened his face was, for a moment, entirely unguarded: startled, young, guilty as a boy caught in a larder. Then the face she knew came back over it, smooth and finished, like a lid.

"Miss Vail," he said. "I followed you over the Causeway. Badly, I expect. I wanted to talk where nobody from the Shelf would hear." He looked past her at Teo and Saskia. "I hadn't allowed for a deputation."

"My brother's in there," Hester said. "Fourth row."

That stopped him. He looked at the door, and then at her, and did not say *I'm sorry*. She would have thought less of him if he had.

"The *Constant Wren*," he said instead, low. "You said the name to me in the Stacks. I'd never heard of it. The firm's ships aren't considered my department." He said *considered* with a little twist. "I went home. I asked my father."

"And?"

"And he told me to drop it. He wasn't angry. That was the strange part. My father is angry about most things; it's how he shows interest. He put his hand on my shoulder, which he hasn't done since I was nine, and told me the Halcyon's business was the Halcyon's and the firm's was the firm's, and that some laundry kept the lamps lit. Then he went into his study and shut the door." He paused. "He sits at the Assize on Thursdays. The firm's books are kept at the house."

"Naturally," said Saskia.

"Naturally," Lucan agreed, and did not smile.

Down the hill, the city's bells began to ring the eighth.

* * *

The light was to be poured at the ninth bell. They fetched Pip from the Glassmarket at a run, because Hennie would not climb a wall at her age and Pip had been promised, and then they went up onto the old sea-wall at the end of Coldwater Street, because Hester said it was the best place and nobody wanted the crowds on the Corn Exchange. Lucan came. Nobody asked him; he simply walked with them. Saskia gave him one look and decided, visibly, that he was not worth the effort of getting rid of.

So there were four of them on the wall, and a child. Lowside had always come up onto the wall on Lantern Night. The broad top was crowded with families in their coats on blankets on the cold stone, passing bottles, telling children to stop climbing on the parapet. On one side lay Lowside and the slope of the city, dark and full of ladders. On the other, far below, lay the White.

The White at night was not white. It was grey-blue, faintly luminous, as if it had kept a little of the day and was letting it out slowly. Out on it, the tiles had begun to sing. In the cold after sunset the great plates of salt contracted and ticked against each other, millions of them, out to the edge of the world, a sound like a cathedral full of glass beads being poured from one hand to another.

Pip, on Saskia's hip, put her hands over her ears and then took them away.

"It's talking," she said.

"It's cooling," said Saskia.

"Same thing," said Teo, and Saskia, Hester saw in the dark, did not argue.

Two miles out, the Halcyon was a dark shape on the dark plain, and the Lantern on top of it was dark too, the only night of the year it was. The mirrors had been turned away at sunset and the tower stood unlit against the stars like a pin stuck in a map.

Lucan stood a few feet off, at the parapet, with his hands in his expensive pockets. Hester went and stood beside him. She did not know why.

"Have you seen it from up here before?" she asked.

"From the roof of Ferrers House. We have a terrace." He did not look at her. "My mother used to take me up. She'd hold the jar while I broke the wax. She died when I was eleven." He said it in the same tone as *we have a terrace*. "I haven't been on the terrace since."

Hester thought of several things to say and said none of them.

The ninth bell began, from the Halcyon first, faint across two miles: a single deep note. Then the Lantern lit.

It did not flash. It *opened*. Light came out of the top of it as if someone had uncorked the sky: a thick honey-gold, the gold of a wheatfield at four in the afternoon in Highsummer, of the light on a wall when you have been asleep in the heat and woken. It poured from the Lantern's head in every direction and lay across the White in a widening disc, and the salt threw it back up, so that the whole plain between the Shelf and the city was a floor of gold under a gold sky.

And then Ambry answered.

On every roof, under every eave, eleven thousand jars were unstoppered at once and tipped. The light did not come out like water, or like lamplight. It came out like summer. It spilled over the gutters and ran down the slates and filled the streets to the first-floor windows. Lowside below them went from black to gold in the time it takes to say so. Each roof was its own colour: the white-gold of noon, the red-gold of evening, the green-gold of a June morning under trees, all running together in the streets until the city was a pan of molten light. And it was warm. Hester felt it on her face, standing on a sea-wall above the White on the last night of Russet: the sun on the side of her face, as on a summer afternoon when you turn toward it with your eyes shut.

There was sound in it. Every year she forgot that, and every year it came back to her like a smell. Out of the gold in Lowside's streets came birdsong, faint and enormous: the larks of Highsummer over the pans, sparrows at dawn in the eaves, a blackbird somewhere at the end of a long June evening. It was a hundred summer days that had been put in jars and carried over the Causeway and poured out on a winter city.

On the wall, people were crying, not loudly. An old man on a blanket with his face turned up, wet. A woman laughing with her hand over her mouth. Children shrieking through the gold, which fell on them like everything else. Pip had both arms out from Saskia's hip and was trying to catch it. Saskia was not looking at the light. She was looking at her daughter, with the face from the shop floor.

Teo stood straight, as he must once have stood in the abbey choir. His lips were moving. Hester did not ask what he was saying.

She thought of the Cold House: the one jar on the felt beside the ledger, the girl at the desk unstopping it. Down there in the gold, under Coldwater Street, the light would be pouring down the six worn steps and over the rows, over three hundred and four casings, over Bett Linnow and the old woman with her hand on the stone, and over a boy with a pencil hand. The casings would take it into themselves and hold it, because that was what they did, and the beads on them would shine.

The light lasted a little under a quarter of an hour. Then it thinned, gold to amber, amber to a kind of rose, and the birdsong went quiet one bird at a time, as an evening goes. Lowside came back, roof by roof, to the ordinary dark of a city at the end of Russet with the Sere blowing salt along its streets. Somebody on the wall began, raggedly, to sing the jar-song, and the children took it up.

Lucan had not moved from the parapet. In the last of the rose light his face was quite still.

"I don't think it was an accident," he said, low, so that only she could hear. "Aske."

"No," Hester said.

Two miles off, the Lantern went out, and the dark came back over the White, and the tiles went on ticking into it, cooling, millions of them, as if nothing had been poured at all.


# Chapter 11: The Sink

> The White does not forgive. The White does not hate. The White is a floor with a hole in it.
>
> — *Weathering field manual issued to first-year aspirants, the Halcyon, rev. 880 Y.E.*

The *Gannet* was a school skiff, which meant she was old, slow by the standards of the White, and built to forgive the stupid. She was a two-master, fifty feet from bowsprit to stern, with a broad flat deck and iron runners as thick as a man's thigh, and she had been repainted so many times that the blue of her hull had layers in it, like the strata in a delver's cut. At the second bell on the sixth of Fallow she lay on the salt ramp below the Halcyon's eastern Gatehouse with her sails slatting in a stiff north-easterly, and twenty-two first-year aspirants stood beside her in borrowed wool coats and glare-goggles, looking at her as cattle look at a river.

Hester had spent twenty-one years at a desk above a harbour full of landships. She had tasted their holds and never once set foot on a deck, because a single crossing to Quell cost more than she earned in a month. She looked at the *Gannet* and felt something she had not felt since she was a child: a plain, uncomplicated want.

"Tib would have given his teeth for this," she said, before she could stop herself.

"Then you'll have to enjoy it twice," said Teo. He was green already, and they had not moved. "I'm told one is sick. Copiously. Over the side."

"You're thinking of water."

"I'm thinking of the theology of motion. It's my area."

The Warden of the Lower Fathoms came down the ramp at a walk, in an iron-grey coat with the collar turned up and a fur hat that had seen better winters, and the aspirants straightened without being told.

"Right," said Orsolya Thane. "Morning, all. You've read the manual. Forget it. Three rules on the White. One: you go where the pilot says and nowhere else. Two: if Ballast lies down, you stop. Three: nobody keeps anything unless I say so." Her eye stopped on Hester, and something in it warmed. "And nobody's sick on my deck. Over the side. Into the wind is a lesson you only need once."

Ballast turned out to be a lurcher, a long grey dog with a rough coat and a face like an apologetic horse. He lay on a coil of rope by the mainmast with his chin on his paws and watched the aspirants board with deep weariness.

"Best sink-sniffer on the Shelf," said the pilot, a lean brown Fellow called Marten Coyle with a sky-blue Weathering cord and a face cracked by glare. "Fourteen years on the White and never put a foot wrong. If he lies flat, you freeze, and you don't move till I tell you. He hears the crust go hollow before the crust knows it."

Lucan Ferrers was the last to board, in a coat that fitted him and tortoiseshell goggles of his own. He stepped over the rail as though he had been doing it all his life, which, Hester supposed, he had. He nodded to her as he had nodded since Lantern Night: a nod with nothing in it but the fact of it. She nodded back.

Coyle shouted, two Fellows on the ramp knocked out the chocks, the sails came round with a crack like a slammed door, and the *Gannet* began to move.

* * *

The runners ground on the ramp and the deck jolted and Teo sat down on a hatch-cover. Then they came off onto the open White, and the grind became a hiss, a long continuous rush under the hull like silk being torn slowly, and the jolting smoothed into a hum she felt through her boots and up her shins. The wind took the mainsail, Coyle put the helm over, and the *Gannet* leaned.

She had not known it would be so fast. The ridges between the tiles flickered past so quickly they became a pattern and then a texture and then nothing at all, a smooth white rushing. The wind came over the bow full of fine salt that stung her lips and made her eyes run behind the goggles. Ahead there was no road, no rail, no edge; only the white going out flat under a hard blue sky to a horizon that was not where it ought to be.

She laughed. She held the rail with both hands and laughed into the wind like a child on a swing, and did not care who heard.

"Nine knots," Coyle shouted from the helm, to nobody. "Ten. She'll make twelve on this reach. Old cow."

They ran east and a little north for most of an hour. The Halcyon fell away to a white smudge with a needle on it, and Ambry sank below the rim of the world. The light came off the salt from below as well as above, so that there were no shadows anywhere, and even through the smoked glass the glare was a white hand laid flat on her eyes.

Then the Pull began. It started as a shimmer at the horizon, as on a road in summer, but it did not stay there. It came in. It lifted the far edge of the plain into the sky, so that the world curved upward at its rim like the inside of a bowl, and a line of low dark shapes appeared ahead, rocks or ships or a city, upside down, hanging above their own reflections.

"Pay it no mind," Coyle called. "That's Harl. Ninety miles off. The White likes to show you things."

"It's not real," said a first-year near Hester, a girl named Imke Dorran with a brown Bench cord.

"It's all real," Coyle said. "It just isn't where it says it is. You walk toward what you see out here, you'll walk till you die. Look at the dog."

Ballast had raised his head. He was regarding the upside-down city with contempt.

* * *

Orsolya came and stood at the rail beside Hester somewhere in the second half-hour. She did not hold on. She stood with her feet apart and her hands in her coat pockets and let the deck do what it liked under her.

"You've a face on you," she said, "like a girl at her first fair."

"I've never sailed."

"No. Lowside doesn't, much." Orsolya looked out at the glare. "My da took me out once. On a delvers' skiff, to the Coldbank cuts, when I was ten. I sat on a crate of picks and shrieked the whole way. He laughed till he cried." She was quiet. "Year after, the cut came in on him."

"I'm sorry."

"Long time ago." She turned her head. Under the fur hat her face was weather-reddened and lined, and her eyes behind the goggles were pale and steady. "Choir's treating you right?"

"Yes."

"You're good. You know that? Ama says you've the deepest hold she's sat beside in ten years. Deep and square, like a well. She says you hold like you're carrying something up a ladder and you'll not put it down." She smiled. "That's not always a compliment, mind. But in the Choir it is."

Hester watched the white rushing past.

"How's your brother?" Orsolya said. "Still at the Cold House?"

"Yes."

"Coldwater Street." Orsolya nodded, looking out at the salt. "They're good there. That old Tider woman. She keeps them like a garden."

Hester turned her head.

She had not told Orsolya about the Cold House. She was nearly sure she had not. On the Causeway, the first morning, Orsolya had said *your brother's kept, Corvin says*, and in the Warden's office Hester had said *I've a brother who's kept*, and that was all. She had not said where.

But Aske had known where. Aske had counted the weeping casings himself, and Aske had been Orsolya's colleague for twenty years, and Readers talked. Corvin told her, Hester thought. He told everybody everything; he couldn't help it.

"She does," she said. "Agathe. Like a garden."

"Good." Orsolya patted the rail twice, as if it were a horse. "You hold on to that."

Then Coyle called *site in sight*, and she went forward.

* * *

The bloom-site lay five miles out from the Shelf on the north-eastern reach. From a mile off it was a faint discolouration in the white, as if someone had breathed on a mirror. From a quarter-mile off it was a stain. Coyle spilled the wind and dropped the brakes, and the *Gannet* came down off her hiss into a long shuddering scrape, and stopped. In the silence Hester heard the tiles ticking in the sun, and Teo, behind her, being quietly and thoroughly sick over the side.

"Into the wind," said Orsolya, without turning round. "Well done, Anselm."

They went down a rope ladder onto the salt in pairs, Coyle first with Ballast on a long lead. Orsolya laid out the rules again, flatly: walk in the dog's track and nowhere else; stop when he stops; touch nothing with a bare hand. Each of them carried a mixing flask on a strap across the chest, a squat brass thing with two chambers, grain in one and water in the other, divided by a wax seal. You cracked the seal with your thumb, shook it, and for a few minutes you had live brine. After that it was salt water.

They walked in single file toward the stain.

A hundred yards out, the hard ringing crust, which gave back each step like a plank floor, went dull, then soft. The colour went from white to grey to a faint green-grey. Then she smelled it. It was a green smell, a wet smell, rotten-sweet like the Weeps at low water in summer, but not rotten: under it was something sharp and clean, the smell of a thing that is growing.

"Weed," said Orsolya, from the front. "That's what the old ballads called it. Don't ask me how they knew."

The site was a rough circle about forty yards across. In the middle the crust had gone dark and wet and soft, like snow in a gutter in Thaw. Small pools of brine stood in it, the size of plates, and every few breaths the surface of every pool shivered at once, as if something had been dropped into it from a height. Steam came off the dark crust faintly. Hester felt the warmth on her face from twenty feet off, as she had felt the light on Lantern Night.

"This is a bloom," Orsolya said. They stood in a half-ring at the edge of the dark crust, behind the dog. "A quickening that's come up near the top. We've a dozen like it on the north-east reach and three on the south. The Fifth's full of them, in the deep. Most never come up. When they do, they do this, and then they skin over in a month or a year and you'd never know." She crouched and put a gloved hand flat on the dark salt. "Feel it. Gloves on. Go on."

Hester put her gloved palm on the crust. It was warm through the wool. And under the warmth, so faint that she thought at first it was her own blood in her ears, there was a sound. A long slow hush, and a pause, and a hush. Like breathing. Like the noise in a shell held to the ear.

She had heard it in the Choir, in the deep, with sixty voices round her and the stay-line cold under her hands. *The sea.*

"What is it?" said Imke Dorran. Her voice was small.

"Pressure," Orsolya said. "Residue. Ask Reader Sorle and he'll tell you it's the Ebb trying to un-happen, and charge you a sol for the privilege." Some of them laughed. "It's grain that's gone quick. It's warm, it's wet, it moves, and if you see it on the White, you go round."

"And the sound?"

Orsolya was quiet, with her hand on the salt.

"The sound's the sound," she said. "Up you get."

Ballast whined.

Hester heard it because she was nearest. The dog had been standing at the end of his lead with his ears up. Now he did not lie down so much as fall, all four legs going out at once, chest and belly pressed to the salt, ears flat to his skull.

"*Freeze*," said Coyle.

Twenty-two aspirants froze. For a moment there was no sound but the tiles ticking and the dog whining and the long slow hush of the bloom.

Then the White opened under Lucan Ferrers.

* * *

It did not crack. There was no sound of breaking. Lucan was standing at the end of the half-ring, three paces to her left, a little forward of the line, where he had stepped to see the pool better.
