The crust under his boots simply went soft and down, like a sheet drawn off a bed. A hole the width of a cart opened in the white, black and wet, and he went down into it.

He did not cry out. His arms went up. His face, as it went, was surprised.

Hester had the flask off her chest before she knew she had moved. She cracked the seal with her thumb, shook it once, hard, and threw.

Not the flask. The brine. She flung it out of the open neck in a long flat arc, as she had flung slops across a yard every morning of her childhood. It went out of the brass in a sheet, glittering, and came down across Lucan's raised arms and head and shoulders and the black wet lip of the sink at the instant his chest went under the rim.

And she held.

There was no time to decide. She went down into the hold as you go down a stair in the dark, all at once, by the feel of your feet. Breath gone. Heart slowed to nothing. Every muscle set like mortar. She had kept a falling drop of brine in Crisp's class, a leaf, a moth. She had never kept anything larger than her hand. She had never kept a living person.

She kept him.

It came out of her like water through a broken sluice. The stillness poured from her into the brine, down her own arm, still outstretched from the throw, and across the air between them, and she felt the brine take it and set. Then there was a weight on her, enormous, as if she had caught the whole falling man by the wrists and was holding him over the drop with nothing under him but black.

When she could see again, Lucan Ferrers was hanging in the mouth of the sink.

A casing had set round him, rough and lumpy where the brine had splashed, clear in places and white in others. It ran from the top of his head to just below his ribs, and below that there was only black, wet, open nothing. His arms were up, the hands spread, and where the brine had splashed across the lip of the sink the casing had fused to it. It held him by its own edges at the rim of the hole, like a stopper in the neck of a bottle. His mouth was open. One lock of the wet-straw hair stood up from his head, held, mid-fall.

Then Orsolya was beside her, and Coyle was flat on his belly at the rim with a rope, and somebody in the half-ring was screaming, and Ballast was howling.

"*Don't touch him*," Orsolya was saying, sharp, not to Hester. "Coyle. Rope round the casing, below the arms. Don't you *touch* that rim. Anselm, Dorran, on the rope with him. Gloves." Then, close to Hester's ear: "Hester. Can you hear me?"

"Yes."

"Good. You kept him. You did right." Orsolya's voice was steady and fast. "Now listen. That casing's on the rim, and the rim's soft, and it's going to go. When it goes, the weight takes him down, and the sink skins over in a minute and he's under the White kept, and there's nobody on the Rim can get him out. Do you understand me?"

"Yes."

"So you've to return him. Now. While they've the rope on him."

The rim was going. A thin black crack had opened in the softened crust a hand's breadth from where the casing was fused, and wet was coming up through it. The casing tilted a quarter of an inch. Coyle, Teo, Imke and two others had the rope round it and were leaning back with their heels dug in, white-faced.

"Return him," Orsolya said.

Hester tried.

She knew how. Crisp had taught it again and again, and she had failed it nearly every time. You stayed in the hold; you put your attention on the keeping; you *let it go*. Crisp's word was *unclench*. Hester tried, and what came instead was the feeling of a fist closing tighter. Her hands were shaking out in front of her, and she could not tell if they were open or closed. If she let it go he would fall. That was the thing her body knew and would not be talked out of. She held.

"I can't."

"Yes, you can."

"He'll fall—"

"He'll not fall. They've the rope. Look at them, they've got him." The crack widened. The casing tilted another half-inch. The rope creaked. "Hester. Don't push it out. You're trying to shove it out of you like a cart. That's not how. You're holding him in your fist and trying to throw him, and you can't throw what you won't let go of." Orsolya's cold hand came down over Hester's shaking one. It did not grip. It lay there. "Open your hand."

"I—"

"Open your hand, chick. That's all. Just open it."

Orsolya's hand on hers was cold as stone and perfectly still. Hester thought, for no reason, of Saskia on her knees on the shop boards, not reaching, waiting with her hands on her thighs, while the child came across the floor at her own pace.

She opened her hand.

The weight went out of her all at once. The casing went with a sound like a pane of ice dropped on a stone floor, one great flat *crack* that came up through her boots and slapped her in the face. White dust burst from the sink's mouth, and out of the dust came a hot wind, hot as an oven door opening, that knocked her back on her heels. In the middle of it Lucan Ferrers, whole and wet and gasping, was hauled over the lip on the end of a rope and dragged across the soft crust on his belly, five yards, ten, coughing, alive.

Behind him the sink skinned over. She watched it happen: a slow wet white closing, like a mouth.

Lucan lay on his side on the hard salt with Coyle kneeling on him, making a sound she had not heard a man make before, a high, airless, astonished wheeze, one hand clutching at his side.

"Ribs," said Coyle. "Two at least. The snap. Hold still, sir, you've no breath to waste. Hold *still*—"

"Good," Orsolya said beside her. "Oh, that's good. That's a lovely release. That's—"

Hester turned her head to look at her, and the world stopped.

* * *

It did not go dark. She did not faint, and there was no pain. There was just no time.

She was looking at Orsolya's face, at the cold red cheeks under the fur hat and the mouth open on *that's*. And then she was looking at the sky.

It was a different sky. The same hard blue, but the sun had moved; she could see it had moved. The few shadows there were had swung round, as shadows swing on a sundial. She was lying on her back on the salt with a coat under her head. Teo was kneeling beside her with her hand in both of his, his face grey under the freckles, his eyes wet. Lucan was not on the salt any more.

Her mouth was so dry her tongue stuck to her teeth.

"Water," said Teo, and she drank all of it, and it was not enough.

"What—"

"You lagged," Orsolya said. She was crouched on Hester's other side. "You kept a grown man, first time, with a throw, and then you returned him, and the lag waited till you'd put the weight down. That's how it comes. Four minutes. You've been out four minutes."

Four minutes.

She lay on the salt and looked up at the moved sun. She had been here, all of her, eyes open; Teo said afterward that they had been open the whole time. Orsolya had talked to her and she had not answered. Teo had held her hand and it had been as cold and stiff as a gate-latch. And there was nothing. Not even the memory of an absence. Orsolya had said *that's*, and then the sun had moved.

She had stood beside Tib's casing a hundred and thirty times and told him the news. *Sere's in. Daisy's had another one.* She had never once in eleven years thought about what it was like for him, inside. Whether it was like this. Whether it was like anything at all. Whether, if they returned him tomorrow, he would think he had blinked.

She started to shake.

"Here." Orsolya was wrapping her in a blanket, in Coyle's coat. "It's the cold. It's only the cold. It takes everyone like this the first time. Anselm, rub her hands. Not like that. Like you mean it."

Teo rubbed her hands like he meant it.

"Is he—"

"Ferrers? Two ribs, maybe three, and a bruise where his pride was. He's on the *Gannet*, telling Coyle how to splint himself."

Hester shut her eyes. That was a mistake. The dark behind her lids felt too much like the four minutes she did not have, and she opened them again at once.

"Four minutes," she said.

"Aye." Orsolya's hand rested on her hair, cold and light. "You'll not remember them. But you'll get used to there being a gap. Everyone below has a few." She looked out toward the bloom, steaming quietly in the sun. "I've lost about a month, all told, in thirty years. A minute here, an hour there. You stop counting."

* * *

They sailed home at a walking pace under a single reefed sail, because Coyle said the deck was full of invalids and he would not have them shaken. Lucan lay on a pallet by the mainmast with his chest strapped and Ballast's chin on his ankle. He did not look at her. Hester sat on a hatch-cover in two blankets, drinking from a flask Teo kept refilling, and did not look at him. She did not suppose there was a correct thing to say. She had kept the son of the Lord Exchequer in salt over a hole in the world and then dropped him on the White like a sack of meal, and broken him.

Halfway home, when the Halcyon was a needle on the horizon again, Lucan spoke without opening his eyes.

"Vail."

"What."

"You threw it. The brine. You didn't pour it, you threw it, like—" He breathed in carefully, around the ribs. "Like a scullery maid. With a bucket."

"Laundress," Hester said. "My mother was a laundress. Slops go across the yard."

He was silent a while.

"Remarkable," he said, and went back to sleep.

* * *

The aspirants scattered off the ramp quickly and quietly, as people leave a funeral. Orsolya took Lucan to the Physic ward herself on a litter. Before she went she put her cold hand on Hester's shoulder and said, "Hot bath. Hot food. Drink till you're sick of it. You did well, chick." Then she was gone down the Stair, and Teo was steering Hester up through the Rookery passages, talking the whole way about the probable theological status of a lurcher, and not once letting go of her elbow.

There was someone waiting at the door of R-41.

He was leaning on the passage wall in a black-corded gown that had been good once, with a stick in one hand. His white hair was tied back with its piece of string, and the Sere had pulled half of it loose. He was older than she had thought him from the lecture benches, and thinner. He looked her up and down, blankets, salt-crust, blue lips, without any expression at all.

"Reader Sorle," Teo said, and stopped walking.

Sorle looked at Hester.

"You kept a man," he said. "On the open White. With a *throw*."

She was too tired to be anything but honest. "Yes."

"Badly."

"Yes."

"Lumpy casing, splash-fused to the rim. A release like a cart going over a cliff. Two of the Lord Exchequer's son's ribs, which I gather the Lord Exchequer will want to discuss with somebody." He tilted his head. "And four minutes of lag, Coyle says, on a first keeping of a grown man. Some don't come back at all." He looked at her a moment longer, his mouth turned down at the corners in an expression she could not read. "Interesting."

He pushed himself off the wall with his stick.

"My study. Second Fathom, the black door at the end of the Horology gallery. The day after tomorrow, at the fourth bell. Don't be late. I've no patience and less time." He limped away down the passage, and stopped, and said over his shoulder: "And bring your tongue, Miss Vail. Corvin always said it was the best thing about you."

His stick tapped away toward the Stair.

"Did he just—" Teo began.

"I don't know," said Hester. She went into the cell and lay down on her bed in her boots and her coat and her two blankets, with her eyes open, looking at the salt ceiling, faintly pink in the last of the window light, and did not close them.


# Chapter 12: Sorle's Folly

> Reader Sorle's hypothesis was found to be without foundation in observation, unsupported by the arrears calculus as correctly applied, and an embarrassment to the Bench. The Conclave records its censure. The Reader is thanked for his candour.
>
> — *Minutes of the Conclave of Readers, 14 Cinder 871 Y.E.*

The black door at the end of the Horology gallery had no name on it. It had a brass knocker in the shape of a closed eye, and a card pinned beneath the knocker that said in a thin furious hand: *NOT NOW*. Hester knocked anyway, at the fourth bell exactly, because she had stood outside it for ten minutes waiting for the bell. The door opened before her hand had left the knocker.

"You're punctual," said Benedek Sorle. "I find that suspicious in a person. Come in. Don't touch anything. Don't sit there, that's Lise's chair. Sit there."

*There* was a three-legged stool beside a desk so heaped with paper that the desk itself was a rumour. Hester sat on it.

The study was long and narrow and cold. It was one of the old Readers' rooms on the outer gallery of the Second Fathom, with a slit of salt-glass for a window, high in the wall. It let in a grey light that came from a light-shaft somewhere above and had been reflected too many times to have any warmth left in it. Every wall was shelves, and every shelf was books, and where there were no books there were slates. Dozens of slates, the size of tea-trays, stacked and leaning and propped. Every one was covered edge to edge in chalk: columns of numbers, curves, the strange compressed notation of the arrears calculus that Sorle wrote on the Black Bench board in lectures and that half the aspirants copied without understanding. Some of the slates had been rubbed half out and written over. Some had dates on them. One, propped against the coal-scuttle, said at the top *870*, and the chalk on it had gone yellow.

There was a fire, small and bad. There was a kettle on it.

On the desk, among the paper, under a glass bell the size of a teacup, there was a moth.

It was kept. She knew it before she looked properly; she could feel it from the stool, a small weight at the back of her eyes, as if somebody had laid a single grain of salt on each lid. It was a grey moth, an ordinary house-moth with soft dusty wings, held in a casing so fine and clear she could only see the casing's edges where it caught the grey light. Its wings were open. It had been kept mid-flight, a quarter-inch above the surface of the desk, so that the casing touched the wood only at one point, a sort of glass stalk. The moth hung above it, flying, going nowhere.

On the wall beside the window there was a portrait. It was a small oil, not well painted. A woman of forty or so with a broad face and her hair pinned up untidily, in a Physic's red cord, laughing at whoever was painting her.

"Lise," said Sorle, following her eyes. "My wife. Dead twenty-three years. The Settle. She was a Physic on the Brinery wards. Kept people for a living. I'm told I'm very bad company." He lowered himself into the chair that was not Lise's, which creaked. He had his stick across his knees. In the grey light his face was all lines and nose, and his eyes, deep under the white brows, were pale and bright and unkind. "Tea?"

"Please."

He did not move to make it. He looked at her.

"Tell me about Ferrers," he said.

* * *

She told him about Ferrers. It took a long time, because he would not let her tell it.

"You threw it," he said. "Not poured. Threw."

"Yes."

"From how far?"

"Three paces. Four."

"So the brine was in the air for—what? A second? Less. And you went into the hold *before* it landed, or after?"

"I don't know. Before. As I threw."

"*Before.*" He leaned forward. "Before. So you were holding while the brine was in flight. You lent your stillness to a stream of brine in the air, which by any orthodox account should have been impossible, because the brine wasn't touching anything you were trying to keep. Except you, at one end, and Ferrers, at the other, and the air between. And by the time it reached him it was already setting. You were keeping the throw." He sat back. "Crisp will have a fit. Go on."

She went on. He stopped her again at the casing.

"Describe the grain."

"Rough. Lumpy where it splashed. Clear in the thick places, white where it was thin."

"Which way did it grow?"

She looked at him.

"Which way," Sorle said patiently, as to an idiot, "did the casing grow? From him outward, or from outside in?"

She thought. She saw it again, the glitter of the brine coming down in a sheet across his raised arms, the setting.

"From outside," she said. "In. It started where the brine landed and grew in toward him. The outside set first."

"Yes," said Sorle. "Yes, it did. Because you poured it. Or threw it, which is the same thing with more swagger. Every poured casing grows from the outside in, Miss Vail. It's the first thing Crisp should have told you, and the last thing anybody remembers, because nobody pours casings any more except on the Brinery wards, where they brush it on." He reached for the kettle, finally, with a hand that was not quite steady, and began to make tea. "Whereas when a worker keeps himself—and it can be done, it's been done, it's a stupid thing to do and Corvin did it every other month to test his own reading—the casing grows from the skin outward. It has to. The worker's lending his stillness from inside. It starts where he starts. It grows out from him like rime from a twig."

He poured. The tea came out black. He put a pinch of salt in hers without asking and handed it to her.

"Which way," he said, "did Corvin Aske's casing grow?"

Hester held the cup in both hands. It was scalding. She did not drink.

She saw it. She had stood in the corridor outside Cell 3-17 in the press of gowns at first bell on the seventeenth of Russet, looking at a thin man held standing in the middle of his own workroom with one arm reaching. She had looked at the casing with her assayer's eye, because there was nothing else she could do with it. She had seen the grain, and the way it lay, and she had thought, before the Proctor's voice came down on all of it, *that's wrong*. And then she had put the thought away, because there was a Proctor and a verdict and a hundred people in the corridor, and she was nobody.

"In," she said. "From outside. It was poured."

Sorle nodded slowly, as if she had passed a test he had not expected her to pass.

"Corvin couldn't keep himself in a pickle jar," he said. "Not a whole-body casing. Not like that. He'd sleeve a hand to test his reading, or keep a finger, and come to my door with his hand in a block of salt like a boy with his fist stuck in a vase, wanting me to release it, because his release was worse than yours. And his hold was radial. Fine and radial, like a star. Every casing he ever made had a little starburst in the grain where he started it." He drank his tea. "That casing in 3-17 has no starburst. It was poured, from outside. By somebody else. A child could see it. Gage saw it. Gage isn't a child; he's a soldier, which is worse, because a soldier knows when he's been told to stop looking."

"Then why—"

"Why didn't I say so?" Sorle put the cup down. "I did. I said so to Gage on the morning of the seventeenth, and I said so in writing to the Deputy Chancellor on the eighteenth, and I said so to the Conclave on the twenty-second, where I was thanked for my candour." The word came out of him like something spat. "*Thanked for my candour.* They've a phrase for everything in Conclave, Miss Vail. That one means *sit down, Benedek, nobody believes a word you say*. They've been thanking me for my candour for thirty-two years."

* * *

He told her about the sixteenth of Russet without being asked.

"Corvin came to my door that afternoon," he said. "Third bell. He'd a sheet of figures in his hand and ink on his face, which was usual, and he was frightened, which wasn't. He wanted me to look at some numbers. Pressure figures. Stay returns from the Fifth over five years. He wanted me to run them through the calculus for him, because Corvin's sums were like his release; he got there, but you wouldn't want to watch. I said I was busy. I was busy. I was writing a reply to a Fellow in Harl who'd published something stupid." His mouth turned down. "He said: come to my Cell tonight, then. The tenth bell. Hester Vail's coming at the tenth; come at half-past. I'll show you both, and you can tell me I'm mad in person. I said I'd think about it."

"And you came."

"I came. At the half after the tenth. I don't sleep. I thought I might as well be insulted by Corvin as by the Harl Fellow." He turned the cup in his hand. "The corridor was dark. His door was shut and there was no light under it. I knocked. Nobody. I thought he'd gone up to bed and forgotten, as he did; he'd forget his own name if it weren't written on his gown. So I went back up to the Second."

"By the Stair?"

"By the Readers' back stair. I came down it and I went back up it. My rooms are on the Second, above his, and I've not used the main Stair after dark in ten years." He looked at her. "Why?"

"Because I was there. At the tenth. I waited outside the door and he didn't come. And while I waited—" She stopped. She had told the Proctory this, and Teo, and nobody had thought it worth a second question, and she had stopped thinking about it. "While I waited I heard wheels on the Stair, below, and went to the rail. A trolley, coming up from the Fourth or the Fifth. Two or three people in grey, and a blue lantern, and something under a canvas. Bumping up a step at a time. I thought it was porters with brine casks."

"At the tenth bell? On the Stair?" Sorle's eyes had narrowed. "The porters use the lifts after the eighth. The Stair's for feet. Nobody drags a trolley up the Stair. It's a hundred and nine steps from the Fourth to the Third, and they're worn into bowls."

"Then who—"

"Somebody who didn't want to use a lift," Sorle said. "Lifts have lift-men. Lift-men have mouths." He sat back. "And when did you leave?"

"Before the half. Just before. I was cold, and I was angry. I went up."

"Up the Stair."

"Yes."

"And the trolley?"

"It hadn't got far. They kept stopping to rest. It was still below me when I went."

They sat. The fire made a small sound. On the desk the moth hung above its stalk of glass, flying.

"So you went up the Stair just before the half," Sorle said slowly, "and I came down the back stair at the half and went back up it, and the corridor was dark, and his door was shut. And at first bell a porter found him in the middle of his workroom in a poured casing of Hush grain with his arm out. And between the half and first bell, somebody brought something up the Stair on a trolley, from below, and didn't use a lift." He looked at the slates on the wall as if they had personally failed him. "Well. I don't have a sum for that."

"He wasn't in his Cell," Hester said. It came out of her flatly, the way her grades came out. "When I knocked. He wasn't there. He'd gone below. Something happened below. And then he was brought back up."

Sorle said nothing for a long time.

"Yes," he said at last. "I think that's very likely."

* * *

"What's the Held Moment?"

She asked it because she had to ask something, and because the slate by the coal-scuttle with *870* on it had been looking at her for an hour. Sorle's mouth went thin.

"Do you want the version they teach second-years as a cautionary tale," he said, "or the version that's true?"

"The one you believe."

"That's not the same as the one that's true. Nobody in this building knows the one that's true. That's my whole complaint." He levered himself up out of the chair with the stick and went to the coal-scuttle and picked up the old slate and blew the dust off it and looked at it, as a man looks at a likeness of himself young. "All right. Here it is. Grain keeps. Why?"

"Nobody knows."

"Nobody knows. Correct. The mineral school says stillness is a property of the crystal formed under pressure at the Ebb, like hardness, or colour. That's not an answer, that's a description with a hat on." He put the slate on the desk in front of her, beside the moth. "Look at this."

It was a curve. A long curve, chalked in a firm young hand, rising slowly and then more steeply, labelled at intervals with depths in fathoms and with numbers she did not understand.

"Stillness by depth," Sorle said. "Every grade, every boring, every reference jar from the Shelf to the deepest the Fathomers had reached in 870. I spent four years getting the numbers. Grain gets stiller as you go down. Everybody knew that. But nobody had ever plotted it. Look at the shape."

She looked.

"It's not a straight line," she said.

"No. If stillness were a property of crystal formed under pressure, it ought to go up with depth more or less in a line: more pressure, more stillness. It doesn't. It goes up like *that*." He drew his finger along the curve, up into the steep part. "It goes up like the arrears on a keeping. Exactly like. Mass times duration times liveliness. The same curve you get if you plot the stillness of a casing against the size and life of what it's keeping." He tapped the slate. "Grain from the Hollow Sea has the stillness curve of a *keeping*, Miss Vail. Not a mineral. A keeping. The deeper you go, the more it behaves like the casing round something enormous, and alive."

Hester sat on the stool and looked at the curve.

"So I stood up in Conclave," Sorle said, "in Cinder of 871, thirty-two years old and stupid with it, and I said: the sea didn't go. The sea's still there. It's held. The salt is the casing. The Ebb wasn't the sea leaving; it was the sea being kept, all at once, in one instant, by God knows what. And every dram we dig and dissolve, we're spending a bit of that held moment, like lamp-oil."

He put the slate back against the scuttle.

"They laughed," he said. "Not all of them. Some of them were very polite. And then a young Fathomer stood up—twenty-six, Lowside, sharp as a delver's pick, three years in the Choir and a gift for sums like nothing I'd ever seen—and took me apart. In public. In an hour." There was something in his voice that was not bitterness, or not only. "She showed that my numbers from below the Fourth were bad. They were; the borings were contaminated, I'd been careless. She showed that my arrears terms were wrong at the top of the curve. They were; I'd used Halloway's constants and Halloway was an ass. She showed that if the Hollow Sea were a keeping, the arrears on nine hundred years of a sea would be so large that the crust should be warm to the touch from here to Quell, and it isn't. And she was right about all of it." He sat back down in the chair. "She was right about my maths. She was wrong about the world. And I couldn't prove that, and I still can't, and that's the whole of my career. Orsolya Thane."

Hester's cup was cold in her hands.

"The Warden," she said.

"She wasn't Warden then. She was a chorister with a slate. She made her name that day. I lost mine." Sorle looked at the moth. "We haven't exchanged forty words since. She runs the deep like a ship's captain and she's very good at it, and she thinks I'm a dangerous old fool, and she may be right. She's right about most things. That's what makes her unbearable."

Hester thought about the bloom on the White, and the warm crust under her glove, and the hush and pause and hush in it. She thought about Orsolya crouched beside it with her hand flat on the salt, saying *the sound's the sound*.

"The bloom-sites are warm," she said.

Sorle looked up.

"On the White. The quickenings. When they come up. They're warm. I had my hand on one two days ago. And there's a sound in them." She stopped. "And in the Choir. When we stay. You hear it through the line. Everybody does. They call it the Roar. But it's not a roar." She did not know how to say it. She had not said it to anyone, not even Teo, not properly. "It has a shape. It goes up and down. Like breathing. Like—"

"Like waves," Sorle said quietly.

She had never seen waves. Nobody on the Rim had seen waves. She had seen the pictures in the tavern signs and the drawings in the ballad-books, curling white lines like a row of fingers.

"I don't know," she said. "I've never heard waves."

"No," said Sorle. "No. None of us have."

* * *

He would not sponsor her. She had not asked; she had gone to him a fortnight ago and asked, and he had said *I don't take charity cases from the dead*, and she had not forgiven him for it. She did not ask again. He told her anyway, at the door, as she was going, as if she had.

"I'll not take you onto my Bench on a story," he said. "Not on a taste and a trolley and a casing that grew the wrong way. I've been thanked for my candour for thirty-two years, Miss Vail, and I'll not be thanked for yours. If I stand up in Conclave again, I'll stand up with something I can put on a slate." He leaned on his stick in the doorway, thin and old and furious. "Bring me evidence. Not tastes. Evidence. A thing I can weigh. A thing Gage can't lose."

"And then you'll sponsor me."

"And then," said Sorle, "I'll think about it." He began to close the door. Then he stopped it with his stick and said through the gap, in a different voice: "You lagged four minutes. On a first keeping. Do you know how many people on the Shelf could have done what you did on the White?"

"No."

"Nor do I. I'd like to find out. That's all." And he shut the door, and the card that said *NOT NOW* swung on its pin.

* * *

The Physic ward of the Halcyon was on the Third Fathom, behind the Glaziers' kilns, in a long low room that smelled of camphor and warm brine. It had a row of iron beds along one wall and a row of shallow baths along the other, sunk in the floor. They were tiled in white and steaming faintly, for the slow returns. Two of the baths had kept people in them, lying under a hand's depth of warm brine, their casings softening by degrees. A Physic in a red cord sat between them on a stool, reading a book and touching each casing every few minutes with two fingers, like a woman testing bread.

Lucan Ferrers was in the end bed by the wall, propped on pillows, with his chest strapped and a book open face-down on the blanket. He was looking at the ceiling. When he saw her he did not look away, which she had half expected. He watched her come down the length of the ward with no expression at all.

"Vail," he said.

"Ferrers."

"Have you come to see the damage?"

"I've come because Teo said if I didn't he'd bake me nothing till Midwinter." She stood at the end of the bed with her hands in her gown. "How many?"

"Three." He breathed in carefully. "Two cracked, one broken. The Physic says I'm to lie still for a fortnight and think about my sins. I've told her I haven't enough to fill a fortnight. She wasn't amused." He looked at her. "My father's written to the Chancellor."

"About me?"

"About the *Gannet*. About the Warden letting first-years walk a bloom-site in Fallow. About the dog, possibly; I didn't read the whole letter. My father writes letters the way other men break furniture." He moved a hand on the blanket. "Your name is not in it. I asked him to leave it out."

She had not expected that. She did not know what to do with it.

"Why?"

He was quiet for a while. The Physic between the baths turned a page.

"I've never owed anybody anything," Lucan said. He said it to the ceiling. "Do you understand? Not once. Not in my life. Everything I've ever had was either mine already or bought. Even my tutors were paid in advance. And then I went through the floor of the world in front of twenty people, and the last thing I saw was you, with a brass flask in your hand, and your face." He stopped. "You had a face like somebody catching a pot off a shelf. Annoyed. As if I'd fallen on purpose to inconvenience you."

"You had," said Hester. "You stepped forward. The Warden said walk in the dog's track. You stepped forward to see the pool better. You always step forward to see better."

His mouth moved. It was nearly a smile.

"Yes," he said. "That's fair." He looked at her at last. "Thank you. That's what I'm trying to say. I'm bad at it. I've never had to practise. Thank you."

"You're welcome," Hester said, and found that she was bad at it too, and that her face was hot. She looked at the baths. "It was a lumpy casing. Sorle says."

"Sorle." Lucan's eyebrows went up. "You've been to see Sorle?"

"He asked."

"He never asks anyone anything. He tells them they're wrong." He paused. "What did he want?"

She looked at him. She looked at the Physic, who was absorbed in her book. She looked down the length of the ward toward the door. Then she sat down on the edge of the bed, carefully, so as not to jog him. She told him, low, about the casing that grew from the outside in, and the trolley on the Stair before the half, and Sorle at the half.

Lucan listened without interrupting. When she had finished he reached, wincing, for the book on the blanket, and took from between its pages a folded paper: a page torn out of a ledger. He had torn it badly, she noticed, with the clumsiness of a man who had never needed to tear evidence out of anything.

"Thursday," he said. "While my father was at the Assize. The *Wren*'s cargo isn't in the firm's books, not under that name. But there's a supplies ledger, brine and Middling to brineries all over the city, and there's this. Highsummer."

Hester tilted it to the lamp over the bed.

*Cold House. 40 fk. live brine (Deep). Cistern mntce. Acct: Halcyon — per W.L.F.*

Three letters at the end of the line, in a clerk's copperplate. She had seen them at the foot of a bill of lading and on a catalogue card in the Stacks, each time in a different hand. This was the third.

"The Cold House hasn't a cistern," Lucan said. "I went down on the Friday with my Exchequer face on and walked round the whole building, and the yard. No tank. No reservoir. Only a drain in every row of that ward, running east. I put my hand down one. It was wet. Water doesn't run in a dry house in a dry season." He looked at her. "What's under that building, Vail?"

"The founders' Cistern," Hester said. "Bricked up in four hundred, the Water Board says. And a line out of it under the White, to the Fifth."

He lay with that for a while. She gave him back the page.

"Keep it somewhere better than a book," she said. "It's your father's."

"Yes," he said slowly. "I suppose it is." Then: "And Sorle wants evidence."

"Something he can weigh. Something Gage can't lose."

Lucan turned his head on the pillow and looked at her, and his face was not smooth at all.

"Then we'll have to go and get some," he said. "Won't we."

"*You*'re lying still for a fortnight."

"I'll lie still for a week. I'm a fast healer. It's the only thing about me my father admires." He shut his eyes. "Go and find out how to get under that building, Vail. I'll find out how to pay for it."

She sat a moment longer on the edge of his bed, in the warm camphor air, with the steam coming off the baths and the Physic turning her pages. Then she got up and went out, up the Third Fathom corridor toward the Stair. She passed the shut door of Cell 3-17, which had a Proctory seal across it now, red wax on black ribbon. She did not stop. She had seen the map in the Brinestacks. She knew where the Great Cistern lay. She knew which street was over it, and whose cellar would have the door. She went up the Stair to find Teo, already counting in her head what a lantern would cost, and a rope, and a saw that would cut brass.


# Chapter 13: The Cistern

> The founders' cistern beneath Coldwater and the Eastern Wards is bricked, and shall not be reopened, its brine being unwholesome and its passages unsafe.
>
> — *Minute of the Ambry Water Board, 400 Y.E.*

Lucan Ferrers brought a saw.

He brought it up the Rookery stair at the ninth bell on the thirteenth of Fallow, wrapped in a good wool blanket, and laid it on Hester's bed as if he were laying out a body. It was a short bow-saw for metal, with a rosewood frame.

"Where did you get this?"

"From the estate carpenter at Ferrers House. He was asleep." Lucan sat down on Teo's bed without being asked, which he had begun doing, and held himself upright, which he had not stopped doing since the sink. The ribs were mending; she could see it in his breathing, shallow at the top and careful at the bottom. "I left him a note. And a sol. I think he'll consider himself well paid for a saw that cuts brass like cheese."

"You think we'll need to cut brass."

"I think you think so, or you wouldn't have asked me for plans." He took a roll of paper out of his coat. "The Water Board survey of 398, two years before they bricked it. My father keeps the originals of everything that might one day be worth money. I traced them last night. Don't look at the lettering."

Teo spread the roll on the boards between the beds and weighted the corners with his boots and a plate of saltcake, and they bent over it, all three, in the light of the one lamp.

Hester had seen the shape before, in faint red on a mouse-eaten sheet in the Lattice gallery: the long ribbed oval under the foot of Coldwater Street, the line running out of it straight as a rule. This was the same thing drawn by a man who had walked it. *Great Cistern: capacity 40,000 tuns*, in heavy ink, ribbed across with vaulting. A short passage ran west from it, up under the street, to a square marked *Excise Store — cellar — inspection grate*. And from the cistern's eastern end the double line ran out under the sea-wall and the dotted shore and on across two feet of blank paper that was the White. The draughtsman had written along it every few inches, in a small precise hand: *Main. Main. Main.* Where the paper stopped he had written: *to the Collegium. Not surveyed beyond.*

"It isn't a pipe," Lucan said, pointing to an inset: a horseshoe of brick with a man drawn in it for scale, his hat brushing the crown. "It's a tunnel. You could drive a pony down it."

Hester put her finger on the square marked *cellar*. She had walked over that spot on the first of every month for eleven years.

"Agathe said yes?" said Teo.

"Agathe said yes."

She had written on the ninth, and the answer had come with the carrier on the eleventh, in Agathe's round copybook hand. *Mr. Pell is to go to his sister's in Harl on the fourteenth for three nights, it being her lying-in. The cellar door is mine to keep, as the oil is kept there. I do not ask what you want with the cellar, my dear, and I do not want to know, except that if it is to do with the weeping I shall pray for you, and if it is not I shall pray for you anyway. Come by the yard. Eleventh bell. A.L.*

"She's seventy," Lucan said. "And a Tider. And you're asking her to let three Halcyon aspirants into a bricked civic works in the middle of the night. If it's found out, she'll lose her place."

"I know."

"Does she?"

"Yes," said Hester, "she does," and Lucan looked at her a moment longer and nodded.

They planned it on the floor, over the plan. Three kept-light lamps, which needed no air and would not go out if the tunnel was foul. Rope. A crowbar. The saw. Water, because Hester did not intend to come out of that hole without doing grain-work. A tin box lined with oilcloth for anything they took. Teo's chalk.

"And if somebody's down there?" Teo asked.

"Then we come back up," said Hester.

"And if they don't let us?"

Lucan touched his ribs lightly. "Then I'll tell them who my father is," he said. "It's nearly the only thing I'm good for."

* * *

The fourteenth of Fallow was a dark night with no moon and a hard thin wind out of the north. They took the last Causeway dray into the city at the tenth bell, on the tailboard among sacks of Halcyon laundry, and by the eleventh bell they were in the yard behind the Cold House among the oil-drums and empty brine casks, and Agathe was standing in the cellar door with a candle, in her grey shawl, looking exactly as she looked at the second bell of the morning.

"You'll be the Ferrers boy," she said to Lucan.

"I am, madam."

"You've your mother's chin. She came here once, to give money. She stood in the ward and wept like a child." Agathe looked at Teo. "And you're the one from the Abbey. They'll have taught you to be quiet." She stood aside. "Mind the step. There's eleven of them and the sixth is gone."

The cellar was low and long and smelled of lamp-oil and old brick. Oil barrels stood in racks along one wall, the brinewright's stores along the other. At the far end, where the cellar ran under the charity ward, iron gratings the size of a door were set flush with the brick.

Agathe stopped by the last of them and lowered her candle.

"I come down here for the oil every morning," she said. "Thirty-one years. And in the spring, about the time the first of them started weeping, this grating was different. Somebody had had it up." Round its edge the mortar had been raked out and renewed, pale and clean against the old black iron. "I asked Mr. Pell. He said it was Halcyon maintenance on the old cistern taps, under the Charter, and no business of mine." Her mouth did not move. "I've kept the oil thirty-one years. I know what's my business."

Lucan went down on one knee, carefully, favouring the ribs, and put his fingers to the mortar.

"This has been opened more than once," he said. "They've raked it out and reset it, and raked it out again. There's three colours."

"Then they come and go," said Teo.

"One of them was a Reader," said Agathe.

Hester looked up.

"Yours, my dear. Reader Aske." Agathe's candle did not waver. "He came in Russet to count the weeping, and I walked him round. Then he came back on the twelfth, after dark, by the yard, as you have, and asked me for the cellar key. Mine, not Mr. Pell's; there's only the two. He said it'd go easier if Mr. Pell didn't hear of it. I gave it him." She looked at the grating. "He was down two hours. He came up white to the lips with his boots wet, and gave me back my key, and said, *Mother, I'll tell you when I'm sure.* Five days after, the Board had a letter from the Halcyon saying he was dead."

Hester had known that Aske had counted the casings. She had not known he had been under them.

"You never said."

"You never asked me about the cellar till the ninth. And he asked me not to tell anybody, and I'd no notion who *anybody* was." Agathe's voice stayed gentle. "I've thought since that I should have. Tide forgive me, I didn't know what to be afraid of."

"Then we're going where he went," said Hester. She took the crowbar off her shoulder.

The new mortar came out in crumbs, and the grating, lifted lately, wanted to lift. When they had it up and leaning against the wall, a breath came out of the hole beneath it. It was too slow for a draught. The air simply moved, upward, past their faces.

It was cold. It was sweet. Hester knew it before it reached her mouth: the cellar-of-apples sweetness of the charity ward on the six worn steps. And under the sweetness, far down at the back of the tongue, the thing she had come for.

Quick.

Agathe stood with her candle and looked at Hester's face.

"Go on, then," she said. "I'll not leave the hole." And then, which Hester had not expected: "Tide carry you."

* * *

An iron ladder went down twelve feet into a sloping passage, just high enough to walk in if you stooped, that ran east perhaps thirty yards. Then the walls fell away on either side, and the lamps that had been filling the passage with amber were lighting nothing at all.

Lucan opened his shutter wide, and the light went out and up and up and found, at last, a ceiling.

The Great Cistern was a hall. It had been built to hold water, and had held it for a hundred years before the Ebb, and had held nothing for nine hundred since. Rows of brick piers as thick as houses marched down its length, each rising into a vault, so that the roof was a field of shallow domes, like the underside of a quilt. At about twice a tall man's height a pale line ran round every pier and every wall, exactly level. It was where the water had stood.

Down the centre of the dry brick floor, from the west wall to an east wall they could not see, ran a gutter of dressed stone a foot wide and a foot deep. The channel was not dry.

It was full of brine. It lay in the stone black and motionless in the lamplight, with a skin on it like the skin on milk, and the sweetness came up off it so thick she could taste it with her mouth shut.

"That," said Teo quietly, "is live."

"Yes."

"It shouldn't be. Live brine dies in minutes." He crouched, not touching. "Somebody's keeping it alive."

"Nobody's keeping it," Hester said. "It's being fed."

She did not say what with. She knew what she thought, and she would taste it before she said it. You did not grade by hoping.

They followed the channel east.

The brick swallowed every sound. Somewhere far off something dripped, with a long interval between each drop.

Every twenty yards a smaller gutter came into the channel from the north side, from under the street. Each arm ran back to the foot of a pier, and above each pier, set into the vault, was a round iron grating. Hester stopped under the third and held up her lamp. From the grating, eight or nine feet over her head, hung a rod.

It was brass, perhaps an inch thick, and it was new: bright, unscratched, with none of the green that brass took in damp. It came down out of the grating and ran down the pier in iron clips, then bent and ran along the floor, sleeved for its whole length in a trough of hammered tin. The trough was full of the black brine, and the rod lay in it like an eel in a river. It joined the arm. The arm joined the main.

Once she had seen one she could see them all, glinting under every grating along the north side of the hall. She walked down the row with her lamp and counted.

Fourteen.

"Hester," said Lucan.

She had stopped under the seventh, at the iron clip that held the rod to the pier. On its plate was a small clean punch-stamp: a square grid, three by three, like a window, or a crystal of grain seen end-on, and under it the number *23*.

"That's a Lattice mark," said Lucan behind her. His voice was strange. "The Lattice Yards. Third Fathom. They stamp everything they make. My father has a gate of theirs on the Exchequer Yard, and it's on the hinges."

"Halcyon work," said Teo.

"Halcyon work," said Lucan.

Hester took off her right glove.

She had been an assayer since she was nineteen, and what an assayer does with a thing she does not understand is put it on her tongue. She knelt and put the tip of her smallest finger into the black brine. It was so cold it burned. She put the finger in her mouth.

The weight came down on her like a roof.

She had tasted the Deep at a hundred and forty, and the *Constant Wren*'s cargo, and the First Taste on the Long Table that made a hall of adults weep. None of them was this. This did not press. It *settled*. It came down behind her eyes and lay there and went on not moving. Its stillness was so old and so complete that for a moment she could not tell if she was breathing.

It was stiller than grain. It was stiller than anything that came out of the ground, because the ground had not put it there. Ten, twenty, thirty years of a life held still had put it there, the stillness soaking into the salt around a body, day after day, until the salt was saturated. *Old keepings are the stillest things in the world.* Sorle had said it in the Black Bench with his chalk, and she had written it down without understanding it.

And under the stillness, faint, as there had been on the bead on Tib's casing: the bright wet startle of the brine around it, moving. Being drawn.

She took her finger out of her mouth.

"This is them," she said.

She was on her knees on the brick, and her voice was the flat level voice she had used to read a grade aloud to the excisemen, and she held on to it. "This is what's weeping off the casings. It's going down the drains into these rods, and the rods are taking it to the channel, and the channel's taking it east." She swallowed. The weight was still behind her eyes. "This is Tib."

* * *

Teo knelt beside her. He put out his hand, and she nodded, and he dipped one finger in the black brine and put it, not in his mouth, but against the hollow under his ear where the bone was thin, as Reader Fane taught her Bench to listen. He shut his eyes.

It took him longer than it took her. His hold had always been bad. She watched his breath try to slow and fail and try again. Then it caught. The colour went out of his lips. His face emptied, as faces did in the hold, all its quick warmth draining away until it was only a face.

He was gone long enough that Lucan shifted and looked at her, and she shook her head.

When Teo came out he sat back on his heels. He was crying, and she did not think he knew it. The tears left white tracks on his cheeks.

"What did you hear?"

"The Roar. Like always. But it's far off. Thin. It's not the sea in this. It's not old enough." He wiped his face with his sleeve and looked at the white on the wool. "Under it. Under the Roar." He stopped.

"Teo."

"Breathing," he said. "People breathing. Lots of them. All at once and none of them together. Like a ward at night. Like the infirmary at the Abbey when the fever came through and I sat up with the brothers." He looked at her. "And some of it's small. Quick and small and light. Hester, some of it's children."

The brine lay in its sleeve, black and perfectly still, and fed, and was fed upon.

Lucan said, in the voice he used in Vey's Brinescript class to say something rude politely: "Then I think we had better take one with us."

He unwrapped the saw.

* * *

Lucan chose the length of the seventh rod between the last clip and the bend, where it stood clear of its sleeve. Hester held the lamp. Teo held the rod above the cut with both gloved hands, wincing at the cold through the leather.

"If this is full," said Lucan, "it'll spill."

"Then it spills."

"It's *people*, you said."

"It's what's been taken off them. It's no more them than the water you wring out of a shirt is the shirt. What's in that rod is going east whatever we do. Cut it."

He looked at her a moment, then put the blade to the brass and drew it.

The saw went into the brass with a high thin singing that the brick swallowed at once, so that the noise was in their ears and nowhere else, as if the three of them were inside a bell no one outside could hear. Halfway through, the rod began to sweat. Beads stood out on it above and below the cut, in lines. They ran away from the cut along the rod, east, toward the bend, toward the channel.

"Still runs to quick," Teo whispered.

"It's going where it's going," said Hester. "Keep cutting."

Lucan's breathing had gone short. He stopped with his hand pressed flat to his side, and Hester took the saw from him and finished it herself. The last of the brass parted with a little *tink*. The downstream end gave a single slow bubble, and brine welled out of it, black and cold, and she caught it in the oilcloth of the tin box. A spoonful. The rod was hollow, she saw: a brass tube bored fine, with brine running down its heart.

They cut again a foot lower, quicker now that they knew how. When it parted she had in her hands a foot of brass tube as thick as two fingers, so cold she felt it through her gloves, with a stamped band round its middle: the three-by-three lattice and the number *23*.

She was wrapping it in the oilcloth when the light came round the pier.

* * *

It was a tin lantern with a horn window, held high by a man breathing hard through his nose. In his other hand he had a carter's oak cudgel. He was heavy, pale and balding, in a coat buttoned wrong. Hester had seen him perhaps five times in eleven years, always leaving.

"Mr. Pell," she said.

"Who's there?" His face in the lantern-light was grey and wet. "You've no right—this is a closed works—this is *Halcyon property*—"

"You're at your sister's," Hester said. "In Harl."

"I came back." He had seen the tin box in her hands, and the saw, and the cut ends of the rod with the black brine welling slowly out of the stub. His face changed. "Oh, God. What've you *done*? They'll know. They'll know straight off. They always know. Put it *back*—"

He came at her. He held the cudgel badly, but he was heavy and frightened, and frightened men do not stop. Hester stepped back and her heel went into the channel. The brine took her boot to the ankle, so cold she cried out. Teo grabbed at Pell's coat, and Pell flung him off, and Teo went down hard on the brick with his lamp skittering away, its light wheeling across the vaults.

"*Stop.*"

Lucan had not raised his voice. He had straightened, all of him, the ribs and the shoulders and the long Ferrers neck, and turned his lamp full on his own face so that Pell could see it.

"I am Lucan Ferrers," he said. "My father is the Lord Exchequer of this city. My father signs the charter of your Charitable Board. My father signs the warrant that pays your salary, Mr. Pell. If you lay that stick on anyone in this hall, I shall see that he signs another one, and you will not care for it." He paused. "Put it down."

Pell's hand opened and the cudgel fell out of it onto the brick. Then he sat down on the floor as if somebody had cut his strings, and put his face in his hands.

"Ferrers," he said through his fingers. "Of course. Of course it's Ferrers."

Hester got her foot out of the channel. It was numb to the shin. She hobbled to him.

"Who are *they*, Mr. Pell?"

"The Halcyon. It's maintenance, it's Halcyon maintenance on the old cistern taps under the Charter, it's all *permitted*—"

"Who told you that?"

"The gentleman." His shoulders were shaking. "From the Warden's office. He came in the spring. He had papers. Seals. He said it was a Charter matter and the Board needn't be troubled with it. Would I see the cellar was open when his men came, and that nobody went down. And there'd be ten sols a month for my trouble." He took his hands from his face. "Ten sols, Miss Vail. Do you know what the *Board* pays me to run three hundred and four casings on charity money and Pell this and Pell that and not one of them—"

"You knew they were weeping."

"I didn't *know*."

"I left you a note. On the first of Russet. Directional, east, quick. I wrote it down for you."

"I didn't know what it meant!"

"You knew enough," Hester said, "to come back from Harl in the middle of the night because you were afraid somebody might be down here."

He looked at her with pale eyes and said nothing.

"Which Warden?" said Teo, from the floor, nursing his elbow.

"What?"

"You said the Warden's office. There are four Wardens. Which?"

"I don't *know*. He said the Warden's office. He had a paper with a seal. He was a clerk. He wore a coat over his gown and he never gave his name and I never asked it." Pell laughed, a terrible small laugh. "I never asked. That was the whole of what the ten sols was for."

Somewhere behind them, at the western end of the hall, a bell rang. A handbell, shaken hard. Faintly, down the passage, Agathe's voice was calling.

"Somebody's watch," said Lucan. "Time to go."

They left Pell sitting on the floor of the Great Cistern beside his cudgel with his lantern guttering. Behind them the cut stub of the seventh rod went on welling, slow and black, into its sleeve.

* * *

Agathe had heard boots in the yard. Three men, she said. She had rung the cellar fire-bell and sent them round the front to a sleeper in distress, and by the time they got there the three aspirants were over the yard wall and running down the alley toward the sea-wall. Hester did not stop to find out whose men they were.

The first dray back over the Causeway went at dawn. They spent what was left of the night in the lee of the old sea-wall at the foot of Coldwater Street, and when the sky over the White went grey they climbed the worn stair and sat on the top among the iron mooring rings, legs hanging over the drop. Hester's right foot was a dead thing in a wet boot. Teo had his elbow in his hand. Lucan sat with his arms folded tight across his ribs and his face turned east.

Out on the White the Fallow dawn came up as a line, then a band, then the whole flat edge of the world going rose. Two miles out, on the only rise in all that flatness, the dome of the Halcyon caught the first light.

Hester looked down at the foot of the wall. Under all that salt the double line ran. *Main. Main. Main.* On the Lattice gallery sheet it had had an ending: *Line enters at 5th Fm.* She had copied that out three weeks ago as a fact about the year 400. She had not thought of it as a fact about tonight.

"It's not smuggling," she said. "I thought somebody was taking grain out. Out of the Halcyon, to sell. The *Wren*. I thought that was what it was." She opened the box and looked at the foot of brass in its oilcloth, the little lattice and the 23. "It's not coming *out*. It's going *in*. They're not taking the grain out to Ambry. They're taking Ambry in to the grain."

"The stillness," said Teo.

"Out of the casings. Down the drains. Into the rods. East." She shut the box. "Somebody inside the Halcyon is drawing the keep off three hundred sleepers in a charity ward and piping it two miles under the White into the Fifth Fathom, and paying Pell ten sols a month to keep the cellar shut. And Aske went down there on the twelfth of Russet and saw it, and four days later he was in a casing."

"The Warden's office," said Lucan.

"There are four," said Teo.

On the dome the Lantern's mirrors came round and flashed. Hester sat on the cold wall with the box in her lap and her brother's stillness in a foot of brass, and looked at it. She thought of Corvin Aske in his casing in the Quiet Rooms, one hand out, his mouth open on a word he had never finished. *Ask O.*

"Then we find out which," she said.


# Chapter 14: What Is Owed

> Every keeping is a lie we tell time, and time collects.
>
> — *B. Sorle, Lectures on Arrears (unpublished; circulated in fair copy among the Black Bench)*

"Don't," said Sorle.

He did not look up. He was at his desk with his back to the door and a moth-eaten black shawl round his shoulders, writing small and fast and crossing out as much as he set down. The kept moth sat in its lump of clear grain at his elbow with its wings half open.

"Don't what?"

"Don't take it to Gage." He blotted a line, examined it, and crossed it out. "You are standing in my door at the second bell with a box under your arm and a face like a summons, Miss Vail, smelling of wet brick and Lowside. I deduce that you have been somewhere you were not supposed to be and brought something back. I further deduce, from the way you are holding the box, that you mean to carry it straight down to the Proctory and put it on the Chief Proctor's desk. Don't."

"It's evidence."

"It is a length of brass."

"With a Lattice Yards mark on it. Out of the founders' cistern under the Cold House, drawing the keep off a charity ward and carrying it two miles east, to here." She put the box on the one corner of his desk that was not under paper and opened it. "You told me to bring you evidence. Not tastes. Something you could put on a slate. That's it."

Sorle turned round in his chair.

He looked at the brass for a long time without touching it. Then he looked at Hester's right boot, still dark to the shin with salt-tide, and then at her face.

"Sit," he said.

She told him all of it, from the grating to the sea-wall, in order. When she came to Pell, and *the Warden's office*, and *ten sols*, he shut his eyes. When she had finished he kept them shut a while, and then said, "And now you wish to take it to Halloran Gage."

"He's the Chief Proctor. Somebody in this building is drawing stillness out of living people without their consent or their families'. That's—" She had looked it up in the Brinestacks on her way down, with her boot squelching. "That's Article Thirty. Improper working upon a kept person. It's his job."

"It is his job," Sorle agreed. "So is closing files." The chair complained as he leaned back. "Let me tell you what will happen. Gage will listen to you with every appearance of gravity, because he is a grave man and it costs him nothing. He will take the rod, and give you a receipt, and promise an inquiry. And then the rod will go wherever things go in this Halcyon when they are inconvenient to someone who outranks the Chief Proctor. And you will have nothing. Not even a length of brass."

"You don't know that."

"I have been a Reader of this Collegium for thirty-four years and censured by it for thirty-two. I know it as you know Shelf from Middling."

"Then what would you have me do? Sit on it?"

"Yes. Keep it. Show it to no one. Find out which Warden, and why. And when you can prove it, go over Gage's head and over the head of whoever is over Gage's. Go to the Assize, if you must." He paused. "If you take it down the Stair today, you take it down alone. I'll not come with you."

She looked at the rod, at the lattice and the 23. She thought of Pell on the floor of the Cistern: *that was the whole of what the ten sols was for.* She thought of Tib in the fourth row, weeping east.

"I've sat on things before," she said. "Eleven years. It's not done him any good."

She closed the box. Sorle did not try again. He turned back to his desk and said, to the paper, "Shut the door on your way out. The moth dislikes a draught."

* * *

The Proctory lay on the First Fathom behind the Hall of Assay, down a short corridor hung with framed copies of the Charter. It had a front room with a rail and a sergeant behind the rail, and an inner office with a desk of Hask oak and a map of the Fathoms stuck with pins.

Chief Proctor Halloran Gage listened to her from behind the desk. He was a broad, grey, close-cropped man of fifty-odd who sat as if the chair had been issued to him and would have to be returned in good condition. He wrote in a leather book in a clerk's hand.

When she had finished he picked up the brass, turned it over, and held it to the window so the light went down its bore.

"Twenty-three," he said.

"I don't know what the number means."

"Lot number. The Yards stamp one on everything they cast. Twenty-three would be—" He stopped himself. "That's for the inquiry." He set the rod down. "You understand, Miss Vail, that you entered a closed civic works at night without a warrant."

"Yes, sir."

"And interfered with Halcyon property."

"With something that has a Halcyon mark on it, sir, in a place the Halcyon has no warrant to be either, so far as I know. I looked. Article Thirty."

He considered her. His eyes were small and pale and steady, and she could read nothing in them, which she supposed was the point of them.

"I know Article Thirty," he said. "I'll take this, and your statement, signed. I'll open an inquiry. It'll go to the Master of the Lattice Yards for the lot, and to the Water Board for the works, and—" He paused. "And to whatever office is concerned. You'll hear from me."

"When, sir?"

"When I've something to tell you." He wrote her a receipt on a printed slip. *Received from H. Vail, aspirant, one length brass tube, stamped, approx. 12 in., in connection with a matter under Article 30. H. Gage, Ch. Proctor.* As she turned to go, he said, in a slightly different voice: "Miss Vail. Do you go to the Cold House on the first of the month?"

She stopped. "Yes."

"Then go on the first of the month," said Gage. "And not otherwise. Not at night. Good morning."

* * *

She heard nothing on the fifteenth, or the sixteenth. On the morning of the seventeenth a sergeant in a buff coat was waiting at the door of the Benches after Crisp's class. Miss Vail was to step down to the Proctory.

Gage was standing at the window of the inner office with his hands behind his back. The chair behind the Hask oak desk was not empty.

The man in it was tall, silvered at the temples, handsome in an expensive way, his gown open over a dove-grey coat, with the ochre cord of the Brinescript Bench at his shoulder and over it the narrow silver chain that only one man in the Halcyon wore. Deputy Chancellor Ilario Vey. She had never spoken to him. Her signed statement lay on the blotter in front of him.

"Miss Vail," he said, as if they were old acquaintances. "Do sit."

She sat. Gage did not turn round.

"I've read this twice," said Vey, touching the statement with one finger. "It's very good. Dates, times, distances. Fourteen rods; you counted. A lot number. Article Thirty, cited correctly, which is more than half my Bench could manage. Corvin always said you had an eye." He smiled. "He was a diligent man too. The Conclave thanked him for it."

"The brass, sir."

"Is a Halcyon matter, and is with the office that concerns it." He said it pleasantly, as if he were telling her where the Bursary was. "The Chief Proctor has been good enough to pass it on. You'll keep your receipt, of course. Receipts are important. I'm told you were in customs."

She looked at Gage's back. It did not move.

"There are thirteen more," she said. "Under the Cold House. I can show you tonight."

"I'm sure you could. I'd rather you didn't." Vey turned a page of her statement that did not need turning. "You walked here from Lowside with a dead man's privilege on your shoulder. I admire that; I do. But a privilege is not a Bench. An aspirant without a sponsor holds her place at the Chancellor's pleasure, and the Chancellor"—a small, regretful movement of the hand—"tires. These things come to my desk now. Midwinter isn't far off." He squared the statement against the blotter's edge. "And your brother is kept on a charity form, I think. The Board is very careful with its charity forms. So am I."

It was said so kindly that it took her a moment to hear it.

"Is that all, sir?"

"That's all. Diligence is a fine quality in an aspirant." He stood, so she stood. "So is discretion. The best of them learn both."

He went out past her, unhurried. The sergeant behind the rail stood up straight, and Vey's steps went away down the corridor of framed Charters.

Gage turned from the window. For the first time something moved in his small pale eyes, and was put away again.

"You've your receipt, Miss Vail," he said. "Keep it. Good morning."

* * *

Sorle did not turn round. "Well?"

"Gone," said Hester.

He laughed, once. It was not a pleasant sound, and it was not, she thought, aimed at her.

"Sit down, Miss Vail."

She sat. Afternoon light came down the shaft in the corner of the study, a long dusty bar of it from the Lantern's mirrors. It lay across the chalked slates against the bookcases and across the portrait of the broad-faced woman with Physic red at her collar.

She told him: Gage at the window with his back to her; Vey in Gage's chair; *diligence*; *the Chancellor's pleasure*; the charity form.

"Vey," said Sorle, as if tasting the name and not caring for the vintage. "Who thanked Corvin for his diligence in Conclave and moved on to the price of lamp oil. Who has the Chancellor's ear, because the Chancellor no longer has the strength to hold it herself." He tapped his pen on the blotter. "It need not be Vey. Vey is the sort of man through whom things pass. That rod may have passed through him on its way to somewhere else. But it passed."

"You told me so."

"I did. I've been right about the Halcyon for thirty-two years and it has never once given me pleasure." He swivelled to face her properly, and the shawl slipped off one bony shoulder, and he let it. "What will you do now?"

"I don't know."

"An honest answer. Rare."

"I've a receipt," said Hester. "And a Cistern with thirteen more rods in it that somebody will have had out by tonight, if they've any sense. And a man in a cellar who'll swear he never saw me. And a brother whose casing is thinner every week." She heard her voice go and brought it back. "And no Bench. The Registrar's written again. Midwinter, or I'm sent down. Crisp's full. Bassano's full. Orsolya can't take me till her third-years assay out, and she's been kind, but kind isn't a Bench. The rest of them look at me as if I'd come to read the meter."

Sorle looked at her for a long while.

Then he worked loose the top right drawer of his desk, which stuck, and took something out of it, and held it a moment before he put it on the blotter between them.

It was a Bench cord, looped and knotted in the Halcyon fashion. It was black, and not new. The black had gone brown at the knot, where a thumb had worked it.

"I have two aspirants," said Sorle. "Both third-years. Both will assay out this Rime, with distinction, God help the Fellowship. After that I had intended to have none. I've taken no one new in nine years; the Registrar has stopped sending me lists." He looked at the moth, not the cord. "It is not a good Bench to be on. You will be known as Sorle's, which is not a recommendation in this building. It will close doors Aske's white cord would have opened, and Aske's cord, you will forgive me, is no longer opening anything. Some of your examiners will mark you down for the colour on your shoulder before you have lifted a spoon. Crisp won't, because Crisp is honest. Vey will."

"Why are you doing it?"

The bar of light had moved onto the portrait, across the broad face and the red collar.

"Because Corvin asked me to," he said at last. "Not in words. Corvin never asked anyone for anything in words; he was too busy talking. But he spent his privilege on you, which in twenty years he had not spent on anyone. And on the sixteenth he asked me up to his Cell, and I found a dark corridor and a shut door and went back to bed in a temper. You know that part." His mouth twisted. "I owe him a half-hour. I can't pay it to him. You'll do."

He pushed the cord an inch toward her.

"And because I have nothing left to lose," he said, "and you have even less. That's a Bench."

She did not pick it up at once. She thought of the white cord on her shoulder, which Aske had looped there in the Gatehouse on Matriculation Day, fumbling, humming. Her eyes had gone hot. She did not let them do anything about it.

She took off the white cord, clumsily, folded it and put it in her pocket, where she would keep it. Then she looped the black one at her shoulder and pulled the knot tight.

"Ta," she said.

"Don't thank me. You'll hate it within the week." Sorle pulled a slate toward him and picked up the chalk. "Thursday. The fourth bell. Bring paper; I don't lend it. We'll begin with the arrears calculus, since you clearly can't be trusted to understand anything without first putting it in your mouth."

* * *

The arrears calculus was, Hester discovered over the next three days, a great deal of arithmetic dressed up as philosophy. She was good at arithmetic. She was not good at philosophy, and said so. Sorle said that put her ahead of most of the Conclave, who were good at neither but could tell the difference at dinner.

He wrote fast, in a hand that sloped backward, and wiped out what he had written with the side of his fist.

"Arrears," he said, "is pressure. Not a metaphor. A keeping holds a thing at one moment. The world goes on to the next, and the thing does not. Between where the thing is and where the world is, there is a difference, and the difference *wants*. It wants to close." He drew two lines, one flat, one rising away from it. "That wanting is arrears. It grows with time. It grows with the mass of what is kept." He drew the line steeper. "And it grows with how *lively* the thing was. A stone hardly minds being kept. A man minds a great deal. A man running minds more than a man asleep. A sea—" He stopped, and wiped the slate with his fist. "Never mind a sea. Not today."

"Why not today?"

"Because today you are learning arithmetic. Every casing pays its arrears out of its own stillness: the grain's, and what soaks into the grain from what it holds. And so. *Still runs to quick.* You've seen it twice now, once in my class and once on your knees in a hole in Lowside. Join a full old casing by live brine to something with a great deal of arrears and little stillness to pay them with. What happens?"

"The stillness runs out of the casing," said Hester. "Into the thing that's owing."

"It pays the other thing's debt." He drew an arrow. "And if the other thing is large enough, and old enough, and owes enough, it will drink a charity ward dry and still be thirsty."

She looked at the arrow.

"A quickening," she said.

"I didn't say that."

"You didn't have to." The study was always cold, but she was colder. Two miles east, and a Halcyon at the end of it, and five fathoms down, pockets that were warm and wet and owed a great deal, and a Choir that sang slow to pay them. She had stood in that line and poured herself into it with sixty others and felt it drink.

She looked at the thought, and then she put it down, as you put down a sample you have not yet weighed.

"There are a great many rooms in the Halcyon," said Sorle, watching her. "You have a pipe that goes east and a building at the end of it. That is all you have."

"I know."

"Then go on with the arithmetic." His eyes were sharper than his voice. "If a stay is a pouring-in of stillness to pay a quickening's arrears, what does the quickening do?"

"Quiets."

"For how long?"

She thought of the brass stay-lines in the Fifth, and of a pocket that had been held down on the Tuesday and was warm again by the Saturday.

"Not long," she said. "And then it's back. Worse."

"Why worse?"

"Because you've not paid anything. You've just added to it. You've kept the keeping. It still owes everything it owed, and now it's had longer to owe it."

"Stays saturate," said Sorle softly. "Pour enough still into a quickening and it quiets, and grows hungrier. The Fathomers know it. Orsolya Thane knows it better than anyone alive; she's been pouring for thirty years. They've no other tool, and they've never wanted one. A stay is a lid, Miss Vail. That is all a stay is."

Hester did not move.

*You lid a spitting pan, she'll blow the lid through the roof.* Her father in the steam, drawing the fire out a shovelful at a time.

"What if you let it out?" she said. "What if you didn't pour stillness in. What if you let the owing out. A bit at a time. So it's paid."

"Then the moment *proceeds*." Sorle's voice was quiet. "A kept thing let out goes on from where it stopped. That's the Third Law; a child knows it. A kept apple goes on being an apple. A kept man goes on bleeding; a kept corpse blooms grey in a month or so, whatever the grade, and goes on being a corpse. And a quickening—" He put down the chalk. "A quickening is not an apple. We do not know what a quickening is. We know it is warm and wet and sounds like surf. If you let one go on, you would find out what it stopped *in the middle of*. In the Fifth Fathom of a building with three thousand people in it."

"Not in the Fifth. Somewhere else. Somewhere it couldn't—"

"No." He wiped the slate, all of it, the lines and the arrow and the rising curve. "That is not a thought for a first-year with a black cord three days old. You know what one thought about the sea has cost me. I am still paying for it." He turned back and wrote, fast, a line of arithmetic that had nothing to do with anything. "Arrears of a moth. Kept forty years. Mass a quarter-grain. Work it."

She worked it. She got it right. He grunted.

But when she looked up his chalk had stopped. He was looking at the portrait on the wall, at the broad-faced woman with the Physic red at her collar, with the expression of a man listening for something in another room.

"*A bit at a time,*" he said, under his breath. "*So it's paid.*" And then, without turning: "Arrears of a man, kept three months. Mass ten stone. Hush grain. Work it."

She looked at him.

"Work it, Miss Vail."

She worked it. It was a very large number. She did not say whose it was. Neither did he.

* * *

Agathe's letter came with the carrier on the twentieth of Fallow, in the round copybook hand.

*My dear. Mr. Pell has been quiet since he came back from his sister's and has had the cellar grating mortared over in the old way, thick, by a man from the Water Board. He has said nothing to me and I have said nothing to him. On the morning after, I went down for the oil and the drain was dry, and the weeping on our people stopped for two days. On the third day it began again. I do not know what that means. I expect you do.*

*There is a thinness at the foot of your lad's casing that I can see my candle through if I hold it low.*

*A.L.*

Hester read it twice at the window of R-41, with the White going grey behind the glass. She put it in the tin box under her bed, where the rod had been and was not, and sat down on the bed with the black cord at her shoulder and looked at her hands.

Teo came in at the sixth bell with saltcake wrapped in a cloth and found her still sitting there. He did not ask. He put the saltcake on the sill and sat on his own bed and waited. She held out the letter, and he read it, and gave it back.

"Two days," he said.

"Two days."

"One rod cut, out of fourteen, and it stopped for two days, until they'd mended it." He turned the cloth over in his hands without opening it. "So it can stop. That's what it means, Hester. It can be stopped."

"For two days."

"Two days," said Teo, "is not nothing."

The Lantern came round and flashed, and the light went across the White and the window and the two of them, and was gone. Hester sat with her hands on the letter and did the arithmetic of a thinning casing in her head. She did it again. It came out the same both times.


# Chapter 15: Ferrers

> The Purchaser shall make no enquiry as to the provenance of the said grain beyond the Vendor's warrant, and shall hold the Vendor harmless in any matter arising from its resale.
>
> — *Contract of sale between the Office of the W.L.F. and Messrs. Ferrers & Lisk, clause 7*

Lucan went home on the twenty-second of Fallow and came back the same night with a face like a shut door.

Hester was on the Choir Steps when he found her. It was the hour after the Lantern Hour, and most of the Halcyon was still at the Long Table. The outdoor amphitheatre on the dome's eastern flank was empty, its tiers of salt gone blue in the dusk. In Fallow nobody came, which was why Hester did. She sat on the third tier with her gown wrapped round her knees, looking east, where there was nothing but white going grey and, a quarter of a mile off, the low dark hump of the Well-Cap on its chains.

He came down the tiers behind her carefully. The ribs were a fortnight mended, and he still took steps as if each one might have an opinion. He sat on the tier above hers, a little to the side.

"I've seen my father," he said.

She waited.

"We had a turbot at dinner, kept, from Hask. My father is fond of fish. It reminds him that he can afford to be." His voice was perfectly even. "Afterward I opened the drawer where he keeps the year's Ferrers and Lisk contracts, because I was eleven when I found out where he hides the key, and he has never moved it. It is inside a hollowed copy of *The Duties of a Magistrate*, which no one in our family has ever opened for any other reason."

"Lucan."

"I'm coming to it." He took a folded paper from inside his coat. "I didn't take the original. I copied it at his desk, by his lamp, with his pen, and put everything back. Then I went and asked him about it." He held it out without looking at her. "Read it first."

It was a long sheet in Lucan's tidy, slanting schoolroom hand, faster and less tidy toward the bottom.

*Articles of agreement made this 3rd of Thaw, 903 Y.E., between the Office of the W.L.F., the Halcyon Collegium of the Grain (hereinafter the Vendor), and Messrs. Ferrers & Lisk, Exchequer Yard, Ambry (hereinafter the Purchaser).*
