*1. The Vendor shall supply, at such intervals as are convenient to the Vendor, quantities of residual stay grain, being grain removed in the course of maintenance from the lower workings, in sealed cases, at the Purchaser's landing at the Dry Harbour.*

*2. The Purchaser shall convey the said grain by its own vessels to such markets beyond the Rim as it sees fit, and shall not offer it for sale within the Free City or within one hundred miles thereof.*

There were prices per case, and they were high. There was a clause about relabelling at the Purchaser's discretion for the convenience of foreign customs. There was clause seven, about provenance. And at the bottom Lucan had copied the signatures as he found them, imitating the hands as far as he was able:

*For the Vendor: E. Dunmore, clerk, per W.L.F.*

*For the Purchaser: A. Ferrers.*

Hester read it twice, and the signatures a third time.

"*Not within one hundred miles,*" she said. "And the *Wren* came into the dry harbour with forty crates of it marked Shelf for curing."

"I asked him about that." Lucan was looking at the Well-Cap. "I said, Father, this grain is not Shelf. It's from the stays, below the Fifth very likely, quickened; the Customs schedule hasn't even a word for it. It is a felony under the Charter to sell it at all. And you have been sending it to Quell to have its papers changed and bringing it back into Ambry to sell to the curing-houses at Shelf prices with Deep profits, because the Hask market has gone soft this year. Which," he said, "I had worked out on the Causeway dray, and was rather proud of."

"What did he say?"

"He asked me if I wanted more port." Lucan's mouth moved. "Then he said that the Halcyon sold him the grain, and the Halcyon's warrant was on the contract, and if the Halcyon had a Charter it might look to its own Charter. Then he said the thing about laundry and lamps again. He'd said it to me in Russet, with his hand on my shoulder. This time he didn't bother with the hand." When he went on, his sentences had got shorter. "He said I was to stay out of it. He said that if I had any notion of carrying tales to the Proctory I should remember who paid my fees. And who got me onto the Deputy Chancellor's Bench, and why. He was calm. He's never calm with me."

The salt of the Choir Steps was cold through Hester's gown. The last light went off the Well-Cap.

"Why did Vey take you?" she asked.

"Because my father asked him to. Over a turbot." He laughed, slightly. "I thought it was because I was good."

"You are good."

"I am adequate. I am also the son of the Lord Exchequer." He turned and looked at her for the first time. In the dusk his face was younger and more tired than she had seen it. "That is how it works, Vail. Everything in this building is somebody's favour to somebody. My father does the Deputy Chancellor a kindness and the Deputy Chancellor does my father one, and somewhere at the bottom of it is a ward full of people in the Cold House. And the man at the top eats turbot and tells his son to stay out of it." He looked at the paper in her hands. "He signed it. *A. Ferrers.* I watched him sign things my whole childhood. I'd know that A anywhere."

There was nothing to say that would not have been a kindness, and she did not think he wanted one. She folded the paper and held it out to him. He shook his head.

"Keep it," he said. "I've no drawer that isn't his."

* * *

She did not need to look it up. She looked it up anyway, because that was what she did.

The Halcyon Calendar was printed every Thaw by the Collegium press and stood in a row of fat grey volumes in the Brinestacks reference alcove, one for each year back to 612. At the front, under *Abbreviations Used in Collegium Correspondence*, it listed the offices by their initials. She had glanced at that page on her second day. She had not remembered what was on it. She had remembered that it was there.

She found it by lamplight at the eleventh bell, with the Brinestacks empty round her except for Callow on the far side of the gallery, so still at his desk he might have been one of the cases.

*W.U.F. — Warden of the Upper Fathoms.*

*W.L.F. — Warden of the Lower Fathoms.*

*W.S. — Warden of the Stacks.*

*W.L. — Warden of the Light.*

She sat with the book open in front of her for some time.

*Warden of the Lower Fathoms.* Orsolya.

Then she did what she had spent twenty-one years teaching Customs House clerks not to do. She did it carefully, step by step, as a clerk adds a column he wants to come out a certain way.

The contract was signed *E. Dunmore, clerk, per W.L.F.* Not by the Warden. *Per.* On behalf of the office. And the office of the Warden of the Lower Fathoms was not one woman. It was a gallery in the Fifth with three clerks in it, and a store, and the Choir rolls, and the pension book, and letters to the Delvers' Guild and the Lattice Yards and the Water Board. It put its initials on a hundred letters a week, most of which the Warden would never see.

And there had been a man in grey wool at the dry harbour, by the dray, with a lantern and a paper. A clerk. Pell had said it too: *a clerk; he never gave his name.*

*E.D.* Edwin Dunmore. The bland man with the inky cuffs who sat at the outer desk of the Warden's Gallery, wrote down the Choir's hours, and handed out the stipend on Fridays. Hester had collected three sols from him every week since Russet and could not have told you the colour of his eyes.

Dunmore, who signed for the office and had the seals. Who, if he was selling quickened grain to Ferrers and Lisk behind the Warden's back, would want the stays held down whatever it cost. Who answered, perhaps, not to the Warden at all but to somebody who could make a length of brass disappear out of a locked Proctory store. To a man in a dove-grey coat who had sat in the Chief Proctor's chair and praised her diligence.

It added up. It added up quite well, if you were careful which figures you carried.

She knew she was carrying some figures and not others. She carried them anyway. Orsolya had put a cold hand on her shoulder at Aske's door. Orsolya had stood over her after her first stay, while Ama wrapped her in a blanket, and said *well done* in a voice that could have come from Coldwater Street.

Orsolya was Lowside.

Hester shut the Calendar and put it back on its shelf, in its place, between 902 and 904.

* * *

"And the Warden?" said Lucan.

They were in the Reading Well off the north gallery of the Brinestacks the next afternoon, a small round room with a brine basin set in the floor and a bench round the wall. Teo sat with Lucan's copy of the contract on his knee. Hester had laid it out for them: Dunmore; the office; *per*; the clerk at the dray; the clerk in Pell's cellar; the vanished rod; Vey in Gage's chair.

"It isn't her signature," Hester said. "It's her clerk's. Clerks sign for their offices every day. Mallory's sub-clerk signed for the Customs House forty times a morning." She heard her voice harden and could not stop it. "She's not a thief, Ferrers. She's held the Fifth for thirty years. She's buried eleven of her own. You've not stood in that line. You don't know what it costs her."

"I know what it costs the Cold House," said Lucan.

The basin in the floor had a skin of dead brine on it. Teo looked down at it and did not look up.

"I'm not saying she did it," Lucan said, more gently. "I'm saying you've not asked."

"I'll ask. She's offered to help me with my release. Private lessons, Wednesday. I'll ask her then about the residue contracts. I'll ask her about Dunmore."

"And if she says what you want her to say?"

"Then she'll have said it."

Lucan looked at her a long moment. Then he said, without any heat at all, "You know, you are doing what I did with my father. For about twenty-three years."

She had no answer. She went out. Behind her she heard Teo say, low, "Leave her be," and then the door of the Reading Well swung shut on its weight, and she was among the stacks, where ten thousand salt tablets in their cases glimmered in the lamplight, remembering being touched.

* * *

Crisp's release hour fell on Wednesdays too, at the Pans. Hester kept her drop and tried to give it back, and could not; it hung over the dish in its bead of salt while the first-years either side of her let theirs fall, one and then another. Crisp stopped behind her. "You let one go on the White, Vail, with a boy's life on it. So it's in you. You're sitting on it." She lifted the bead off with the rod and set it in the dish with the others. There were nine.

The Warden's Gallery was the last landing before the iron gates of the Fifth, a long low room cut into the raw salt. Dunmore's desk stood at one end and the Warden's at the other, and between them were the Choir rolls on their racks, a stove, a horsehair settee and a stack of grey wool blankets. On Wednesday at the fifth bell there was no one but Orsolya, because she had sent them all away.

"Sit down before you fall down." She poured. "You look like a wet week in Thaw. That Sorle working you?"

"Arithmetic."

"Ha. He would." Orsolya put the cup into Hester's hands and closed her own round it briefly. Her fingers were cold as stone. Thirty years of holding had done that; it had started whitening her hair at thirty and slowed her heart to something Hester could feel, if she stood close, like a clock in another room. "Black cord. I saw. There's worse than Benedek, though I'd not say it to his face. He hates me, you know. I took him apart once in Conclave. I was twenty-six and pleased with myself, and he was wrong in a way that would have got people killed." She sat down on the settee with her own cup. "Still. He's no fool. If he's taken you, he's seen something."

"He says I hold like a miser."

"Crisp said that. Everyone heard it." Orsolya laughed, a short Lowside bark. "She's not wrong. You hold like a Coldwater landlady with the rent. Nothing comes out once it's in. There's no shame in it; in the line, it's what's wanted. But you'll not pass the Assay if you can't let your own moth go, and Maudelaine'll not give you so much as a *wanting* if you can't. So." She set down her cup. "Let's see you."

They worked for an hour. Orsolya kept drops over a basin of live brine by the stove, and Hester put her wet hand to each bead and went into the hold and tried to come out of it the other way.

She could not do it. She could feel the drop in its casing, still, held, owing; she could feel exactly what it wanted. She could not open her hand. Each time she tried, something in her closed tighter, as a fist closes round a coin when someone jostles you in a crowd.

"No. You're pushing." Orsolya's cold fingers were on her wrist. "You can't push a release. It's not a door. Open your hand."

"I am opening it."

"You're thinking about opening it. That's not the same." Orsolya sat back with her head on one side. "Who taught you to keep?"

"No one."

"Somebody did. Everybody learns it off somebody. You learned it off somebody you watched, who never let anything go." She did not wait for an answer. "I'll tell you what my da used to say. He was a delver at Coldbank, the old pit east of the Weeps. Haskish man, big as a door; he married a Lowside girl and never went home. He used to say, the salt's not yours, Orsi. You only borrow it. Hold it like a borrowed thing." She looked at the stove. "The Coldbank roof came in when I was eleven. Eighty men. He was in the third gallery. The Halcyon had a Physic on the site, and she kept him with Middling out of a bucket, standing up, as they found him, under the beam. The women at the pit-head kept saying *if they'd shored it, if they'd only shored it*. I can hear them yet." She drank her tea. "He was in the Cold House nineteen years. Then the casing started weeping, and the brinewright said it wouldn't take another coat. I signed for him at thirty. That's how I know your Coldwater Street, chick. I've stood where you stand."

Hester had not known that.

"Well," said Orsolya, briskly. "Enough of that. Watch me. Maybe it'll go in through your eyes, since it won't go in through your hands."

She lit an ordinary tallow candle at the stove and set it on the stand beside the basin. The flame stood up in the still air of the Gallery, yellow, smoky at the tip. Orsolya dipped her left hand in the brine to the wrist and held it, dripping, beside the flame.

"Kept flame," she said. "You'll not have seen one. They're not allowed in lamps; somebody always forgets and returns one in a hayloft. But it's good for teaching. A flame's nothing *but* motion. If you can keep a flame and let it go clean, you can keep anything." She went still. Hester watched it happen: the slowing breath, the colour leaving her lips, the stillness spreading down through her shoulders into her arm. Then Orsolya flicked her wet fingers at the candle.

The brine took the flame in mid-air.

It did not put it out. That was what caught Hester's breath. The flame stopped. It hung on its wick inside a skin of clear salt the size of a hen's egg, yellow and smoky-tipped and perfectly motionless, every edge of it sharp, as if somebody had cut it out of a painting of a candle and stood it on the stand. It gave no heat. It gave light, and the light did not flicker.

And in the salt around it, as the casing set and cleared, a pattern came up into the grain.

It was fine. Hester would not have seen it if she had not been looking for nothing, as she looked at everything. Faint lines in the crystal, like the whorl in a thumb, all curving the same way. A spiral. It began at the base of the casing where it met the wick and wound up round the flame and closed at the top. It turned to the left, against the sun.

"There," said Orsolya, coming out of the hold, blinking, with a craftsman's satisfaction. "See that? Every worker leaves a hand in the grain. Like your writing. Yours is all squares. I've seen it. Like a ledger. Maudelaine's is flat, like pages in a book; Corvin's was all spokes, God rest him, like a cartwheel." She tilted her head at the spiral. "Mine's a corkscrew. Always has been. My mam said I came out twisting."

Hester looked at the spiral.

Something in her went toward it as a tongue goes toward a broken tooth. There was a place in her mind where she had put something down, at a cold door on the Third Fathom on the seventeenth of Russet: Aske's casing under a low lamp, and in the clear salt a hair below the surface, fine curving lines in a long slow coil that went up his body from the floor. Turning left. *Widdershins.* She had noted it and not understood it, and then there had been Gage, and the porters, and Orsolya's hand on her shoulder. She came back to it now. It was the same hand.

She kept her eyes on the flame. It was the hardest hold she had done in the Halcyon, and there was no brine in it. "It's beautiful," she said, because it was, a fold of fire stood up in glass, and because her face needed something to do. Tib would have liked it. And when Orsolya said, "Now. Watch the release," she watched the release.

Orsolya put her wet fingertips to the casing and went still again, and this time Hester saw something. The stillness did not go out of Orsolya so much as *loosen*, as a knot loosens when you stop pulling on both ends. Her hand did not move. It opened from inside. The salt clouded, softened, ran, and the flame was flickering on its wick in a little puddle of brine, warm and ordinary. It gave off a single sharp tick of heat that Hester felt on her face, like an oven door opened and shut.

"Arrears," said Orsolya. "Half a minute's worth of candle. See how I did it? I didn't push. I just stopped holding on. Now. Try it with the drop."

She tried it with the drop. She could not do it. But she was nearer, she thought; for an instant she felt her hold go soft at the edge, like the skin on a pan, before it closed again.

"Better," said Orsolya. "That's the first time I've seen you *want* to."

* * *

Afterward, with the kettle on again, she asked. She had decided how on the Stair: plainly, looking at her cup, so that she could watch Orsolya's hands and not her face. She said she had seen the office's initials on some papers in the Brinestacks, and did not say which papers. She said she understood the Warden's office sold off the grain it took out of the stays, and wondered how that worked under the Charter. Whether it was quickened grain. Whether it was Hush. She had worked in Customs, she said, and could not help wondering.

Orsolya neither stiffened nor looked away. She leaned back on the horsehair settee and stretched her cold hands toward the stove and said, comfortably, "Oh, that's Edwin's. The residue contracts. Edwin handles all that; I'd not know a bill of sale from a hymn sheet." She nodded down the room at Dunmore's empty desk, with its neat stacks and its inkstand. "What we take off the stays has to go somewhere. You can't put it back in the ground, and you'd not want it in the Halcyon; it spits. So it goes for sale abroad. Hask buys it for the mines. They want quick grain for blasting, of all things; it's got a kick to it. It's all above board. Conclave-approved, years since. The money goes back to the Choir. Pensions. Widows. God knows they need it."

"It's Hush grain, though," said Hester. "Some of it. The schedule says—"

"The Customs schedule." Orsolya smiled at her, fond and a little rueful, a Lowside woman's smile for a Lowside girl who has been reading too much. "That was written by a man in Ambry who's never been below the First Fathom. Stay residue's not Hush grain, not as the Charter means it. It's spent. It's had its stillness poured out of it into a stay, like your twice-used casing grain. There's nothing in it but a kick." She drank. "Edwin keeps it all in order. He's a dull dog, but he's never lost a sol."

Her hands had stayed open toward the stove the whole time. It was a good answer, warm and plain, with the clerk's name in it before Hester had asked for one.

"Ta," she said.

"Don't *ta* me. Drink your tea. You've a shift at the ninth."

* * *

The ninth-bell shift went down singing slow on the Stair, forty of them in grey wool. Hester was in the second rank, between Ama Tessaly and a broad young delver's son from Coldbank called Fenwick, who sang flat and held like a wall. They went through the iron gates of the Fifth and down the long galleries, where the brass stay-lines ran along the walls in their sheaths, to the eastern stay, which had been warm on the Monday.

It was warm again. The salt underfoot was damp and soft, and the gallery smelled of something green and cold that Hester had no name for. Behind the walls, faint, a sound like breath drawn in through teeth and let out, and drawn in.

They took their places along the line and laid their wet hands on the brass. They sang the first slow bars of the work-song to lock their breathing, *hold the line, hold the line*. Then they went down into the hold together and poured.

And it was easy.

It had been easy on the Monday, and the Friday before, and every shift since she had joined. She had nothing to compare it to, only Ama's face and Fenwick's. Fenwick said, after, that he'd never known a season like it; some autumns in his three years the stays had nearly had him off his feet. Ama, forty years on the line, said nothing at all.

Tonight Hester felt it, because tonight she was feeling for it. In the hold, with her stillness running out through her wet fingers in the brine-slots of the brass and away toward the warm wall, she felt that the brass was not empty. Something was in it already, before her, beside her, under her: something cold and old and deeply still, already in the line when the Choir's pour reached it. It took the weight of the pour as a full bucket takes the next cup, without needing much. And it came from the other direction, from behind her, from the east trunk, where the main stay-line came in through the gallery wall from somewhere far off.

The line was already full.

She could not taste it. You could not taste through your hands. She could only feel it, as you feel through a floor that there is somebody in the room below.

She came out of the hold at the end of the shift cold and slow, the lag sitting on her like wet wool, and Ama put a blanket round her shoulders. Up in the Gallery, Dunmore was at his desk writing down the hours in his neat hand, not looking up. At the other end of the room Orsolya was on the settee with her eyes closed and a cup going cold in her lap. The kept flame was gone from the stand. Only a ring of dried salt showed where it had been.

Hester stood in the doorway with the blanket round her and looked at the ring of salt until Ama touched her elbow.

She went up the Stair slowly, because her legs were slow, and on the long climb she carried all the figures, the ones she had been leaving out.

W.L.F. A spiral round Aske's arm, and the same spiral round a candle-flame, turned by the same left wrist. A line that came in full from the east, and a Warden who had held these stays for thirty years and called them kind. It added up without any care at all about which figures you carried.

She could not prove a line of it. A hand in the grain was not a signature. And the woman it pointed at had signed for her own father at thirty, and paid the three sols a week that kept Tib in the fourth row. If Hester said it aloud, Teo would not be able to look down the Long Table without Orsolya knowing by the end of supper, and Lucan would want the Proctory, which had already lost one piece of brass. And if she was wrong, she would have said it of the one person in the building who talked like home.

She wanted to be wrong. She noticed that, and did not trust it.

So she would tell no one. She would stand in the line on Friday, and on the Monday after, with her hands on the brass, and find out what was in it. By the time she reached the Rookery she had put the name where no one else could reach it, as she had once put away the last thing Tib said to her, and she knew exactly what she was doing, and did it anyway.


# Chapter 16: Annealing

> Glass remembers how it cooled.
>
> — *Proverb of the Quell glassmakers*

The boots were the first thing.

They were Quell boots, which meant they were good: black calf, cut high, laced through brass eyes, with a welted sole that would take the Rookery's salt-glazed floors for ten winters without splitting. Hester knew what boots like that cost because she had once seized forty pairs of them at the dry harbour from a Hask trader who called them "personal effects," and Daisy had valued them at a sol and a half the pair, wholesale. Saskia Orme came down to the Long Table in them on the second of Cinder and ate her porridge as if her feet had always been inside good leather.

Hester noticed; she noticed most things that had a price. People bought boots.

The second thing was the hours. Saskia's cell was three doors along from R-41, and its door had a hinge that shrieked. Twice that week Hester, lying awake with the Fifth Fathom's cold in her bones from a Choir shift, heard it shriek after the twelfth bell. The second time she got up and looked out. Saskia was coming along the passage in her outdoor coat with her collar up, carrying her boots so as not to wake anyone. There was salt-mud on the coat's hem to the knee. Not the grey mud of the Pans or the Causeway: the white paste of the harbour ramps, where the harbour-men raked the salt smooth every morning and the landship runners churned it every night.

The third thing was the money.

Hester did not go looking for it. She was in the Bursary on the First Fathom on the third, paying her Choir fine; she had lagged at the Stay the day before and come round ten minutes after her relief should have taken over, and the Choir docked you for that whether it was your fault or not. While the clerk, a Harlish boy with a cold, wrote her receipt, she read the open ledger at his elbow upside down, because she had read ledgers upside down for twenty-one years and could not stop. *Orme, S. Lumen. Fees Thaw and Turning, paid in full. Additional: remitted to Glassmarket Infant School, Ambry, for P. Orme, the year in advance, 6 sols.*

Saskia paid her fees by the term, late, with a note; Hester had seen her do it in Russet at this same window with her jaw set.

Hester took her receipt and went up the Stair doing the sum, and did not like the answer.

* * *

She found her in the kilns.

The Lumen kilns were on the Third Fathom, at the end of a corridor that grew warmer every yard: the only warm place in the Halcyon below the Crown. By night only the annealing ovens were kept going. The glaziers called them lehrs: long brick tunnels, warm as a bread oven, where the day's lantern-jars crept on iron trays from the hot end to the cool end over twelve hours. Somebody had to sit with them, turning the dampers and walking the trays forward a hand's breadth at a time. Saskia took that duty three nights a week for a fleck an hour. She had told Hester so in Russet, when they were still needling each other, as evidence that some people had to work.

She was sitting on an upturned crate at the cool end of the long lehr, in her shirtsleeves, with her new boots crossed at the ankle and a ledger of tray-times on her knee. In the dull red of the lehr's mouth and the gold of three kept-light jars on a shelf, her face was all angles.

"Vail," she said, without looking up. "If you've come to borrow heat, it's a fleck a quarter-hour. Bassano's rule."

"I've come about your boots."

Saskia did look up then. "They're very nice. Thank you. Quellish. My cousin makes them."

"Your cousin charges a sol and a half, wholesale. I've valued them." Hester stood just inside the door. She had come down the corridor with the sum in her head and nothing else. "And you paid two terms' fees on the first. And six sols for a year at the Glassmarket school. That's thirty sols, near enough, since Russet. You're on a Lumen bursary and a fleck an hour for watching ovens."

Saskia set the ledger down on the crate beside her, carefully, as if it were a tray of glass.

"You read my bursary page."

"It was open."

"You *read my bursary page*." Her voice was level. "Do you do this to everyone? Or only the ones from across the White? Do you go through people's pockets for contraband?"

"Only when there's contraband in them."

It was the wrong thing to say, and she knew it as she said it. Saskia stood up.

"Get out of my kiln."

"Who pays you, Orme?"

"Get *out*."

"You come in at the twelfth bell with harbour paste on your coat to the knee," Hester said. "Ramp-salt, from the landship quays. I walked on it for twenty-one years. Three nights a week, and not the nights you sit with the lehr." She heard her voice go flat and hard, the voice she had used on Mallory, and could not find another. "There's a man in grey wool who oversaw forty crates of felony grain being carried out of the Ambry bond-shed in the middle of the night. Somebody carried that grain out of here in the first place. Was it you?"

The lehr ticked.

Saskia looked at her a long time. Then she sat down again on the crate, slowly, and put her elbows on her knees and her face in her hands. When she took them away her eyes were dry and her mouth was tight with something nearer disgust than shame.

"Two sols a run," she said.

* * *

It came out in pieces, not in order but in the order they were hit.

She had come over from Quell in Thaw with Pip and her mother and forty sols, which was what was left of Jory's landship after the Exchequer took its cut and the insurers found a clause. Forty sols, in a city that charged rent like a toll-gate. Bassano's bursary paid her fees and nothing else. She had sold Jory's sextant in the spring—"before you say it, Vail, yes, his father's, I sold it to a Hask trader for nine sols and I'd do it again"—and by midsummer she was choosing between her mother's coal and Pip's shoes.

"And then a man came to the kilns," she said. "One night at midsummer. A clerk. Bland, ink on his cuffs. Pleasant. He'd heard I was a hard worker who needed evening hours. He said the Warden's office wanted a courier who could keep her mouth shut and her hands steady. Residue grain. Stay salt, up from the Fifth in sealed boxes, down to a Ferrers freighter at the harbour, under contract. Conclave-approved. They wanted somebody from outside the Choir because Choir people talk, and somebody poor because poor people turn up."

"Dunmore," Hester said.

"I never asked his name. It was on a docket once. E. Dunmore." Saskia's mouth twisted. "He gave me a pass for the box-lift and a barrow. It runs from the Fifth to the Third and stops nowhere between but a locked service gate at the Fourth. Three nights a week I'd meet it at the tenth bell at the Third Fathom landing, and he'd bring the boxes up. Four, five, sometimes ten. I'd barrow them out through the Gatehouse on the porters' road and down the Causeway, and a freighter's mate would sign for them. Two sols, every time. In silver, in my hand."

"You didn't ask what was in them."

"I knew what was in them. Grain. It said so on the docket."

"You didn't ask why a Conclave-approved contract needed a woman with a barrow at night and not the porters' drays."

"*No*, Vail. I didn't ask." Saskia's voice came up for the first time. "Because if I'd asked, he'd have found someone who didn't. I've a daughter. She's six. Her feet grow. Have you ever had to look at a child's feet and do sums? No. You've a brother who doesn't grow at all. You're lucky."

The word stayed in the hot air between them.

"The boxes were warm," Hester said. "You'd have felt it through the wood. Quickened grain is warm. And you see colour at the edges of things, Orme; you'd have seen it bleeding round the corners like a lamp through a sheet. I tasted that grain at the Customs House. It came in on the *Constant Wren* labelled Shelf for curing, which was a lie. It was quickened Hush, which is a felony to sell, and it tastes exactly like the brine beading on my brother's casing. You've seen that casing. You stood by it on Lantern Night. And somebody walked that grain down the Causeway in a barrow for two sols a run."

"The *Wren*." Saskia had gone pale under the red light. "That was high summer. Some of them went east. I didn't know—"

"They went to Quell and came back with a new label."

"I didn't *know*—"

"You didn't ask."

"And you did?" Saskia was on her feet again. "You *did*? Look at your own shoulder, Vail. What's that?"

Hester did not look. A Choir badge: the little iron wave on a grey ribbon, pinned under the black cord of Sorle's Bench.

"Three sols a week," Saskia said. "Out of the Warden's office. The *same office*. Paid over by the same bland little man with ink on his cuffs, I'll lay money. Where do you think the Choir stipends come from? The Conclave wouldn't pay for a candle-end if it could keep it. You take his money every Friday, Vail, to pay your brother's fee, and you go down into the Fifth and do whatever it is they do there, and you've never once asked where the silver comes from. Have you? Have you *asked*?"

Hester said nothing.

"No," said Saskia. "Because you needed it. Because it was for Tib." She laughed, short and ugly. "You took that gown on a dead man's privilege. You took a Choir place from a Warden you'd known a fortnight. You'd have taken it from the Chancellor's own hand if it paid the Cold House. You're Lowside, so you think you're clean. Lowside isn't clean, Vail. It's just poor. Ask anyone in Quell."

"I didn't carry felony grain to the harbour in a barrow."

"No. You just let other people's dead pay your rent."

That one went in, under the ribs. And because it went in, Hester did what she had done all her life when something went in. She hit back with the nearest heavy thing.

"Jory would be proud," she said.

She saw it land. Saskia's face went still, not with the grain-stillness but with the other kind, the kind that comes before a person decides whether to strike you. She did not strike. She looked at Hester for three ticks of the lehr. Then she sat down on her crate and opened the ledger of tray-times on her knee.

"Get out of my kiln," she said, quietly. "Please."

Hester went.

* * *

She walked. It was what she did. She went up the Stair to the Crown and out onto the dome, and round the Lantern's foot in the cold with her hands in her armpits. It was a hard clear night. The White lay under the stars like a sheet laid out for a body, and far to the west the lamps of Ambry were a smudge of yellow at the edge of the world.

She knew she was right about the grain. Saskia had carried felony salt for money, and some of it tasted like Tib's beads. But Saskia was right too. The silver in Hester's own purse came up the same Stair from the same office, and she had never asked. Orsolya had sat her down in that warm office in Russet and given her tea and said *I need holds like yours*, and Hester had been so grateful, so Lowside-grateful, to be wanted by someone who talked like her mother's sister, that she had not looked at the money at all. She had only counted it. She was looking at it now, and at the woman who paid it.

And she had said the thing about Jory, who had gone into a sink on the eastern White with a full crew. Saskia had told that story on Lantern Night in the Glassmarket, quickly, with Pip on her hip, as if it were the price of something.

Hester stood at the dome's edge and looked toward the Well-Cap on its chains, never opened. She thought of her father drawing the fire from under a spitting pan a shovelful at a time. *Lid's for when you're in a hurry.*

She went back in.

* * *

Teo was baking.

Nobody was supposed to bake in the Rookery. Fires were forbidden on the dome, and the Proctors checked. Teo had solved this in his first week with a spirit stove smuggled up the Stair in his laundry, which he lit with the window cracked and a towel along the foot of the door, and which baked one small saltcake, badly, under a covered tin. A cracked kept-light jar on the sill did for a lamp. R-41 consequently smelled most nights of burnt sugar and hot salt. Teo sat cross-legged on his bed in his grey Tider shawl, watching the tin as other men watch a sick child.

"You've been fighting," he said, without looking round.

"How do you know?"

"You've got your customs face on. And Saskia Orme came past an hour ago and didn't sneer at our door, which she always does, so either she's dead or she's been in a fight and lost, and she doesn't lose." He lifted the lid a crack and let it down again. "Ten minutes. Sit."

She sat and told him, badly, in the wrong order: the boots and the bursary and the barrow and the *Wren*, then what Saskia had said, and at the end what she had said herself. Teo listened without interrupting.

"Well," he said. "That was a horrible thing to say."

"I know."

"No, I mean truly horrible. It was well made. You've a gift for it." He lifted the lid again. "And she's right about the stipend. And you're right about the grain. And she's right that you're Lowside and think you're clean, and you're right that she should have asked. I don't think anyone in this conversation was wrong about anything, Hester, which is generally how the worst conversations go." He let the lid down. "In the Order we'd have said you were both in a state of grace and both damned, and sent you to scrub the refectory together. It works better than you'd think."

"I'm not going to scrub anything with her."

"No," said Teo. "But you might eat something."

There was a knock at the door. Not a Proctor's knock, which was three and loud. One knock, hard, as if whoever made it had thought about it for some time and decided it would be only one.

Saskia was standing in the passage with a bottle of Quell clear in one hand and her new boots in the other. She held up the bottle.

"I've come about your manners," she said.

"I've no manners."

"No. I noticed." Saskia came in without being asked, sat on the end of Teo's bed, and put the boots on the floor and the bottle on the window ledge. "That's my cousin's clear. It's illegal on this side of the White. You can seize it if you like. It's for drinking."

Teo produced three tin cups from somewhere in the shawl.

Quell clear tasted like having your mouth set on fire by a person who loved you. Nobody apologised. Hester did not know how she would have done it if she had tried, and Saskia, she suspected, knew even less. They sat on the two narrow beds with the White outside the salt-glass and the burnt-sugar smell of Teo's tin. After the second cup Saskia said, to the window, "Jory would have asked."

"I shouldn't have said it."

"No. But he would have. He asked about everything. He asked the insurers why their clause was in Ambry black and the rest of the contract in Quell blue. They didn't pay out anyway, but he asked." She turned the cup in her fingers. "I'm not Jory. I'm the one who does sums."

"So am I," Hester said.

"I know. That's why I hate you." Saskia drank. "Some days."

The saltcake came out black on the bottom and raw in the middle and sweet as a sin, and they ate it with their fingers off the lid. Teo said a Tider grace over it, composed, he said, for the breaking of a fast after the death of an abbot, and suitable for most occasions involving sugar and recrimination.

* * *

"Tell me about the lift," Hester said, later.

The tenth bell, Saskia said. The Third Fathom landing, where the box-lift from the Fifth came up behind an iron grille that was locked at night. Dunmore in the cage with the boxes stacked round his feet like a man in a pantry. Always alone. He never let her go down.

"How did he open the grille?"

"Keys. He's got a ring. He keeps it in his desk in the Warden's antechamber, the left-hand drawer. He took it out in front of me the first time, showing me the route." Saskia frowned, remembering. "A big ring with a brass tag: *W.L.F. Duplicates.* Twenty keys, near enough. Grilles, stores, the Choir lockers. And one big ugly old one, black iron, longer than my hand, with a bird on the bow. A little bird sitting on a line." She drew it in the air. "I asked what that was for, as a joke. He said it was for the Hush, and nobody went there, and I should mind my barrow."

Hester and Teo looked at each other.

"The Hush key," said Teo. "On a duplicate ring. In a clerk's desk."

"Bureaucracy," said Saskia. "It's the same in Quell. The keys to the bishop's crypt are in the sacristan's tea-caddy."

"Did he ever speak about the Warden?" Hester said. "Did she ever come to the lift?"

"Never. I never saw her once." Saskia hesitated. "He said once, when I dropped a box on the landing, *Careful. The Warden says the Choir eats on this.* I thought it was a manner of speaking."

*The Warden.* Hester thought of a coil of grain turning left round a candle-flame, and of brass in the Fifth that was full before the Choir's pour reached it, and she kept her face exactly where it was.

"He'd use her name," she said. "He's her clerk. Mallory used the Exchequer's." It came out easily. She had said true things in that flat voice for twenty-one years; she had not known it would serve as well for the other kind. "It's Dunmore. He signs for the office. He has the seals and the keys and the lift. Dunmore's the one we watch."

Teo said nothing. He was looking at her with the expression he had sometimes in Fane's lection classes, when he had heard something under a tablet that nobody else in the room could hear and was deciding whether to say so.

"There's one more thing," Hester said, because it had to be asked. "Aske's note. It ends *Ask O.* Did Aske ever speak to you? About the boxes? About anything?"

Saskia stared at her. Then, for the first time that night, she laughed properly.

"*Orme?* You thought O was *Orme*?" She shook her head. "Vail. Corvin Aske didn't know I existed. I said good morning to him once on the Stair and he walked into the wall. He was reading a sample." She laughed again, less certainly. "I'm flattered. Nobody's suspected me of anything interesting since I was nineteen. So who's O.?"

"Somebody Aske meant to ask," said Hester. "Somebody who knows the Fifth."

"That's half the Choir."

"Then it's half the Choir," Hester said, and reached for the bottle, so that she had something to do with her hands.

* * *

So Hester told her the rest. Saskia had stood in the Cold House on Lantern Night and seen the beads. She had not seen what came after.

Hester told her about the casing in Cell 3-17 and the sheet in the blotter; Lucan's torn page, *per W.L.F.*; the Cistern, and the brass rods with Lattice marks sleeved in brine so still it made her eyes ache; the rod that had gone into the Proctory and not come out; Lucan's contract, signed by *E. Dunmore, clerk*, and what the letters stood for; and her brother's casing thin enough at the foot to see a candle through.

She did not tell her what Tib had said on the night they kept him, or what she had seen in the grain round a candle-flame. There were limits.

Saskia did not interrupt, though she made faces, and once she said, "*Lattice* marks? Then they were made here," and Hester said yes. When it was finished she sat looking into her empty cup.

"Who else knows?"

"Teo. Sorle. Lucan Ferrers."

"Ferrers went down a hole in Lowside with you?" Saskia looked as if she had bitten a lemon. "With his ribs? Against his own father's firm?"

"He's—" Hester stopped. She did not know how to describe Lucan Ferrers to someone who had last seen him standing a careful yard apart from them on the sea-wall. "He's not what he looks like."

"Nobody is. That's the whole trouble with people." Saskia set the cup on the ledge. "What do you want from me?"

"I don't know yet."

"Well, decide. I've a lehr to walk at the first bell." But she did not get up. She sat in her stockinged feet with her good boots on the floor beside her and looked out at the dark White. "I don't do causes, Vail. I told you so on Lantern Night. I do rent."

"I know."

"So understand me. I'm not doing this because I'm sorry." She said it to the window, evenly. "I am sorry. I'm sorry I carried that grain, and sorrier I didn't ask, and I'll be sorry for longer than you'll be angry, I expect. But that's not why. There are three hundred people kept in that ward, and some of them have daughters. And if somebody in the Warden's office is bleeding them to pay my two sols a run, then my daughter's school was bought with them. I'll not have that. I'll not have her feet in their shoes." She turned from the window. "So. At cost. Whatever this is, I'll do it at cost."

"At cost," Hester said.

They did not shake hands. Teo poured the last of the Quell clear into the three tin cups, and they drank it without a toast, because nobody could think of one.

* * *

Lucan came the next night, because Teo sent for him.

He stood in the door of R-41 in his Upper Ward wool with the ochre cord of Vey's Bench on his shoulder and took in the cell: the two beds, the burnt tin, Saskia Orme cross-legged on Hester's blanket with a ledger, Hester at the window. His face went to the polished blank that meant he was thinking fast.

"Orme," he said.

"Ferrers."

"I was told there was a meeting."

"There's cake," said Teo. "Of a kind."

Lucan looked at the cake, and at Saskia, and at Hester, and she saw him understand that Saskia knew everything and that it had been Hester's choice. He did not argue it. He folded himself onto the floor by the door with his back to the wall, ribs still careful, and accepted a piece of black-bottomed saltcake from Teo with the gravity of a man accepting a treaty.

"Well," he said. "Here we are."

Hester stood at the window with her back to them and listened to the three of them start to talk, cautiously, then less so. Saskia asked Lucan about the contract seal; Lucan asked Saskia about the docket. Teo read Aske's sheet out again in his careful brine-script voice: *Cold House, fourteen weeping. A draw? Still runs to quick. Cistern?? Old lines. Ask O.* Then Lucan turned and asked what she made of the clerk, and Hester gave them Dunmore: the ring, the drawer, the lift, the docket hand. She said his name three times before the cake was finished. Each time, under it, like the low note under the Choir's work-song, she heard the other.

Her ledger lay open on the sill. Under the heading she had written in Russet, *Declaration of Interest*, she had crossed out *Orme* that afternoon with a single neat line. Below it she had written *E. Dunmore*, plainly, where anyone in the room could read it. The other name she had not written anywhere.

She looked at the page in the glow from Teo's cracked jar. Then she closed the ledger and sat down on the floor with the others, and took the last piece of cake before Lucan could, because he was nearer and slower and had cracked ribs, and somebody had to teach him.


# Chapter 17: The Silent Supper

> On the ninth of Cinder, at the Lantern Hour, the whole Halcyon shall be still for one minute, and the Settled shall be named.
>
> — *Customs of the Collegium, as recorded by the Warden of the Stacks, 412 Y.E.*

They gave out the chalk at the sixth bell.

It came up from the Bursary in wooden trays, one stick for every soul in the Halcyon who could hold one: Readers and Fellows and aspirants, porters and cooks, the delvers who worked the Halcyon's own pits under the Shelf, and the Proctors, who took theirs without expression and went to their places like men on parade. Hester's stick was new and square and left a powder on her fingers that tasted of nothing at all. Varrow chalk. Dead stone. Teo said it was the one place in the Halcyon where nobody wanted anything kept.

The Stair ran from the Crown to the Fifth Fathom in a single great spiral cut into the living salt, nine hundred years of feet worn into its treads, and its outer wall was written on the whole way down. Names, in columns, each with a year. Salt drew damp out of the air and damp drew the chalk out of the salt, so that by Rime the oldest names near the Crown were ghosts, legible only by a lantern held flat to the wall. On the ninth of Cinder they were all written again.

Every aspirant was given a stretch of wall and the Bursary's list for it, and told to write each name over its ghost exactly. Hester's stretch was on the Second Fathom, and it ran from 641 to 655 Y.E. *Odo Marchant, Choir, 641. Wenna Hale, Choir, 641. Brother Cole Ansty, Fathoming, 642.* She went down the column on her knees with her lantern on the step, and found she was writing in her best customs hand, the hand she used for seizure notes, as if somebody were going to check.

*Hesk Rowan, Choir, 649. Tobit Marr, Choir, 649.* There were a great many Choir. In some years the whole column was Choir.

Below her, round the curve, Teo was saying names under his breath as he wrote them, half to himself, as he said grace. Above her someone was crying quietly. She did not look up. That was the custom too.

When she had finished she sat back on her heels and looked at the column of fresh white letters on the pink salt. Then she went on down, because the Choir was to gather on the Fifth Fathom landing at the seventh bell, and she was Choir.

* * *

The Warden of the Lower Fathoms wrote her own names.

Hester came round the last turn of the Stair with Ama Tessaly and two others, and there at the foot, in the cold blue light of the Fathom lamps, was Orsolya Thane, kneeling on the bottom tread with a stick of chalk in her left hand.

These were the newest names. They had their own panel at the bottom of the Stair, a smooth stretch of salt the width of a door where the Fathom's own dead went. There were eleven, from the last five years. The Bursary's list lay folded on the step by Orsolya's knee, unopened. She did not need it. She wrote each name in big plain capitals, slowly, pressing hard, so that the chalk squeaked. Her hands were blue at the knuckles with cold, as they always were.

*IDRIS FELL, CHOIR, 898.*
*MARRA COLE, CHOIR, 899.*
*JONAH SAINT, CHOIR, 899.*

The Choir stood on the stairs above her and waited. Orsolya wrote all eleven. Then she sat back on her heels and looked at them, and put the chalk in her pocket, and stood up stiffly, one hand on the wall.

"Right, then," she said, in a perfectly ordinary voice. "Supper. Up you go. Mind the Stair, it's wet at the turn."

"She does that every year," Ama said softly. "Won't let anyone else. Says it's her hand put them there."

Hester looked at the eleven names in their white capitals as Orsolya went up the Stair ahead of them.

The capitals leaned, every one. A small steady lean to the left, as if drawn by a wrist that turned against itself.

She looked for exactly as long as a chorister might look at the names of the dead, and then she went up after the Warden, and said something to Ama about the cold.

* * *

There were six hundred people at the Long Table, and more standing.

Hester had eaten there every day for two months and had stopped seeing it. Tonight she saw it: three hundred feet of table down the centre of the great hall on the First Fathom, carved from the living salt nine centuries ago and worn by elbows until it shone. There were no dishes yet, only a tallow candle at every place. No grain lights. The Lantern Well, which on every other night of the year poured kept noon onto the table at the Lantern Hour, was dark. On the ninth of Cinder they kept no light. They burned it.

The Chancellor was at the head of the table.

Hester had seen her twice, at a distance. Perpetua Holm was seventy-eight and had been Chancellor for thirty-one years, and she was Settled further than anyone Hester had seen still upright. She sat in a high-backed chair of black Hask wood with a rug over her knees and her hands on its arms, white as the salt, and in the twenty minutes Hester watched her she did not visibly move them. Her eyes moved. Slowly, face by face, down the length of the table, as a lighthouse travels a coast. Beside her Vey sat upright and elegant in black, saying something now and then in her ear.

The Choir sat together at the foot, nearest the Stair-head. Hester was between Ama and a broad silent delver-born man called Pask. Three places down, Orsolya sat with her hands round a cup of halcyon tea, her face tired and calm. Further up, among the first-years, Hester could see Teo's grey Tider shawl and Saskia's dark head beside it, and, among the Upper Ward aspirants at Vey's end, the back of Lucan's.

"First time?" said Ama.

"Yes."

"Nothing to fear, lamb. It's only a minute." Ama was sixty-six, small and round-shouldered, with the sweet slow voice of the long Choir. She had wrapped Hester in a blanket after her first stay in Russet and had been wrapping her in things ever since: scarves, advice, slabs of Hask honey-cake from a tin in her Choir locker. Her lips were blue, and when she laughed, which was often, it took her a long time to stop. "You hold if you can. Most of the Readers do. But with all of us together, lamb—" She made a bowl of her hands. "You'll feel it. It's like the Choir, only the whole house."

The Chancellor's bell rang.

It was a small handbell of old brass at Holm's elbow, and Vey lifted it, because her hand could not. The hall went silent in a wave from the head of the table down, a ripple of hush running three hundred feet along the salt like wind over grass. When it reached the Choir's end it stopped. Six hundred people sat at the Long Table and did not move.

* * *

Hester did not decide to hold. She found, after some seconds, that she was already doing it, as you find you have fallen asleep in a warm room.

Breath slow. Heart slow. Hands flat on the cold salt. It was the hold Crisp had drilled into her at the Pans, and she went into it as she had a hundred times since Russet, the coins coming down on her eyelids, the weight settling behind her face. Only this time she did not go in alone.

She felt the table first. It was one stone, the dome's own living grain, and six hundred hands lay on it. A good half of them belonged to people with the sense, and most of those were holding, or nearly. She felt them as she felt grain, through her palms, as weight. Here a Reader holding deep and steady, like a lead sinker in a pond. There a nervous first-year holding in little jerks. Three places down, someone holding so cold and so deep it was like a well: Orsolya. Beside her, Ama, soft and sure. And all of it running into the one stone, joining, a slow vast stillness spreading through the salt from six hundred places at once.

The candles stood up straight.

Before the Minute the flames had been leaning and fluttering in the draughts from the Lantern Well. Now, one by one down the whole length of the table, they stood up. They did not go out. They did not flicker. Six hundred small yellow spear-points burned in a line in the dark, so steady they looked painted, and the smoke rose from them in six hundred straight grey threads to the roof.

She could hear her own heart. Between one beat and the next there was time to count to four.

And under the stillness, in the stone, the sound.

It was the Roar, the thing she had first heard in the *Wren* grain and heard every Choir shift since: the long low rushing, the rise coming again before the fall had finished. She had never heard it in the Long Table. The dome's own grain was Shelf-depth, too shallow to hold more than a whisper. But with six hundred of them holding together, the whisper was there under her hands, a breath going in and out of the salt beneath the whole Halcyon. She thought, from a long way off, *it sounds like something asleep*.

Ama's hand found hers on the table and held it, cold and dry.

*Idris Fell*, Hester thought. *Marra Cole. Jonah Saint.* She had never met them. The names came up through the stillness like bubbles through brine. *Odo Marchant. Wenna Hale.* And somewhere behind her eyes, where the coins were, Tib's hand curled on his chest, holding its pencil.

Then the bell rang.

Not the Chancellor's handbell. The other one.

* * *

It was iron, and it came from below.

Every Fathom had a bell in its Stair-head with a rope that ran down through the floors to the Fathom beneath. Hester had learned the rings her first week in the Choir. One and one was a summons. Two and two was fire. Three and three, iron on iron, hard and fast, meant a pocket had broken.

*Clang-clang-clang.* A breath. *Clang-clang-clang.*

The Minute broke like a plate.

Hester came out of the hold too fast and felt the world tilt and right itself. The candles were leaning again, all six hundred, thrown sideways at once by six hundred people moving. Near the head of the table a chair went over backward with a crack like a shot. A first-year girl was saying "What is it, what is it?" to nobody. And at the Choir's end, before Hester had her breath, Orsolya Thane was on her feet.

"Choir," she said.

She did not shout. Her voice went down the table like a hand laid flat on it, and the Choir rose: sixty people in grey wool rising in one movement out of six hundred, like a flight of salt-larks off a pan. Ama's hand pulled out of Hester's, and Ama was up, and Pask, and Hester with them before she had thought about it at all.

"Pask, the stay-lines from the Fourth store, all of them. Ama, the trunk valves. Edwin—" Dunmore was at Orsolya's elbow from nowhere, ink on his cuffs. "Edwin, the Proctors. Clear the Fourth east gallery. Everyone out who isn't Choir." Her eyes found the rest of them. "With me. Run if you can. Don't if you can't. Down."

In the one glance Hester gave back before the Stair took her, the Chancellor sat in her black chair with her white hands on its arms and did not move. Her eyes followed them to the Stair-head, the lighthouse look, travelling the coast. Then the curve of the wall cut it off.

* * *

They went down the Stair in a grey river, past the fresh white names.

The Fourth Fathom held the Quiet Rooms and the Choir hall and, to the east, the long gallery where the Brinestacks kept their overflow: records too bulky or too damaged for the shelves above, much of it salvage from the old fire, in iron cases floor to ceiling. It had always been cold, dry and silent.

It was not silent now. The sound came up the passage to meet them as they ran, a sound the Halcyon did not make: a rushing, and a slapping, and under it a long hollow boom that came and went, so that the floor gave a small shiver each time it came.

"Tide take it," said Pask, beside her, quietly. He was a delver's son. He had heard the White crack under him before.

Then they were through the door, and Hester saw water.

It was running across the floor of the east gallery, a sheet of it an inch deep, black in the lamplight, carrying scraps of paper on it like leaves on a stream. Fifty yards down, the gallery's end wall was wet from floor to ceiling, shining like a cliff in rain. Out of a crack at about the height of a man's shoulder, water was coming in a thick clear rope. Every few breaths the rope swelled and spat, and that was the boom: a deep thump from inside the wall, and a gush.

The smell hit her a second after the sight. It was cold, and green, and strong, a smell she had met only once, faintly, in the warm wet salt of her first stay. It was like the Weeps at low water in summer, but cleaner, and brighter, and enormously more. It was the smell of a great many things growing in water.

"Sweet sea," someone whispered.

The water came over Hester's shoes, bitterly cold, the cold of deep winter. She bent and put two fingers in it and put them to her mouth before she had decided to.

Salt, but not grain, not brine that anyone had mixed. It tasted as the *Wren* grain had tasted under her tongue at the Customs House: bright, metallic, startled. And there was something living in it, green and faintly of iodine. It tasted of the cry she had heard in that cargo, if a cry could have a taste. It tasted *quick*.

"Vail!" Orsolya's voice. "Line. *Now.*"

* * *

The stay went up in four minutes. Hester did not see most of it, because she was part of it.

Pask and the others came at a run with the stay-lines, long hoses of oiled leather sleeved in brass, coiled on their shoulders like rope. They ran them from the trunk valves at the gallery door, where Ama knelt with a spanner opening the cocks, out across the flooded floor to the wet wall. The Choir followed them in and formed. Hester had done it a dozen times in drills on the dry Choir floor, never in water with the boom in the walls. It made no difference. The Choir knew its own shape: sixty in a long curved line facing the wall, each with a hand on the shoulder of the one before, each line-leader with a stay-line clamped to his belt and its brass mouth pressed to the weeping salt.

Live brine came rushing down the leather from the trunk. Every chorister put a pinch of Middling under the tongue, as they always did, to tune, and went in.

And Orsolya sang.

It was a note, low and long, held for a full breath, then let go, then held again, and every voice in the line took it and held it with her. The note was a rope you could all hold at once. Your breath went out on it as slowly as hers did, and your heart came down to meet your breath, and sixty holds became one hold, poured down the stay-lines into the wet salt.

*Hold the line,* Orsolya sang. *Hold the line.*

*So the others can sleep,* the Choir answered.

The stillness went out of Hester through her hands and the soles of her feet in the cold water, down through Pask's shoulder under her palm and into the brass and the wall. She felt the wall take it. A quickening was a hungry thing; that was the first thing they taught you in the Choir. The boom came again, weaker, and the rope of water thinned and shuddered.

She was deep now. The coins had become a weight on her whole face, and the cold of the water had stopped being cold and become simply a fact, like the dark. She was listening, as she had learned to, to the line itself: the long brass trunk running from the valve at the door away into the Halcyon's walls, to the stores, to the Fifth, to wherever stays drew from.

Something was coming up it, from behind her. From the east.

She had felt this before. In Fallow, on the night shift after the candle-flame, with her palms on the brass in the Fifth: something cold and old already in the line before the Choir's pour reached it. You could not taste through your hands. She had stood in the line every shift since, waiting for a mouthful.

It came now in a flood. Not the Choir's stillness, which she knew after six weeks as she knew her own breath: sixty human holds, warm at the edges, a little ragged, smelling of wool and fear. This was colder than any hold she had ever felt, and perfectly smooth, the stillness of something that had not moved in years and years. It came up the trunk and into the wall like a tide of cold oil. Under its weight the quickening's boom faltered, and went *tick*, and *tick*.

The stay-line at Pask's belt bucked with the load. Where its brass mouth met the wall it spat, a short hard jet, and brine struck Hester across the cheek and the mouth.

The Middling under her tongue was long gone. What was on her lips was the line itself.

She knew it at once, as she knew the reference jars on her old bench. She had tasted it in Fallow, on her knees in the founders' brick under Coldwater Street with Lucan's lantern shaking, a fingertip of brine from the sleeve round a brass rod. *This is them.* She had smelled it for eleven years in the cold sweet air of the charity ward, standing by casing two hundred and eleven. It was the stillness of the Cold House, the stillest thing in the world, because old keepings were.

It was Tib.

It was coming up the stay-line past her and pouring into the wall, and the wall was drinking it, and the stay was holding.

*Hold the line,* Orsolya sang.

Hester held. She did not know what else to do. Her hand was on Pask's shoulder and her brother was on her lips and going past her through the brass a yard away, into the Warden's stay, under the Warden's song. She held, and the Choir held, and the rope of water thinned to a trickle and then to a weep and then to nothing. The black sheet on the floor slowed and began, at its edges, to frost into salt.

* * *

The hold broke in a long ragged ending, the Choir coming out of it one by one like people waking in a cold room. Somebody coughed. Somebody was sick into the water. The lag took Hester for a moment, the world going grey and soundless, and then let her go. She was standing in water iced to the ankle with her hand still on Pask's shoulder, and her teeth were chattering so hard she could hear them.

"Down," Pask was saying. "Down, down. Ama, love. Ama."

Ama was sitting in the water.

She was sitting where she had stood, at Hester's left, in the line. She had folded down so gently that Hester had not felt her go: knees bent, back straight, hands open on her lap. The water had frozen to salt around her skirt and her shoes. Her eyes were half open. Hester bent beside her in the ice, and heard, faintly, the note.

She was still singing. Not aloud; the breath of it. The long low Choir note was going out of her on a breath that did not end, the hold still in her, held past the place where it could be let go.

"Ama," Hester said.

The note went on. Then it was quieter. Then it was a breath. Then it was not anything.

Hester put her fingers to the old woman's throat, as her mother had taught her when she was ten, and felt for the beat. It was there, a long way off. One. She counted to twenty. She counted to forty. It did not come again.

"She held too long," Pask said above her, roughly. "She always held too long. Every stay, a breath past the rest of us, the old fool. She said someone ought to be last out." He knelt in the water on Ama's other side. "Oh, you old fool."

Feet in the slush. A lantern. Hester knelt with two fingers on Ama Tessaly's throat and did not let herself think it. She did not take her fingers away, because taking them away would make it a fact.

Then Orsolya was there.

She came through the Choir, which parted for her, and looked down at Ama sitting in the frozen water with her hands open, and her face did not change at all. She went down on her knees in the ice and took Ama's face between her two cold hands, carefully, as you might take a bowl of something hot.

"Ama," she said. "Oh, my girl. Oh, my girl."

And then she wept.

Hester had seen people cry in the Cold House every first of the month for eleven years. This was not like that. Orsolya knelt in the frozen sea with her friend's face in her hands and wept without a sound, without bending. The tears whitened as they ran. By the time they reached her jaw they were salt, little ridged crusts on her skin, the long Settle showing in her as it showed in all of them. She did not wipe them. Somewhere far behind the wall there was a sound like the sea turning over in its sleep.

"Twelve," Orsolya said at last. To nobody. To Ama. "That's twelve."

She looked up. Her eyes found Hester's across Ama's bowed head, a foot away, wet and grey and tired beyond anything Hester had words for. Hester looked in them for guilt, because she had been looking for it since Fallow. There was none, and no fear, and no calculation. Only grief, and under it something that might have been love, for the whole cold shivering line of them standing in the dark.

"Help me get her up, chick," Orsolya said. "She'll not want to be sat in the wet."

* * *

They carried her down on a Choir blanket, Pask and Orsolya and Hester and three others, past the fresh white names. At the Fifth Fathom landing they laid her at the foot of the panel where Orsolya had written the eleven, and Orsolya stood looking at it.

Hester did not stay. She went back up the Stair alone, round and round, past the Fourth and the Third, to her own stretch on the Second Fathom: Odo Marchant and Wenna Hale and Brother Cole Ansty in her best seizure hand. Below the last of them was a smooth blank stretch of pink salt above the step. Nobody would mind, she thought. Nobody would look.

She took the chalk out of her pocket and wrote, in capitals, pressing hard so that it squeaked:

*AMA TESSALY, CHOIR, 903.*

Then she sat on the step under it with chalk on her fingers and the Cold House still on her lips.

She did not suspect any longer. She knew. The Warden's stays drank the sleepers. Not Dunmore's: the Warden's, sung down by the Warden's voice and held by the Warden's line, and Hester had laid her hands on that brass every shift since Russet and poured herself in beside her brother, and been paid three sols a week for it, and sent the three sols to the Cold House to keep him in the casing they were drinking. She thought of the eleven names at the foot of the Stair, leaning a little to the left. She thought of Orsolya on her knees in the ice with Ama's face in her hands, and could not make the two women into one, and could not make them into two. She thought of Orsolya's voice in the dark, *so the others can sleep*, and of Orsolya in Russet, in the warm office, saying tiredly, as if it were a small mercy: *The Stays have been kind this season. Kinder than they've any right to be.*

She licked her lips, and tasted it again, and sat there until the first bell.


# Chapter 18: The Fourth Sleeper

> Signature of next of kin. Relationship to the kept. Date. The house accepts no responsibility for what returns.
>
> — *Form of Release, Ambry Charitable Brinery*

Agathe's letter came up the Causeway on the sixteenth of Cinder with the afternoon post, in a cheap grey envelope with the Cold House's name printed on the flap. Hester's name was on the front in a round careful hand that had been taught its letters by Tider sisters sixty years ago and had never seen a reason to change them.

*My dear. Bett Linnow is coming back. The casing has cracked at the foot and the brinewright says there is no more to be done and Mr. Pell is at his sister's. Her mother is here. She asks for someone who understands the salt. I do not, not as you do. If you can come, come. A.L.*

Hester read it standing in the Rookery passage. Then she put on her coat and went.

It had been raining on the Rim for two days, the short hard rain of Cinder that came over the Varrow and fell on Ambry and never reached the White. The Causeway was black and shining, and on either side of it the salt was bone-dry, as if the rain had come to a wall. She walked the two miles fast, with her collar up and her hands in her armpits, and was at the Cold House by the ninth bell.

Nobody was at the desk. The great ledger lay open on the felt with a pen across it. She went past it and through the double doors and down the six worn steps. On the third step she smelled it: not the cold sweet cellar smell of the charity ward, but something warm. Warm, and sharp, and faintly like hot iron.

* * *

Bett Linnow's plinth was the third from the end of the sixth row.

Agathe had shown it to her on Lantern Night. Before that Hester had passed it a hundred and thirty times on the first of the month without looking: a stout woman of about thirty in a brown dress, kept on her side with her knees drawn up and both hands pressed to her mouth, as if she had coughed and been surprised by it. From the aisle she had looked asleep. Most of them did.

She did not look asleep now. Agathe had hung a lantern above the plinth and the casing in its light was not clear any more. It was white. Not the grey of the dead, but a fine white crazing, like ice on a puddle when you put your heel on it, spreading from a crack at the casing's foot as long as Hester's hand. Brine was running out of the crack in a steady thread. The thread did not drip onto the floor. It ran sideways along the plinth's edge and down the eastern side and into the drain-grille beneath, as if it had somewhere to be.

The casing was warm. She could feel it from a yard away, like a stove going out. The arrears were coming.

An old woman sat on a stool beside the plinth with both her hands flat on the stone, as close to the casing as the stone allowed. She was small and upright, in a black wool coat that had been good once and a black bonnet. Agathe stood behind her with a hand on her shoulder.

"Ruth," Agathe said. "This is Hester. Tobias Vail's sister, from the fourth row. She's at the Halcyon now. She understands the salt."

The old woman looked up. Her eyes were pale and wet and enormously tired.

"Can you stop it?" she said.

* * *

Hester tried.

She knew before she began that she could not. She tried anyway, because the old woman had asked, and because it was the only thing in the world she knew how to do.

The house kept its recasting brine in a copper tank in the brinewright's closet, Middling grain dissolved in water, to be worked within the hour. Agathe drew a jugful. It was not good brine, and not fresh, and Hester was not licensed, and there was no Reader in the building to say so. She knelt by the cracked foot of the casing, poured a thin line of brine along the crack, and went into the hold.

To recast you had to find the hand that had made the casing and lay your own stillness along it, as a mason lays a new course of brick on an old wall. Bett Linnow's casing had been made twenty years ago by some Cold House brinewright long dead. Its hand was coarse and quick, a charity hand. Hester found it with her eyes shut and her palms flat on the crazed white salt, the warmth coming up through them. She laid herself along it, and held, and felt the fresh brine begin to take.

Then she felt it go.

It went east. As soon as the new stillness was in the crack it was moving, not down but sideways, along the brine-thread, into the drain, under the floor, away. She held harder. It went faster. She was pouring into a sieve. Under her hands the crack widened with a sound like a fingernail drawn across slate.

"No," Hester said aloud. "No, no, come on."

"My dear." Agathe's hand was on her shoulder, harder than Hester had known it could be. "Leave it now. You'll lag."

She left it. She sat back on her heels with her hands burning cold. The crack was longer than it had been. The white was spreading up the casing toward Bett Linnow's hands.

"I'm sorry," she said to Ruth Linnow. "It's being drawn. Something's pulling it out faster than I can put it in."

The old woman nodded slowly, as if this were what she had expected.

"Then sit with me," she said. "Please. Until."

* * *

Bett Linnow had been a laundress at the Glassmarket. Her mother told it while they waited, in a low steady voice, to the casing and not to Hester, as if Bett could hear.

On the twelfth of Rime in 883 a brewer's dray had come down Glass Street too fast on the ice, and its near-side wheel had gone over Bett where she had slipped at the kerb with her basket. She had been thirty-one, with a husband who drank and a boy of four. The dispensary physician said she was bleeding inside and would not see the morning, and the Cold House had a bed. Ruth had signed. Bett's husband had been at the Anchor and could not be found until the next day. By then it was done.

"He's dead now," Ruth said. "Ten years. The boy's twenty-four, a cooper in Harl. He comes on her birthday. He doesn't remember her, only from me telling." She looked at the casing. "I've paid twenty years. I sold the house in Glass Street to pay. I live in one room over the chandler's. I told myself, Ruth, they'll find a way. They'll mend the inside of a person, and then I'll take her home." She smiled. It was a terrible thing to see. "I'm seventy-nine. I thought I'd see it."

Hester said nothing. There was nothing in her mouth to say.

"Do you think she knows?" Ruth said. "That it's been twenty years?"

"No. It's no time at all, for her. It's the same moment."

"Then she'll not know me."

The casing cracked.

It was not loud. It was a sound like river ice going in Thaw, a deep flat crack that Hester felt in her teeth. The white crazing ran over the whole casing at once, top to bottom. Then the warmth came off the plinth in a wave, the arrears of twenty years, a dry hard heat like opening an oven. The lantern on its hook swung. The casing fell in.

It did not shatter. It slumped, as a snow-figure slumps in sun, all at once. The salt lost its hold and became hot brine, pouring off the plinth on every side and steaming on the cold floor. Inside it, Bett Linnow moved.

She drew in breath, a great dragging gasp, the second half of a breath begun twenty years before in the gutter of Glass Street. And she screamed.

It was the scream she had been screaming when they kept her. Hester understood that a moment after she heard it. It was not a new scream; it was the rest of an old one. It went up and broke. Bett's body arched on the stone. Her hands came away from her mouth dark and wet and there was blood on the plinth, a great deal of it, running into the hot brine and turning it pink.

"The wheel," Bett Linnow said. "Mam. The *wheel*—"

"I'm here," said Ruth. "I'm here, my lamb."

Bett's eyes opened. They went round the ward, the high windows, the lantern, the rows of glittering casings, and understood none of it. Then they found the small old woman in the black bonnet bending over her, with a face like a dried apple. Hester saw them look.

"Mam?" said Bett Linnow, uncertainly. Not as if she knew. As if she were asking.

"Yes," said Ruth. "Yes, my lamb. It's me."

Bett frowned. It was a young woman's frown, puzzled, a little cross. She opened her mouth to say something else. Then her eyes went past her mother's face to the window, and stopped there, and the frown went out of them, and so did everything else.

It took perhaps three minutes from the crack. Hester counted afterward. Three minutes, for twenty years.

* * *

Agathe did what had to be done. She had done it before. Hester helped her because her hands needed something to do. They washed Bett Linnow in the warm pink brine, and closed her eyes, and laid her straight under a grey Cold House sheet. Ruth sat on her stool and held her daughter's hand through the sheet and did not cry. When the porters came with the long basket she walked beside it to the door without looking back. At the foot of the six steps she turned and said to Hester, in a perfectly clear voice, "Thank you for sitting with me." Then she went up, a step at a time, holding the rail.

Hester stood in the aisle. After a while she went to the fourth row, and under the third window, in the dark, found casing two hundred and eleven by memory.

Agathe hung the lantern over Tib's plinth without being asked and stood back.

The beads were still there, more of them than in Russet, and fatter, running in their lines toward the eastern foot and joining into runnels. At the foot itself, where the casing curved down over his heels and his rough boy's boots, the salt had gone thin. A month ago it had been a full two inches of clear grain. Now, at the very heel, it was perhaps half that. She could see the leather of his boot more sharply than she had ever seen anything of him through the salt. She could see a thread hanging loose from the stitching, a thread their mother would have snipped.

It was still clear. It was not crazed. It was not white.

"How long?" Hester said.

Agathe looked at the heel for some time. She was seventy and had walked these rows twice a day since before the Great Bloom, and Hester had never heard her say anything she did not believe.

"Weeks, my dear," Agathe said. "Not months." She hesitated. "I'll write. Every day, if you want."

"Every day."

Tib lay with his head turned a little to the window, his eyes half open, the pink of the bloom high on his cheeks. Hester looked at the pink, as she always did.

"Do you know what it is?" she said. "The bloom. Where it comes from."

"Nobody knows that." Agathe drew her shawl round her. "It's an old sickness. Older than the Ebb, they say. The old ballads have it. The sea-cough, they call it there. *She coughed the sea-cough and she went down to the water.* My grandmother used to sing that one." She looked at Tib. "It was in the world before the salt was. That's what the Order says. It came with the sea and it stayed when the sea went, like the tide-marks on the harbour wall."

"The sea-cough," Hester said.

"It's only a song, my dear."

* * *

She did not go to her father that night, or the next. She went back over the Causeway and did a Choir shift with her hands on the brass, knowing what ran in it, and the taste of hot iron still at the back of her throat. Agathe's first letter came on the seventeenth: *No change. A.L.* On the eighteenth Hester went down to the harbour and found her father at the Drowned Man.

It was the tavern he drank in when he did not want to be found in Lowside. It had a bowed ceiling and a fire that smoked, and over the bar the bowsprit of a real sea-ship, black with age, carved with a woman's face worn to a smooth knot. Aldous sat under it on a bench by the wall with a pot of Harl cider between his burned hands. He looked up when she came in. He saw her face and put the pot down.

She sat across from him and told him about Bett Linnow, plainly, in order. The crack, and the heat, and the scream, and *Mam?*, and the three minutes. Then she told him about Tib's heel.

He listened without moving. When she had finished he drank the whole pot, slowly, and put it down.

"Weeks," he said.

"Agathe says."

"Agathe's right, generally." He turned the empty pot on the table. "I'll go up tomorrow."

She did not understand him at first. Then she did.

"No," she said.

"I'll go up tomorrow and I'll sign the paper. Release. I'm his father, Hes. I'm next of kin before you, in law.
