I let you sign the first one because I was drunk and you were twenty-two and I hadn't the stomach. I've the stomach now." His eyes were small and red and steady. "I'll not have him come back like that lass. Screaming. In the dark. With nobody he knows."

"He'll come back with me."

"He'll come back with a woman of thirty-three he's never seen. He'll come back in the last hour of the bloom with his lungs full and he'll die in three minutes, *like that lass*, and that'll be the last thing he sees. If they do it now, quiet, with a physician and a draught—"

"They can't do it quiet. Nobody can. The arrears—"

"Then they'll do it loud. But they'll do it *for him*." His voice had come up. People at the bar were looking round. He did not lower it. "Not because a crack ran up the side. Not because you couldn't make the fee. Because it's time, and it's been time for eleven years, and somebody in this family's got to say so."

"He's *alive*, Da."

"He's kept."

"He's alive in law. His casing's clear. If they find a cure—"

"Who's they?" Aldous put both his burned hands flat on the table. "Who's *they*, Hes? You've been at the Halcyon two months. Have they a cure? Have they so much as looked for one? Or are they too busy keeping their lamps lit?"

She had no answer to that. She did not try to find one.

"I'll not let you," she said.

"It's my name on the form."

"I'll stop you."

"How?"

She did not know. She sat across from him in the smoke with her hands clenched in her lap and could think of nothing. Not law, not money, not a single argument that would stand up in the Drowned Man under the black bowsprit with its worn-away face. All she had was *no*, and she said it again. Her father looked at her across the table for a long time. Then he said the thing.

He said it quietly. He was not a cruel man and he did not say it cruelly. He said it as if he had been carrying it a long way and had finally set it down.

"You didn't keep him for him," Aldous said. "You kept him for you."

She stood up.

She did not remember deciding to. She was simply standing, and her bench was over on the floor behind her, and the whole Drowned Man had gone quiet. She could not see her father's face clearly, and did not know why until she understood that her eyes were full. She felt the tears come over her lids and run down, and felt them, as they ran, go stiff and gritty on her cheeks. *Salt.* Eight weeks in the Choir. It was starting already.

"Hes," her father said, differently.

She went out.

* * *

She did not remember the Causeway. She remembered the brass Line in the Gatehouse floor, and stepping over it in the dark, and the Stair, and the door of R-41, and Teo looking up from his bed in his Tider shawl with a book in his lap and the cracked kept-light jar glowing on the sill.

He took one look at her and put the book down.

She sat on her own bed. She told him about Bett Linnow, and Tib's heel, and the Drowned Man, and what her father had said. Teo listened, as he always listened, without moving and without helping. When she stopped he said nothing for a while. The jar on the sill made its faint summer sound, a hum like bees, the ghost of a July noon.

"Was he right?" Teo said.

"I don't know."

"Yes, you do."

She looked at him. He was not being unkind. He had the face he had in Fane's classes when he heard something under the Roar and had decided, this time, to say so.

"You told me about the night he was kept," he said. "In Russet, the night we read Aske's sheet. The fever, and your father, and the desk. You told it well. But there was a place in the middle where you went round something. I could hear it. Like a step missing on a stair."

The jar hummed. Out on the White the tiles ticked in the cold.

"He said *let me sleep*," Hester said. She heard her voice from a long way off, flat and exact. "He opened his eyes and he looked at me and he knew me. He was quite clear. He said, *Let me sleep, Hes. Just let me sleep.* And he said, *I'm so tired.* And he said, *Don't make me do it any more.*" She stopped. "He meant breathing. He'd been fighting to breathe for three weeks. He didn't mean sleep. He meant let him die. He knew it. I knew it when he said it. I knew it all the way down Coldwater Street."

Teo did not say anything.

"And I went to the desk," Hester said, "and I signed. Because I was twenty-two and I'd buried Mam and I wasn't going to bury him. Because it was there and it was six sols a month and I could keep him. So I did. And for eleven years I've told everyone, I've told myself, that keeping's sleep. That I did what he asked." The salt was stiff on her cheeks. She did not touch it. "He asked me to let him go and I kept him. That's what I did. That's all I've ever done."

She had not said it aloud before. Not to Agathe. Not to Daisy. Not to her father, who had heard it from the stair and known. Not even to herself, alone in the room in Tanner's Row with the landships on the walls. Saying it now was like returning a keeping. Something came out of her with a crack and a heat, and left a space behind it that had been full for eleven years.

Teo was quiet for a long time.

He did not say *it's all right*. He did not say *you did your best*. She had known he would not, and she had told him for that reason. He had sat nine years in the confessional at Tidewater Abbey and he did not give absolution lightly; she suspected he did not believe he had any to give. He got up and came and sat beside her on her bed, close, his shoulder against hers. The cold of him came through his shawl. He was always cold, like all of them.

"Then you've been keeping two people, Hes," he said.

It was the first time he had called her that. She heard him choose it.

"Him," Teo said. "And the girl who signed. Eleven years. Both of them. Exactly as they were." He did not look at her. He looked at the jar on the sill. "That's a great deal of stillness for one person to hold."

She did not answer. After a while she put her head down on his shoulder, on the rough grey wool of the Tider shawl, and he let her, and neither of them said anything else. The jar hummed out its old July. Outside on the White the cold came down and the tiles ticked, ticked, ticked.

* * *

She did not sleep. In the small hours she got up and dressed and went out, and Teo did not wake.

She went down the Stair, past the Second Fathom and Ama Tessaly's name in her own hand, past the Fourth, where the east gallery was boarded off with fresh planks and a Proctor's notice and smelled, still, faintly green. Down to the Fifth.

Every stay down here was fed from the Cold House, and every one of them was Orsolya's. Hester could go over Gage's head to the Assize, as Sorle had said. If they believed her, they would cut the line, and the Cold House would keep its own again. And the stays would starve. Nine days ago she had watched one quickening come through a wall with sixty of them holding and the Cold House pouring in behind. Take the Cold House away and the Fourth was the whole Halcyon: three thousand people, and whatever the salt had stopped in the middle of. To save her brother she had to name Orsolya, and naming Orsolya would drown the house. She could not find the third thing. She went on down looking for it.

Her Choir pass took her through the grille at the landing. The Fathom lamps were turned low and blue, and somewhere far off a skeleton watch of four choristers was holding the night stays in a slow murmur. She kept away from them. She went along the inner gallery to the Choir store: a long low room cut into the salt, with shelves of oiled stay-lines and racks of brass fittings and barrels of Middling for tuning. And behind a cage of iron wire at the back, the residue.

She had seen it on every shift and never looked at it. The warm wet Hush-grade crust the Choir scraped off the stays after a quickening was held, packed into sealed boxes to be carried up the lift. A dozen of them stood stacked inside the cage, numbered in chalk, waiting for a clerk with ink on his cuffs. The cage was padlocked. The wire was old. On one box near the front the lid had been knocked askew in the stacking, and through the gap, in the blue lamplight, she could see grain: white, faintly blue, sharp-cornered. She could feel the warmth of it through the wire.

If she could not stop the draw, she could outrun it. Hush grain kept anything clear, almost at once. A handful in fresh brine, laid along the heel of casing two hundred and eleven, would close that thinning in a single hold, better than any recast. Nobody counted residue by the handful. Nobody would know.

She put her fingers through the wire.

The gap between the box and the cage was a hand's width. She could reach the grain. It was warm, a warm-stone warmth, and the taste of it came up her arm into her mouth before it ever reached her tongue, the vast bright startled weight of it. *Quick.* Her fingers closed on a little of it. Enough, perhaps. She stood in the cold blue dark with her arm through the cage to the wrist and her fist full of felony salt.

*Kept beyond return.*

The Physic's voice, in Cell 3-17, over Aske's reaching arm. *You'd never get the arrears out of Hush grain without killing him.*

She would mend his heel, and she would be laying along the foot of her brother a stillness no one alive knew how to give back. She would never be able to let him go. Not for a cure. Not for her father. Not if Tib himself sat up in his casing and asked her. She would have made sure of it.

And some part of her—she felt it quite clearly, standing in the cold with her fist in the cage—*wanted that*. It wanted it more than it had ever wanted anything. To have him kept so well that no one could ever take him, not the draw, not the arrears, not her father with his paper, not the boy himself.

*You didn't keep him for him.*

She opened her hand.

The grain fell back into the box with a small dry hiss, like sand. She drew her arm out of the cage. Then she stood in the Choir store with her hands at her sides and found that she was shaking from head to foot, as she had shaken in the ice of the east gallery. Not from cold. From the lag of something she had nearly done.

The four night choristers murmured on somewhere in the blue dark, holding the line.

She went up as far as the Rookery well-room and no further. The pump-handle was furred with frost. She worked it until the water came, and held her hands under it, and washed the salt-dust off her fingers, front and back and between, and under the nails, slowly, until there was nothing left on them at all.


# Chapter 19: The Notebook

> The archive is a keeping like any other, and like any other it is owed.
>
> — *Wynn Callow, Under-Archivist, the only sentence he is known to have published (*Proceedings of the Brinestacks*, 851 Y.E.)*

Agathe wrote every day, as she had promised. Most of the letters were a line long. *No change. A.L.* The one dated the nineteenth of Cinder was longer, and Hester read it standing at the post-tray in the Gatehouse with her back to the room.

*My dear. Your father came to the desk this afternoon. He asked Ennis for the form and he gave it him. He sat with the pen in his hand for as long as it takes to say the Ebb-prayer twice through. Then he put the pen down and went out. He did not sign. He did not come down to the ward. I thought you would want to know it from me. Tib is as he was. A.L.*

She folded it along its creases and put it in her pocket with the others. She did not go to the Pans. She thought of her father's burned hands round a pen, holding it the way he held a rake, and could not think about it for long.

* * *

Wynn Callow had been sitting at his desk in the Brinestacks for longer than most of the Readers had been alive.

He sat at the foot of the Second Fathom's main stair, behind a desk of black oak worn pale where his forearms rested: a small man in a grey gown so old the wool had gone the colour of ash, his white hair standing out a little from his skull like a dandelion's. His hands lay on the desk one over the other and did not move. He had the Settle so deep in him that he breathed perhaps four times a minute, and an aspirant could stand at the desk asking for a catalogue number for a full minute before deciding there was nobody there. Hester had decided exactly that on her first day. Teo said everyone did.

*Corvin sat there too.* He had said it to them in Russet as they were leaving, out of nowhere, and then, *There. On the first of the month.* The only words she had ever heard him speak. She had taken them for an old man's remark about a dead colleague. They came back to her the night after Bett Linnow died, lying awake in R-41. *Sat there.* Not *read there*. Sat, as you sit at someone's elbow when you want something from them and know it will take time.

She wanted proof. She had had a name in her since Fallow, since a candle flame in the Warden's Gallery, and she had said *Dunmore* aloud to Teo for a month while she thought it. A name was not proof. Aske had been taking his proofs somewhere.

So on the twenty-fifth of Cinder, at the fifth bell, when she should have been in the Black Bench taking down Sorle's lecture on the decay curves of kept light, she took a stool from the reading room, set it at the end of Callow's desk, and sat down.

He did not look at her. She had not expected him to.

"Under-Archivist," she said. "I'm Hester Vail. Reader Aske's privilege. Reader Sorle's Bench, now." She waited. "You said Corvin sat here too."

Nothing. Two second-years went up the stair behind her arguing about the Assay. The lamp on Callow's desk was an old kept-light lamp, its glass yellowed, giving off the faint unchanging warmth of a summer afternoon that had been over for decades.

She folded her hands in her lap and let her breathing slow, not into the hold, only toward it.

After a time Callow's eyes moved. They came round from the middle distance to the corner of the desk, where her hands were, and rested there.

The sixth bell rang somewhere above. Faint through the salt she heard the Long Table being laid for the Lantern Hour. Her left foot went to sleep. She did not move it.

"He brought," said Callow.

His voice was dry and soft and astonishingly clear, like paper being unfolded. Then it stopped. She counted, and got to forty.

"Something back."

"Back where?"

Fifty. Sixty. Somebody laughed in the reading room and was shushed.

"Where it was not," said Callow, "to be looked for."

"Where's that?"

He did not answer for so long that she thought he had gone. Then, with a deliberation she would not have believed of a living man, Wynn Callow stood up.

It took him nearly a quarter of an hour. Callow did not so much stand as unfold, a joint at a time, each movement starting so slowly that she only knew it had happened because his shoulder was higher than it had been. She did not help him. It would have been like helping a glacier. Then he drew a key on a black string out of the breast of his gown, a long brass key with a halcyon on its bow, and held it in his palm.

"Where the founders," he said, "sleep."

* * *

The Founders' Gallery was at the far end of the Second Fathom, through a door of lattice-grown salt with a brass grille in it and a sign: RESTRICTED. BY LEAVE OF THE WARDEN OF THE STACKS. It took Callow twenty minutes to walk there. Aspirants coming the other way stopped and stared, and stopped again at the corners to look back.

The key turned with a sound like a cough.

Inside was cold and dry and dark. Hester found a kept-light lantern on a hook by the door and dissolved its cake, and a spring morning came up yellow-white around them and showed her a long vaulted room lined to the roof with drawers: thousands of them, small and shallow, in black wood, each with a label in faded copperplate.

*230 Y.E. — Fire-salvage — Founders' Tablets — I.*

On and on along the wall, into the dark, drawers of salt tablets burnt black and slumped like candle-wax.

Callow had already started down the gallery, trailing his fingertips along the drawer-fronts. At XIV he stopped. Then his hand came up, a fraction at a time, toward the brass pull.

"I'll do it," she said.

He took his hand away.

The drawer was full of char, like the others. She pulled harder and it came out of its runners altogether, heavier than she expected, and she set it on the floor. Behind it, where the back of the drawer should have met the back of the cabinet, there was a gap of perhaps four inches. Wedged upright in the gap was a bundle in brown oilcloth tied with tarred string.

It was the size and weight of three thick books. Something small and hard shifted inside it. She sat back on her heels and untied the string.

Two marbled stationer's notebooks of the kind the Glassmarket sold for four flecks, their pages swollen from use; and the black leather one from his table, with a stub of torn stitching near the back where a leaf was gone. Ink showed through in black and blue in a small fast sloping hand. Tucked between them was a horn case no bigger than a thumb. She knew it before she opened it. Inside, on green baize, lay a tasting spoon of fine white metal, finer than any on her old rack at the Customs House.

*May I?*

She closed the case, and had to sit a while.

When she looked up, Callow was watching her. Not the middle distance: her. It might have been grief in his pale eyes, or satisfaction, held still so long they had become the same thing.

"He said," Callow said, "if anyone came."

She waited.

"Who sat."

* * *

Teo did not go to bed that night. Neither did she.

They sat in R-41 with the door wedged shut and the candle on the floor between them and the three notebooks open on Teo's blanket. Teo had his Tider shawl round his shoulders, a pencil behind his ear and another in his hand, and a slate on his knee. He had eaten, by her count, four slices of saltcake since the tenth bell without noticing any of them.

"It's not a cipher," he had said at the start. "Don't hope for anything dramatic. It's cutters' shorthand. The Abbey used it for the tablet copies. Nobody uses it any more except old copyists and, apparently, Reader Aske, who uses it badly, with personal abbreviations, in two colours, for reasons known only to God." He had squinted. "Blue is grain. Black is people, I think. Give me an hour."

It had taken him six.

He read aloud as he went, in pieces, and sometimes stopped for twenty minutes over a single line and swore at it softly in the vocabulary of a man who had spent nine years in a religious house. Hester sat against the wall with her knees up and refilled his water.

The first marbled book began in Thaw of 902 and was grain, in blue: a crate of curing salt from a Ferrers yard that tasted of the Fifth. Another. *Warm. Bead. Spat in the basin.* By midsummer he had stopped being surprised.

The second was people. Black ink. It began with Dunmore.

*E.D. — Edwin Dunmore, clerk to the Warden's office. Handles the residue contracts. Residue = quickened grain cut out of the stays when the stays are re-laid. Should be dissolved in the Fifth and the brine pumped to the sinks. Is not. Is boxed. Boxes go up the box-lift, Fifth to Third, at the tenth bell, three nights a week, carried by aspirants paid two sols a run.*

Teo looked up. "That's Saskia."

"I know."

"He doesn't name her. Just *aspirants*." He read on. *Ferrers & Lisk freighter. East to Quell, a Ferrers warehouse, relabelled Shelf, then back west and sold as curing grain to the Hask kept-fish trade. Hask buyers complain their fish spit when the keep is broken. Nobody listens to Hask buyers.* And under that, in a different pen: *W.L.F. per E.D. on the ladings. Warden of the Lower Fathoms, per Dunmore. Bold as brass. The office has signed residue contracts for two hundred years. Nobody reads them.*

"You knew that," Teo said.

"I knew that." She had known it since Lucan came back from the Upper Ward with the contract in his coat. She had given Teo a small, neat, clerkish theory to go with it, with Dunmore at the centre: a bland man who sold what he was told to destroy, and a Warden who signed what her clerk put in front of her. She had not believed a word of it herself.

Teo opened the black book. The ink was blue again.

*1 Russet. Stacks: Founders' Cistern plans, for the east trunk survey. Why from the city?*

*1 Russet. Miss Vail. Taste and weight. Graded my twelve blind. Named No. 9 as twice-kept. Heard a cry in the Wren grain, did not know it. I did not tell her. God forgive me, I want to see her face when she hears it in the Stacks.*

Teo stopped and looked at her over the candle. She looked at the window.

"Go on," she said.

*5 Russet. Cold House, Coldwater St. Director at his sister's; Mother Agathe walked me down the rows. Miss V.'s brother in 211. 14 weeping. All east. Taste: quick. Tasted the run-off myself. Quick, and under it something stiller than I have ever had in my mouth. Old keepings get still. These are being bled.*

*Harl St Brinery — 0. Varenne Private — 0. Charity only. Of course charity only. Nobody audits charity.*

*A draw? Still runs to quick. It wants a brine contact. A line. Cold House stands on the old reservoir—Cistern?? The founders' lines. Bricked in 400. Bricked by whom?*

"That's the sheet," Hester said. "From his blotter." She put her thumb to the torn stitching. "That's the leaf. He tore it out for me and took the book away."

Teo read ahead with his lips moving. "Hester. He went into the Cistern."

*12 Russet. Under the Cold House by the cellar. Agathe's key. The old main line is open. Brass sleeves, new, Lattice marks. Live brine in the sleeve. Still as death. Runs east.*

*13 Russet. Walked the plans. The main line runs under the White, two miles, and comes up in the Fifth Fathom at the Old Tap. Founders' brass. I have passed it a hundred times. The east gallery stay-lines are fed from it. The stays have been holding beautifully all summer, and I am a fool.*

*14 Russet. Measured. The east stays draw 30 parts in 100 of their stillness from the Old Tap. Without it they would have failed in Seedmonth. Without it the Hush would be in the Fifth by now.*

Teo put down the pencil.

"Thirty parts in a hundred," he said. "From the Cold House."

Hester did not answer. She had tasted it at the Silent Supper, with the brine splashing her mouth and the sea roaring in the walls: the cold, old stillness of the line under Coldwater Street, coming up the stay-line from the east. She had known since that night that the stays were fed by the sleepers. Here it was in a Reader's hand, measured. Tib had been in the line that night, holding the east gallery up with her.

"Is there more?" she said.

"The last page."

"Read it."

Teo read it slowly, in the voice he used for scripture when he did not want to mock it, and she listened with her eyes shut.

*15 Russet. Told Miss V. tomorrow at ten. Showed her the beating bead. She went white. She has the tongue for this, God help her, and she has a brother in the line.*

*I know whose office this is. I have known since the 13th and pretended not to, because I have known her near thirty years and she taught me to hold in the Choir when I was nineteen and too frightened to breathe. She is the best of us. I think she has done this because she is the best of us.*

*She is right that it will flood. She is wrong that it must be held. The pressure wants a road, not a lid. Vent? Let a little of it go somewhere it can do no harm, a little at a time. Need Sorle's arrears for the shaft—he'll crow for a decade, let him. I'll ask O. tomorrow, before I tell Gage. I owe her that.*

*If anything—tell Hester to taste it.*

The candle had burned to a stub. The moon had gone behind the Lantern, and its mirrors made a thin black bar across the White.

"She," Teo said quietly.

"I heard."

"Dunmore's not a she."

"No."

He closed the notebook and sat with his hand flat on its black cover, and looked at her, and saw that she was not surprised.

"How long?" he said.

"Since Fallow. She kept a candle for me in the Gallery and showed me her hand in it." She heard how flat it came out. "I wanted to be wrong. I'm on her books. She put a blanket on me after my first stay. I wanted it to be Dunmore so badly I said it out loud to you every night."

He did not say the name either. He was too kind to, and she loved him for it and hated him for it, both at once.

"*Ask O.*," he said instead. "And you let me go on about the Order."

"Yes." She stood up. Her knees were stiff and she was thirsty, the Halcyon thirst, as though she had been holding. "He said taste it. I haven't. I've had a candle and a notebook. A notebook says *she*." She took her gown from its peg. "If I'm going to say it, I'll say it off the salt. Off my tongue."

"It's the middle of the night."

"Then nobody will be in the Quiet Rooms."

He looked at her over the guttering candle, crumbs on his chin.

"I'll be here," he said.

* * *

The Fourth Fathom in the small hours was dark and cold, and there was no sound but the hum, the long low pressure from below that she had felt in her teeth on her first day and long since stopped noticing. She noticed it now.

The Quiet Rooms were not locked. Nobody locked them. Who would steal a keeping?

Aske's casing stood where the porters had set it in Russet, between a girl kept in the act of laughing and a Reader in a gown three hundred years out of fashion. Hester set the lantern at the plinth's foot and looked up at him.

He had not changed. That was what keeping meant, and still it went through her. His arm was out, the ink-stained fingers spread. His mouth was open on whatever he had been about to say. Through the casing, clear as clean water, she could see the frayed cuff of his shirt, and a fleck of salt-mud on his gown at the knee.

The casing was smooth as glass at its outer face and frosted against his skin, set from the outside in. Poured, by someone standing in front of him. Sorle had said it in Fallow: *a child could see that casing was poured*.

She knelt, took off her right glove as she had in the Cold House on the first of Russet, and put the tip of her smallest finger to the casing where it met the plinth. She touched the finger to her tongue.

The weight came down. Hush grain: the door closing, the white, the vast pressing on the far side of her face. She went into the hold. It came easily now; three months ago it had been something she found by accident on a customs stool, and now she could put it on like a coat. The taste opened, and she read.

She did not try to read Aske. She did not want to hear what he had been saying when the brine went over him. She went past the moment and into the hand of whoever had set it. She knew what she was looking for. She had known since Fallow. She had come down here to be told she was wrong.

The grain turned. Under the weight and the white she felt it as a slow corkscrew of set salt, layer winding on layer, every one turning the same way: from the right hand toward the left, widdershins, against the sun. At the end of each turn there was a small hard counter-flick, like the snap of a wrist.

*Every worker leaves a hand in the grain.* Orsolya in the Warden's Gallery in Fallow, a tallow candle between them and a dish of live brine, her wet left fingers flicking at the wick, the wrist snapping back. The flame going still, a little yellow tongue in a bead of clear salt. *Mine's a corkscrew. My mam said I came out twisting.* Both of them laughing, and something at the back of Hester's mind giving a small hard tug, like a fish on a line. She had felt it then. She had gone up the Stair with it and told no one.

It was the same hand. Not like it. The same.

She came out of the hold too fast, with a sound in her throat.

She was on her knees on the Fourth Fathom floor, her bare hand flat on the plinth. Above her Aske reached out of the salt toward nothing.

She had carried the rest for weeks, figure by figure, and had told herself each one could be carried another way. She went down them now with the hand under her tongue, and none of them could.

The Causeway at dawn on the sixth of Russet, and a woman in iron-grey coming up behind her from the city, bone-cold of hand, with salt-mud dried to crusts on her boots. There was no salt-mud on the Causeway. There was salt-mud at the end of Coldwater Street, by the old sea-wall, where the brine seeped up between the cobbles from the reservoir underneath.

The Warden's Gallery, and tea. *The Stays have been kind this season. Kinder than they've any right to be.* Thirty parts in a hundred.

The skiff on the White in Fallow, Orsolya easy at the rail. *How's your brother? Still at the Cold House?* Hester had never told her which brinery. Orsolya's da, nineteen years in the Cold House, explained the street. It had never explained how she knew Tib was in it.

The sixteenth of Russet, a little before the half, the cold corridor outside Cell 3-17. Iron wheels on the turn below; Hester at the rail; a small blue lantern and a trolley under canvas, and two or three people in grey wool, hauling, and then, when she turned back to the door, stopping. Waiting, she saw now. Waiting for her to go.

*Edwin handles the residue contracts; it's all above board, Conclave-approved.*

*Oh, Corvin. Oh, you daft man.*

Hester knelt by the plinth with her hand on the cold salt, and the hum came up through the floor into her knees, and she did not get up for a long time.

* * *

She went back to Callow on the twenty-eighth.

She had not slept much in between. She had done a Choir shift in the east gallery and felt the cold old stillness come up the line from the east, and had held anyway, because the stay needed holding and there were twenty people in the line whose lives depended on it. Orsolya had walked the line in her iron-grey, a hand on a shoulder here, a word there. *Steady, chick. That's it.* She had put her hand on Hester's shoulder. Hester had not moved. When Orsolya walked on she had found that she was shaking, and had told the chorister beside her it was lag.

She had gone to Crisp's release hour at the Pans as well, because it was on the board and missing it would be noticed. Crisp walked the rim with her hands behind her back. "You are strangling it, Vail. Again." Hester kept a drop and closed on it, and closed on the next. On the third the drop went soft along one edge, a hair's breadth of water, before her hand clamped. Crisp stopped behind her. "Better," she said, and walked on, which from Crisp was a medal.

There was one thing left. He had said it to her in Russet, and she had heard it as an old man's kindness: *The dead bloom. A month or so. Whatever the grade.* She had been carrying it for two days like a stone in her shoe, not looking at it, because looking at it would mean hoping.

She sat on the stool at the end of Callow's desk. It was the fifth bell again.

"Under-Archivist," she said. "I need to know something about Hush grain."

She waited.

"The living keep clear and the dead keep grey. The Chief Proctor told me, the morning they found Reader Aske, that Hush grain's different. That it keeps everything clear, so the clear casing meant nothing. And I took his word, because I'd never had Hush grain in my mouth. You told me otherwise, and I didn't listen. Is it true?"

She counted to sixty, and past it. She watched his face, which did not move, and his hands, which did not move.

At a hundred and twenty his eyes came round to the corner of the desk.

"Hush grain," said Callow, "keeps clear."

Her heart went down like a dropped weight.

He breathed. One breath. It took a long time.

"Quickly."

She waited.

"Then it blooms," said Callow, "like any other."

The lamp gave off its old summer warmth.

"How long?"

Another breath.

"A month."

She did not move. She did not trust anything she might do if she did.

"He's been there since the sixteenth of Russet," she said. "Two months and more. I was in the Quiet Rooms three nights ago with a lantern. His casing is as clear as the day it was poured."

Callow said nothing. Then, so slowly that she was not sure until it was done, one of his hands turned over on the oak, palm up, and lay open there in the lamplight.

She looked at it, and understood that it was not helplessness. It was a release, as Crisp taught it at the Pans to first-years who could not do it, as Orsolya had taught it on the White over a man-shaped casing hanging in the mouth of a sink. *Don't push it out, chick. Open your hand.*

The living keep clear.

* * *

She went down to the Quiet Rooms again that evening, in the Lantern Hour, when the whole Halcyon was at the Long Table under the poured noon light and the Fourth Fathom was empty.

She took no lantern. There was light enough from the Well, where it passed through the Fourth on its way down to nowhere: gold and borrowed, a summer noon from some year she did not know, falling in a long column through the dark and making the casings along the gallery glitter like ice on a pond.

Aske reached out of the light toward her. His spectacles were full of gold.

She stood with her hands in the sleeves of her gown, against the cold.

"You daft man," she said.

He did not answer. He was not dead. He was between one moment and the next, and had been for ten weeks. In the moment he was in, he had his arm out to stop a woman he had known for thirty years from doing what she was doing. And she had a dish of live brine in her left hand, and had turned her wrist back on itself, and poured.

"I tasted it," Hester told him. "Like you said."

It was not so different, she found, from the fourth row of the Cold House. You stood beside a casing and told the person in it what you knew. You did not expect anything back. She had not said the name aloud to anyone, not even to Teo.

"It's Orsolya," she said.

The words sat in the cold air. The Well-light poured past. Far above, faint through two fathoms of salt, the Long Table was laughing and scraping its benches, six hundred people at their supper.

"It's Orsolya," Hester said again, as though she were writing it in a ledger in ink, so that it would be there in the morning. "And you're alive."


# Chapter 20: The Warden

> I have buried eleven of mine. I'll not bury more.
>
> — *Orsolya Thane, Warden of the Lower Fathoms, address to the Stay Choir on the Silent Supper, 899 Y.E.*

She told nobody.

It did not feel like a decision. It felt like reasoning, and she reasoned it through in the cold of R-41 with Teo asleep across the cell and the three notebooks wrapped in their oilcloth under her mattress. But it had been settled before she began, somewhere at the back of her, in the place where she had settled the night at the Cold House desk eleven years ago without consulting anyone, least of all herself.

If she told Sorle, Sorle would go to the Conclave with his slates and his thirty-two years of being laughed at, and Vey would thank him for his diligence and move to the price of lamp oil, and the notebooks would be fire-salvage for good.

If she told Lucan, Lucan would go to the Assize, and if by some miracle they were believed, the first thing the Assize would do was order the draw cut. *Without it the Hush would be in the Fifth by now.*

If she told Teo, Teo would come with her.

And under the reasons, which were good reasons, there was a thing she did not look at directly. *I'll ask O. tomorrow, before I tell Gage. I owe her that.* Aske had owed Orsolya thirty years and a Choir bench at nineteen. Hester owed her twelve weeks. A blanket on the Choir floor after her first stay, a voice on the White saying *open your hand*. The only Lowside voice under the Shelf except Hester's own.

Aske had gone to ask, and had been standing in the Quiet Rooms with his arm out ever since.

She knew that. She went anyway.

She wrote two lines on a page of her ledger, folded it, and slid it under Teo's pillow without waking him. *If I'm not back by the first bell, take the notebooks to Sorle. Under my mattress. H.* Then she put on her gown and her gloves and went down.

* * *

It was the second of Rime, the eleventh bell. The Assay was nineteen days off and the Rookery had gone quiet early all week, aspirants in their cells with their slates and their dishes of practice grain. On the Second Fathom the chalk-names went past her in the lantern-light, and among them, in her own seizure hand, AMA TESSALY, CHOIR, 903. She did not stop.

The Fifth Fathom was never asleep. A half-Choir of twenty-two held the east gallery through the night in four-hour watches, and their singing came up the galleries faint and slow, like a congregation heard through a church wall. She felt it in the soles of her feet as she came off the last turn of the Stair. The Warden's Gallery opened off to the left: a long room cut into the salt, with a row of brass gauges along one wall, needles trembling behind glass, each labelled for a stay; a table, a stove, a kettle; shelves of returns bound in iron-grey cloth; a single kept-light lamp.

Dunmore was coming out as she came in, a stack of ledgers under his arm and ink on his cuffs, with the bland, faintly put-upon courtesy of a clerk who has been kept late.

"Miss Vail. The Warden's in."

"Ta."

He went past her toward the lift. She heard the cage rattle and go up. A man who did what he was told and went home at a reasonable hour.

Orsolya was at the table with her back to the door and a shawl round her shoulders, writing.

"Is that you, chick? There's tea in the pot. It's stewed. I'll make fresh."

"Don't."

Orsolya's pen stopped.

She put it down. She sat a moment with her hands flat on the table either side of the page. Then she turned round on her chair, slowly, as Settled people turned, and looked at Hester in the doorway. She looked at her face for a long time, and Hester let her.

"Well," Orsolya said. "Sit down before you fall down."

* * *

Hester did not sit. She stood on the other side of the table, under the lamp, and told it in order, evidence first. Her voice came out steadier than she had any right to.

The *Constant Wren* and *W.L.F. per E.D.* The fourteen casings weeping east. The founders' line under Coldwater Street, sleeved in new Lattice brass, running to the Old Tap. Pell's ten sols a month. Thirty parts in a hundred. *She is right that it will flood. She is wrong that it must be held.*

"And his casing," Hester said. "Poured, not grown. The grain turns widdershins, with a flick at the end of every turn, like a wrist snapping back. I watched you keep a candle flame in Fallow. You showed me." She did not stop. "On the sixth of Russet you came up behind me on the Causeway from the Ambry end with salt-mud on your boots. There's salt-mud at the end of Coldwater Street, where the old reservoir seeps. And on the White you asked me if my brother was still at the Cold House. I never told you which brinery. I never told anyone."

There was nothing more. She had laid it all on the table, and it lay there.

"It's you," she said. "It's been you all along."

The kettle on the stove ticked as it cooled.

Orsolya turned her left hand over, palm up, and looked at the wrist as though she had never seen it before.

"My mam always said I'd get caught by it," she said. "That wrist. Said I'd never pass for anybody's hand but my own." She almost smiled. "Corvin knew it too. That's what he said, at the Tap, before. *I'd know your hand anywhere, Orsolya.* Like it was a compliment."

"Tell me why."

"You know why."

"I want you to say it."

Orsolya looked up at her. In the lamplight her face was older than Hester had seen it: the white hair, the slack skin of the neck, the slow blue at the lips that every Choir worker carried. The eyes were the eyes Hester had liked on the Causeway, quick and grey and Lowside-shrewd. They were not ashamed. They were tired past shame.

"All right," Orsolya said. "But I'll not say it to you standing over me like a Proctor. Sit."

Hester sat.

* * *

Orsolya got up, slowly, and took down a ledger bound in iron-grey cloth and opened it on the table between them, turned so the columns faced Hester. Returns: stays by number down the left, dates across the top, in each square a figure in Orsolya's upright hand.

"That's 896," Orsolya said, putting a cold finger at the top of the page. "Four quickenings in the Fifth. Same as my first year as Warden, same as it had been for as long as anyone kept the returns. We'd stay them, and they'd quieten, and every few years one would break out again and we'd stay it again. Like weeding a garden." She turned the page. "897. Six." Another. "899. Nine." Another. "This summer, fourteen. And bigger. Hotter. Coming up through the Fifth from the Hush like water through a cellar floor, and I've had one break into the Fourth, you were there. Above the stays. That's never happened. Not in nine hundred years of returns."

She turned to the back of the ledger. There was a list of names, eleven of them, with dates, and at the bottom a twelfth, the ink still dark. *Ama Tessaly. 9 Cinder.*

"Them," Orsolya said. "That's what a stay costs, chick. You've held. You lend it and it doesn't all come back. Choir does twenty years if it's lucky; it's not lucky now. With fourteen pockets and twenty-two in a half-Choir, you hold double watches and you Settle double fast. Danny Horrocks was thirty-four. He sat down on the floor of the east gallery in Thaw of 901 and didn't get up. Leni Ash was twenty-nine. She had a baby." Her finger moved down the list without touching it. "I buried them. Me. I went to their mams."

"You could have—"

"I went to the Chancellor." Orsolya's voice did not rise. It went flat and plain. "Seedmonth of 901, with that ledger under my arm. I showed her the figures and the names. I asked for an inquiry. I asked for money for twice the Choir. I asked for the Delvers to be stopped cutting the Shelf pits any deeper, because every fathom they take off the top is a fathom of weight off the Hush. And she sat there, the old woman, still as a stone, and the Deputy did the talking. The Halcyon could not afford to be *seen* to have a problem in the deep. Not with the Exchequer renegotiating the Charter tithe. Not with the Lodge at Quell petitioning the Concord. *Manage it quietly, Warden. You've always managed. We have every confidence.*" She shut the ledger. "No inquiry. No money. No noise."

Hester thought of Aske in Quill Street, turning his cup: *the Deputy Chancellor thanked me for my diligence and moved to the next business.*

"So I managed," Orsolya said.

She sat down again and put her hands round the cold teapot, as if for warmth.

"I went to the Stacks looking for anything the founders had done about pressure in the deep. They built down here. They must have known. And I found the Cistern plans, the old ones, from before the Fire. Water Board had them filed as municipal brine supply." She laughed, a short dry sound. "Nobody pipes brine two miles under the White to fill Lowside's washtubs. It's a stay-line. The biggest ever cut, from the old reservoir under Coldwater Street straight to the Old Tap. There's a halcyon cut on the valve-wheel. I've had my hand on it. They built the Cistern for this. To feed the stays from the city, when the stays ran short." She looked at Hester. "It was always meant."

"Meant to feed them with what?"

"With whatever was stillest. Whatever the city kept. Cellars of kept fish, in their day, I suppose. I don't know what the founders kept." She shrugged, a small heavy movement. "I know what we keep."

* * *

"Tell me about the sleepers," Hester said.

Her voice cracked on *sleepers*, like a pan-skin. She put both hands flat on the table and held them there until it passed.

"There's three hundred and four in that charity ward," Orsolya said. "You've walked the rows. Bloomers, mostly. Girls kept in childbed. Old folk kept with growths nobody's found a cure for in a hundred years. And the Coldbank men from 856, that the Guild paid to keep and stopped paying for in 870." She did not look away. "My da was one. I paid the rest of him myself. Nobody's coming back from that ward. Not cured. Agathe knows it. Pell knows it, God knows. They're kept because somebody couldn't bear the bill of letting them go. And the families pay. Six sols a month, year on year. Ruth Linnow paid for Bett twenty years on a laundress's wage. That's the better part of fifteen hundred sols, for a lass who was never going to wake up and live. And when they can't pay they borrow, and when they can't borrow they sell the chairs. I've seen Lowside rooms with nothing in them but a bed and a Cold House receipt pinned to the wall. So have you."

"So you took them."

"I took what they had." Orsolya leaned forward. "Old keepings are the stillest things in the world. Sixty years in a casing, eighty, and the salt soaks up the life inside it till you could stay a quickening with a fingernail of it. Three hundred of them in a cold room on top of the founders' own line, costing their mams' furniture. And down here I've a Choir dying at thirty and a pocket in the east gallery that'll put the sea up the Stair the night it breaks." She opened her hands. "I didn't kill anybody, Hester. I let them go. Slowly. Usefully. Every one of them that's held up that gallery this summer has kept fifty of my Choir alive. They're holding the line so the others can sleep."

"Bett Linnow died gasping on a plinth," Hester said, "with her mother holding her hand."

"Bett Linnow was dying gasping in 883. That's when she was kept. I didn't do that to her. Somebody else did, by signing a paper. All I did was spend the twenty years she'd been given, that weren't doing her a blind bit of good."

"You didn't ask."

"Ask who?" Orsolya said. "Bett?"

The kettle ticked on the stove.

"I'll tell you about asking." Orsolya's voice had gone quiet and very plain. "Nineteen years I stood by my da on the first of the month. He was kept standing up, the way they found him, under the beam. When his casing wept and the brinewright said it'd not take another coat, I signed. I was thirty. I'd been paying since I was twenty-five, on a chorister's three sols, and I'd have paid till I died, and I signed. They took the beam off him in the bath and he came back with his chest stove in and the third gallery's dust in his mouth, and he looked at me, a woman of thirty with white coming in her hair, and called me by my mam's name. Then he died. It took about as long as it took your Bett." She turned the cold teapot a quarter-turn on the wood. "That's what nineteen years bought him. Not a day. Not an hour he could use. So don't tell me what I'm taking off them. I know to the fleck what I'm taking off them. It's nothing they'll ever have." She looked at Hester. "Nobody asked him, either. He'd have been hard put to answer, under the beam. Did your lad sign his own form, chick?"

Hester could not move. Everything in her was running too fast to be still and too fast to move, the hold turned inside out. Orsolya watched her, not unkindly. She could not know. She could not possibly know. It was only that she was Lowside, and so was Hester, and on Coldwater Street nobody signed their own form.

"No," Hester said at last.

"No. Nobody does." Orsolya sat back. "So don't tell me about asking."

* * *

"Reader Aske," Hester said.

That one landed. Orsolya's face did not change, but her hands came off the teapot and lay in her lap, and for a while she said nothing at all.

"He sent a line down that afternoon," she said. "The sixteenth. *Old Tap, ninth bell, a word.* I knew what it meant, and I went down early. He was there before me, with his hands on the wheel." She swallowed. "He'd found it all, you'll have read it. And he'd made up his mind, Corvin, all at once, like a man jumping off a wall. He was going to shut the Tap, and be back up the Stair for you at the tenth, and go to Gage in the morning."

"And you stopped him."

"If he'd shut that Tap, the east stays would have starved inside the hour, and the gallery with them. Twenty-two of my Choir were on watch. I told him. I begged him. He kept saying *there's another way, Orsolya, there's always another way*, like he was explaining a sum. And the wheel was turning." Her left hand moved in her lap, a small twist back on itself, and stopped. "I had the dish in my hand. I'd brought it down to top the stay. Hush brine, from the line. And I—"

She stopped.

"I kept him," she said. "God help me. I didn't kill him. I kept him. It's the only thing I know how to do."

Hester's throat had closed. She waited for it to open.

"You knew," she said. "When Gage was saying *kept beyond return*. You knew he was alive."

"Of course I knew. What d'you take me for?" It was the first anger she had shown. "Edwin and I brought him up on a cask-trolley. It took us both; a casing weighs. We were coming up the turn below his Cell when you looked over the rail. I saw your face. We stopped, and turned the lantern down, and stood there with him under the canvas till you'd gone." She did not look away from it now. "Then Benedek came round by the Readers' stair at the half and stood in the dark and went back up, and we waited for him too. We had him in a little after the half. We put him in his own Cell, and I put the dish in his basin so Gage would have something to write down, and I went to my bed and didn't sleep. And in the morning I came up and stood there and cried over him, and every tear of it was true." Her mouth worked. "You can't return a Hush keeping fast. The arrears would stop his heart. I'll bring him back myself when the Hush is quiet. Slow, in a warm bath, the way Physic does the old ones. I'll sit with him every hour of it. I kept him till it's over, chick. That's all."

"And when is it over?"

Orsolya did not answer that.

* * *

They sat across the table for a time Hester did not measure. The half-Choir's singing came and went down the gallery. A needle on the wall dipped and steadied.

"What are you going to do?" Orsolya said at last.

"I don't know."

"You'll go to Gage."

"Gage lost the rod I brought him."

"So he did. Vey had a word. Vey doesn't want anybody looking at the deep." She considered Hester with the quick shrewd Lowside look she had given her on the Causeway. "You'll go to Benedek, then. He's waited thirty years to see me in the dock; let him have his day. But you go to Benedek, or Gage, or the Assize, and somebody shuts that Tap, and the east gallery wakes. And then it's my Choir on the floor, and then it's the Fifth, and then it's the Stair, and then it's the Rookery with three hundred aspirants asleep in it. Your Teo. That Quell lass with the little girl." She said it plainly, not as a threat. "That's the bill. You're an assayer. You know how to read one."

She reached across the table. She did not take Hester's hand. She put her own cold hand down on the wood beside it, close, not touching.

"Your lad's line," she said softly. "Two hundred and eleven. Fourth row, under the third window. He's got a pencil in his hand."

Hester went cold to the roots of her hair.

"I go down to the reservoir every week or two to check the sleeves. I come up through the cellar in the small hours, before first bell, when there's nobody but Agathe asleep in her chair. I've walked the rows. I know where they all lie." She paused. "I didn't know he was yours. Not till Corvin put your name on his privilege and I looked you up in the Customs returns and saw *Vail*. I've been sorry for it every day since."

"Don't."

"I can route round him."

The words lay on the table, next to the ledger with the twelve names in it.

"It's just sleeves," Orsolya said. "Brass, and a bit of lattice. Each plinth's got its own drain to the main; I put them in myself, in Thaw. I can close his. I can go down tonight and close it, and his casing'll dry inside the day, and he'll hold. Till spring and past. As long as he's held these eleven years. Nobody need know. Not Pell. Not Agathe. Not your clever friends." She sat back. "There's three hundred and three others in that ward and I'll not pretend I can spare them all. But I can spare one. I'd like to do it for you."

Hester looked at the cold hand on the table. She looked at the twelve names, and the gauges trembling behind their glass, and the doorway to the gallery where twenty-two people were singing slowly in the dark so that the sea would stay where it was.

She did not say yes. She did not say no. She sat with her mouth shut and her hands flat on the wood. She knew that she had not said no, and she knew that Orsolya knew it.

After a while Orsolya nodded, as if that were an answer.

"Go on up," she said. "It's late, and you've the Assay in nineteen days, and you'll want to pass it, whatever else you want." She went to the stove and lifted the kettle and shook it, and set it back. "I'll not stop you. Tell who you like. I'll not lay a finger on you; I've not that in me, not for you. But you think about what it costs, before you tell." She turned round, and looked at Hester as she had on the Choir floor in Russet, with a blanket in her hands. "You're Lowside. You know what it is to choose who drowns."

* * *

Hester did not remember the climb. Teo was asleep on his side with one hand under his pillow, over the folded note. She drew the note out without waking him and put it in the stove. Then she lay down in her gown on top of her blanket and did not close her eyes until the window went grey.

She went to lectures. She sat in the Black Bench while Sorle talked about the decay of kept heat, and wrote down every word and heard none. Twice at the Long Table Teo looked at her across the bench, and twice she looked away.

The letter came at the Lantern Hour, on the post-tray with the Rookery letters: a single sheet folded in three, sealed with a blob of tallow, *Miss H. Vail, Aspirant, the Halcyon* on the outside in Agathe's careful schoolroom hand. Hester broke the tallow with her thumbnail and read it under the poured noon light with the whole Long Table chattering round her.

*My dear,*

*I write because I promised. Something has changed and I think it is for the good. When I went round after noon today your Tib's casing was dry. Not a bead on it. The lines are all gone, and the salt at his feet is as dry as the day he was kept, and I put my hand near it and it is cold and still, as it should be. The others are as they were, I am sorry to say, but his is dry. I do not know why. Mr. Pell does not know. I have told him nothing.*

*Tide willing it will last. Come when you can.*

*A.L.*

She read it twice. She folded it along its creases and put it inside her gown, against her ribs, where her purse used to sit when she had a purse.

*I can go down tonight and close it.*

She had not said yes.

She had not needed to.

* * *

Later, when the Long Table had emptied and the Well had gone dark, she went down the Stair to her own stretch of wall on the Second Fathom and sat on the step under the chalk.

The salt was cold through her gown. Above her head, in her square hand, it said AMA TESSALY, CHOIR, 903, and above that, in older hands, the names of the Settled went up the curve of the Stair into the dark.

Below her the hum came up out of the deep into the step and into her bones. Below that, so faint she could not be sure she was not imagining it, the half-Choir was singing. *Hold the line, hold the line.*

Two miles west, under Coldwater Street, in the fourth row, under the third window, her brother lay in a casing as dry as the day she had signed for it, and held. He was not holding the east gallery up any more. Somebody else's brother was, or somebody's mother, or somebody's girl with a red ribbon in her hair. They were holding it in his place, because a woman who was the best of them had closed a brass sleeve in the night, for Hester, without being asked.

She sat on the Stair with Agathe's letter against her ribs and did not move for a long time.


# Chapter 21: Still Runs to Quick

> Law the Fifth. Still runs to quick. Stillness moves through continuous live brine from the stiller to the quicker, as heat moves through iron from the hot end to the cold. It does not leap a gap. It does not answer a wish.
>
> — *The Halcyon Primer, for the use of aspirants*

She told them in Sorle's study on the fourth of Rime, everything, in order.

There were five of them in a room built for one old man and his books. Sorle had the chair behind the desk. Saskia had the other chair, because she had got to it first and did not apologise. Lucan stood under the slit of salt-glass with his arms folded over ribs that still pained him in the cold. Teo sat on the floor against the bookshelves with a cup of halcyon tea going cold between his feet. Hester stood.

She told them about the office in the Fifth and the spiral and the notebook. She told them about the twelve names, and the Chancellor who had given Orsolya nothing. She told them that Aske was not dead, only waiting, because the woman who had kept him meant to let him out when she was finished. She told them about the offer.

Then she told them the rest.

"Agathe wrote yesterday," she said. "Tib's casing's stopped weeping. The east foot's dry." She looked at the floor between Teo's boots. "I didn't say yes. I didn't say anything. She did it anyway."

Teo set his cup down.

"So that's where I am," Hester said. "My brother's safe because a woman I'm going to see arrested decided to make him safe. There's a dozen others still weeping in that ward, and there'll be more. And if the draw stops, the Hush comes up through the floor and drowns everyone in this building. That's the account." She turned to Lucan and Teo. "You'll be going under the Cold House again. To count the rods."

"On the seventh," Lucan said. "Why?"

"Open his sleeve. Two hundred and eleven, fourth row, under the third window. Put him back on the line with the rest of them."

"Hes," said Teo.

"He's drawn like the others till the cut, or I'm hers." Her voice did not stay as level as she wanted. "I'll not be hers. I'll not have him held up by her and the Corries bled for it."

"Salt and sorrow," said Teo softly.

Saskia was looking at her as she looked at a lantern-jar with a flaw in it, deciding whether it would hold.

"You could have kept your mouth shut," she said. "About the lad. We'd never have known."

"I'd have known."

Saskia held her eyes a moment longer, and nodded once.

* * *

They went at it for two hours. It was the most honest argument Hester had ever been part of. Nobody in it was stupid and nobody was wrong, and the room got hotter and the tea went undrunk.

"The Chancellor," said Lucan. "It's the obvious course, and I'd like it on the record that I proposed it before anyone sneered."

"I sneer in advance," said Sorle. "It saves time. Go on."

"Thane has committed a felony under the Charter and kept a Reader against his will. The Chancellor must act."

"Thane went to the Chancellor," Hester said. "Before any of it. Holm gave her nothing. Told her to manage it quiet."

"So Thane says."

"I believe her."

"You believed her before," said Lucan, not unkindly.

That went in. Hester let it.

"Gage lost the rod," said Teo from the floor. "And Vey was coming out of his office. I'd want to know who I was telling before I told them."

"The Assize, then," said Lucan. "I know four aldermen who would give their teeth for a scandal under the Charter."

"And the Assize sends the watch along the Causeway," said Sorle, "and the Proctors close the Gatehouse, and there follows a fortnight of very fine correspondence, during which the Hush continues to fail." He took his spectacles off and pinched his nose. "Mr. Ferrers, your instinct is admirable and your sense of time is a lawyer's. The Hush does not attend hearings."

"Then we cut it," said Saskia. "Tonight. Lucan and Teo know the way down from the Cold House cellar. Saw the rods out and let the sleepers keep their own."

"And the stays starve," Hester said. "The Choir's a third short. They've been holding the Fifth on the sleepers for a year. Cut the line and they've nothing to hold with."

"Then what?" Saskia said. "Three doors, and we've just walled up all three. Tell the people who already know. Tell people who can't do anything. Or drown everyone to save a dozen. Pick one."

"That's what she said," Hester said. "*You're Lowside. You know what it is to choose who drowns.*" She found she had sat down after all, on the edge of Sorle's desk among his papers. He did not object. "That's the whole of her case. A weight on one side and a weight on the other, and you pick the lighter one to lose, and you hate yourself, and you do it."

"Isn't that what grown women do?" said Lucan quietly.

"I don't know." Hester looked at her hands. "I've spent eleven years thinking there were two choices for Tib. Keep him, or let him go and die. I picked one and hated myself and paid for it on the first of every month, and I never once thought there might be anything else. That's the thing about a scale. It's very good at telling you which side's heavier. It doesn't tell you whether you've put the right things on it."

"Go on," said Sorle.

* * *

She did not know, when she began, that she knew it. It had been lying in her for weeks in separate pieces, and she had only needed to be angry enough to tip them out on the table.

"Aske's last entry," she said. "Teo. Read it again."

Teo had not let the notebook out of his reach since the night they found it. He opened it at the ribbon.

"*She is right that it will flood. She is wrong that it must be held. The pressure wants a road, not a lid. Vent? Need Sorle's arrears for the shaft.*"

"A road, not a lid," Hester said. "My father said that, near enough. About a pan." She saw Lucan's face. "Brine-pans. When a pan spits, the trick everyone's taught is to drop the lid on it. My father doesn't. He draws the fire a shovelful at a time and lets her talk. Lid her, and either she blows the lid through the roof, or she goes quiet and you lift the lid later and it all comes up in your face at once. Everything she's been saving."

"The stays are lids," said Teo slowly.

"Every one of them. You pour still in on top of a quickening and it goes quiet, and the Fathomers write it off as held, and it isn't. It's saving. That's why they come back bigger. Sorle, you said it, in Fallow. *Pour enough still into a quickening and it quiets, and grows hungrier.*"

"I said it," said Sorle. He had put his spectacles back on. "I also said, when you asked what would happen if one let it out, that the moment would proceed. I said it as a warning. You appear to be repeating it as a recommendation."

"It is one. Saskia. Lantern Night. The jars. Tell him what you told me."

Saskia frowned. Then her face changed.

"Annealing," she said. "Glass that's cooled fast keeps its stress inside. One day you put it down on a table and it bursts. So you put it in the lehr and bring it down a few degrees at a time, hours, days for the big pieces, and the stress walks out of it. You don't stop the stress. You can't. You give it time to leave." Her eyes had gone narrow and bright, the look she got in the kilns. "Hold, drop. Hold, drop. Too big a drop, it cracks anyway. You'd have to know how much stress was in it to set the steps."

"Arrears," said Lucan.

They all looked at Sorle.

He sat with his hands flat on the desk on either side of the place where Hester was sitting, and looked at the wall of slates behind her, and his lips moved slightly, as they did when he was counting.

"No," he said at last. "It's quite mad. You propose to take a pocket of the held moment under nine centuries of arrears and let it *go*. Into what? Into the Fifth? You will flood the Fifth. Into the Hush? It *is* the Hush. The pressure wants a road, Corvin says; Corvin always did write like a man composing his own epitaph. A road to where? We are in a dome of salt in the middle of a dead sea. Every direction is down or sideways, and down and sideways is more salt and more moment and more—" He stopped.

"Up," said Hester.

He looked at her.

"The Well-Cap," she said. "The Proctor showed us, the first day, from the Lantern gallery. A quarter-mile east, an iron lid on the salt with a chain on it. The Founders' Shaft, he said, all the way down to the Hush, and nobody's opened it since they capped it."

"The Founders' Shaft," said Teo.

"It goes up," said Hester. "Out onto the White. Where there's nothing to drown but salt."

Sorle got up. He walked round the desk, past her, to the long wall of slates, took the cloth off its nail and wiped the first slate clean in four strokes. Then he took a stick of chalk from his waistcoat pocket and began to write.

* * *

He did not stop for four days.

He wiped every slate in the study, then sent Teo for the six big slates from the Black Bench lecture room, then sent Lucan to the Brinestacks with a list. Lucan came back with the founders' survey of the Hush under his coat and did not say how he had got it out. When the slates ran out Sorle wrote on the plaster between the shelves.

On the first evening Lucan asked, as if reading from a contract, whether Reader Sorle would consider a transfer to his Bench of an aspirant presently attached to the Deputy Chancellor's. Sorle did not turn round. "Can you do sums?" "Yes." "Then sit down and do these, and stop dripping on my floor." In the morning there was a black cord on the chair where Lucan had slept. Hester saw him touch it twice before noon.

She did not understand most of the slates. But she sat with him through long hours of it, because he needed someone to tell him what things tasted like.

"Quickened grain at the Fifth east gallery," he would say, without turning round. "How heavy?"

She gave it to him in thornes, her private scale since the customs stool, named for old Mr. Thorne, and watched Sorle's face as he learned that a unit of Horology was now called after a dead customs man. The *Wren* cargo was eleven thornes. The Fifth east gallery was fourteen. The line under the Cold House, the stillness of three hundred sleeping people flowing east through the dark, was forty. Sorle put them through something that took up most of a slate, and stood back from it for a long time.

"What?" said Hester.

"It agrees with Corvin. Corvin and I have never agreed on anything in twenty years, except the quality of the Harl cider." He wiped his chalky hand down his waistcoat, leaving a white stripe. "It can be done. I want to be exact, Miss Vail, because it will be quoted back to me at my trial. It can be done if the following things are true." He ticked them on the slate with the side of the chalk. "One. The pocket must have a continuous road to a place where its motion does no harm. That's your shaft, if the founders' survey is honest. Two. The release must be staged. Annealed, as Mrs. Orme says. A third of the arrears at a time, with a hold between, or the shock cracks the Hush floor and we all go into the dark together. Three. Someone must make the release. A worker, in the brine, at the boundary of the pocket, hands on the salt, giving the held moment leave to proceed a step at a time." He looked at her over his spectacles. "It will be the hardest release ever made in this building. It is also, by your own account and Maudelaine Crisp's, the thing you are worst at in the world."

"I know."

"And four," said Sorle, "which is not on any slate. The moment proceeds. Whatever is held in that pocket will move again. Water, certainly, nine hundred years old, up the shaft and out onto the White." He set the chalk down on the ledge, precisely. "And if I have been right for thirty-two years, it will not only be water. I will find out that I was right in public, at the worst possible time. I find I don't greatly mind. But you should know what you're starting."

She thought about the cry in the *Wren* grain. Two notes, and a third that cracked.

"I know what I'm starting," she said.

"No," said Sorle. "Nobody does. That's rather my point."

* * *

They chose the night on the sixth of Rime, in R-41, with the frost feathering the corners of the window. It was Saskia who said it, and as soon as she did, it was obvious.

"Assay Night."

After the Assay, by a custom older than the Charter, the whole Halcyon fed, the doors open to Ambry and half the city come over the Causeway. The Choir was stood down for the night, all but a skeleton watch, because you did not keep your own from the Assay Feast. The Fifth on Assay Night, said Saskia, who had met its box-lift three nights a week all summer and heard what came up in it, was emptier than on any night of the year.

"Fewer people down there if it goes wrong," said Teo.

"Fewer people to stop us," said Lucan.

"Both," said Hester. "Mostly the first."

Nobody wrote anything down. Hester's ledger-mind was screaming at her to, and she did not.

Lucan and Teo would go to Ambry after the Assay with the crowd, and down through the Cold House cellar into the Cistern. Agathe would let them in; she had said yes before Hester finished asking. At the twelfth bell they would saw the draw-rods out of the founders' line, every one, and the sleepers' stillness would stop at the Cold House, where it belonged.

"And the moment we cut," said Lucan, "the stays begin to starve."

"The moment you cut, I'll know," said Hester. "I'll be on the line in the Hush. I'll taste it go. And from then till the shaft takes the water, there's a skeleton watch on the east stays with nothing in the line. That's the hole. I'll not pretend it isn't."

"Then fill it," said Saskia.

"Orsolya will fill it."

There was a silence in R-41 of a kind Hester had not heard in it before.

"She'll be at the Feast," Hester said. "She'll feel the line go soft before you've put your saws down; she's had her hands on it every day for a year. She'll come down. And when she sees the shaft take water, she'll hold the stays."

"Or she'll shut the shaft," said Lucan. "Or you. She has already kept one person who stood in her way at that Tap."

"It's not a plan," said Saskia. "It's a prayer with a name in it."

"Miss Vail," said Sorle, quite gently, "that is madness."

"I know her." Hester heard how it sounded, and said it anyway. "She's buried twelve of them. She went to their mams. Whatever else she is, she'll not stand in the Fifth and watch the Choir go under when there's a road open. She'll not let them drown."

Saskia would get the key from Dunmore's left-hand drawer: the brass-tagged ring, *W.L.F. Duplicates*, and on it the long black iron key with a little bird sitting on a line on its bow. She would take it during the Feast, when Dunmore would be at the Long Table eating for once.

"And if he's not?" said Teo.

"Then I'll think of something. I'm a widow with a child and a Quell accent. People tell me where things are to get me out of their offices."

Hester and Sorle and Saskia would go down through the empty Fifth and across the Hush to the foot of the Founders' Shaft, and open the plug, a lattice-grown salt seal six feet thick. Then, when Hester tasted the cut, they would go to the boundary of the east quickening and begin.

"Three steps," said Sorle. "Mrs. Orme counts. Mrs. Orme has the best sense of an interval of anyone I have met in forty years."

"I'll allow it," said Saskia.

"And you?" said Teo.

"I hold the slate," said Sorle, "and I tell Miss Vail when to stop. And if she cannot stop, I hold her."

"Hold her how?" said Teo.

"You will see. I hope you will not."

"Every part of this is a felony," said Lucan, in his precise voice. "Breaking the Hush seal. Unlicensed release. Interfering with Halcyon infrastructure. I wanted to be sure I'd said it, so that afterward I can't pretend I didn't know." He unfolded himself from the floor, wincing at his ribs, and stood in the low doorway with his head bent under the lintel. "I'll cut your line, Vail. I'd have cut it in Fallow if you'd let me."

* * *

Agathe's letter came on the morning of the ninth of Rime.

*My dear. Your Tib is wet again at the foot since the seventh, the beads running east as before. I am sorry. I know you will want it plain. And the Corrie twins have begun to craze, Nell first and Jem after her, fine lines all down the east side like a cup that has been in the oven too long. Mr. Pell says a crazed casing may go a month or may go in a week. A.L.*

She read it twice at the post-tray. Then she took it to Sorle's study and put it on the slate he was working at, over the chalk.

"Sooner," she said.

He read it. They went through it aloud, and she could not make the sums come out another way. The key was in Dunmore's drawer and Dunmore never left his desk but for the Feast. The Fifth was never empty but on Assay Night. The plug wanted the survey Lucan had not finished copying. And she could not yet give a drop of brine back out of her hand.

"Assay Night is not the convenient night," Sorle said. "It is the first one. I am sorry, Miss Vail."

She went to see her father that afternoon.

He looked up when she came along the pans. He stopped raking, which he had never done for her in her life, and stood with the rake in his hand in the steam and waited.

"Da."

"Hes."

She had come with a speech. It went out of her head at the sight of him. He looked three weeks older.

"Agathe says you came to the desk," she said.

"Aye."

"You had the pen."

"Aye."

"Why didn't you sign?"

He drew the rake through the crust to the far rim and back, and knocked it on the iron, and did not answer for so long she thought he would not. The steam came up between them and went away.

"Pen was heavy," he said.

She waited. She had learned waiting from Callow.

"Lad put the form in front of me and I read it. All of it, every line. Takes me a while." He looked at the pan, not at her. "There's a box at the bottom. *Signed for the family.* And above it, where they put who signed before, *H. Vail.* Your hand. Square, like you do it. Eleven year old, that ink, and still black." He laid the rake across the rim. "And I sat there and I thought: she put him in on her own, at twenty-two, with her mam a month buried and me on the stair with my mouth shut. And now I'll take him out on my own, and she'll come up the street on the first and find him gone. That's two of us did it alone, and him in the middle. That's no way to finish a thing. You don't carry half a load and call it delivered." He looked at his burned hands. "Your name's on the first one, Hes. The last one wants both. I'll not sign it without you. And you'll not keep him without me, neither. Not any more."

She could not speak for a moment.

"I said a hard thing to you," he said. "At the Drowned Man."

"It was true."

"Aye. Didn't make it kind." He was quiet a while. "Agathe says he's wet again. She says he was dry four days."

"He was. Somebody dried him who'd no right to, for my sake. I had him put back."

He looked at her a long time through the steam. Then he nodded once, as if she had told him the price of coal and it was what he'd expected.

"Then there's summat you mean to do."

"Yes. And I need to ask you something."

He waited.

"How do you let a pan down slow?"

She thought he might laugh, or tell her to go away. He wiped his hands on his apron, and when he spoke it was in a voice she had not heard from him since he had taught her to read the tide-marks on the harbour wall.

"First thing is you listen to her. Before you touch anything. Every pan talks different. A pan with muck in the bottom ticks, small and quick, like a clock. A pan with air under the iron where the bed's cracked thumps. Slow. Deep. Like a heart. The one wants the fire down quick and the other'll crack her iron if you do it quick. So you stand there and you count and you listen, and you don't do anything at all for as long as you can stand, and longer.

"Then you go to the fire-door. You don't open it wide. You open her a hand, and you wait for her to answer. She'll go louder first. Always does. That's the bit that frightens the young lads, and they drop the lid. She goes louder because she's felt the change and she's not sure of it yet. You let her be louder. You stand there and let her shout at you. Then she goes quieter, a bit, and then you draw the first coals. A shovel. Lay them on the brick to the side where you can get them back if you need them. And she'll go louder again, not so loud, and quieter again, and you draw the next. Each time you wait till she's said all she's going to say before you take the next shovel. You hurry her and she'll go up all at once, and then it doesn't matter how careful you've been.

"And the last thing." He looked down at his left hand, at the two shiny stiff fingers. "When she's talking, you don't stand over her. You stand to the side, with your face out of it. Because you're letting something out, Hes, and it's coming out where it wants, not where you want. You've given it the road. You don't get to pick which way it looks when it comes up." He picked the rake up. "That's how. Took me forty year and two fingers. You can have it for nowt."

She stood in the heat with her eyes stinging, and told herself it was the steam.

"Ta," she said.

"Hes." He did not look at her. "Whatever it is. Stand to the side."

"I will."

She did not tell him about the Assay.

* * *

The release was the thing she could not do, and there were twelve days to learn it.

Sorle could not teach her. He said so at once. "I am a theorist with indifferent hands. I could no more teach you a release than teach you to whistle."

Crisp's release hour had got her, since Fallow, to a drop half back on a good night. Only one person had ever got a whole release out of her: on the White, with Lucan's casing hanging in the rim of the sink, a low Lowside voice in her ear. *Don't push it out, chick. Open your hand.*

She would not go to Orsolya. She went instead to the Pans on the Crown every night at the eighth bell, when the rose and green water was black under the stars and nobody else would be fool enough to be out in the cold. She knelt at the edge with a flask of live brine and a dish. She kept a drop, and returned it. Kept a drop—

And it would not come back. It sat in her palm, clear and round as a seed pearl, and she could feel herself holding it, all of her clamped round it as her fist clamped round her purse in a Lowside crowd. She knew the theory. Every time she reached the place where she should have let go, something in her said *not yet, it'll be lost*, and closed.

On the fourth night Teo came out and sat on the rim of the pan beside her with a blanket round both their shoulders and watched her fail for an hour.

"What are you thinking about," he said finally, "when you try?"

"The drop."

"No, you're not. You're thinking about what happens if you let go and it isn't there any more. You're not holding the drop. You're holding the not-losing it." He pulled the blanket tighter. "At the Abbey, at Vigil, you carried a candle cupped in both hands down to the water-stair, and at the bottom you let the wind take the flame. The novices always held their hands round it to the last second and then snatched them away, and the flame went out because they'd snatched, not because they'd let go. The old sisters did it like this." He held his hands out over the black water, cupped, and then, slowly, turned them palm-up and flat, and opened his fingers. "You don't take your hands away from it. You take your hands away from *round* it. It's still in your palms. It just isn't trapped."

"Did you ever manage it?"

"Once. In my last year. I'd stopped believing the sea was coming back by then." He smiled, a little crooked. "Maybe that's why I could. I wasn't trying to keep anything for it any more."
