She kept a drop. She did not try to let go of it. She thought of Teo's hands turning over in the dark, and her father standing to the side of the pan with his face out of it. She opened her hand: not away from the drop, but out from round it, flat, as you hold your palm out to a horse.

The drop went soft. It slid down the crease of her palm, live brine, warm for an instant with the tiny shove of its own arrears, and dripped off the side of her hand into the pan, and was water among water.

"Again," she said.

She did it eleven times more that night, and failed on seven. On the ninth night she failed on two, and afterward, in R-41, she took down the two dishes that had stood on her shelf since Russet with her first two kept drops in them, both from the Pans. She held her hand over them a long time. Then she put them back on the shelf. *Not yet.*

On the eleventh night Sorle came out onto the Crown in two coats with a kept moth from his desk, a brown one six years in its casing, and stood over her while she returned it. It sat on her palm for the length of a breath, stunned, with the faint heat of six years coming off it, and then opened its wings and went up off her hand into the dark, toward the Lantern.

The rush came after it: a racing in her chest, a heat in her face the frost could not touch, the stars too sharp. Sorle knelt, stiffly, and tipped his flask into her dish over her wet hand, and laid his own hand flat on hers in the brine. It was cold, and very still.

The racing slowed. She felt it go out of her as warmth goes out of a cup.

"That is how," said Sorle. "Physic does it at the bath when a returner's heart runs away with him. Still runs to quick, Miss Vail. It runs into a racing heart as well as into a quickening." He took his hand away and looked at it. "It is not free. It is merely shared."

She thought: one moth. She thought of the east gallery, fourteen thornes, and the black water at the Silent Supper, and the Founders' Shaft going up through six hundred feet of salt to an iron lid in the dark.

She thought of Tib's hand, curled on his chest, holding its pencil.

Then she filled her flask from the pan and went in, because it was the nineteenth of Rime, and in two days she would have to do it in front of the whole city, and then again in the dark, where it counted.


# Chapter 22: The Midwinter Assay

> Discern. Keep. Return.
>
> — *The Assay formula, spoken by the presiding Reader before each candidate*

The Hall of Assay held eleven hundred people, and on the twenty-first of Rime it held more.

Hester had seen it empty, in Russet, on the tour. A dome of carved salt rose from the First Fathom floor so high that the light-shafts let into it from the Lantern were only threads at the top. Galleries ran round it in three tiers. Twelve chandeliers hung on chains as thick as her wrist, salt grown by the Lattice-masons over years on wire frames into branching trees of crystal. Empty, the hall had been dark, and had smelled of cold and old dust.

Full, it smelled of wet wool and oranges and people, and it was lit.

At the first bell the Glaziers had gone up into the dome on rope-cradles and dissolved a measure of kept noon into each chandelier's heart, and the light had gone out along the crystal limbs. It ran from cube to cube and broke on every face. The twelve trees hung burning over the hall, white-gold, and threw on the packed galleries and the upturned faces a light like nothing on the Rim in winter: the light of a particular afternoon in Seedmonth, warm and slanted, with a faint sound in it, if you listened, of bees.

Hester sat on the aspirants' benches on the hall floor with the rest of the Turning Intake in grey, and listened to the bees, and was afraid.

Agathe's letter had come up in the first-bell post-bag. It was inside her gown now, folded small against her breastbone.

*My dear. Jem Corrie came back on the nineteenth, near the tenth bell. The crazing went right through him all at once and he was out on the plinth before I could get Mr. Pell. He knew me, I think. He was gone by the half. Nell is holding, tide willing. I have oiled her more than I should. Do not come. Do what you must. A.L.*

The nineteenth. She had been on the Pans that night, opening her hand over a moth. She had had Agathe's letter of the ninth, *the Corrie twins have begun to craze*, and had sat in Sorle's study with his slates and chosen the twenty-first anyway, because the Assay emptied the Fathoms and no other night did. Twelve days. She had done the sum, and Jem Corrie was the remainder. She had shown the letter to no one. It was hers to carry.

She had sat on a customs stool for fourteen years and never been frightened of a grade in her life. But she had never graded anything before a thousand people. The galleries were full to the rail: Upper Ward furs, Exchequer black, and Lowside in its Sunday coats, packed into the top tier where tickets were free, leaning over the rail with oranges. Children sat on shoulders. Three Proctors stood at the doors in their dark-blue capes trying to look as if this happened every day, which, once a year, it did.

And on the dais at the hall's east end, under the biggest chandelier, the panel.

There were five of them at a long table draped in grey. Reader Crisp, in the centre, presiding, small and upright. Reader Bassano on her left, in a gown that was technically grey and actually silver. Deputy Chancellor Vey on her right, smooth, attentive, with a slight smile that he seemed to have set at the first bell and would take off at the ninth. Warden Thane at the end.

And at the table's other end, in a chair a little apart from it, the Chancellor.

Hester had seen Perpetua Holm twice before, both times at a distance, both times sitting. She was very old and very small, swaddled in grey wool to the chin, with a white cap and white hands folded in her lap. Her face had the pale glaze of a Settled worker's face, like a pot that had been fired too often. She had not moved since the hall filled: not her hands, not her head, not her pale eyes, fixed on some point in the middle of the hall floor. If it had not been for the faint lift of the wool at her breast, every half-minute or so, slow as a tide, you would have taken her for one of the Quiet Rooms' keepings, set out on the dais for the occasion.

Orsolya was looking at the aspirants' benches. Hester did not look back.

* * *

They went in order of name. Anselm was the third called.

Teo walked up the dais steps in his grey gown with the green cord at the shoulder and his old Tider shawl folded underneath it, where nobody could see it but Hester. He looked, going up, like a man walking to the gallows who has decided to be polite to the hangman. He turned and bowed to the panel. Crisp spoke the formula, *Discern, keep, return*, in her dry small voice that somehow reached the top tier. The Assay began.

Discernment was a tray of seven grains in seven silver dishes, set by the panel. The candidate named each aloud for grade, depth and provenance, and a Proctor with a ledger wrote it down. Most aspirants named grade and depth and stopped, sweating. Teo did not taste. Teo never tasted. He put his fingertip to each dish, and bent his head close, and went into a light hold, and *listened*. Then he straightened up and told the panel, in his warm, slightly apologetic voice, what each grain had heard on the day it set. The sixth dish had been taken from a pan on the Harl margin in a hailstorm; you could hear it on the tiles. The second had been kept, briefly, round a dead bird. The fourth had come up out of the Fifth Fathom, and there was singing in it. *Choir singing, I think, Reader. Low. They were holding something.*

Bassano laughed aloud and applauded, alone, until Crisp looked at him.

Then the keeping. A Proctor lifted the cover off a jar, and a brown moth climbed out onto its rim. When it flew, the candidate was to keep it in the air with a flick of live brine, and then, when the panel had examined the casing, return it and let it go.

Teo's hold was the worst in the Turning Intake. Crisp had once said in class that holding with Anselm was like trying to frame a picture of a fountain. When the moth flew he flicked the brine and missed, and the hall drew one breath. He flicked again and caught it. The moth went still in the air with one wing kept and one half-kept, crooked, a casing like a broken lantern. It dropped into the dish with a click. The panel bent over it. Crisp's mouth went thin.

Then Teo put his fingertip to the casing and returned it, and his return was beautiful. It was easy and complete, like a man setting down a cup. The moth sat up in the dish with both wings whole and went off the dais toward the chandeliers, and the hall let out its breath in a long sigh.

"Assayed," said Crisp.

It was the lowest pass. Teo bowed to the panel as if he had been crowned, and came down the steps grinning so widely that Hester, who had not smiled all morning, found that she was.

* * *

It went on. It went on all morning and into the afternoon. The kept-spring light from the chandeliers did not change. Hester's backside went numb on the salt bench, and her hands went cold, and she drank from her flask until it was empty and then sat with it in her lap.

Ferrers was called in the early afternoon. Lucan went up with his black cord at the shoulder, new enough that the knot still sat stiffly. In the second tier, among the Exchequer men, a tall grey-haired man in black sat forward and did not take his eyes from him. Lucan did not look up at that tier once. He discerned all seven grains for grade and depth and named two of them by the pit they had come from. He kept the moth in a casing so precise and so thin that you could see the dust on its wings through it, and returned it cleanly. When Crisp said "Assayed with distinction," the man in black stood up slowly and clapped three times, and sat down. Lucan came down the steps with his face like a shut door. When he passed the bench he caught Hester's eye, and for an instant the door was open on something that looked more like grief than triumph.

Orme was called after the fifth bell. Saskia walked up the steps as she walked into the kilns, briskly, rolling her sleeves. She named the grains in her flat Quell voice as if she were pricing them. Her keeping was the best of the day. She did not flick the brine at the moth. She held the flask still and let the moth fly into the drops, and the casing closed round it as smooth as poured glass, and the light from the chandeliers went into it and came out the other side in colours. People in the galleries said *oh* aloud. Bassano put both hands to his heart. Her return was neat and dry. "With distinction," said Crisp, and Saskia nodded to her as one tradeswoman to another. Then, coming down, she looked up at the top tier, where a small fair-haired girl was standing on the rail with her grandmother's arms round her middle, waving with both hands. Saskia waved back once, briefly, with her whole face changed.

Then there were others. The light did not change. The bees went on.

At the eighth bell the Proctor with the ledger said, "Vail. Hester. Bench of Reader Sorle," and she stood up.

* * *

Her knees were stiff. She went up the dais steps carefully, as if they were the six steps down to the charity ward, worn into dishes in the middle.

At the top she turned and bowed to the panel, the way the others had. Crisp looked at her without expression. Bassano smiled. Vey's set smile did not alter. The Chancellor did not move.

Orsolya looked at her.

Hester had known she would. She had spent nineteen days making herself ready for it, on the Pans every night, failing and then, slowly, not. It was still worse than she had prepared for. Not accusing, and not pleading: the look Orsolya had given her on the Causeway on the first morning, with the salt-mud on her boots and her hands tucked under her arms against the cold. *You're Aske's privilege. Well done, chick.* It was warm, and tired, and proud of her.

Hester turned to the tray.

"Discern," said Crisp. "Keep. Return."

There were seven silver dishes. She rinsed her mouth from the jug the Proctor held, and spat into the basin, and took up the horn spoon from the rack. Behind her, eleven hundred people went quiet. It was the quiet of an assay room at the second bell before Daisy came in. She found she had stopped being afraid.

She tasted the first grain.

"Shelf," she said. "Two fathoms. Southern margin. Somebody's dried it in an oven, and too fast. It's lost its sweet at the corners." The Proctor's pen scratched. "Second. Middling. Thirty fathoms. Halcyon pits, the north face. It's been out of the ground a month."

She went down the tray. Third, Deep at ninety, old, with a tiredness in it that meant someone had kept something in it once and returned it, and she said so. The Proctor's pen stopped. Crisp said "Go on" without inflection. Fourth, dead salt: Varrow rock, put in to trap the unwary. Fifth, Middling, Western Rim, perhaps fifteen. Sixth, Deep at two hundred, from the Halcyon's own reference cupboard, so clean it was almost a pleasure.

The seventh dish was on the end of the tray nearest Orsolya.

Each dish carried a small brass tag on its rim with the setter's initial, face-down. Hester did not turn the tag over. She did not need to. She put the spoon into the seventh dish and lifted a single crystal, white with a little blue in it, sharp-cornered, warm. Warm through the horn. She put it on her tongue.

The weight came down behind her eyes like a door closing.

Fourteen thornes. Sweet to the edge of bitter. Under the sweetness, bright and wet and metallic, the bitten tongue, the startle: quick. And under that, so faint she would not have caught it in Russet, a sound she had not known in Russet. A long low rushing, rising and falling, and the rise coming again before the fall had done.

She put the spoon down in the rack.

"Seventh," she said. Her voice came out clear to the top tier, and she let it. "Hush-grade grain. Quickened. Below the Fifth Fathom, and pushed up into it. It was taken from a stay in the east gallery of the Fifth, within the last week." She lifted her eyes from the tray. "There's sea in it."

The hall did not understand what she had said. She could hear it not understanding: a mutter in the galleries, a rustle, the word *Hush* going along the top tier where the Lowside people were. On the dais, the five of them understood perfectly.

Vey's smile came off. It did not slip; it was taken off, cleanly, and put away. Bassano looked at Crisp. Crisp looked at the brass tag on the seventh dish, and turned it over, and looked at it. Then she looked down the table at Orsolya.

"The east gallery," said Crisp. "Within the week. Vail, that is a level of specificity I would not expect from a Fellow of twenty years. How can you possibly—"

"It tastes of it, Reader."

"She's right," said Orsolya.

Her voice was quiet, Lowside and level, and it carried. "Taken the fourteenth. East gallery, stay nine. I set it." She was still looking at Hester. "Mark it, Maudelaine. She's right."

Crisp looked from one to the other of them for a long moment. Then she nodded to the Proctor with the ledger, and the pen scratched.

At the far end of the table, the Chancellor had not moved. But her eyes were no longer on the middle of the hall floor. They were on Hester. Pale, and wet, and very old, and fixed.

"Keep," said Crisp.

* * *

The Proctor brought the jar and lifted its cover. The moth climbed out onto the rim. It was brown, like all of them, with a darker bar across each forewing. It sat in the burning spring light and opened and closed its wings, and Hester took up the flask of live brine and the dish and stood with them, and went into the hold.

That part was easy. That part had always been easy. Breath slow. Heart slow. Down and down, silt settling in a glass. The hall went far away. The bees went far away. There was the moth, and the brine in her hand, and the deep square stillness in her that Crisp had once called a miser's. Hester had come to think of it as simply her own: the stillness of a woman who had stood at the end of a fourth row once a month for eleven years and not touched anything.

The moth flew.

She did not flick. She had watched Saskia. She lifted the flask and let the brine go in a short flat arc across the moth's path, and the moth flew into it, and she *held*. The brine closed on it in the air. The casing came down round it dense and squared, like a stone fitted in a wall, and the moth hung for an instant in the light, a brown thing in a clear cube. Then it dropped into the dish with a sound like a dropped coin.

The panel bent to it. Bassano took out a glass. Crisp looked a long time.

"Dense," said Crisp. "Squared. Heavy-handed." She sat back. "Not a flaw in it. Return."

Hester set the dish on the table in front of her and put out her right hand, palm down, and laid her fingertips on the casing.

This was the part. She had known it would be, since the first day at the Pans in Russet, when the drop had sat in her palm and stayed there and Crisp had said *You hold like a miser, Vail*, loud enough for Lucan Ferrers to smirk. She could feel it already: the clench. Everything in her that had been settling and stilling began, at the touch of the casing, to close. Not yet. It'll be lost. If you let go of a thing, it isn't there any more.

She thought of Teo on the rim of the pan in the dark, turning his hands over.

She thought of her father standing to the side, with his face out of it.

She did not take her hand away. She turned it. Slowly, in front of eleven hundred people, her right hand turned over on the casing until it was lying under it rather than over it, palm up, as you hold your hand out to a horse. She opened her fingers. Not away from it. Out from round it.

The casing went soft. It ran. It was brine in her palm, warm for a moment with the small shove of the moth's arrears. The moth sat in the middle of her hand in the wet, a brown moth with a dark bar on each wing, and she felt the rush come up in her, small, a flush and a quickening of the heart. The light seemed for an instant too bright and the bees too loud.

The moth opened its wings. It shut them. It opened them.

Then it went up off her palm in its jerky, stupid, moth's flight, up over the dais and the panel, toward the nearest chandelier, toward the light. It went up and up until it was a speck among the burning crystal limbs, and then she could not see it at all.

Someone in the top tier began clapping: hard hands that had raked a pan for forty years, slapping together slow and loud and alone. Hester looked up, and saw her father standing at the rail of the top tier in a coat she had never seen before. It was a good dark coat, brushed, too tight in the shoulders, and he was clapping with his face set and his chin up as if he were daring the whole of Ambry to tell him to sit down.

Daisy Fenn was beside him with a baby on her hip and four children round her knees, and she began to clap too. Then the Lowside tier, and then the second tier, and then, for a moment, the whole hall.

"Assayed," said Crisp, over it, in her small voice that reached the roof, "with distinction."

* * *

She came down the steps without feeling them. Teo caught her at the bottom by both arms and said something she did not hear. Saskia, on the bench, gave her a short nod. Lucan, two places along, did not look at her at all, which she understood to be his version of the same.

She sat. Her palm was still wet. She closed her hand on it.

When she looked up at the dais again, Orsolya was looking at her.

The hall was loud round them. Through all of it the Warden of the Lower Fathoms sat at the end of the panel table with her cold hands folded in front of her and looked down at Hester Vail across forty feet of salt floor and a winter's worth of lies. Hester looked back.

Orsolya knew. Hester saw her know it. It was in the slackening of her face, the way a sail slackens when the wind drops: not surprise, and not anger. Orsolya had seen her name the seventh dish and seen her open her hand. She knew there would be no answer to her offer, because the answer had been given, out loud, in front of the city. Whatever Hester meant to do, Hester had decided to do it.

Then Crisp said "Warden?" for the second time, about the next candidate's tray, and Orsolya turned her head and answered, and it was over.

* * *

The Assay ended at the ninth bell. Crisp spoke the closing formula, and the Glaziers went up in the rope-cradles and let the chandeliers run out. The spring light drained along the crystal limbs and went, and the hall seemed small and cold. Then the great doors at the west end were opened onto the Gatehouse yard, and the smell of roasting came in, and the noise of Ambry on the other side of the Line, and the Feast began.

The Long Table under the Lantern Well was laid from end to end, three hundred feet, with every Reader and Fellow and aspirant in the Halcyon crammed along both sides. In the Gatehouse yard there were trestles and braziers for the porters and cooks and delvers, roast mutton and saltcake by the yard and Harl cider in barrels, and the Lowside Bench singing a ballad about the sea, very badly. Above it all the Lantern's mirrors stood still, turned to the night, and down the Well came the last of the year's kept midsummer, gold, poured out by the Glaziers in honour of the Assayed.

The Choir came up from the Stays in its grey wool at the tenth bell and was cheered along the Long Table. Hester counted them as they came. Fifty-one. She knew the Choir's roll: sixty-two. Eleven left below, then. A skeleton watch, as Saskia had said.

Orsolya came up with them. She sat at the Readers' end of the table between Crisp and the Chancellor's empty chair, and did not eat, and did not look down the table.

Aldous found Hester in the yard. He had his good coat buttoned to the throat and a cup of cider he had not drunk, and he stood in front of her among the braziers and did not seem to know what to do with either of them.

"Didn't know you could do that," he said.

"Nor did I. Till this week."

"With the moth." He turned his hand over in the air, palm up, clumsily. "That."

"You taught me."

He looked at her. He looked down at the cider. Then he drank the whole of it in one swallow and gave her the empty cup, as if that were a thing a man did, and said, "Stand to the side," and went off through the gate toward the Causeway without looking back.

At the half bell after the tenth, Lucan and Teo went out through the gate with a crowd of Ambry people going home. Teo had a sack over his shoulder that clanked once when he set it down to pull his collar up. Lucan, among the furs and black coats, was suddenly only another Exchequer man's son walking his friend into town. Neither of them looked back at her. Teo, at the gate, lifted one hand above his head without turning round, the fingers open.

Saskia had gone a quarter of an hour before. She had kissed Pip goodnight at the gate and walked back in along the First Fathom past the Long Table. As she passed the clerks' benches she looked at a gap in them: an empty place halfway down, where a plate of mutton sat untouched with a knife and fork laid neatly across it. Hester had seen her see it.

Then Saskia had gone down the Stair.

Hester stood in the yard with her father's empty cup in her hand and the brazier-smoke in her eyes. Her palm was dry now, but she could still feel the place in the middle of it where the moth had sat. Against her breastbone, Agathe's letter had gone warm.

At the eleventh bell, Sorle came out of the Gatehouse door in his black gown with a lantern and a canvas satchel over his shoulder. The satchel was full of slates, and they knocked together as he walked. He looked at her across the yard, and did not need to speak.

She set the cup down on the edge of a trestle, and went in.


# Chapter 23: The Old Tap

> Take her off the heat slow.
>
> — *Saying of the Lowside brine-boilers*

The Stair was empty all the way down.

On ordinary nights there was always someone on it. Tonight the whole Halcyon was up at the Long Table or out in the Gatehouse yard. The noise of the Feast came down the Well behind them, fainter at every turn, until by the Third Fathom it was only a murmur, like the sea in the Roar. Then it was nothing.

Sorle went ahead with the lantern. He took the steps one at a time, his free hand trailing along the wall, over the chalk-names of the Settled. Hester came behind with the satchel of slates, which he had let her carry after he had stopped twice to breathe. Neither of them spoke. On the Second Fathom she passed one that was newer than the rest and still sharp at the edges, in her own square hand. AMA TESSALY, CHOIR, 903.

At the Fourth they left the Stair. The Choir hall opened out to their left, black and empty. On a bench near the bottom tier someone had left a grey wool scarf. Sorle looked at it as they passed and did not stop.

Saskia was waiting at the box-lift's Fourth Fathom gate, as she had said she would be.

The box-lift was the porters' cage at the back of the Choir hall. It ran from the Fifth to the Third, with a gate here at the Fourth. Three nights a week all summer, at the tenth bell, it had carried sealed grain boxes up from the Stays to the Third Fathom landing, where Saskia had met it with a barrow on Edwin Dunmore's pass. Saskia sat on an upturned crate beside it with her sleeves still rolled from the Assay, a shuttered lantern at her feet and a canvas sack across her knees. She stood and held out her hand, palm up, and on it was a black iron key longer than her hand, with a little bird sitting on a line on its bow.

"Took you long enough," she said.

"He's sixty-four," said Hester.

"I am present," said Sorle, "and sixty-four and a half. Was there any difficulty?"

"No." Saskia closed her hand on the key again. "That's what I don't like." She looked at Hester. "His desk was locked. I had the drawer open in a count of twenty. The ring was there, *W.L.F. Duplicates* on the tag. The key was there. And his chair was empty, and his coat was gone off the hook. And his plate at the Long Table hadn't been touched."

"Dunmore doesn't miss a Feast," said Hester.

"Dunmore doesn't miss anything." Saskia hitched the sack onto her shoulder; it clinked. "And the key to the Hush was sitting in his drawer where anyone could take it. So either he's stupid tonight, which he isn't, or he's somewhere else with something better to do."

They looked at each other in the lantern-light. Nobody said *Orsolya*.

"Well," said Sorle. "We knew we should not have the night to ourselves. We merely hoped." He held the lantern up to the lift-cage. "In we go."

* * *

The Fifth Fathom was warm.

The Fifth was warm as a cowshed is warm, damply, as if something large were breathing in it. The walls sweated. The salt underfoot was tacky, and their boots made a faint sucking sound on it. Brass stay-lines ran along the walls at shoulder height in sheaths green with age, and where they passed into the salt to feed a stay, the joint was beaded with brine like a glass of cold beer on a summer table.

Far off along the galleries to the west, people were singing.

Eleven voices, Hester thought, low and slow. The skeleton watch, holding the east stays from the Warden's Gallery. The song came along the stay-lines more than through the air; she could feel it in the brass when she put her palm flat on it. *Hold the line, hold the line.* It went on and on, patient, like a woman rocking a cradle in another room.

"This way," said Saskia. "East. Service passage. Nobody comes this way at night."

The service passage was narrow and low and smelled of weed. They went along it in single file. The warmth grew and the singing faded behind them, and another sound came up under it, out of the salt itself. Hester had heard it first in the *Wren* grain, at the Customs House, in another life: a long, low rushing. It rose and fell, and the rise came again before the fall had finished.

"Do you hear that?" said Saskia, low.

"Yes," said Hester.

"I never heard that in the summer."

"No," said Sorle. "I don't suppose it was so loud in the summer."

* * *

The passage came out at the lowest gallery of the Fifth, at the foot of a short flight of steps cut by the founders: shallow, worn into dishes, much older than the Stair. At the bottom the gallery widened into a round chamber. On its far side was the Hush door.

Hester had imagined it many times, and wrongly. It was a door a little taller than a man, set flush in the salt wall. But it was made of salt, lattice-grown so fine and so thick that it was nearly white, like frost on a window, and grown in the shape of the Halcyon's emblem, the bird with its wings folded, sitting on a single straight wave. In the centre of the wave, at the height of Hester's breast, was a keyhole of plain dark iron.

And to the left of the door, coming out of the floor and going into the wall, was the Old Tap.

She knew it from Aske's small sloping hand. A pipe of founders' brass as thick as her thigh, pitted and green-black, rose out of the chamber floor, bent once, and went into the wall beside the Hush door. At the bend was a valve-head the size of a cartwheel. Its spokes were cast in the shape of fishes, worn smooth by centuries of hands. The green on the spokes was rubbed through to bright brass in four places, recently, where hands had been.

From the valve-head a newer pipe ran off along the gallery wall toward the west, brass still yellow under its varnish, to join the stay-lines. The eastern trunk. The line from the Cold House came up out of the floor here and fed every stay in the Fifth.

Hester put the satchel down. She went to the Old Tap and laid her palm on the great curve of the pipe below the valve, slowly, as you lay a hand on a sleeping animal.

It was cold. It was the only cold thing in the Fifth.

There was a tasting-cock at the bend, a small founders' tap the size of a thimble with a fish for a handle, for drawing samples from the line. Someone had used it often; the fish was bright. She turned it a quarter-turn. A bead of brine swelled at its lip, clear, and hung. She took it on her fingertip and put it on her tongue.

Forty thornes.

The weight came down behind her eyes not like a door but like a mountain: the stillness of three hundred people lying in rows under high windows, drawn out of them through the dark. In it, under it, fainter than anything, she could taste—not taste, there was no word, there would never be a word—something that was not stillness at all, but was in the stillness as a voice is in a room. A boy who had whistled. A pencil.

She stood with her hand on the pipe and her finger in her mouth like a child.

"Hester," said Saskia, quite gently, behind her.

"I know." She took her finger out of her mouth and wiped her eyes with the back of her wrist, and was not surprised to find salt on it. "It's his. It's all of them." She turned. "It's not twelve yet."

Sorle had his watch open under the lantern, a fat Hask silver turnip that had been his wife's father's.

"Seven minutes before the half," he said. "Something over half a bell before Mr. Ferrers puts his saw to it, if Mr. Ferrers keeps time, which he will. He is the kind of young man who arrives early to his own funeral." He snapped the watch shut. "Mrs. Orme. The key."

* * *

The key turned stiffly, with a long grinding sound in the lattice. It went round once and stuck, and Saskia leaned on it with both hands and her whole weight, swearing in Quellish, and it went round again. Something deep in the door let go with a soft heavy click, like a bone going back into its socket.

The door swung inward on no hinge she could see. And the hum came out.

She had felt it in Russet, on the tour, as a faint pressure in her back teeth. It came out now through the open door and went straight into the bones of her face. It was quiet, and it was in her jaw and her breastbone and the roots of her hair. The lantern-glass sang faintly with it. Sorle put his hand to his ear and took it away.

"Well," he said. His voice was not quite steady. "There you are."

They went in.

The Hush was not a room. It was a floor.

It went back beyond the lantern's reach in every direction, flat and level, its low roof held up here and there by pillars of lattice-grown salt, white and slender, like birch trees in a winter wood. Old founders' brass with fish-head joints ran pillar to pillar and across the floor in channels, and every channel was dry. Here and there something brass glinted that she did not understand at all: a wheel, a great ring set in the floor, a bank of small handles like the stops of an organ.

The floor was salt. Not white salt. Clear.

Saskia had stopped. She was holding her lamp out at arm's length and looking down, and Hester looked down too, and stood still.

The light went into the floor as it would go into clear water, and it did not stop. It went down a fathom, two fathoms, more, and the salt below them was shot through with the fine planes of its crystals like the grain in a sheet of glass. Below the lantern's last reach, where the light failed, the dark had a shape.

She could not have said what shape. It was too large to be a shape: a darkness that had edges, far down, and the edges went on past where the light could follow. Rock would have been grey. This was black, and it was long, and at one place, where Saskia's lamp swung, Hester thought she saw a line along it, ruled, as straight as a thing that had been made. Then the lamp swung back and it was only dark.

"What is that?" said Saskia. Her voice was hardly a breath.

Sorle did not answer. He was standing beside them with the satchel of slates over his shoulder, looking down. His lips moved.

"Benedek," said Hester.

He did not move.

"Benedek. *Don't.*"

He looked up at her. In the lantern-light his face was naked. He had been looking for something under the salt for thirty-two years, and they had laughed at him in Conclave for it. Now it was under his feet. He could have stood there till he Settled.

"Not now," said Hester. "After. I swear to you. After, we'll come back with every lamp in the Halcyon and you can look till you're blind. Not now."

He looked at her a moment longer. Then he nodded, once, and took his eyes off the floor, and she saw what it cost him.

"East," he said. "The survey says east."

* * *

They heard the quickening before they saw it.

The rushing in the salt had been growing all across the Hush floor, rising under the hum until it was louder than the hum. Then there was a smell, and it was the smell from the Silent Supper: cold weed, green and alive, nothing rotten in it. The salt underfoot went from dry to tacky to wet. Then the lantern found the east wall.

It was wet from roof to floor. Not beaded, running. Brine came down the salt in thin glittering sheets and pooled in the old channels at the foot of the wall, and the channels were not dry any more. The wall was warm to the hand. It *moved*. When she put her palm on it she could feel it under her skin, a slow thrust and fall, like the side of a horse when it breathes. And the sound came out of it, and it was the sea: waves breaking on something a long way off, and wind, and once, far and faint, a cry she knew. Two notes, and a third that cracked.

Fixed into the wall at shoulder height was a great stay-cock of founders' brass. A founders' line came to it from the Old Tap, all the way across the Hush roof, to pour the trunk's stillness into the face of the quickening. The cock was open. The pipe was cold. Where it went into the salt the wall was not wet but dry, a great dry fan spread out round the joint like a hand laid flat over a mouth.

A lid, Hester thought. A lid with a hand on it.

"There," said Saskia.

The Founders' Shaft was ten yards along the wall to the north, at the foot of it. A collar of bronze as wide as a cartwheel, green with age, was set upright into the salt at the floor, and filling the collar, flush with it, was the plug: a disc of lattice-grown salt, white, smooth as a millpond and hard as marble. Behind it, the survey said, a short adit ran six yards into the wall and turned straight up, six hundred feet through the dome to the surface, a quarter-mile east of the Lantern, to a lid with a chain on it.

"Six feet thick," said Sorle. "Per the survey."

"Per the survey," said Saskia, "the founders were honest men."

She swung the sack off her shoulder and opened it. In it were two crowbars, a mallet, her glazier's chisel and a copper pan with a lid. Packed in sacking were six of the Lumen kilns' heat-jars, salt kept round a summer afternoon at the furnace mouth, which were not supposed to leave the kilns. Saskia unstoppered the first into the copper pan with a dipper of live brine from Hester's flask, and the summer let go into the brine with a soft cough, and steam came up off the pan in the warm dark.

"Well?" she said. "It won't dissolve itself."

* * *

It would not dissolve at all.

Sorle's slates said they need not go through it: free the seam where nine centuries had grown the plug into its collar, all round the rim, and the plug would come out like a cork. He had allowed an hour for it. They poured the hot brine into the seam and it ran off. They poured it again, slower, a cupful at a time into a notch Hester cut with the point of the crowbar, and it hissed and ate in a little and stopped. Lattice-grown salt was not mined salt. It had been grown on purpose, crystal locked into crystal along a worker's template, and it was as hard as a thing is when somebody has meant it to be.

So they did it the slow way. Hot brine into the notch; the chisel into the soft; the bar behind the chisel. Lever, crack, prise, and a flake of white came away no bigger than a coin. Hot brine again. Saskia worked the pan and the jars and set the chisel for each cut with a glazier's tap of the mallet. Hester lay on her side in the wet and worked the bar, because she had the arms for it; she had raked pans at ten and carried crates for twenty years, and the old work came back into her shoulders as if it had been waiting. Sorle held the lantern and read the watch and, when he could not stop himself, took the second bar, useless and stubborn, until his breath was coming in long hoarse pulls and his hands were bleeding where the iron had taken the skin. Hester took the bar off him without asking.

"Sit down."

"I will not—"

"Sit *down*, Benedek, before I keep you sitting."

He sat on the wet floor with his back against the wall, and the lantern in his lap, and the watch in his bleeding hand, and laughed. It was a short cracked sound.

"Corvin would have enjoyed this," he said. "Corvin always said I'd never done an honest day's labour."

They worked the south side first, where the seam was widest. The notch became a groove, and the groove a slot that took the bar to half its length, and she was working with her cheek on the wet salt and the steam in her eyes. The brine ran back down her sleeves to the elbow, hot and then cold. Every so often she stopped to breathe and Saskia went in with the dipper, and every so often Sorle said the time. Eleven and three-quarters. Twelve, less ten. Less five.

"Hester," said Sorle.

She came out from the plug. He was holding the watch up to the lantern.

"Twelve, less two," he said. "If you want to be at the line when it goes."

She went to the stay-cock and found its tasting-cock, and drew a bead, and put it on her tongue.

Forty thornes. The mountain. Tib.

She stood with the bead on her tongue and her eyes shut, and waited. Behind her the bar had stopped. The wall beside her breathed in and out against her shoulder, and the sea went on breaking a long way off inside the salt. Six fathoms above her, beyond hearing, the Halcyon was ringing the twelfth bell.

"Twelve," said Sorle.

The weight changed.

It did not stop. It thinned. A door had opened very far away and a draught had come down a long corridor; she felt it go out of the stillness before she knew what it was. Thirty-five thornes. Thirty. Then faster, all at once, as if a rope had been cut and a weight had dropped off the end of it. Twenty. Ten. The coins came off her eyelids one by one, and the mountain went, and the weight behind her eyes went light, lighter, gone. On her tongue was a bead of nothing but brine. Old brine. Flat. Tasting of nothing but the pipe it had come through.

Two miles away, under Coldwater Street, a young man who arrived early to his own funeral had put his saw to a brass rod, and Teo had put his to another.

"They've cut it," she said.

Her voice did not come out as she meant it to. She found she had both hands flat on the stay-cock, holding on.

"They've cut it. It's not coming any more." She turned round. "It's *his* again. It's staying with him."

Saskia's face across the lantern was wet, from the steam or not, and she did not wipe it.

"Good," she said. "Now get back on that bar."

* * *

The quickening knew before they did.

The wall beside the stay-cock gave a long shudder under Hester's hand. The great dry fan of salt round the joint, the hand over the mouth, went dark from the edges in, as a cloth goes dark when you drop it in water. Then it was wet. Then it was running. Then, with a sound like a cork coming out of a bottle, a jet of water as thick as her finger shot out of the wall a foot from her face and hit the brass and broke into spray.

Salt water. Cold as the sea at the bottom of the world. It ran down her cheek and into her mouth, and she tasted it before she could stop herself. Not brine. Not the sweet weight of grain. It was salt, and something green, and something alive, and there was not a grain of stillness left in it. It was water that was going somewhere.

"*Hester!*"

She went back to the plug. The noise was rising behind her, the whole east wall a drum. Water ran ankle-deep in the old channels, and the founders' brass in them rang faintly, like a struck glass. The hum of the Hush had risen too, and was in her teeth so hard that her eyes watered.

"Bar," she said.

Saskia put the bar into her hands. Hester lay down in the water and drove it into the slot. The brine ran down her arms. She struck and levered and struck. Saskia poured, and poured again, and swore, and opened the last of the heat-jars into the pan without measuring, and the summer coughed out into the steam. Sorle was up off the floor again. He stood with his back to the breathing wall and the lantern high and the watch open in his other hand, saying *steady, steady, steady*, to nobody, or to all of them.

The bar went in to its full length, and something gave: a long low groan inside the plug, like a ship's timber taking strain, and the whole south side of the disc shifted under the bar by a hair and settled. A thread of cold came out of the seam over Hester's face. Air. Cold, dry, smelling of frost: air from six hundred feet above, from the open White under the midwinter stars, coming down the Founders' Shaft through a hole she could not see.

"It's through," said Saskia. "Hester, it's *through*—"

"Not enough." The thread of cold was the width of a straw. Hester put her hand on the north rim, and the north rim might have been part of the wall. "Loose this side. Not that. It'll want the whole throat."

Behind them a second jet broke from the east wall, and a third, and the sea in the salt roared.

And far above them, faint, and then not faint, a bell began to ring.

It was not any of the Halcyon's hours. It was a bell Hester had heard only once before, in Cinder, in the middle of the Minute: hard, flat, fast, the alarm from the Warden's Gallery in the Fifth. It came down the stay-lines into the Hush and rang in the brass all round them. Under it, faint, she heard the skeleton watch's song in the west galleries break off in the middle of a note. A little later it started again, ragged, louder, more voices than eleven, as the alarm went up the Stair into the Feast and the Choir came down.

Saskia looked at Hester. Hester looked at Sorle.

"The stays," said Sorle. "With the draw cut, every stay in the Fifth is starving. They'll have felt it go in the Warden's Gallery the moment it went. They'll think it's the Hush failing. They'll be right." He closed his watch. "They'll be coming down."

"Not to us," said Saskia. "To the stays. They don't know we're—"

"One of them knows," said Hester.

She knelt by the plug with the bar in her hands and the brine running down her arms and the sea breaking at her back. Across the Hush, far off, in the open door under the lattice bird, a light had come.

A lantern. Then two. Then three. They came through the door into the Hush and stopped, and swung slowly as their bearers looked out across the floor of clear salt. Then they came east. The bearers did not hurry, and they did not need to look for anyone. They walked steadily across the Hush toward the one place where anyone could be.

The first lantern was carried low, and the hand under it was steady, and the face above it was in shadow. But Hester knew the walk. She had seen it on the Causeway on the first morning, coming from Ambry with the salt-mud on its boots. She had seen it come down the gallery at the Silent Supper with the water running round its ankles, leading the Choir.

"Hester," called Orsolya Thane across the Hush.

Her voice was low and Lowside and level. It carried over the water and the hum and the sea as if they were not there.

"Hester. Lass. What have you done?"


# Chapter 24: The Hush

> Hold three. Open two.
>
> — *Chalked on the back of one of Reader Sorle's slates, 21 Rime 903*

The plug moved.

It moved a finger's width, no more, with a grinding sound that Hester felt in her knees before she heard it. A breath of air came out of the crack around it, cold, smelling of frost. Sorle, on his back with the long crowbar braced across his chest, swore in Harlish. Saskia poured the last of the hot brine from the copper into the crack. It steamed and sank and the crack drank it.

"Again," Hester said.

"I am sixty-four years old, Miss Vail."

"Again, Reader."

He set the bar. She put her shoulder under the other end of it. For nine hundred years the plug had sat in its bronze collar and grown into it, as a stone grows into a field. After an hour they had it loose on one side. On the other it held.

Behind them, the Hush roared.

It had only hummed when they came in. Now the quickening at the east end had felt the sleepers' stillness stop, and woken.

She could hear it over the grinding of the bar: surf, loud, close, inside the wall, as if the wall were a cliff and the sea were throwing itself at the far side of it. The whole face of the wall over the pocket was running, and the warmth came off it in a breath.

"It's climbing," Saskia said. She had her watch out on its ribbon, and her lantern on it. "Seventeen minutes since the cut. It's twice as loud as it was at ten."

"I can hear it."

"I'm telling you the rate, Vail. Not the volume."

The plug moved again. The crack opened wider, a dark half-moon along its lower edge, and the cold of the White at night came down six hundred feet and out of it. Sorle let go of the bar and lay back on the salt and breathed like a broken bellows.

"Enough," he said. "That will have to do. The water will take the rest."

"Will it?"

"If it doesn't," said Sorle, with his eyes shut, "we shall none of us be in a position to complain."

* * *

The three lanterns came on across the Hush.

She had known Orsolya would come. She had bet on it. The cut would show in the stays above within a quarter of an hour; the Warden would feel it in her hands before any alarm was rung. Hester had known all that. She had not known what it would be like to see her.

Orsolya covered the last yards with her lantern held high. Two Fathomers in iron-grey followed her, a young man and an older woman. Behind them came Dunmore, in his coat, holding his lantern too low, as if he thought he could avoid being seen by it. Orsolya's white hair was loose. She stopped at the top of the founders' steps at the east end, three shallow treads where the brass trunk of the stay-lines came down out of the roof, and looked at it all: the plug, the bars, Sorle on his back, Saskia with her watch. Then she looked at Hester, standing at the east wall in brine to her ankles with her sleeves rolled to the elbow.

"Oh, lass," she said.

"We cut the draw," Hester said. "That's what I've done."

"I know you've cut the draw. I've two hundred feet of stay-line up there going soft as butter. I've the east gallery of the Fifth sweating like a cheese." She came down the steps. Her voice was quite level. Her hands, Hester saw, were shaking: the Choir's hands, white with cold. "You've killed us all. You know that? You've killed us all, and whoever you've got at the Cistern, and your brother's folk in Lowside. When this goes, it'll not stop at the Hush. It'll not stop at the Halcyon."

"It's not going to *go*. It's going up." Hester pointed at the shaft. "That's the Founders' Shaft. It comes out at the Well-Cap, on the open White. We're giving it a road."

Orsolya looked at the black half-moon under the plug. Then she laughed, short and dry, like salt underfoot.

"A road," she said. "You've been talking to Benedek."

"I've been talking to Corvin."

That stopped her.

"I read his notebook," Hester said. "*She is right that it will flood. She is wrong that it must be held. The pressure wants a road, not a lid.* He'd worked it out. He was coming to tell you. And you kept him."

Behind Orsolya, the young Fathomer turned his head sharply and looked at his Warden. Dunmore, on the bottom step, did not look at anyone.

"Warden," said the older Fathomer. "Warden, the gallery. The Choir's at the east stays and they're failing. Half of them are on the floor."

As if it had heard her, the east wall spoke.

* * *

It spoke as a change in everything at once. The surf behind the salt stopped, as a man stops between one breath and the next. In the silence the lantern flames leaned toward the wall, all of them together, every flame in the Hush bowing east. Then the skin of salt over the pocket broke.

It did not crack. It *opened*, all along a seam the height of a man, the way a fish's belly opens under a knife. Out of the seam came the sea.

It was black as ink in the lantern-light, and colder than any water she had ever put her hands in, and it came out of the wall in a solid curved sheet and hit the clear floor of the Hush and spread. It was round her ankles and then round her shins before she had taken a step. It smelled. Oh, it smelled: weed and salt, green and cold and living, nothing rotten in it at all, the smell of a thing that had been alive in great quantities, all together, nine hundred years ago, on the last afternoon there was a sea.

And it was alive. She knew it in her mouth before she knew it any other way. The spray hit her lips and she tasted it, and it was not grain. It was not stillsalt, not sweet, not heavy on the eyes. It was quick. Everything in it was quick. It was the bead in the crystal at the Customs House and the beads on Tib's casing, all at once, the startle and the bitten tongue. There was nothing still in it anywhere.

Saskia was shouting. Sorle was on his feet, somehow, with his slate in one hand. The water reached the open plug and found the half-moon crack and went into it with a sound like an enormous throat swallowing. For an instant all of it went into the shaft.

Then the seam in the wall opened wider, and more came, and the shaft could not drink fast enough.

Where it ran, the clear floor went soft under it. It pitted, and then it was gone a finger deep, a hand deep, as frost goes off a step under hot water. When the next surge slapped up Hester's arm she tasted it again, and it had changed: quick still, but sweet now, and heavy behind the eyes. The sea had eaten the clear floor. It ran thick with dissolved Hush, live brine to the elbow.

"*Hold it!*" That was Orsolya. She had come down into the water, waist-deep in it now at the bottom of the steps, and she had her hands out in front of her, under the surface. She was going into the hold. Hester could feel her going, as you feel cold coming off a window, a deep old practised stillness reaching out through the brine toward the seam. "Hold it, all of you, hold it shut—"

"No!" Hester was at the wall. She did not remember crossing to it. She was standing in the black water to her knees with her hands flat on the salt on either side of the open seam, and the cold of it was in her wrists like knives. "No. Don't hold it. Give it the shaft. Saskia. *Saskia.* The count."

"I know the count!" Saskia had climbed onto the founders' brass housing beside the plug, out of the water, her lantern on her watch. "On your release. Hold three, open two. Hold three—*Vail, you have to open it.*"

She had to open it.

Hester stood with her hands on the salt and the sea coming past her knees. She went into the hold, because that was where you started, and the hold came to her as it always came: deep, square, immovable, a door shutting. The salt under her palms answered it. She felt the seam stiffen and the water slacken through it. The stillness went out of her into the wall, the way it went into the stay-lines in the Choir, the way it had gone into the brine over Lucan's falling body on the White. The seam began to close.

"*No!*" Saskia's voice cracked. "You're holding! Vail, you're *holding* it—"

She knew. She could feel herself doing it. It was like watching her own hand close on a coin. She told it to open. It did not open. The sea pressed on the far side of the salt, nine hundred years of it, and her hold met it and held. The pressure climbed. She felt it climb through her palms into her wrists and her elbows and her teeth. This was no longer the warmth of the quickening but the arrears, the whole debt of the moment piling up behind the seam like water behind a dam. She was the dam.

"Release it, girl," said Sorle, close beside her. He had come into the water. "Release it. If it builds behind you and you let go all at once, it'll take the floor out."

"I can't."

"You can."

"*I can't.*" She had never in her life been able to. She had kept a falling drop for Crisp and stood there with it kept until Crisp sighed. She had kept Lucan Ferrers in the mouth of a sink and stood over him while the casing dragged, until Orsolya talked her through it. She had stood in front of a moth in the Hall of Assay and— She had done the moth. She had opened her hand for the moth. But a moth was a moth, and this was the sea.

The salt under her hands was creaking, a long low groan like a ship's timbers in a story. Little fractures ran out from the seam across the clear floor of the Hush, white lines in the black water, spreading.

* * *

Behind her Saskia was still counting, *hold three, open two*, to a woman who could only hold.

Hester thought of her father.

Not the man in the tight coat at the rail of the top tier. The man at the fire-door of pan nineteen with his face out of the steam, drawing the coals one shovelful at a time and laying them on the brick where he could get them back. *You hurry her and she'll go up all at once.* She had the fire-door clamped shut, and the pan was cracking its iron.

And under that, under everything, a boy's voice in a hot room above Tanner's Row. He had opened his eyes in the middle of the night and found her and said, as clearly as he had ever said anything: *Let me sleep, Hes. Just let me sleep.*

She had known what he meant. She had stood at the desk on Coldwater Street with his voice in her ears and known what he meant, and signed. She had told herself every month for eleven years that keeping was sleep and he had asked for it. She had kept him because she could not open her hand. Not for him. For her.

She was holding the sea shut for the same reason.

*Open your hand.* She had first heard it on the White, in another woman's voice, and then it had been a thing done to her. It was hers now. She had learned it on the rim of the Pans in the dark, failing seven times in twelve and then two, with Teo's hands turning over beside her: not away from it. Out from round it. Flat, as you hold your palm out to a horse.

"Saskia," she said. "Count."

"Hold," said Saskia. Her voice was steady now. "Hold—hold—*now.*"

Hester opened her hand.

Keeping was a door shutting. This was a door opening onto a wind, and the wind was the sea. She let the stillness go out of her palms, not pushed but let go, and felt the salt soften under them, and the seam give, a little, a hand's breadth. The water came through it harder and went across the floor and into the shaft-mouth. The plug, under the weight of it, tore loose from its socket with a crack like a tree going over, and the sea took it into the adit and up the shaft ahead of itself like a cork. The whole round mouth of the Founders' Shaft stood open, black, roaring.

"Hold," said Saskia. "Hold—two—three. *Now.*"

She opened again. The sea went up.

She could hear it going: a long hollow rising roar inside the shaft, climbing as the water climbed, like nothing she had a word for. She could feel the arrears go with it, the piled-up debt of the moment running out of the salt through her hands and away. A shovelful, and a pause.

Then the cold at her back lifted, as a hand lifts off a shoulder. Orsolya had stopped holding.

Hester looked round. The Warden stood waist-deep at the foot of the steps with her hands still under the water, holding nothing, and her face the colour of the salt. She was looking at the shaft: at the open black mouth of it, and the sea going into it in a hard black rope, and up.

"It's taking it," Orsolya said.

"Yes."

"Up to the White."

"Yes."

Orsolya looked at the shaft for three breaths. Hester knew what she was seeing, because Orsolya had told her twice. A girl of eleven at the Coldbank pit-head among the women, on the day the roof came in. Under her feet, in the third gallery, the Physic was keeping her father standing under the beam, and all round her the men were saying *if they'd only shored it, if they'd only*.

"*Shore it,*" Orsolya said. Then, louder, turning: "*Shore it, lads.* Brannoc. Iley. Up the steps, both of you. Fingers in the trunk, every slot you can reach, and hold it. All of it. Every stay in the Fifth. You hold that gallery dry while this lass does what she's doing, and you don't let go till I tell you."

"Warden—"

"*Now*, Brannoc."

They went. The young man went first, splashing, and the older woman after him. At the top of the three steps, where the brass trunk of the stay-lines came down out of the dark from the Fifth and ran along the Hush wall, they knelt and pushed their fingers into the brine-slots in its flank, where the live brine stood in the brass. Hester felt them go into the hold together, a smaller, colder stillness, running up the line.

"Mr. Dunmore," Orsolya said. "Go with them."

There was no answer. On the bottom step where Dunmore had stood there was a lantern, set down carefully, still burning. Far back across the Hush, in the dark, feet were running on salt toward the door. Then they were not.

Orsolya made a small sound that might have been a laugh. She waded back through the water to the steps and climbed them and knelt at the trunk between her two Fathomers, and put her white shaking fingers into the slots. Her stillness went out of her into the line, deep and old and enormous. Hester felt it pass along the wall above her head, a cold river going upward, and somewhere above them in the dark the stays of the Fifth Fathom took it and stiffened and held.

"There," Orsolya said, to no one, to the line. "There, now."

*I know her*, Hester had said in Sorle's study, to people who thought her mad. *She'll not let them drown.*

"Hold four," said Sorle. He was beside her in the water with his slate held up out of it, a stub of chalk in his blue fingers, writing. "The floor's taking strain on the north side. Give it four, Mrs. Orme."

"Hold four," said Saskia. "One. Two. Three. Four. Now."

Hester opened her hand. The sea went up.

It went on like that. Sorle said, later, forty minutes; Saskia said fifty-two, and hers was the watch that had stayed dry. Hold and open. Hold and open. Each time she opened, the sea went into the shaft in a black pulse and rose, roaring, until she lost it six hundred feet above her head, where the Well-Cap sat on the salt under the stars. Each time she held, the white fractures in the floor stopped spreading. Each time Saskia said *now* it was a little easier to open, and a little harder to come back.

Because it cost. The rush, the Primer called it, as if it were a thing that happened to other people. Every time she let go, the stillness she let go came back up her arms as heat, and the heat was in her chest, and her heart was going faster and faster: not the pounding of fear but something simpler and worse, a wheel spinning free. Sweat ran into her eyes and did not sting. The lanterns had got too bright. Saskia's voice came to her too fast, each word clipped off at the end, as if someone were winding the world on a key. The sound of the sea went up in pitch until it was nearly a whistle.

"Hold," said Saskia, from a great way off. "Hold. Vail? *Vail.* Now."

She opened her hand, and this time, when Saskia said *hold*, the roar did not fall back.

It went on, long and steady, no longer in pulses. The sea was going up the shaft without her: a river climbing, the pressure behind the seam running out evenly, of itself, the way a pan goes on talking quietly for an hour after the fire is drawn.

"It's going." Saskia's voice cracked upward. "It's going on its own. Vail, it doesn't need you. Take your hands off—"

She could not take her hands off. The wheel in her chest had no brake on it any more.

The whole Hush went white.

* * *

She was on her knees in the water. She did not remember kneeling. Her heart was going so fast it was no longer a beat but a hum, like the Hush's hum, like a bee in a jar. Her hands were still flat on the salt. She could not take them away. She could not see. Someone had her by the shoulders.

"Still runs to quick," said Sorle's voice in her ear, and it was the first time she had ever heard him sound frightened. "Still runs to quick, damn you, hold *on*—"

He had his hands over hers on the wall, in the water: the Physic's way, a still hand laid over a racing one in shared brine. The live brine was over all four of their hands, thick with the dissolved Hush. She felt his hold go into it, an old man's hold, thin and dry and enormously patient, a stillness that had sat at a desk for thirty-two years with nothing to do but think. It ran out of him into the brine and through the brine into her, because that was the law, because still runs to quick, and nothing in the world had ever been quicker than Hester Vail at that moment. It went into her hands and up her arms. It found the spinning wheel in her chest and laid itself across it like a palm across a top.

The hum slowed. It became a beat. Fast, too fast, but a beat. She could hear it. She could hear Saskia sobbing somewhere and saying *now, now, now* to nobody, and the sea going up the shaft on its own in its long steady roar.

Then Sorle's hands went slack on hers. He sat down in the water.

"Reader," she said. Her voice was nothing. "Reader."

He did not answer. He was sitting up to his chest in black water with his eyes open and his chalk still in his fingers. She knew what it was. She had done it herself on the White, for four minutes, and come back with the sky changed. He had given her his stillness, and now he was lagging.

She held his hand and waited. She counted. At a hundred and eighty he blinked, once, slowly, like an owl. At two hundred he said, in a perfectly ordinary voice, "—*on*, Miss Vail," finishing the sentence he had started three minutes before. Then he looked down at himself, at the water, and said, "Ah."

He lifted his left hand out of the brine. It was white to the wrist, the white of a Choir hand, and it stayed as it had lain over hers on the wall, flat, the fingers a little parted. She watched him tell it to close. It did not close.

"Ah," he said again, more quietly.

She meant to answer that. Then Saskia had her face between both hands and was saying her name, and Sorle was sitting further off on the edge of the brass housing, and the lantern had been moved. She had lost a minute. Two. The lag had waited, as lag did, until she put the weight down.

The roar in the shaft was dropping.

It dropped as a wind drops at evening on the White, in long lowering breaths. The seam still ran with water, but less, and less. The black flood across the clear floor of the Hush had stopped rising; it was going down, drawn back toward the seam in long slow currents, into the hollow behind the wall where the quickening had been. The lantern flames had stopped bowing east. They stood up straight.

The surf behind the wall was gone. The hum of the Hush came back, the old pressure in the teeth. But it was lower, Hester thought. Lower than it had been. As if something very large and very far away had let out a breath and not yet drawn the next one.

Saskia was holding her watch in both hands like something that might break. She looked at it, and at Hester, and put it back in her pocket, carefully, and sat down on the housing with her face in her hands.

For a while none of the three of them could manage a word.

* * *

When Hester could stand, she stood. Sorle had to help her and she had to help him, so it took some time. The water was below her knees now and going down, and she did not look at what the floor showed her under it.

At the top of the three steps, by the brass trunk of the stay-lines, the two Fathomers were sitting against the wall. The young man was awake and shaking, his teeth chattering, his arms wrapped round his knees. The older woman was lagging still. Her eyes were open on nothing.

Orsolya was kneeling between them, where she had knelt. Her hands were still at the trunk.

No. They had come out of the slots. They lay in her lap, palms up, open, the white fingers a little curled, as a sleeper's hands are curled. She was sitting back on her heels with her head bent a little forward, as though looking down into her own palms for something written there. She did not blink when Hester's lantern came up the steps.

"Warden," said the young Fathomer, Brannoc, through his chattering teeth. "She let go when it slowed. She said, *that's it, that's done.* And then she just—sat."

Hester knelt down in front of her. She knew the look of it from the gallery floor of the Fourth Fathom on the night of the Silent Supper, when Ama Tessaly sat down in the middle of a stay and did not get up again. But Ama's lips had gone grey, and Orsolya's had not. Orsolya's chest was moving, slower than sleep, a breath to every ten of Hester's.

Settled. Not to death. For hours, perhaps. Perhaps a day. Thirty years in the Choir and one more hold than her heart had wanted to give.

Hester sat back on her heels opposite her, so that they were two women kneeling face to face on a step in the dark with the water going down below them. She found that she was crying, and that her tears were leaving salt on her cheeks, and that she did not care.

"It went up," she told Orsolya. "It went up the shaft. All of it. The Fifth held. You held it. I told them you would."

Orsolya's open hands lay in her lap.

Below them in the Hush the last of the black water slid back through the seam into the emptied dark behind the wall, and was gone, and left the floor shining, pitted and scored with white fractures, drying already at the edges into a thin new skin of salt.


# Chapter 25: The Gull

> And the grey birds cried over Ostrine water
> where no grey bird has cried since.
>
> — *"The Last Fleet," Rim ballad, traditional*

They carried her up the Stair in a blanket slung between two porters, because her legs would not do it. She remembered pieces. The chalk-names going past at the height of her eyes, Ama Tessaly's new and white among the old. Saskia's voice somewhere below saying *mind her head, mind her head, for pity's sake*. A Proctor's lantern. The cold. The cold most of all: her clothes were soaked to the chest with seawater, and the seawater had begun, in the long climb, to dry on her into a crust, and the crust was not grain. It kept nothing. It only itched.

At the First Fathom someone tried to take her to the Physic wards and she said no, so clearly that the porters stopped.

"The Well-Cap," she said. "I want to see."

"Miss, you're not fit—"

"Take her," said Sorle's voice, from somewhere behind. He was walking, with a stick he had got from somewhere, and a Proctor holding his other arm. "Take her up, man. She has earned the view. So, God help me, have I."

They came out of the Gatehouse into the dark before dawn.

It was the hard dark of midwinter on the White, the frost so deep that the air seemed to ring with it. The Lantern stood up black against a sky going grey at its eastern edge. The salt of the Shelf was stiff with rime and squealed under the porters' boots. And it was full of people: Proctors with lanterns, Readers with blankets over their gowns, aspirants still in their Assay Night finery, half-drunk and white-faced, porters and cooks, all standing in the frost on the eastern flank of the dome, in a long uneven line, looking east.

There was steam in the east.

The porters carried her round the foot of the Lantern and set her down on the salt at the edge of the crowd, and Saskia came and knelt and put a second blanket round her and held it closed at her throat. Hester looked.

A quarter of a mile out on the White, where the Well-Cap had sat for nine centuries on its square of founders' brick, there was no Well-Cap. There was a lake.

It lay on the white plain like ink spilled on paper, roughly round, four or five hundred yards across, perhaps more. It was steaming. The water was warmer than the midwinter air, and the steam hung a man's height above it and drifted south on the faint wind. Near the middle the water was still welling, slowly, from the mouth of the Founders' Shaft, in a low dome a few yards wide, like a spring in a field. At the edges it had frozen. All round the lake, where the black water met the white salt, a rim of ice was forming, grey and thin and creaking, a yard wide, two yards, and the frost was growing out across the water on it in long feathered spears.

The iron Well-Cap lay on the salt a hundred yards from the shore, upside down, where the sea had thrown it.

"Oh," said Saskia, beside her, on a long breath. That was all.

The whole long line of them stood on the frozen dome and watched the steam go up. Somewhere behind Hester a woman was crying, quietly, the way you cry at a wedding. The sound of the water came to them across the quarter-mile of salt. It was a soft lapping, a small slap and suck where the ice met it, and it was a sound that not one person standing on the Shelf had ever heard in their lives, except in a bathtub.

* * *

The light came up slowly. That was midwinter: the sun took its time. The sky went from grey to pearl to a thin cold rose, and the White went rose with it, and the steam on the lake went gold at its upper edge where the first light caught it. And as the light came, people began to walk out.

A few aspirants first, daring each other. Then Bassano, unmistakable in a fur hat. Then more, and Proctors half-heartedly trying to stop them, and then not trying. Hester got up. Saskia said "Vail—" and then saw her face, and got up too, and put an arm under her shoulder, and they walked out across the frozen salt together, very slowly. Sorle came behind them on his stick, saying nothing at all.

The shore of the lake, when they reached it, was a ridge of new ice standing up from the salt like the lip of a cup. Beyond it the grey rim of frozen sea ran out a few yards and thinned and went black. Hester stood at the ridge and looked down.

The water was clear. She had thought it was black; it was not. It was black the way deep water is black, and at the shallow edge, where the ice was thin, she could see down through it to the salt floor of the lake a yard below. On the salt floor, lying in the slow current, there was weed.

It was green: not the copper-green of the pans, but green as new grass is green, as Harl cabbages are green in a market in spring. Long ribbons of it, a dozen, more, lifting and lying down again in the welling current, and the light coming through the water lit them from inside. As she watched, one ribbon lifted and turned over and showed the paler underside of itself, and on it, clinging, there was something small and dark and many-legged that moved.

"Crab," said Saskia, in a strange voice. "That's a crab. Isn't it? Pip has a picture book. That's a crab."

It was a crab. It was the size of a sol, dark green and mottled, and it went sideways off the weed and down onto the salt floor and stopped there, waving one small claw at nothing.

And past it, in the deeper water beyond the ice, something flicked. A glint, silver, there and gone. Then another. Then, quite clearly, five or six of them together, small and quick and silver as new flecks, turning all at once in the current as if they were one thing: fish. Living fish, under the ice, in a lake on the White, going about the business of being fish as though nothing had happened to them at all. As though the afternoon on which the sea had last been a sea had simply gone on.

Hester knelt down on the ice. She put her bare hand flat on it, over the place where the crab was. She did not need the hold. The ice was cold and wet and tasted, when she put her fingers to her mouth, of salt and of nothing else. It was not sweet. There were no coins on her eyelids. It was only salt: the plain bright startled taste of something that was alive and moving and had no intention of being anything else.

Quick, she thought. All of it quick.

* * *

The gull came out of the steam a little after sunrise.

She knew it before she heard it properly. She had heard it once, in a single grain of warm white salt on a customs bench, on the first morning of autumn: a high falling cry, two notes and a third that broke. Teo had found it for her in a hymnal woodcut in the Brinestacks. *And the gulls, the birds of the sea, crying.* There were no gulls. There had been no gulls on the Rim for nine hundred years.

It came out of the steam near the middle of the lake, in the water, struggling: a lump of wet grey and white, low in the water, beating at the surface with something that might have been wings, and going under, and coming up. The crowd on the shore had seen it. A sound went along them, a long indrawn *oh*. The thing in the water beat at the surface again, and got its head up, and Hester saw a yellow beak and a round furious eye. It was swimming. It was swimming toward the ice on the northern side, where the frozen rim was widest, and it reached the ice and got its breast up onto it and hung there, its feet going, and then hauled itself out, all at once, and stood.

It was the size of a hen, perhaps, or a little bigger, grey on the back and white below, with black at the tips of its long wings. It stood on the ice with the black water behind it and the steam going up, and it shook itself. It shook itself from the head down, all over, the way a dog shakes, and the water flew off it in a spray that caught the low sun and went gold. It opened its wings and shook them too, and folded them, and opened them again.

Then it put its head back and cried.

Two notes and a third that cracked. A sound like a gate hinge. Like a child calling from a long way off. The sound went out across the White, over the frozen salt and the silent crowd and the black lake steaming, and came back off the dome of the Halcyon as an echo. It was the loudest thing in the world.

Beside her, Saskia had both hands over her mouth.

The gull cried again. Then it ran three steps along the ice and opened its wings and was in the air. It went up into the light, over the steam, beating hard. It circled once over the lake, low, as if to see what it had come out of. Then it turned west, toward the city, toward the rose-lit roofs of Ambry two miles off on the old shore, and flew. It was a grey speck and then nothing.

It was a long time before anyone on the shore moved.

* * *

When Hester turned round at last, looking for Sorle, she saw the Chancellor.

Perpetua Holm had been carried out of the Readers' Close in a chair. It stood on the salt at the foot of the Lantern, a carved chair with poles for bearers, and four porters stood beside it, stamping in the cold. The Chancellor sat in it as she had sat in the Hall of Assay the day before: upright, wrapped to the chin in black fur, her hands folded on her knees, her white face turned east. She had not moved, as far as Hester could tell, since the porters set her down. She was looking at the place in the sky where the gull had gone.

Hester did not decide to go to her. She found she was walking, with Saskia's arm under hers, to the foot of the Lantern. Up close, the Chancellor's face was very old and very still and quite composed. It was wet. There were lines of salt drying on it, from the corners of her eyes down past her mouth, as the tears of the Settled always dried. Hester had expected many things of that face. She had expected anger, or calculation, or the sharp blank fear that had been on Deputy Vey's face on the dais the day before. She had not expected grief.

There was grief in it, a great deal of grief, like a weight at the bottom of a scale, and no surprise at all.

"Chancellor," Hester said.

The old eyes moved and found her. It took some time. Holm's voice, when it came, was the voice of the Settled, a long slow whisper with pauses in it, as if each word had to be carried up from a long way down.

"Miss Vail," she said. "Aske's aspirant. Sorle's, now, I hear."

"Yes, Chancellor."

"Assayed with distinction." A pause. "Yesterday."

"Yes."

The Chancellor looked past her, east, at the lake. The steam was lifting off it now in the sun, thinning, so that you could see the whole black width of it. There was a line of ice all round its rim, like a ring of salt round the edge of a glass.

"It was always going to come back, Miss Vail," said Perpetua Holm. "We only hoped to be dead first."

Then she closed her eyes. One of the porters looked at another, and they bent and took up the poles, and the chair rose and turned and went away toward the Readers' Close, swaying, with the old woman in it upright and motionless and wet-faced. Hester stood in the frost and watched it go, and did not know what she had just been told, and knew that it was something.

* * *

The rest came fast, as the rest always does. The Halcyon was an institution, and an institution has a way of turning even a sea on the White into paper.

Orsolya Thane woke on the evening of the twenty-second, on a cot in the Physic wards, twenty hours after she had sat down at the top of the three steps. She asked for water, and for Chief Proctor Gage, and for Hester Vail.

Hester was four cots down under two blankets, with Rule's orders not to stand. She stood. A Physic's assistant opened his mouth and shut it again.

Gage came with a clerk and a lamp. He set a stool square to the cot and sat on it with his cap on his knee, and the clerk opened a ledger on the next bed.
