Orsolya was sitting up against the salt wall with a cup in her two hands. She could not quite close them round it. She looked at Hester a long moment, and then at Gage.

"I'd like her to hear it," she said. "Write down that she's here."

"Aspirant Vail, present." The clerk wrote. "Warden, before you say anything, you should know that—"

"It goes in that book. I know. That's why I asked for the book." Orsolya set the cup on the blanket. "Caution me, Chief Proctor. Then we'll start."

He cautioned her, in the old form, clipped and complete. She heard him out. Then she began, not at the Old Tap, but at the beginning.

"Seedmonth of 901," she said. "The Chancellor's rooms in the Readers' Close, at the Lantern Hour. I took up the returns ledger and the Choir's sick-roll and put them on her table. Perpetua Holm was in the chair. Deputy Chancellor Vey was standing at her shoulder. I asked for an inquiry. I asked for money for twice the Choir. I asked for the Delvers to be stopped on the Shelf." Her voice did not change. "The Deputy did the talking. The Chancellor sat and let him. His words were, *Manage it quietly, Warden. You've always managed.* Write that down, lad. Those words."

The clerk's pen had stopped. Gage looked at it, and it went on.

She gave the rest in order, with dates, the way a Choir roster is called. The sleeves under Coldwater Street and the brinewright who had fitted them, by name. The contract with Ferrers and Lisk, and the three letters Edwin signed it with, on her word, and where every sol of it had gone: stipends, widows' pensions, never a fleck to her. Bett Linnow, Jem Corrie on the nineteenth, and the three others, by name and plinth. Ama Tessaly, and every shift she had let her sing. Then the sixteenth of Russet.

"Corvin went down to the Old Tap at the ninth bell to shut it," she said. "He meant to be back up for this lass at the tenth. I kept him there, with his hand on the wheel, in Hush grain. Edwin Dunmore and I brought him up the Stair on a cask-trolley, because the porters have the lifts after the eighth. We waited on the turn below his Cell while she stood at the door. We waited again while Benedek Sorle came and went. We had him in a little after the half, and I put a dish in his basin for you to write down."

Hester did not move. She watched the clerk's pen, and the lamp on the ledger, and Orsolya's open hands on the blanket.

It took an hour and a half. At the end of it Gage stood, and put his cap under his arm, and said the words of arrest, and Orsolya bowed her head to them as if to a hymn.

Only then did she look at Hester again.

"Did it all go up?"

"All of it."

Orsolya shut her eyes. "Good," she said. "Then that's done."

Jerome Pell was taken at his sister's house in the Middle Ward on the twenty-third, with the Cold House's Halcyon maintenance receipts in a tin box under her spare bed. Edwin Dunmore had bought passage on an eastbound skiff at the second bell of the twenty-second, paying in silver and not haggling. The skiff was overdue at Quell.

Lucan Ferrers walked into the Assize Chamber on the morning of the twenty-third, without an appointment, in the coat he had worn into the Cistern, which still had brick-dust on the cuffs. He laid on the clerk's table a fair copy, in his own hand, of the residual grain contract between the Office of the Warden of the Lower Fathoms and Messrs. Ferrers and Lisk, Exchequer Yard, with a sworn statement that he had found the original in his father's study. By noon it was in the *Courant*.

Teo told her about the Cistern that night, sitting on the end of her cot with a twist of saltcake he was not supposed to have brought in.

"You'd think the dark would be the thing," he said. "It wasn't. It was the noise. You know I hear it as voices. All the way down from the grate, the line was full of them. Three hundred, near enough, in the brass. Not words. Like a congregation told to hum that's forgotten the hymn. And the rods were cold enough to take your skin off. Lucan had his watch open on a brick and counted the minutes from the half out loud. I don't think he knew he was doing it.

"We couldn't hear the bell, of course. At twelve he just said 'Now,' very quietly, as if he were asking for the salt, and we put the saws in. Brass is stubborn. It took longer than you'd credit. He slipped and laid the back of his hand open and never said a word. He kept sawing with the blood running into the cut."

He turned the saltcake over and did not eat it.

"And when the rods parted, the voices stopped. Not all at once. They went *back*. Like people coming out of chapel and going home down different streets, one and then the next and the next. I knelt there with my hand on the pipe and listened to every one of them go home. One of them was Tib, I think. Don't ask me how I knew." He smiled, crookedly. "Then Agathe came down the cellar steps with a lamp and a bowl of water for Lucan's hand, and said, 'Well, they're dry, the lot of them,' and boxed both our ears for frightening her. I've never been so glad to be hit."

Hester said nothing. She took the saltcake out of his hand and broke it in two, and gave him half.

The Deputy Chancellor issued a statement on the twenty-fourth, read aloud at the Long Table at the Lantern Hour. It described the events of the night of the twenty-first of Rime as a natural quickening event of unusual magnitude, vented through a disused shaft by the timely action of members of the Halcyon, whom it did not name. It promised a Board of Inquiry. It did not mention a gull, or Seedmonth of 901. Somebody among the third-years laughed out loud at *natural*, and the clerk read on over him.

One pulse of the vent had backed up an old founders' adit into the east gallery of the Fourth, before Sorle lengthened the count, and the overflow stacks there had been under brine for an hour. Wynn Callow had himself carried down to look at them, and sat in the doorway most of an afternoon, and said nothing to anyone about what he had seen.

Agathe's letter came up on the twenty-fourth. *My dear. They are dry, every one. Your Tib is clear. Nell Corrie is crazed all over like a dropped plate but clear under it, and holding. They have sent us a Mrs. Arden from the Board who seems sensible. Come when you are able. Tide willing. A.L.*

And on the shore of the lake, through all of it, people came: across the Causeway by the hundred and then by the thousand, in carts and on foot and in landships that tacked up and down the black water as near as they dared. They brought children. They filled jars and carried them home. Lowside had already named it the Tear. On the second day the gull came back. It sat on the upturned Well-Cap for an hour in the afternoon sun with the crowd watching it in perfect silence, and then flew off again toward the city, crying.

* * *

Annis Rule had listened to Hester's chest on the morning of the twenty-second with a horn trumpet and her eyes shut, for a long time.

"Your heart keeps its own time now, Miss Vail," she said at last. She let Hester listen to it through the horn: small and quick and not quite even, a tick with a catch in it, like a pan with muck in the bottom. "That will not mend. You may release small things, slowly, with someone counting. But another rush like that will stop it."

Hester buttoned her shirt. "Ta," she said.

They vented Corvin Aske over six days, and they did it small. Rule had said small things, slowly, with someone counting, and Sorle took her at her word: a sixth of the arrears a day, a finger's width of salt at a time, his count, and Rule's fingers on Hester's pulse throughout.

Sorle would not let them do it faster. He had done the arithmetic on four slates, sitting up in a Physic bed, and done it again with Hester checking every line. A man kept in Hush grain for three months, he said, carried arrears enough to boil the blood in his veins if they came out at once. Spread over six days, in a warm bath, in stages, they might come out as a fever.

"Annealed," Saskia said.

"Annealed," said Sorle, with no trace of irony. "Thank you, Mrs. Orme. Yes."

They did it in the Physic wards on the Third Fathom, in the long tiled bath-room at the end, where the Physicians returned the Halcyon's own kept staff when a cure was found. Rule oversaw it. She had returned two hundred people in her life, and said on the first morning that she had never returned one who had been kept against his will and was glad to begin. When the sheet came off the casing, Hester stood and looked at it in the bath-room lamplight: the thin man standing in clear salt with one arm reaching and his mouth open on a word, and the faint widdershins spiral in the grain of it, turning round him like a shell.

Clear. Three months in Hush grain and still clear, without a breath of bloom. *The living keep clear.*

They lowered him into the warm brine. And every morning and every evening for six days, Hester went into the release with her hands on the casing under the water, and opened them, a little. Sorle sat on a stool at the bath's edge with his slate and his watch and told her when, his left hand in his lap in a wool mitten. It had not closed since the Hush, and Rule did not think it would. Saskia came between her lamp-work shifts and counted. Teo came and sat on the tiled floor with his back against the wall and his shawl round him and did not count anything, but stayed. The salt went down around Aske a finger's width a day, and the water grew hotter, and Rule's assistants drew it off and ran in more. On the third day the casing was thin enough that Hester could see his eyes clearly through it, open and short-sighted and fixed on something on the far side of the room that had not been there for three months. On the fourth day his skin above the waterline began to steam. On the fifth day the bath-room was hot as the Pans, and Hester came out of every release with her heart ticking and catching and her shirt soaked through, and Sorle made her sit with her hands in a basin of cold brine, his own good hand laid over them, until he could count her pulse without losing his place.

On the morning of the sixth day, the twenty-eighth of Rime, the last of the salt went.

It went as the last of the ice goes off a pond, all at once, the final thin sheet cracking and sliding off him into the water. And Corvin Aske's reaching arm finished its movement. His hand closed on the air where the Old Tap had been on the night of the sixteenth of Russet, and his mouth finished its word.

"—*you can't* hold it, Orsolya, it'll only grow—"

He stopped. He was standing waist-deep in a hot bath with steam rising off his shoulders, his spectacles crooked on his nose where they had sat for three months, and an assistant on either side holding his elbows. He blinked. He looked at the tiled walls, and at the steam, and at Reader Rule, and at Sorle on his stool, and then at Hester, standing in the water in front of him with her hands still open on nothing.

"Oh," he said. "You tasted it."

"I tasted it."

He took that in. Hester watched him take it in: the bath, the Physicians, Sorle, the heat. He had always been quick.

"How long?" he said.

"Three months."

"Three—" His eyebrows went up over the crooked spectacles. "Have I missed the Assay?"

"You have."

"Did you pass?"

"With distinction."

"Well, of course you did," said Corvin Aske, and began, absurdly, to laugh, and then to shake, and the assistants caught him as his knees went. They got him out of the bath and into a blanket and onto a bench. He sat there with the blanket round him and his teeth chattering and the steam coming off his hair, and held out his right hand to look at it.

It did not open properly. The fingers stayed curled, stiffly, as a man's fingers stay when he has held a pen too long in the cold. He tried to straighten them with his left hand and they went straight and then curled again, slowly, of their own accord. Reader Rule bent and looked at them and said nothing, and then said, in her grey voice, "That will be the arrears. Six days of heat through one hand. Some of it may come back. Some of it will not."

Aske looked at his hand for a long time. Then he looked up at Hester.

"Orsolya," he said.

"In the Proctory cells."

"Is she—"

"She held the Fifth," Hester said. "While I vented. She sent her people to the trunk and held it herself. She's Settled some. She's all right. She told Gage everything. I was there."

Aske closed his eyes behind the crooked spectacles. When he opened them they were wet.

"Of course she did," he said. "Of course she did, the daft woman." Then, more quietly: "I was going to ask her first. Before Gage. I owed her that. I thought—" He stopped. "Never mind what I thought. It's three months ago." He turned the stiff hand over in his lap and looked at its palm. "Is there tea? I'm told there's always tea."

Sorle was already pouring it, one-handed. He put the cup into Aske's left hand, since the right would not hold it, and put a pinch of salt in it without asking. Aske drank, and made a face, and drank again.

"Disgusting," he said. "Tell me everything. Start with what you tasted."


# Chapter 26: What the Salt Keeps

> What is kept is not lost.
>
> — *Motto of the Halcyon*

The Proctory cells were on the First Fathom, behind the Chief Proctor's office: three small rooms cut square into the salt, each with a cot and a stool and a door of iron bars. Somebody, a long time ago, had scratched a halcyon into the wall of the middle cell, a crude one, with a crooked wave under it.

Orsolya was in the middle cell, on the stool, wrapped in an iron-grey Choir blanket, looking at the halcyon.

"Hester," she said, when the Proctor had unlocked the door and gone back to the end of the passage. She said it without turning her head, and she did not say *chick*. "Sit down. Before you fall down."

Hester sat on the edge of the cot. She could see Orsolya's hands in her lap under the edge of the grey wool. They were still white. They lay open, palms up, as they had lain on the founders' step in the Hush, as if the Warden had forgotten how to close them and had stopped trying.

"Corvin's up," Hester said.

"I know. Gage told me. His hand?"

"Stiff. Rule says some of it will come back."

"Some of it won't." Orsolya nodded slowly. "No. It wouldn't."

"He asked after you. First thing, nearly."

Orsolya closed her eyes, briefly. Then she looked at Hester for the first time, a long look, Lowside to Lowside, the look of one woman checking another for a short weight.

"I'll not say sorry to you," she said. "I'll say it to Corvin. I'll say it to Ruth Linnow, if she'll have it, and to Nell Corrie, and to the families of the other four, God help me, and to the Choir. Ama was sixty-six." She was quiet a moment. "But I'll not say it to you, Hester. You'd not believe it. And you'd be right not to, because I'd do half of it again. Not Corvin. Not the Cold House. But I'd go down to the Fifth again tomorrow and hold that line, and I'd hold it with whatever I had. You know that."

"I know."

"Good."

"Why the seventh dish?" Hester said.

Orsolya was a while answering. "I'd said it to Perpetua Holm in her own rooms, and she sat there like a stone and let Vey answer for her. I wanted her to hear it said out loud. In the Hall, before the whole city, by somebody she couldn't send down to the Fifth or pension off. A lass off a customs bench with a tongue like Corvin's." She looked at her open hands. "She heard it."

"And your hand? At the stove. You showed me the turn."

"I did." She did not go on at once. "I've asked myself that. You'd stood at Corvin's door the morning we found him. I knew you'd seen the grain. And I showed you anyway, the left wrist and all." She turned one hand over in her lap. "Part of me wanted somebody to see it. You can carry a thing like that a long while on your own. Not for ever."

She looked at the halcyon on the wall. "Brannoc came by. He says the Choir's to be stood down a month while the Board looks at the stays. Says the Hush has gone quiet as a church. First time since I came into the Choir." She breathed out. "Your road."

"Corvin's road."

"Your hands, though."

Hester said nothing. When Orsolya spoke again her voice had gone lower and plainer, the voice she had used on the White with Lucan's casing dragging at the lip of the sink.

"Did it hurt?" she said. "Letting go?"

Hester thought about the wheel spinning free in her chest, and Saskia's voice going fast as a wound clock, and Sorle's old hands over hers in the black water. She thought about a boy's voice in a hot room above Tanner's Row, and her own hand turning over.

"Yes," she said.

Orsolya nodded. For the first time since Hester had come in, something in her face eased.

"Good," she said. "Means you did it right."

* * *

On the afternoon of the twenty-ninth of Rime, Hester went to the Cold House, and her father came with her.

She had not asked him. She had gone to the Pans because she had told him she would, and he had looked up at her through the steam from pan nineteen as he always did. Then, for the first time in her memory, he had set down his rake in the middle of a boil, told the boiler on pan eighteen to mind it, washed at the pump, put on the clean coat from the Assay, and walked out beside her into the cold without a word.

They did not talk as they went. At the top of the rise on Chandler's Lane, where you could see the roofs and the sea-wall and the White, they both stopped. There was a black mark on the White now, two miles out, east of the Halcyon's dome: a long black oval with the low sun on it, like a coin dropped on a tablecloth. Aldous said, "Well," and started walking again.

The board over the door said THE LIVING KEEP CLEAR in its two hands, the old and the careful. Her father stood on the step and looked up at it, and Hester saw his jaw work.

"Da," she said. "You needn't."

"I know I needn't."

He went in.

There was a new card beside the ledger: *Mrs. H. Arden, Director (acting)*. Hester signed the visitors' book and held out the pen, and her father looked at it before he took it. He had sat at this desk once already this winter with a release form in front of him and the pen in his fist, and put the pen down, and gone out. He took the pen now and signed under her, slowly, in big careful capitals: A. VAIL. FATHER.

Hester asked for Mrs. Arden. She came out of the back office, a square grey woman with ink on her second finger, and when Hester asked for the keeping form for two hundred and eleven she looked at her a moment and went and found it without asking why.

It was the same form. Hester had not seen it in eleven years and knew it by heart: the printed heads, *Sleeper*, *Condition*, *Signatory*, *Relation*, and her own name under them in a younger, tighter hand. *Hester Vail, sister.* There was no line for what the sleeper himself might have said. There was a margin.

She took the clerk's pen and wrote in it, small and square:

*He said to me: Let me sleep. The night before Lantern Night, 892. H. Vail, 29 Rime 903.*

She blotted it. Her father read it upside down across the desk, his lips moving. Mrs. Arden read it too, and put the form back in its file, and the file in its drawer, and locked the drawer. It was on the record.

Then they went through the double doors, which he had not been through in nine years, and down the six worn steps into the cold sweet air.

The light came in long bars through the high windows and lay across the eight rows. The sleepers lay as they had been laid: the old man with his mouth open on a word, the girl with the red ribbon, the woman with the baby. Mother Agathe was at the far end of the second row with her brush and her oil can. She saw them and straightened, slowly, and did not come. She lifted one hand a little and let it fall, and went back to her brushing.

By the eastern wall there was an empty plinth where Bett Linnow had lain. Someone had put a sprig of Hask juniper on it.

There was another in the seventh row. Hester went to it first, while her father stood at the foot of the steps. The card was still in its brass holder: *J. Corrie*. The stone had been swept and oiled, and there was nothing on it. On the next plinth Nell Corrie lay in a casing crazed from end to end with fine white lines, like a dropped plate held together by its glaze, and she was clear under it, and dry. Agathe had oiled her till she shone.

Hester stood between them. She had nothing to put on the stone. She had chosen the night, and the night had cost this, and it would go on costing it. She put two fingers on the empty plinth, where his feet would have been, and then went back up the row.

Aldous had not moved. Hester stopped beside him and looked down the fourth row to the third window, and waited. It took him a long time. Then he went along the row, and she went with him, and they came to two hundred and eleven.

Tib lay on his back with his head turned a little toward the window. His eyes were half open. His hand was curled on his chest round a pencil that was not there. His casing was clear from end to end and perfectly dry. The light came through it and lay on him, and you could tell where the salt ended and the air began only by a faint brightness along its edges.

Aldous looked at his son.

He stood beside the plinth with his big burned hands hanging at his sides. Then he put out his right hand, the good one, and laid it flat on the casing over Tib's chest, over the curled hand. Do not touch the casings. He left his hand there. His shoulders moved once, and then were still.

Hester stood on the other side of the plinth. She had thought since the Hush about what she would say, and had not got further than the first line. She did not need to. You did not push it out. You opened your hand.

"Tib," she said. "It's Hester. Da's here. He's come to see you."

The light lay across the rows.

"I've to tell you something," she said, "and I should have told you eleven years ago, and I'm telling you now with Da here so I'll not be able to pretend afterward that I didn't. You said something to me. The night before Lantern Night, in the room in Tanner's Row. You were burning up. You opened your eyes and you said, *Let me sleep, Hes. Just let me sleep.*" Her voice was steady. That surprised her. "And I knew what you meant. You didn't mean keep me. You meant let me go. You were fourteen and you'd had the bloom three weeks and you knew what it was. And I came down here and I signed the paper anyway. I told myself you'd asked for sleep, and keeping's a kind of sleep, so I'd done what you asked. I told myself that every month for eleven years. It isn't true. I kept you because I couldn't stand to let you go. Not for you. For me."

Her father's hand on the casing had not moved. She did not look at his face.

"I'm sorry," she said. "That's the first thing. It was your sleep, and I took it off you, and I've been paying six sols a month so I'd not have to think about it.

"And the second thing is this. I'll not let you go today. I want you to know why, because you've a right to know. It's not because I can't. I think I could now. I've learned how." She looked at the clear salt over his face. "It's because I don't know enough yet. There's a lake out on the White, Tib, a real one, with fish in it. There's a gull. There's things that have been kept nine hundred years and come back alive and whole. And I don't know yet what that means for you. Nobody does. So I'm going to find out.

"And I promise you this." She put her own hand flat on the casing, beside her father's, over the curled fingers. The salt was cold and dry. "When I know how to bring you back properly, or when I know how to let you go, I'll do it. Whichever's right. For you, not for me. And before I do it, whichever it is, I'll tell you first. Here. Out loud. The way I should have done."

She stopped. The ward was quiet. Down at the end of the second row Agathe's brush had stopped.

Aldous took his hand off the casing. He looked at the print of it, a faint mist on the salt that was already fading, and then at his daughter across the plinth. His eyes were red and wet and small.

"Well," he said. His voice was thick. Then, after a long while: "He'd have told you that was a daft promise."

"He would."

"Made it anyway."

"Yes."

Her father nodded. He looked down at Tib a while longer. Then he did a thing she had not seen him do in eleven years, or in thirty-three. He bent over the plinth and put his lips to the salt over his son's forehead and kept them there. Then he straightened, and wiped his mouth with the back of his wrist, and turned and walked away up the row without looking back.

Hester stayed. Mother Agathe came up the row with her can and her brush and stopped beside her and looked down at two hundred and eleven.

"He's clear, my dear," she said. "Look at him. Clear as the day they did it."

"I know."

"The living keep clear," said Agathe, and patted Hester's arm once with her small dry hand, and went on down the row.

* * *

On the morning of the thirtieth Sorle sent for her.

His study was as it had been in Fallow, after the sink: books on every flat surface, slates propped against the books, the kept moth on the desk in its globe of salt. One slate, propped against a pile of Horology quarterlies, bore in large chalk capitals the words *NATURAL QUICKENING EVENT*, underlined twice, with a row of exclamation marks after it that went off the edge.

"Sit," said Sorle.

She sat. He did not. He took down a long narrow ledger bound in black cloth gone brown at the corners, and laid it open on the desk in front of her, so that the kept moth had to be moved. It was the register of the Black Bench of Horology. The names went back up the page, a few to a year, and then whole years with no name at all, and then, three years ago, his two third-years, who were terrified of him.

Under them, in fresh ink, still a little shiny, he had written:

*Hester Vail. Turning Intake, 903. Assayed with distinction, 21 Rime 903. Admitted to this Bench, 30 Rime 903, by B. Sorle, Reader.*

"You've been on my Bench since Fallow," he said. "Informally. Under protest from the Registrar. This makes it law." He held out the pen. "You sign under it. There."

She signed. Her hand was steady. She blotted it.

"There," said Sorle. He closed the ledger one-handed and sat down at last on the other side of the desk, his white left hand lying open on the blotter where he had put it, and looked at her.

"I should tell you," he said, "that you will be a very great trial to me. You have no theory. You have no manners. You hold like a miser and release like a drunkard. You think with your tongue. You took a matter of the gravest institutional importance into your own hands without the slightest reference to proper authority, and you took me with you, and I have not been so cold or so wet or so frightened since I was a child."

"Yes, Reader."

"And you were right," said Sorle. "Which I find, after thirty-two years, I had entirely forgotten the taste of." He looked at the slate with *NATURAL QUICKENING EVENT* on it. "It is not, I discover, a sweet taste. It is very like salt."

* * *

She found Lucan on the Choir Steps in the afternoon, which was not a place she had expected to find him.

Snow had drifted into the corners of the tiers in thin grey crusts. He was sitting near the top, alone, with his arms round his knees, looking east at the Tear: the black oval on the white, the crowd along the near edge like a line of ants, the little sails of the landships tacking up and down the water.

She climbed up and sat on the tier below him. He did not seem surprised.

"My father has resigned the Exchequer," he said, without preamble. "This morning. *For reasons of health.* The Assize will hold a hearing in Thaw. Mr. Lisk is in Hask, it seems. Visiting relations." He looked at his gloved hands. "He has not spoken to me since the twenty-third. My sister writes that he has had my rooms in the Upper Ward cleared and my books sent to the Halcyon in crates, addressed in his own hand. She thinks that's a hopeful sign."

"Is it?"

"I've no idea. I've never had a hopeful sign from my father before. I don't know what one looks like." He was quiet. "Sorle has written me into his register. Properly, this morning, under you. He said that if he must suffer one insufferable aspirant he may as well suffer two, since the insufferability appears to be cumulative rather than multiplicative, and he would like to test the hypothesis."

"God help us both."

"Yes." He turned his head and looked down at her. His face was thin in the cold light. There was a scab across the back of his right hand where the saw had slipped in the Cistern, healing badly. "Vail. On the White, in Fallow. When you kept me. I said I'd repay it."

"You've repaid it. You cut the line."

"That wasn't repayment. That was—" He looked out at the Tear. "That was what wanted doing. You taught me that. It's a Lowside phrase. I've caught it like a cold." He was quiet for a while. Then, in a different voice, shorter: "I've never owed anyone anything before. I didn't like it. I thought it would be like a bill. Something one paid, and was done with."

"And?"

"And I find I don't mind owing you," said Lucan Ferrers.

She looked up at him. He looked back, and did not look away, which was new.

"That's a start," Hester said.

They sat in the cold a long time after that, watching the landships on the black water. When the light went, he stood and offered her his hand to get up. She took it, though she did not need to.

* * *

The Ebbtide Vigil fell on the last night of the year.

Every year of her life Hester had watched the Tiders come out of the Lowside chapels at dusk in their grey shawls, with their candles, and walk out past the end of the Causeway onto the bare salt, singing, to meet the tide. They stood in the cold and waited for the sea, and the sea did not come, and at the twelfth bell they walked home. Lowside called it the Wet-Foot Walk and made gentle jokes about it, and gave the walkers hot cider at Eastgate coming home, because it was a cold thing to do every year for nine hundred years and be wrong.

This year the whole city came.

They came out of Eastgate at dusk like a river, and the river was lit: Tiders and not, Lowside and Upper Ward, delvers and glaziers and clerks and children, down off the Causeway and out across the White in the dark, not a procession any more but a great slow field of small lights a quarter-mile wide, going east, toward the black water. From the dome of the Halcyon, as Hester came down off it with the others, it looked as if the sky had been laid on the salt.

Teo had taken his grey Tider shawl out of his chest that afternoon and looked at it a long while and put it on. Hester, in the same hour, had taken down the two dishes that had sat on her shelf since Russet, with their two kept drops of brine, and put a finger to each, and opened her hand. They had gone to water at the first touch.

They walked together. Saskia was beside Teo with Pip on her hip and Hennie Orme on her other arm. Pip held a candle in both fists and asked every few yards whether there would be fish. Lucan walked a little apart, and then not quite so far apart. Corvin Aske came as far as the Gatehouse on Sorle's arm, both of them wrapped to the eyes, and stood there to watch them go, and lifted his good hand. And at the foot of the Causeway, where it came down onto the salt, a short wide man in a clean coat was standing out of the stream of people with a candle that had gone out. He fell in beside Hester without a word, and walked with her a quarter of a mile, perhaps more. Then he stopped, and said, "That'll do me," and touched her arm, and turned back toward the city.

The shore of the Tear was a ridge of grey ice, and the candles came down to it and spread out along it, left and right, until the black water had a rim of light all round it. The Tiders began the hymn they always sang, the old one about the tide coming home over the sand. Hester had heard it all her life and never listened to the words. She listened now. People who did not know the words took it up, humming, until the whole shore was singing. The sound went out over the water and did not come back, because there was nothing out there for it to come back off. There was only the dark, and the water, and the faint smell of weed.

Teo touched her arm.

"Come and read it with me," he said.

They went down off the ridge onto the ice-rim, where the frozen sea ran out a yard or two from the shore before it thinned. Teo knelt, and Hester beside him. Under their knees the ice was grey and rough and new, and in it, where it met the shore, there was a thin white crust: salt, coming out of the seawater as it froze, as salt comes out of any sea at its edges in the cold. Salt that had been sea on the twenty-first of Rime and had only begun, these last nine days, to be anything else.

Teo took off his gloves and laid both bare hands flat on it and shut his eyes, and went into his poor restless hold. It was steady tonight. His breath slowed. His lips went pale. She took off her own right glove and touched one finger to the crust and put the finger to her tongue.

It tasted of the sea. Not sweet, not heavy; no coins on her eyelids. Salt and cold and something under the salt, bright and wet and startled, as when you bite your tongue. It tasted like tears, which she supposed it was. It tasted of something living.

Beside her, Teo had gone still. Not the hold's stillness. Something else. His eyes were open, but he was not looking at anything on the ice.

"Teo."

"Wait." His voice was low. "Wait. There's—"

She waited. Up on the shore the singing went on, the tide coming home over the sand. The candles moved along the ice-rim like a slow river.

"There's someone," Teo said.

He did not move his hands. He was listening, as she had seen him listen in the Brinestacks, for a sound inside the Roar, and it was there.

"It's a voice," he said. "Hester. A person. Not the gull. Not the sea. A *person*." His breath was coming fast. "One word. Over and over." He swallowed, and shaped it slowly, carefully, as you shape a word in a language you do not know. "*Aelith?* Like that. Up at the end. Like they're asking."

"What does it mean?"

"I don't know." He opened his eyes and looked at her. In the candlelight his face was wet. "I don't know what language it is. I've never heard it." He laughed, once, a sound with no breath in it. "I left the Order because I wanted to know. And there's someone in the salt asking a question, and I don't know what it is."

Hester looked past him, out over the black water, east, toward the Deep: three hundred miles of nothing, the White going on under the stars to the end of the world.

The wind was coming off the water. She had not noticed it start. It came across the Tear out of the east in a long, slow, steady breath, and it was not the Sere, which was dry and smelled of nothing. This was wet. It smelled of weed and salt and something under the salt she had no word for. It moved the candle-flames along the shore all together, every one of them bowing west, and lifted the edge of Teo's grey shawl, and went on past them over the ice toward the city.

She knelt on the new ice with the wind in her face. Her bare hand was on the crust, flat, the fingers open. She left it there.

Behind them on the shore, the whole city was singing a hymn about the tide. Out on the black water, very faint, very far off, something answered it. It might have been a gull.
